    - at least two consecutive guard clauses, `if` statements without `else` whose block is only `return value`, that end a function body before its tail value or final `return`. Earlier guards on another subject are left out.
    - When both sides of a comparison are operands, the subject is the one every condition shares, taken from the left of the first condition when both are shared. A comparison of an operand with itself, such as the `x != x` test for `nan`, is not counted.
    - It suggests `match subject { 4 => ..., < 0x80 => ..., (limit) => ..., > high => ..., _ => ... }`: an `==` test against a constant as the constant alone, an `==` test against another operand in parentheses, and any other as an operator arm.
  - `use-std` flags a function whose body repeats a public function of the standard library and suggests calling it, through the file's import of that module when there is one. Both bodies are reduced to a list of tests and results with the parameters numbered by position: guard clauses, an `if` / `else if` / `else` chain and a `match` with test and operator arms give the same list, `>` and `>=` are turned into `<` and `<=` with the operands swapped, and the operands of `==` and `!=` are put in order. The reduction accepts only literals, parameters, unary and binary operators, and needs at least one test. The parameter and result types must match those of the standard function, a type parameter matching any type that is the same at each of its uses. `prefer-match` does not report on a function that `use-std` reports. A module is not compared with its own functions. The standard functions come from `compiler/lint/std_index.low`, which `compiler/tablegen stdindex std compiler/lint` writes from every public function in `std/` whose parameter and result types are type parameters, numbers, `bool` or `str`. `std_index_matches_generator` in `compiler/tests/tablegen` fails when the committed index differs from the generator.
  - `prefer-loop-label` flags a `break` out of an inner loop that sets a boolean flag to a literal when the statement right after that loop is `if flag { break }` or `if flag { continue }` (or `if !flag` for a flag set to `false`), with no `else`. It suggests labelling the loop that `if` acts on and jumping to it with `break name` or `continue name`, reusing the label the `if` already names. Breaks inside a nested loop, closure, `spawn` or `defer` are not counted.
  - `method-call` flags `push`, `pop`, `truncate` and `delete` called as `push(mut x, v)` rather than as `x.push(v)`.
  - `prefer-const` flags a function with no parameters or type parameters that returns a number, `bool` or `str`, is never used as a value, and whose body is one expression that a constant can hold: literals, constants, operators and numeric conversions. It suggests `const name: T = ...` with the `()` dropped where the function is called.
- `low doc dir` prints, as JSON, every module under the directory with its imports, each with the name it is used through, and its public structs, enums, constants and functions, each with its declaration as written, its file and its line. A struct shows its public fields, and `...` stands for its private ones. A function whose first parameter is a public type of its module, or a pointer to one, names that type as its receiver. Each module lists its examples with their name, source and expected output. `low doc std` documents the standard library, and the website builds its package pages from it with `--target linux/amd64`. `--target` picks the operating system whose files are documented and defaults to the host.
- `low names file.low` type-checks the program and prints, as one JSON array, every name the file mentions: its `line` and byte `column` (both from 1), its `name`, the `hover` text and `doc` the language server shows for it, its `definition` (`path`, `line`, `column`, and `local` when it is in the same file) and `first`, the array index of the first name that resolves to the same definition. Names with the same `first` are one reference group. A field written in shorthand, as `x` in `Point { x }`, groups with the field, and the group of the local it reads starts at the shorthand when that comes first. A key is absent when the name has no such fact. A program that does not check prints the error and exits with status 1. `--target` picks the operating system whose files are checked and defaults to the host. The website build highlights and links every snippet from it.
- Imports come before declarations and top-level statements and apply to their file.
- A module is used through its last path segment, `text.shout(...)`, or through a name given with `import t = ui/text`. A path segment may hold `.` and `-` between identifiers, written without spaces, as in `import github.com/acme/ui`; when the last segment is not an identifier, the import needs a name: `import colors = color-names`.
- Types are written `geometry.Shape` and built with `geometry.Point { ... }`. A variant can be named `geometry.Shape.Dot` or, where the type is expected, `Dot`.
- Declarations and struct fields are private to their module unless marked `pub`. A `pub` enum's variants are public.
- A struct with private fields without defaults cannot be built with a literal outside its module.
- An import cycle is an error, except through the imports of test files, as is importing two modules under one name.

### Projects

```low
import std/build

build.title("Test App")
build.version("0.3.0")
build.dep("github.com/acme/ui", "^0.4")
build.local("../shared")
build.modules("src")
build.program("tool")
build.gui("src/app")
build.icon("assets/app.ico")
build.manifest("assets/app.manifest")
build.file_info("CompanyName", "Example Ltd")
build.file_info("ProductName", "Test App")
build.pre_build("npm", ["install"])
build.post_build("npm", ["run", "clean"])
```

- A `build.low` at the project root describes the project. The compiler reads it from its source and never runs it, so it holds only `import std/build` and top-level calls of `std/build` functions whose arguments are string literals, or slices of string literals for a hook's arguments. Anything else is an error at its position.
- `title` names the project and `version` gives its semantic version. Each is declared at most once.
- `dep(source, range)` names a dependency kept in a `git` repository at `https://<source>`. The source names directories in the module cache, so its `/`-separated segments are not empty, `.` or `..`, and it holds no `\`, `:` or `@`. Its versions are the repository's tags that parse as semantic versions, with or without a leading `v`. The range is `^1.2` (the versions up to the next change of the first non-zero part), `~1.2.3` (up to the next minor version, or the next major version for `~1`), `=1.2.3` (that version alone) or `1.2`, which means `^1.2`. A pre-release version is accepted only by an `=` range that names it.
- `local(dir)` names a dependency in a directory, relative to the `build.low` that declares it; `dir` may name that directory's `build.low`. It has no version and is never fetched.
- `modules(dir)` names the directory under the project root, `.` by default, that holds the project's own modules, so that a project with its code under `src/` imports `lib` and not `src/lib`. It is declared at most once and has the shape of a `program` directory. It changes where an import is placed and what `low test` calls a module, and nothing else: the project root stays the nearest `build.low` or `low.mod`, and hooks, `build.lock`, `dep` and `local` stay relative to the `build.low`. A `low.mod` or `build.low` directly inside the modules directory would take over as the root of everything below it, so loading the project, or the programs it declares, is an error: `<dir>/low.mod marks another project root inside the modules directory`; a build of a path that starts below such a marker finds that marker as its root and reads no declaration of the outer project.
- `program(dir)` declares a console program and `gui(dir)` a windowed program in the directory `dir`, relative to the `build.low`; either may repeat. `dir` is not empty, absolute, rooted or drive-lettered (`/x`, a backslash first, `C:/x`) and has no `..` segment, `\` counts as `/`, and a directory named by two declarations is an error. A declared directory must exist when a program of the project is built, run or installed. A dependency's declarations are checked and otherwise ignored.
- A program the `build.low` declares with `gui` is linked with the GUI subsystem by `low build`, `low run` and `low install` for `windows/amd64`, whichever way its directory is named, as a directory or as a file in it; `--subsystem` on `low build` overrides the declaration. The other targets ignore it, and `low test` never reads it, so a test program is a console program.
- `icon(file)` names a Windows `.ico` file relative to the `build.low`, written as a `program` directory is, and is declared at most once. Every program of the project that `low build`, `low run` and `low install` link for `windows/amd64` carries its images as the executable's icon, which Explorer, a shortcut and a pinned taskbar button show; a window's own icon is set apart from it, through `std/ui`.
- `manifest(file)` names the XML application manifest of the Windows executables, relative to the `build.low` and declared at most once like `icon`. It is where an application asks for per-monitor DPI awareness, long path awareness, the common controls, the UTF-8 code page and an execution level, and the executable carries it as the resource `RT_MANIFEST` number 1, so it needs no sidecar `.manifest` file. The file is written as it is, and must be well-formed XML whose root element is `assembly`.
- `version(number)` and `file_info(key, value)` give the executables their version information, which the file properties dialog and installers read. `version` sets the strings `FileVersion` and `ProductVersion` to the version in its canonical form, `1.4.0` or `1.4.0-rc.1`, and `file_info` sets the string `key`, such as `CompanyName`, `ProductName`, `FileDescription`, `InternalName`, `OriginalFilename` or `LegalCopyright`, to `value`. A `file_info` of `FileVersion` or `ProductVersion` overrides the string `version` gave, so it can carry a fourth number, and `file_info` may repeat for different keys but not for one key. The numeric file and product version of the fixed part come from the leading dotted numbers of `FileVersion` and `ProductVersion`, up to four and zero when absent, so `1.4.0-rc.1` is `1.4.0.0`. Each of those numbers is at most 65535. A project that declares neither `version` nor `file_info` has no version information.
- Every program of the project that is linked for `windows/amd64` carries what `icon`, `manifest`, `version` and `file_info` declare, console or windowed. For any target, a declared file that is missing or is a directory, an icon file that is not an icon file, holds no image or names an image outside itself, a manifest that is not well-formed XML with an `assembly` root, and version information that is too large for its block or has a version number above 65535 is an error at the declaration. `low test` and a dependency's declarations add no resources.
- With no program named, `low install` installs every declared program, and `low build` and `low run` build the one declared. They do this only in a directory that holds no `.low` file, whose project declares programs: any directory of the project qualifies, not only the root. `low run` then takes no program arguments. A directory with `.low` files builds as it did without a declaration.
- `pre_build(program, args)` and `post_build(program, args)` run a program in the project root, with the streams of `low`, before and after `low build`, `low run` and `low install` compile the program, in the order declared. A non-zero exit stops the command with `pre_build <program> exited with status <n>`. `low test` and the other commands run no hooks.
- `low build`, `low run` and `low test` resolve the version-control dependencies of the project and of every dependency's `build.low`, local or fetched. Each source gets the newest version that every range naming it accepts, unless `build.lock` holds a version they all accept, which is kept. `low update [dir]` resolves again to the newest accepted versions and rewrites `build.lock`.
- `build.lock` holds one line per dependency, `source version commit hash`, sorted by source, and is written when the resolution changes. `hash` is `h1:` followed by the standard base64 of the SHA-256 of a listing of the dependency's tree: one line per regular file, sorted by path, made of the file's SHA-256 in lower-case hexadecimal, two spaces, the path relative to the tree root with `/` separators, and a newline. This is the `h1` hash of Go's `dirhash`.
- Fetched dependencies live in a module cache shared by every project of the user. Its location is the `LOW_CACHE` environment variable; otherwise `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/low` or `$HOME/.cache/low` on Linux, and `%LOCALAPPDATA%\low` on Windows. `<cache>/git/<source>` is a bare mirror of the repository, and `<cache>/deps/<source>@<commit>` is the tree of one commit, written from `git archive` with line endings untouched and placed by a rename, so a tree is either complete or absent and projects that fetch at once share it. A tree already in the cache is reused.
- A published version never changes. A tree written to the cache whose hash differs from the one `build.lock` holds for that version fails with an error that names the source, the version, and the expected and actual hashes. When a resolution lists a source's tags and a tag that `build.lock` holds points at another commit, it fails with an error that names the source, the tag, and the old and new commits. The hash is checked when a tree is written to the cache, not on each use.
- The other commands and the language server read the trees in the cache through `build.lock`, and report a dependency that `build.lock` does not list, or whose tree is not in the cache, at its `dep`.
- A dependency's hooks never run.
- A tree written to the cache holds only regular files and directories: fetching a tag whose archive holds a symbolic link, a hard link or a device fails with an error that names the entry, so the hash covers everything the tree contains.
- In a module of a fetched dependency, `embed` and `embed_dir` fail with an error when the file's absolute path, or its real path once symbolic links resolve, is outside the dependency's tree. This covers `..` segments, absolute paths and links. A `local` dependency of a fetched dependency is confined to the same tree. The root project and the `local` dependencies of the root project are not confined.
- A dependency's modules are imported under its name: the source for a `dep`, the directory's last segment for a `local`. `import github.com/acme/ui` loads the dependency's modules directory, which is its root unless its `build.low` declares `modules`, and `import github.com/acme/ui/text` its `text/` module. Of a dependency's declarations only `modules` is used; its `program` and `gui` are checked and ignored. Inside a dependency, `import text` loads its own `text/`, so two projects may each hold a module of one name. A project imports only its own modules and those of the dependencies its `build.low` names. A module path that is both one of the project's directories and a dependency is an error, as is one name given to two dependency directories.
- Warnings are printed for the project's own modules only.

### Doc comments

```low
/// A point on the plane.
pub struct Point {
    /// Distance from the y axis.
    pub x: i64
    pub y: i64
}

/// Returns the point mirrored in the y axis.
pub fn mirror(p: Point) -> Point {
    return Point { x: -p.x, y: p.y }
}
```

- A line starting with `///` documents the declaration, struct field or enum variant directly below it. Consecutive `///` lines form one doc, written in Markdown; one space after `///` is dropped.
- A `///` line anywhere else is an error. `////` starts an ordinary comment.
- The language server shows a doc under the signature on hover, `low doc` prints it, and the website renders it on the package pages.

### Generics

<!-- prelude
struct Pair[A, B] {
    first: A
    second: B
}
-->
```low
struct Stack[T] {
    items: T[]
}

enum Tree[T: Ord] {
    Node(*Tree[T], T, *Tree[T])
    Leaf
}

fn max[T: Ord](a: T, b: T) -> T {
    if a > b { a } else { b }
}

fn sum[T: Number](xs: T[]) -> T {
    var total: T = 0
    for x in xs {
        total += x
    }
    total
}

fn sort[T](mut xs: T[], less: fn(T, T) -> bool) { ... }

print(max(3, 9))
print(max[u8](200, 100))
var s = Stack[str] { items: [] }
let p = Pair { first: 1, second: "x" }
let t: Tree[i64] = Leaf
let pick: fn(i64, i64) -> i64 = max
```

- Functions, structs and enums take type parameters in brackets after their name. Generic types are written with arguments: `Stack[i64]`, `geometry.Labelled[str]`.
- A constraint lists what a type parameter supports, joined with `+`:
  - `Eq` allows `==` and `!=`. It is met by numbers, `bool`, `str`, pointers, channels, and structs and enums whose fields are all `Eq`.
  - `Ord` adds `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=`. It is met by numbers and `str`.
  - `Hash` adds use as a map key. It is met by integers, `bool`, `str` and pointers.
  - `Number` adds arithmetic, numeric literals, conversion with `T(x)` and the `f`, `e` and `n` formats and pictures of interpolation.
  - `Integer` adds bitwise operators and shifts and the `d`, `x`, `X` and `b` formats, and implies `Hash`.
  - `Float` is met by `f64` and `f32`.
  - An unconstrained parameter can only be moved, stored, passed, printed and interpolated, with a width but no format, each instance rendering its own type. Anything else a generic function needs, such as an ordering of its elements, is passed in as a function value.
- A generic body is checked once against its constraints, so a missing constraint is reported at the definition, not at a use.
- Each generic function's type parameters are its own. Two functions may both name a parameter `T`, and a function type mentioning one is distinct from the same text mentioning the other.
- Type arguments are inferred from the arguments, then from the expected type. They can be written explicitly: `max[u8](a, b)`, `Stack[str] { ... }`.
- A generic struct literal or enum variant takes its arguments from the expected type or from its field values. A generic function used as a value takes them from the expected function type.
- Each distinct set of type arguments compiles to its own copy, `max[i64]` and `max[f64]`, so generic code runs as fast as code written for one type.
- Values of different kinds that share behaviour, such as the elements of a UI tree, are structs of function values whose closures capture each kind's state.

### Concurrency

<!-- prelude
let fd: i32 = 0
-->
```low
fn worker(jobs: chan[i64], results: chan[i64]) {
    for j in jobs {
        results <- j * 2
    }
}

let jobs = chan[i64](16)
let results = chan[i64](16)
spawn worker(jobs, results)
jobs <- 21
close(jobs)
print((<-results) ?? 0)
let next = select {
    v = <-results => v ?? -1
    jobs <- 5 => 0
    after(100) => -2
    else => -3
}
sleep(10)
wait_readable(fd)
```

- `spawn f(args)` starts a green thread running the call. The function value and the arguments are evaluated before it starts. `mut` arguments are not allowed. A function that can fail must have its error handled inside the spawned call.
- `chan[T](n)` makes a channel holding up to `n` values. `chan[T]()` is unbuffered, so every send waits for a receiver. A channel is a reference: assigning or passing it shares it.
- `c <- v` sends, waiting while the buffer is full.
- `<-c` receives a `T?`, waiting while the channel is empty. It gives `none` once the channel is closed and drained.
- `close(c)` wakes every waiter. Sending on or closing a closed channel panics.
- `for v in c` receives until the channel is closed and drained.
- `select` waits on several operations at once and runs the arm of the first that can proceed. It is an expression like `match`.
  - Arms are `name = <-c`, `<-c`, `c <- v`, `after(ms)` and `else`. `after` is a contextual keyword: it is an ordinary identifier everywhere except at the start of a `select` arm followed by `(`.
  - Channel and value expressions are evaluated first, in order.
  - Ready arms are taken in order.
  - `else` runs when nothing is ready. Without `else` the green thread waits.
  - `after(ms)` fires once `ms` milliseconds pass.
- `sleep(ms)` pauses the green thread.
- `wait_readable(fd)` and `wait_writable(fd)` wait until a file descriptor is ready, using epoll on Linux. On Windows `fd` is a waitable handle and both wait until it is signalled. One green thread may wait on a descriptor at a time.
- A program ends when `main` returns, whatever other green threads are doing.
- When every green thread is waiting and nothing can wake one, the program panics with `every green thread is blocked forever`. A green thread that exhausts its stack panics with `stack overflow`.

## Builtins

Every module sees these names without an import. A function in the program named `pop`, `truncate`, `close`, `sleep`, `wait_readable` or `wait_writable` takes precedence over the builtin. `compiler/check/builtins.low` is the only table of builtin names, parameters, `mut` markers, receivers and handlers, and `compiler/tests/check` requires an entry here for each public one.

### `bytes`

`bytes(s: str) -> u8[]` copies a string's bytes into a new slice.

### `close`

`close(c: chan[T])` closes a channel and wakes every waiter. Sending on or closing a closed channel panics.

### `cstr`

`cstr(s: str) -> ptr[u8]` is a NUL-terminated copy of a string, for passing to C.

### `delete`

`delete(mut m: [K: V], key: K)` removes a key from a map. Removing a missing key does nothing.

### `embed`

`embed(path: str) -> str` is the contents of a file, read at compile time. `path` is a string literal relative to the directory of the file that calls `embed`.

### `embed_dir`

`embed_dir(path: str) -> [str: str]` is the contents of every regular file under a directory, read at compile time and keyed by the file's path inside the directory. `path` is a string literal relative to the directory of the file that calls `embed_dir`.

### `exp`

`exp(x: f64) -> f64` is e raised to the power `x`. It takes and returns `f32` too.

### `floor`

`floor(x: f64) -> f64` rounds `x` down to a whole number. It takes and returns `f32` too.

### `index`

`e.index` is an `i64`: the index declared with `= n` on the variant an enum value holds. The enum's variants must have indexes; see `### Enums`.

### `len`

`xs.len` is an `i64`: the number of elements in a slice, the number of entries in a map, or the number of bytes in a `str`.

### `load`

`load(p: ptr[T]) -> T` reads one `T` from C memory. A null pointer traps.

### `new`

`new(value: T) -> *T` copies a value onto the garbage-collected heap and returns a pointer to it. A droppable value cannot go on the heap.

### `panic`

`panic(message: str)` writes `panic: ` and the message to standard error and exits with status 2. It never returns, so it can stand wherever a value is expected.

### `pin`

`pin(value) -> ptr` returns the address of a pointer, slice, map or string's data and keeps it alive until `unpin`. Memory C keeps after a call must be pinned.

### `pop`

`pop(mut xs: T[]) -> T?` removes the last element and returns it, or `none` when `xs` is empty. The capacity stays.

### `print`

`print(value)` writes a value of any type to standard output, followed by a newline. The text is the default rendering described under `### Strings`.

### `push`

`push(mut xs: T[], value: T)` appends `value`, growing the capacity to twice plus four when full.

### `sleep`

`sleep(ms)` pauses the green thread for `ms` milliseconds. `ms` is any integer type.

### `sqrt`

`sqrt(x: f64) -> f64` is the square root of `x`. It takes and returns `f32` too.

### `store`

`store(p: ptr[T], value: T)` writes one `T` to C memory.

### `truncate`

`truncate(mut xs: T[], n)` sets the length to `n` and keeps the capacity. `n` is any integer type. It panics unless `0 <= n <= xs.len`.

### `unpin`

`unpin(p: ptr)` releases an address returned by `pin`.

### `view`

`view(p: ptr[T], n) -> T[]` is a slice over `n` elements at `p`, bounds-checked like any slice. `n` is any integer type.

### `wait_readable`

`wait_readable(fd)` waits until a file descriptor is ready to read, using epoll. On Windows `fd` is a waitable handle and the wait ends when it is signalled. `fd` is any integer type. One green thread may wait on a descriptor at a time.

### `wait_writable`

`wait_writable(fd)` waits until a file descriptor is ready to write, using epoll. On Windows `fd` is a waitable handle and the wait ends when it is signalled. `fd` is any integer type. One green thread may wait on a descriptor at a time.

### Types

Calling a numeric type converts a number to it, as in `u8(x)`, `f64(n)` and `i64(f)`.

| Type | Values |
|---|---|
| `i64` | Signed 64-bit integers. An integer literal with no expected type is an `i64`. |
| `i32` | Signed 32-bit integers. |
| `u64` | Unsigned 64-bit integers. |
| `u32` | Unsigned 32-bit integers. |
| `u16` | Unsigned 16-bit integers. |
| `u8` | Unsigned 8-bit integers, and the bytes of a `str`. |
| `f64` | 64-bit IEEE 754 floats. A float literal is an `f64` unless an `f32` is expected. |
| `f32` | 32-bit IEEE 754 floats. |
| `bool` | `true` and `false`. |
| `str` | An immutable pointer and length over UTF-8 bytes. `str(b)` copies a `u8[]` into a string, and `str(p)` copies a NUL-terminated `ptr[u8]`. |
| `ptr` | `ptr[T]` is a nullable pointer to C memory. `ptr` alone is `void*` and converts to and from any `ptr[T]`. |
| `chan` | `chan[T]` is a channel of `T` values. `chan[T](n)` makes one holding up to `n` values, and `chan[T]()` an unbuffered one. |
| `u8x16` | 16 lanes of `u8`, 128 bits. |
| `u8x32` | 32 lanes of `u8`, 256 bits. |
| `u16x8` | 8 lanes of `u16`, 128 bits. |
| `u16x16` | 16 lanes of `u16`, 256 bits. |
| `i32x4` | 4 lanes of `i32`, 128 bits. |
| `i32x8` | 8 lanes of `i32`, 256 bits. |
| `u32x4` | 4 lanes of `u32`, 128 bits. |
| `u32x8` | 8 lanes of `u32`, 256 bits. |
| `i64x2` | 2 lanes of `i64`, 128 bits. |
| `i64x4` | 4 lanes of `i64`, 256 bits. |
| `u64x2` | 2 lanes of `u64`, 128 bits. |
| `u64x4` | 4 lanes of `u64`, 256 bits. |
| `f32x4` | 4 lanes of `f32`, 128 bits. |
| `f32x8` | 8 lanes of `f32`, 256 bits. |
| `f64x2` | 2 lanes of `f64`, 128 bits. |
| `f64x4` | 4 lanes of `f64`, 256 bits. |

### Constants

| Constant | Value |
|---|---|
| `true` | The true `bool`. |
| `false` | The false `bool`. |
| `none` | The empty optional, typed by its context. |
| `null` | The null `ptr`, typed by its context. |

## Standard library

```low
import std/file
import std/http
import std/http/websocket
import std/io
import std/net

fn handle(req: http.Request) -> http.Response {
    if req.path == "/chat" {
        using ws = websocket.accept(req) or return http.response(400, "")
        let m = websocket.receive(ws) or return http.upgraded()
        websocket.send_text(ws, str(m.data)) or {}
        return http.upgraded()
    }
    http.response(200, file.read_text("index.html") or "missing")
}

fn main() ! io.Error {
    let client = http.client().with_bearer_token("token").with_timeout(5000).build()
    let page = client.get("https://example.com/") or return
    using l = net.listen("", 8080)?
    http.serve(l, handle)?
}
```

`import std/<name>` loads a standard library module. The library is compiled into `low`, and the project root is not searched for it.

Every module except `std/math` and `std/sql/sqlite` talks to the kernel through raw syscalls. `std/math` calls libm and `std/sql/sqlite` calls libsqlite3 through `extern` blocks; `std/math/complex` and `std/math/fft` reach libm through `std/math`. A library is linked only when reachable code calls it, so a program stays static unless it calls one of their C functions.

Every public struct, enum, function, struct field and enum variant carries a doc comment, and `StdDocumented` in `compiler/tests/compiler` fails on any that does not. A doc is written for a caller reading it on hover:

- The first sentence is the summary: a verb phrase for a function (`Returns the ...`, `Parses ...`), a noun phrase for a type, field or variant.
- Later paragraphs carry the contract the signature cannot: which error variant is returned and when, whether the call blocks, what dropping or closing releases, limits, defaults and edge cases such as empty input or end of input.
- Identifiers and literals are in backticks. A `low` example block appears only where usage is not obvious from the signature, and it type-checks. It stays a few lines long; a program that shows how to use the module goes in the module's `examples` directory, where it runs.
- A doc states the current behaviour as read from the code.

| Module | Contents |
|---|---|
| `std/sys` | The operating system layer: Linux system calls, and kernel32, ws2_32 and the other Win32 libraries on Windows. It is the only standard library module besides the prelude that calls `__syscall`, and its Windows files call none. It owns every syscall number, flag, errno and structure layout and exposes typed wrappers that repeat an interrupted call and fail with an `io.Error`, so callers match `io.Error` variants. Descriptors: `FdHandle`, a copyable view whose closing is coordinated by an `io.Guard`, and the droppable `Fd` that owns one; `read`, `write`, `write_all`, `stream` and the `_on` forms on a view park the green thread while a non-blocking descriptor is not ready; `pipe`, `pipe_for_child`, `open_pipe` (a client end of an existing Windows named pipe; `Unsupported` on Linux), `set_nonblocking`, `try_read`, `try_write` and `wait_input`. Files: `open` with an `Open` mode, `stat`, `fstat`, `access`, `chmod`, `chown`, `set_times`, `unlink`, `rmdir`, `mkdir`, `rename`, `symlink`, `link`, `readlink`, `statfs`, `lock`, `seek`, `fsync`, `ftruncate`, `list_dir`, `random_bytes`, and `memory_file` and `seal`, which make an in-memory file and seal it against changes. Processes: `fork_exec`, `reap`, `kill`, `kill_group`, signal dispositions and handlers, identity, working directory, `arguments`, `environment`, `executable_path`, anonymous and executable memory, `map_fork_marker` (a page that reads 0 in a forked child) and `exit_process`. `error_message` turns an error number into the system's message text, and `os_code` gives the error number behind an `io.Error` (see `std/os`). File and anonymous mappings: `map_file` and `map_memory` take a `MapMode` (`ReadOnly`, `Shared`, `Private`) and return a `MapView`, on which `sync_view`, `advise_view` and `unmap_view` act and whose offsets are a multiple of `map_granularity` (a page on Linux, 64 KiB on Windows, where a view comes from `CreateFileMappingW` and `MapViewOfFile`, `sync_view` is `FlushViewOfFile` plus `FlushFileBuffers` through a duplicate of the file handle, and `advise_view` is `PrefetchVirtualMemory` for `WillNeed` and nothing otherwise). Limits: `get_rlimit` and `set_rlimit` over `prlimit64`, which fail with `Unsupported` on Windows, and `job_limits`, which reads the job object of the process on Windows and fails with `Unsupported` on Linux. Sockets: `socket`, `socketpair`, `bind`, `listen`, `accept`, `connect`, `finish_connect`, `recv`, `send`, `recv_from`, `send_to`, `recv_from_with_fd` and `send_to_with_fd` (a datagram with a descriptor passed as `SCM_RIGHTS`), `recv_with_fds` and `send_with_fds` (descriptors passed over a stream through `SCM_RIGHTS`, failing with `Unsupported` on Windows), `write_view`, `wait_socket`, `set_option`, `get_option`, `exclusive_address` (`SO_EXCLUSIVEADDRUSE` on Windows, nothing on Linux), `interface_index`, `interfaces` and multicast membership. `interfaces` returns each network interface as an `Interface` with its name, kernel index, `up`, `loopback` and `multicast` flags and its IPv4 and IPv6 addresses as `InterfaceAddress` values of address bytes and prefix length. On Linux it sends an `RTM_GETLINK` and an `RTM_GETADDR` dump request over a blocking `NETLINK_ROUTE` socket and reads the replies, matching addresses to links by index, taking `IFA_LOCAL` before `IFA_ADDRESS`, reading the name from `IFLA_IFNAME` and the flags from `IFF_UP`, `IFF_LOOPBACK` and `IFF_MULTICAST`, and failing with the error number of an `NLMSG_ERROR` reply. On Windows it calls `GetAdaptersAddresses` for `AF_UNSPEC` with anycast, multicast and DNS server lists skipped, grows the buffer while the call reports `ERROR_BUFFER_OVERFLOW`, and reads the adapter list and each adapter's unicast list at their documented 64-bit offsets, every pointer converted to an offset into the returned buffer and checked against its bounds. The name is the adapter's `FriendlyName`, the index `IfIndex` or `Ipv6IfIndex` when that is 0, `up` is an operational status of `IfOperStatusUp`, `loopback` an `IfType` of 24, `multicast` the absence of `IP_ADAPTER_NO_MULTICAST` and a prefix length `OnLinkPrefixLength`. Terminals: `termios`, `raw_mode`, window size and `open_pty`. `inotify_init`, `inotify_add` and `inotify_remove`. Input log: `record_inputs(fd)` makes `record(op, name)` write one line `<op> <len> <name>` to the descriptor `fd` for each file-system and environment input the process reads, and a negative `fd` stops it; leaving a descriptor that is logging writes `redirect 0 ` to it, so its reader knows the log is incomplete; `record` does nothing while logging is off, writes each line in one call under a lock and ignores write errors. `open`, `stat`, `access`, `statfs` and `inotify_add` (`stat`), `list_dir` (`list`), `readlink` and `fork_exec` (`exec`) record the path as passed, and `std/os` records `chdir`, `env` (`getenv`) and `environment` (`environ`, with an empty name). Writes that do not open a file, such as `unlink`, `rename`, `mkdir`, `chmod` and `set_times`, record nothing. The state is the module's `__static_block`. |
| `std/io` | `Error`, the error type shared by the I/O, codec and protocol modules. `Stream`, a copyable struct of `read`, `write` and `close` closures that views a resource. `Guard` and `Lease` (`guard`, `enter`, `retire`), the shared state that makes a closed descriptor fail with `NotConnected` instead of reaching a reused descriptor. A buffered `Reader` over a stream or, with `bytes_reader`, over a buffer, with `read_line`, `read_exact`, `read_up_to`, `read_some`, `read_byte`, `buffered` and `fill`. `write_all`, `read_all`, `buffer(n)`, `copy`, and the fixed-width integer codec in both byte orders: reads (`be16`, `be24`, `be32`, `be64` and the `le` forms as `i64`, `u16_be`, `u32_be`, `u64_be` and the `_le` forms as unsigned types, `uint_be` and `uint_le` for any width from 1 to 8 bytes as `u64`, and `u16_le_str`, `u32_le_str` and `u64_le_str` over a `str`), appends (`put_be16` through `put_be64`, `put_le16` through `put_le64`, and `put_be` and `put_le` for any width up to 8 bytes) and in-place stores (`set_be16` through `set_be64`, `set_le16` through `set_le64`). |
| `std/file` | Opening: `open`, `create`, `append` and `update` return a droppable `File` whose `handle` is a copyable `FileHandle`. On a `File`: `read`, `write`, `write_all`, `seek`, `sync`, `truncate`, `close`, `info` and `stat`. On a `FileHandle`: `read_on`, `write_on`, `write_all_on`, `close_on` and `stream_on`. Whole files and directories: `exists`, `read_file`, `read_text`, `write_file`, `write_text`, `remove`, `remove_dir`, `rename`, `mkdir`, `mkdir_all`, `list` and `stream`. `read_file` sizes its buffer from the file's size; a file that reports no size, such as one under `/proc`, is read into a 64 KB buffer that doubles when full. Permission bits: `create_mode`, `write_file_mode`, `write_text_mode`, `mkdir_mode` and `mkdir_all_mode` take them, the other creators use `0o644` for files and `0o755` for directories, all are filtered by the process umask, and `chmod` is not. Locks: `try_lock` and `unlock` are advisory `flock` locks that report a held lock as `false` instead of waiting. `remove_all` removes a tree without following symbolic links. `temp_dir` creates a private directory under `os.temp_dir_path()`, which is `TMPDIR` when set and `/tmp` otherwise; `temp_file` and `temp_dir_pattern` use it when `dir` is empty and all three draw the random name suffix from `getrandom`. `child_path` joins a directory and an entry name. On Windows `mkdir_all`, `child_path` and the `temp_file` and `temp_dir_pattern` name check treat `\` as a separator as well as `/`, and `mkdir_all` never creates a drive (`C:\`), share (`\\server\share\`) or verbatim (`\\?\C:\`) prefix; the separator and volume rules are `std/filepath`'s, which `copy` and `walk` also use to take apart the paths they are given. `Info` also carries `regular`, `link`, `accessed`, `uid`, `gid`, `device` and `inode`. Further functions: `lstat`, `is_dir`, `is_file`, `is_symlink`, `symlink`, `link`, `readlink`, `realpath`, `chown`, `lchown`, `set_times`, `disk_usage` and `read_dir_info`. `walk` is a sorted depth-first walk that uses `lstat`, never descends into symbolic links and lets its callback return `Continue`, `Skip` or `Stop`. `glob` matches each segment with `path.matches` and supports `**`. `copy` and `copy_dir` keep file modes and recreate symbolic links. `temp_file(dir, pattern)` creates a uniquely named file with `O_EXCL` and mode `0600` and returns a droppable `TempFile` that closes and removes it unless `keep` is called. `temp_dir_pattern` makes a `0700` directory the same way. Mappings: `map` maps a range of an open file and `map_anonymous` a region of zeros, each with a `MapMode` (`ReadOnly`, `Shared` or `Private` copy-on-write) and returns a droppable `Mapping` whose `data` is a `u8[]` over memory outside the collector's heap, so it is neither scanned nor moved and stays valid until the `Mapping` is dropped or `unmap` releases it. A copy of `data` used afterwards, and a write to a `ReadOnly` mapping, panic with `invalid memory access`, and `push` onto a copy reallocates on the heap. `offset` need not be aligned, because the mapping starts at the enclosing page (Linux) or 64 KiB boundary (Windows) and `data` begins at `offset`; a `length` of `0` maps to the end of the file, and a range that leaves the file is `Invalid`. An empty range gives an empty `data` with nothing to release. `sync_map` takes `Wait` or `Queue` (`msync`, or `FlushViewOfFile` and, for `Wait`, `FlushFileBuffers` on Windows) and `advise_map` takes `Normal`, `Random`, `Sequential`, `WillNeed` or `DontNeed` (`madvise`, or `PrefetchVirtualMemory` for `WillNeed` on Windows and nothing for the rest). |
| `std/file/watch` | inotify. `watch` and `watch_with` take paths and `Options` (`recursive`, `access`) and return a droppable `Watcher` with a copyable `WatcherHandle`. On a watcher: `add`, `remove`, `next`, `events` and `stop`. `next` parks the green thread until an `Event` (`watched`, `name`, `path`, `kind`, `dir`, `cookie`) arrives. `events` is a `chan[Event]` fed by a forwarding green thread. `Kind` is `Created`, `Modified`, `Deleted`, `MovedFrom`, `MovedTo`, `Attrib`, `Closed`, `ClosedUnmodified`, `Opened`, `Accessed`, `SelfDeleted`, `SelfMoved` or `Overflow`. |
| `std/net` | Addresses: IPv4 and IPv6 `Addr`, `parse_ip`, `format_ip`, `parse_addr`, `format`, `in_network` (tests an address against a CIDR prefix) and `is_global` (false for addresses the IANA special-purpose registries mark as not globally reachable, and for multicast). Types: the droppable `Listener`, `Conn` and `Socket`, each with a copyable `ListenerHandle`, `ConnHandle` or `SocketHandle` in its `handle` field; `adopt` and `detach` convert between a `ConnHandle` and a `Conn`, and `adopt_socket` and `detach_socket` between a `SocketHandle` and a `Socket`. TCP: `listen`, `accept`, `accept_handle` (returns the connection's view for a green thread to adopt), `connect`, `dial` and their `_within` forms with a connect timeout, `connect_through` (binds the socket to a network interface first), `connect_first` (tries a list of addresses in order through an optional interface), `interface_index` and `interfaces`, which lists the machine's network interfaces as `Interface` values holding `name`, `index`, `up`, `loopback`, `multicast` and `addresses`, each an `InterfaceAddr` of the same `ip` bytes `parse_ip` returns and a `prefix` length, and fails with an `io.Error` when the system's tables cannot be read. Connection I/O: `read`, `write`, `write_all`, `close`, `shutdown_write`, `set_nodelay`, read and write timeouts and `stream`, with `read_on`, `write_on`, `write_all_on`, `close_on` and `stream_on` on the view. UDP: `bind_udp`, `send_to`, `receive_from` and `close_socket`, with `send_to_on`, `receive_from_on` and `close_socket_on` on the view; a `SocketHandle` has separate `fd` and `out` descriptors, so one green thread can wait to receive while another waits to send, and closing the socket fails a receive that is waiting with `NotConnected`. Unix datagram sockets use the same functions: `bind_unix_datagram` binds a pathname or abstract name and unlinks the file when closed, `unix_datagram` makes a sender with no name, whose datagrams `receive_from` reports with an empty `Addr`, and `send_to_with_fd` and `receive_from_with_fd` (with `_on` forms) pass a descriptor along with a datagram. Names: `lookup` returns the addresses of a host through `resolve` with `system_resolver`, so it tries an IP literal, the hosts file, `localhost` and then DNS; it fails with `NotFound` when the name does not exist or has no address. A `Resolver` (`resolver` takes a `ResolverConfig` from `resolver_config`; `system_resolver` reads `system_config`) carries the servers, search list, `ndots`, timeout, attempts, rotation, EDNS(0) and TCP-only settings, an optional `cancel.Token` and the `pick` function `lookup_srv` draws from. `resolve` returns `dns.Error` (`NoSuchName`, `NoData`, `ServerFailure`, `FormatError`, `NotImplemented`, `Refused` or `Io`). `lookup_mx`, `lookup_txt`, `lookup_srv`, `lookup_caa`, `lookup_ptr`, `lookup_cname` and `lookup_ns` return typed records, with MX ordered by preference and SRV by priority and a weighted shuffle (RFC 2782). `parse_resolv_conf` reads `nameserver` (up to three, IPv4 or IPv6), `search`, `domain` and `options ndots timeout attempts rotate edns0 use-vc`, with `LOCALDOMAIN` and `RES_OPTIONS` overriding the file. The search list follows `ndots`: a name with at least that many dots is tried as given first and a name with a trailing dot only as given. The Linux target reads `/etc/resolv.conf` and `/etc/hosts`; the Windows target takes up to three servers and the primary domain from `GetNetworkParams` and reads the hosts file under the system directory. `std/net/dns` holds the message codec. An `Addr` with an empty `ip` is a Unix address: `listen_unix`, `connect_unix`, `connect_unix_within` and `socketpair` return the same `Listener` and `Conn`, abstract names start with `@` or NUL, and a listener unlinks its pathname when closed. `peer_credentials` returns the peer's credentials. `read_with_fds` and `read_with_fds_on` return an `FdRead` (the byte count and the descriptors that arrived with them), and `write_with_fds` and `write_with_fds_on` attach descriptors to a write; the caller closes the received descriptors with `sys.close_fd`. `listen_with` and `bind_udp_with` take `ListenOptions` (`reuse_addr`, `reuse_port`, `backlog`); `listen` sets `reuse_addr` and `bind_udp` does not, so no two UDP sockets share a port unless both ask for it. Socket options: `set_keepalive` and `set_buffer_sizes`. UDP multicast: `join_group`, `leave_group`, `set_multicast_ttl`, `set_multicast_loop` and `set_multicast_interface`. `Dialer` is a function value that makes a connection; `direct_dialer` is the plain one. |
| `std/net/sntp` | An SNTP client (RFC 4330). `request` writes the 48-byte version 4 client packet and `parse` checks a reply against the `Stamp` that was sent, in order for length, mode 4, version, the originate timestamp, kiss-o'-death (stratum 0, with the code as `KissOfDeath`), a leap indicator of 3, stratum 1 to 15 and a nonzero transmit timestamp, then returns a `Reply` with the offset `((t2 - t1) + (t3 - t4)) / 2` and delay `(t4 - t1) - (t3 - t2)` in nanoseconds. A `Stamp` is the 32-bit seconds and 32-bit fraction of an NTP timestamp; `to_stamp` and `to_unix_nanos` convert it with the era rule of section 3, so seconds with the top bit clear count from 2036-02-07T06:28:16Z, for times from 1968 to 2104. `query` resolves a host, sends one request from its own UDP socket with the low 8 bits of the transmit stamp random, ignores datagrams from other sources, and tries the next address when one fails with `Io`. `Options` sets the port, an overall timeout, the clock that supplies `t1` and `t4`, and a cancellation token that ends the wait with `Io(Aborted)`. |
| `std/net/punycode` | Punycode (RFC 3492): `encode` writes a string as ASCII digits without the `xn--` prefix and `decode` reads them back, both failing with `Error`: `Overflow`, `InvalidUtf8`, `BadDigit`, `Truncated` or `InvalidCodePoint`. |
| `std/net/idna` | Internationalised domain names by UTS #46 (Unicode 16.0.0): `to_ascii(domain, Options)` returns the `xn--` form and fails with an `Error` that names the failed check, and `to_unicode(domain, Options)` returns an `Unicode` holding the converted text and the first error. `Options` holds `check_hyphens`, `check_bidi`, `check_joiners`, `use_std3_ascii_rules` and `verify_dns_length`, all `true` by default, and `transitional`, `false` by default. |
| `std/net/publicsuffix` | The Mozilla Public Suffix List, embedded: `public_suffix` returns a `Suffix` with its name and its `Section` (`Icann`, `Private` or `Unlisted`), `registrable_domain` the eTLD+1 and `is_public_suffix` a `bool`, each taking `Rules.All` or `Rules.Icann`. Names go in and come back in the form the caller wrote, ASCII, `xn--` or Unicode, and match through `std/net/idna`. `list_version` and `list_commit` report the embedded list. |
| `std/quic` | QUIC version 1 (RFC 9000, 9001 and 9002) over UDP. `Config` holds the `tls.Config` (ALPN required), idle and handshake timeouts, keep-alive, the flow control and stream limits advertised to the peer, and `retry`. Server: `listen` returns a droppable `Listener` with a copyable `ListenerHandle`, and `accept` and `accept_handle` wait for an established connection; `close_listener` and `close_listener_on`. Client: `dial`, `dial_handle`, `connect` and `connect_handle` complete the handshake before returning. The droppable `Connection` closes with code 0 when released; its `*ConnectionHandle` holds the negotiated `protocol`, the addresses, `group`, `suite`, `peer_certificates` and, once ended, `close_code`, `close_reason`, `closed_by_peer` and `application_close`. `adopt`, `detach`, `close_connection`, `close_on`, `stats` and `stats_on` (a `Stats` snapshot), and `update_keys` and `update_keys_on`. Streams: `open_stream`, `open_uni_stream`, `accept_stream` and `accept_uni_stream` with `_on` forms return a copyable `Stream`; `read`, `write`, `finish`, `reset`, `stop` and `stream`, which adapts it to an `io.Stream`. |
| `std/quic/wire` | QUIC encoding with no I/O: a bounds-checked `Cursor` with varints and packet numbers, `parse_header` for long and short headers, `version_negotiation`, packet protection (`keys`, `initial_keys`, `next_keys`, `seal`, `open`, `peek_key_phase`) with header protection, Retry packets and their integrity tag (`retry_packet`, `retry_tag`, `retry_valid`), the frames as one `Frame` enum (`parse_frames`, `put_frame`, `frame_size`, `put_stream_frame`, `ack_eliciting`, `ack_delay_ns`), transport parameters (`encode_parameters`, `decode_parameters`) and the transport error codes. Every parser fails `Malformed` on hostile input and never panics. |
| `std/quic/recovery` | RFC 9002 loss detection and congestion control with no I/O: `recovery` makes a `Recovery` that `on_sent`, `on_ack`, `on_timeout` and `discard_space` drive per packet number `Space`. It keeps the RTT estimate, declares packets lost by packet and time thresholds, runs the probe timeout and NewReno with persistent congestion, and answers `can_send`, `deadline` and `pto_duration`. `skip` records a packet number left unsent, and an ACK covering one fails `Malformed`. |
| `std/net/dns` | DNS messages (RFC 1035, 3596, 2782, 6891, 8659) with no I/O. `Message` holds the header flags, the 12-bit `rcode` (the extended bits come from the OPT record), `questions` and the answer, authority and additional `Record`s; a `Record`'s `data` is a `Data`: `A`, `Aaaa`, `Ns`, `Cname`, `Ptr`, `MxData`, `Txt`, `SrvData`, `SoaData`, `CaaData`, `Opt` (with `EdnsOption`s; the record's class is the UDP payload size and its ttl the version and flags) or `Raw` for a type it does not know, which keeps its bytes. `encode` writes a message with name compression and fails `Invalid` or `TooLarge` for a label over 63 bytes, a name over 255 octets or a field that does not fit; SRV targets are not compressed. `decode` follows compression pointers that point strictly backwards, at most 127 per name, and fails `Malformed` on a loop, a forward pointer, a reserved label type, a bad length or a record whose data does not fill its length; `decode_head` reads the header and questions only. A decoded message shares no memory with its input and trailing bytes are ignored. Names are strings without a trailing dot (`.` for the root) with `\.`, `\\` and `\DDD` escapes. `query` builds a recursive query with an OPT record when the payload size is positive, `reverse_name` the `in-addr.arpa` or `ip6.arpa` name of an address, `same_name` compares names without ASCII case, `order_mx` sorts by preference and `order_srv` applies the RFC 2782 weighted selection through a `pick` function. `Error` is `NoSuchName`, `NoData`, `ServerFailure`, `FormatError`, `NotImplemented`, `Refused` or `Io`, and the type, class and rcode numbers are constants. |
| `std/socks` | SOCKS5 (RFC 1928, with RFC 1929 username and password) and SOCKS4a clients: `dial`, `dial4a`, `tunnel` and `tunnel4a` over an existing connection, and `dialer` and `dialer4a`, which return a `net.Dialer`. The proxy resolves the target name. |
| `std/ssh` | SSH 2.0 clients and servers. `client`, `dial`, `dial_through` (ProxyJump) and `serve_stream`, `serve`, `serve_on` return a droppable `Connection` whose `handle` is the copyable `*ConnectionHandle`; dropping it stops the reader and fails pending requests. `ClientConfig` carries the user, the host key callback, the `AuthMethod` list (`NoAuth`, `Password`, `PublicKey`, `KeyboardInteractive`), the rekey limits and `handshake_timeout`; `ServerConfig` carries the host keys, `ServerAuth` callbacks, the channel and global request handlers and the same limits. A droppable `Channel` has `read_on`, `write_on`, `write_stderr_on`, `close_write_on`, `stream` and `wait_exit_on`. Sessions: `open_session`, `open_session_on`, `exec`, `exec_on`, `shell_on`, `subsystem_on`, `open_subsystem`, `pty_on`, `env_on`, `window_change_on`, `signal_on`, and on the server `next_request_on`, `answer_on`, `parse_exec`, `parse_pty`, `parse_env`, `send_exit_status_on` and `send_exit_signal_on`. Forwarding: `direct_tcpip`, `direct_tcpip_on`, `direct_streamlocal`, `listen_local`, `listen_remote`, `accept_forward`, `cancel_forward`, `relay`, `serve_direct_tcpip` and `forward_global`. `keepalive`, `key_exchanges`, `wait_on` and `disconnect_on` complete the connection operations. The `*ConnectionHandle` is the thread-safe, copyable view of a live connection: `spawn` takes it where it refuses the droppable `Connection`, and `open_session_on`, `direct_tcpip_on` and `keepalive_on` run on it from any green thread, so a program can run its own accept loop and open one `direct-tcpip` channel per client; global requests take turns on the connection, channel opens are matched to their replies by channel number, and once the connection ends all three fail instead of hanging. |
| `std/ssh/wire` | The data types of RFC 4251 section 5: a `Reader` (`reader`, `read_u8`, `read_bool`, `read_u32`, `read_u64`, `read_string`, `read_str`, `read_mpint`, `read_names`, `read_bytes`, `left`, `offset`, `rest`, `finish`) that fails with `Malformed` on short, negative or non-minimal input, and the `put_*` writers `put_u32`, `put_u64`, `put_bool`, `put_string`, `put_str`, `put_mpint` and `put_names`, with `has_name`. |
| `std/ssh/key` | OpenSSH key formats: public key lines (`parse_line`, `format_line`, `fingerprint`), `authorized_keys` with options (`parse_authorized_keys`, `find_authorized`, `authorized_option`), `known_hosts` with hashed names, `@cert-authority` and `@revoked` (`parse_known_hosts`, `check_host_key`, `known_hosts_verifier`, `trust_on_first_use`), `openssh-key-v1` private keys with `aes256-ctr` and bcrypt KDF encryption and PEM keys through `std/crypto/x509` (`load_private_key`, `parse_private_key`), `Signer`, `sign`, `verify`, and certificates (`parse_certificate`, `certificate_signed_by`, `check_certificate`, `certified_signer`). |
| `std/ssh/agent` | An ssh-agent client over a Unix socket, or on Windows a named pipe: `connect`, `connect_env` (`SSH_AUTH_SOCK`, and on Windows `\\.\pipe\openssh-ssh-agent` when it is unset), `connect_pageant` (Pageant through its window, on Windows), `connect_any` (`SSH_AUTH_SOCK`, then on Windows the OpenSSH pipe and Pageant), `identities`, `sign` and `signers`, which returns `key.Signer`s that `AuthMethod.PublicKey` takes. |
| `std/ssh/sftp` | SFTP version 3. `connect` over a stream and `open` over an SSH connection return a droppable `Client` whose `handle` is the `*Session` every operation takes: `stat`, `lstat`, `set_attrs`, `remove`, `mkdir`, `rmdir`, `real_path`, `rename`, `read_link`, `symlink`, `list`, `open_dir`, `read_dir`, `read_file`, `write_file`, and the OpenSSH extensions `posix_rename`, `hardlink`, `statvfs` and `limits`. `open_file` returns a droppable `File` with `read`, `read_at`, `write`, `write_at`, `seek`, `fstat`, `set_file_attrs`, `sync`, `flush`, `close` and `stream`, which gives an `io.Stream`. `serve` runs the server over a stream and `handler` returns the channel handler that serves the `sftp` subsystem for `ssh.ServerConfig`; both are confined to a root directory. |
| `std/pipe` | Pipelines. A `Pipe` owns a `Reader` and a `Writer`, each droppable so that releasing it completes it, with copyable `ReaderHandle` and `WriterHandle` views for green threads (`adopt_reader`, `adopt_writer` and the `_on` operations). Also segmented buffers, backpressure, `Sequence` and `SequenceReader`, and adapters between pipes and streams. |
| `std/cancel` | `Token` with `token`, `child`, `cancel`, `cancelled`, `check` and `done` (a channel that closes on cancellation, for `select`). `on_cancel` returns a droppable `Registration` that releases its callback; `release`, `on_cancel_handle` and `release_on` work on the copyable `RegistrationHandle`. `pause` is a sleep that ends early on cancellation. |
| `std/header` | The header primitives below `std/http`, `std/multipart` and `std/mail`: `Header`, `get`, `values`, `has`, `add`, `set`, `remove`, `token`, `token_byte`, `valid_value`, `valid`, `quote`, and `parse_value` with `param`, which resolve RFC 2231 and 5987 parameters. |
| `std/http` | Server types: `Request`, `Response`, header helpers over `header.Header` and `parse_url`. `Request.token` is cancelled when the client goes away and when the handler returns. `Request.remote` is the client's IP address for connections served by `serve`, `serve_tls` and `serve_conn`. Serving: `serve`, `serve_tls`, `serve_conn` and `serve_stream`, and `serve_quic` for HTTP/3 over a `std/quic` listener; the `_on` forms (`serve_on`, `serve_tls_on`, `serve_conn_on`, `serve_quic_on`) take the listener's or connection's view. Client: a pooled client configured through `client()` and `with_*` builders, per-request builders, `send`, `stream_to` and `download`. `with_version(Http3)` sends `https` requests over QUIC. `with_interface` binds every connection to a network interface. `with_resolver` substitutes for `net.lookup` on host names; TLS keeps the host name for SNI and certificate checks. `with_cancel` makes a request fail with `Io(Aborted)` once its token is cancelled. URLs: `parse_url`, `resolve` and `with_query_pair` build on `std/uri`. `connect` returns a droppable `Connection` that closes its stream when released; `detach` hands the stream on. Proxies: `with_proxy`, `with_http_proxy`, `with_https_proxy`, `with_no_proxy`, `with_proxy_from_environment` and `with_dialer` send requests through an `http://` proxy (absolute-form for `http` targets, `CONNECT` for `https` targets) or a `socks5://`, `socks5h://`, `socks4://` or `socks4a://` proxy. `no_proxy` follows Go's matching rules. The one content-coding table gives each coding its encoder and decoder; `accepts_coding`, `negotiate_coding` and `encode_body` read it, and the client's decompression and `std/web` compression and static files share it. `Cookie` is the one cookie model: the jar stores it, `request_cookies` reads the `cookie` headers into it and `set_cookie` and `format_set_cookie` write it, and `std/web` sessions use it. `CookieJar` is a shared RFC 6265 jar (`cookie_jar`, `cookie_jar_on` for a jar that reads a `clock.Clock`, `with_cookie_jar`, `jar_set`, `jar_header`, `jar_cookies`, `jar_clear`) that enforces the `Secure` rules and the `__Secure-` and `__Host-` prefixes, rejects a `Domain` that is a public suffix other than the request host, and converts a `Domain` and a URL host with non-ASCII characters to their `xn--` form. A `Response` whose `stream` is a `chan[u8[]]` is written as it is produced, chunked on HTTP/1.1 and as DATA frames on HTTP/2, until the handler closes the channel; its `finish` function, when set, returns the trailer fields sent after the last chunk. Duplex serving: `serve_duplex_conn_on` and `serve_duplex_tls_conn_on` serve one HTTP/2 connection whose handlers receive `Request.upload` while they answer, and stop accepting streams with a `GOAWAY` when the drain channel closes. `receive_body` and `receive_body_until` read an `Upload`. Calls: `open_call`, `call_send`, `call_receive` and `call_close` run one HTTP/2 exchange over the client's connection with a request body that is sent as the caller produces it and a response read as `CallEvent`s. |
| `std/grpc` | gRPC (the HTTP/2 protocol of grpc.io) over `std/http`: `Channel`, `Server`, `Code`, `Status`, `Metadata`, deadlines, compression, `grpc.health.v1` and the support for the services `std/protobuf/proto` generates. |
| `std/http/websocket` | `accept` (the `101` goes through `http.upgrade`) and `dial` over `ws` and `wss` return a droppable `Socket` that sends a normal close when released. Operations: `receive`, `send_text`, `send_binary`, `ping` and `close`, with `_on` forms, and `adopt` and `detach` on the `*SocketHandle`. |
| `std/http/sse` | Server-sent events. Shared: `Event` (`id`, `event`, `data`, `retry`), `format`, `comment` and `keepalive`. Server: `stream`, `stream_with`, `serve_events`, `serve_events_with`, `Emitter` and `last_event_id`. Client: `parser`, `feed`, `parse` and `listen`, which reads a `text/event-stream` response with any chunking and CR, LF or CRLF line ends. |
| `std/http/files` | `serve_file` and `serve_file_with` answer a request from a file. Content type comes from the extension, then sniffing. Weak `ETag` and `Last-Modified` work with `If-None-Match` and `If-Modified-Since`. A single `Range` is answered as 206 or 416, with `If-Range`. `HEAD` is supported and the body is streamed. |
| `std/http/huffman` | The static Huffman code of RFC 7541 appendix B that HPACK and QPACK share: `decode`, `encode` and `encoded_size`. `decode` fails `Malformed` on the EOS symbol and on padding that is longer than 7 bits or not all ones, and never panics on any input. |
| `std/http/qpack` | QPACK (RFC 9204) with no I/O. `Decoder` (`decoder` takes the maximum table capacity, the blocked stream limit and the field section size limit) keeps the dynamic table: `on_encoder_stream` consumes encoder stream bytes split anywhere, buffers at most 64 KiB of an incomplete instruction and returns the blocked streams that can now decode; `decode` returns `Decoded`, either `Fields` or `Blocked`, and queues a Section Acknowledgment; `cancel_stream` queues a Stream Cancellation; `take_decoder_stream` returns the queued decoder stream bytes plus one Insert Count Increment. `Encoder` writes field sections with the static table only, so they never block and nothing goes on the encoder stream; `on_decoder_stream` accepts Stream Cancellations and fails `Malformed` on any other instruction. `decompression_failed`, `encoder_stream_error` and `decoder_stream_error` return the HTTP/3 codes `0x200`, `0x201` and `0x202` for failures of `decode`, `on_encoder_stream` and `on_decoder_stream`. Every entry point fails `Malformed` on hostile input: integers are capped at 2^62-1, and strings, indexes, Required Insert Counts, Bases, entry sizes and capacities are checked before use. `static_table`, `insert_count`, `table_size` and `table_entries` expose the tables. |
| `std/web` | A router with groups, middleware and typed JSON endpoints, problem details (RFC 9457), an OpenAPI 3.1 document built from the types, cookie sessions, CSRF protection, API key, Basic and bearer authentication, CORS and response compression. `static` and `static_with` serve a directory. `events` and `event_stream` serve server-sent events. |
| `std/tls` | TLS 1.2 and 1.3 clients and servers. `client`, `server` and `dial` return a droppable `Session` that sends `close_notify` and closes the stream beneath it when released. Its `handle` is the shared `*SessionHandle` holding `protocol`, `version`, `group`, `suite`, `cipher_suite` and `peer_certificates`. `client_handle` and `server_handle` return the view alone, and `read_on`, `write_on`, `close_on` and `stream_on` operate on it. `quic_client` and `quic_server` run the TLS 1.3 handshake of RFC 9001 through a `QuicTransport` of callbacks that send and receive handshake bytes per `Level` and install each `QuicSecret`, and return a `QuicResult`. `suite_hash`, `key_size`, `cipher` and `expand_label` derive keys for a `Suite`. `Config.staples` holds the OCSP responses a server staples and `Config.revocation` the revocation check of a client; see Certificate status. `Config.credentials` is a `CredentialSource` whose `choose` takes a `HelloInfo` (server name and offered protocols) and returns the `Credentials` (chain, key, staples and the protocol to select) of one handshake. |
| `std/serde` | The `Writer` and `Reader` protocols, `write[T]`, `read[T]` and `schema[T]` (which the compiler derives for every type), and `Field[T, F]`. Readers share `parse_integer`, `parse_unsigned` and `syntax_at`, which builds the `Syntax` error for a line and column; the helpers the derived code calls are private. `Tree`, `Node` and `Member` are the value shape that CBOR, MessagePack, TOML, YAML and template data share: `to_tree` builds one from any value, `tree_reader` reads a `T` from one, and `key_text` and `unsigned_tree` hold the key text and the `u64` range rule. A format maps its own value type `V` to a tree with `node_of` and a `Shape[V]` function, and back with `value_of` and a `Rebuild[V]`. |
| `std/json` | A reader and writer over UTF-8 bytes, the `Value` document model with member builders (`text_member`, `int_member`, `bool_member`, `base64url_member`, `object_member`, `array_member`) and typed getters (`field`, `field_text`, `field_int`, `text_of`, `int_of`), `equal` (compares numbers numerically and objects without regard to member order), and `serialize[T]` and `deserialize[T]` with naming policies. |
| `std/json/pointer` | RFC 6901 pointers: `escape`, `unescape`, `parse`, `format`, `get`, `has`, `set`, `add`, `replace`, `remove` and relative pointers. Edits return a new document. |
| `std/json/patch` | RFC 6902 `apply` (atomic, failing with the operation's index) and `diff`; RFC 7396 `merge_patch` and `merge_diff`. |
| `std/json/path` | RFC 9535 JSONPath: `compile`, `query`, `find`, `values` and `paths`, with slices, filters, `match`, `search`, `length`, `count` and `value`, normalised paths, and syntax errors at a byte offset. |
| `std/json/schema` | JSON Schema 2020-12. `compile` and `compile_with` check the schema, resolve `$ref`, `$anchor`, `$dynamicRef` and registered resources, and precompile patterns. `validate` returns `Violation`s with the instance pointer, the keyword pointer and a message. `unevaluatedProperties` and `unevaluatedItems` use annotations from the branches that passed. `format` annotates or asserts. |
| `std/cbor` | CBOR (RFC 8949): the `Value` model, a decoder with depth and length limits, a deterministic encoder, diagnostic notation, and `serialize[T]` and `deserialize[T]`. |
| `std/webauthn` | A WebAuthn relying party: registration and authentication options as JSON, registration and authentication verification for ES256, EdDSA and RS256 credentials, and COSE key parsing. |
| `std/sql` | The driver contract: `Connection`, the droppable owner that closes the driver connection when released; `ConnectionHandle`, the copyable view of closures that `std/orm` and `std/sql/pool` use, held in the owner's public `handle` field (`adopt` makes an owner from one); `Value`, `Rows`, `Error`, `Options` and `Dialect`. Besides `query`, `each` and `execute`, a handle has `stream`, which passes each result set of a call in order as `Event`s (`Columns`, one `Row` per row and `Done` with its count, `none` when the server reported none and `some(0)` when it reported zero), `cancel`, which another green thread calls to stop the running call with `Cancelled` and leave the connection usable, and `on_notice`, which sets the function that receives the connection's `Notice`s: each message the server sends as a `Diagnostic`, PostgreSQL notifications, and a `Setting` when the server changes a session setting. `query_all` returns every result set of a call with its count, and that count, `Rows.count` and `execute` are 0 where the server reported none. A `Column` carries `precision`, `scale` (also the fractional-second digits of a time type) and `length` (-1 when unbounded), each 0 when the server states none, and `table` and `number`, the OID of the table the column comes from and its attribute number there, which only PostgreSQL reports. `describe` parses a statement and returns its columns and parameter type names as a `Description` without running it, and `stream_prepared` runs exactly one statement as a prepared statement and reports events as `stream` does, so text holding a second statement fails, also without parameters. Both fail with `Unsupported` on a handle that does not set them, which every driver but PostgreSQL leaves as it is. `Options.server_text` asks PostgreSQL and SQL Server for the server's exact text, as `Text`, in place of decoded values. `Error.Server` holds every error of the call in order as `Diagnostic`s, each with a `Level`, a code, a message and the fields its protocol carries; `server_error` builds one from a code and a message. A `Dialect` is a record that owns everything that differs between databases: the OpenTelemetry system name, default port, placeholder prefix, identifier quotes, generated-key and paging syntax, column types, the conditional `CREATE TABLE`, the session lock statements and the write-transaction statement. `postgres()`, `sqlserver()`, `sqlite()`, `mysql()` and `mariadb()` build the five; the `numbered_placeholders` flag, the `LastInsertId` key return, the `LimitUnbounded` paging and the `Upsert` clause (`upsert_clause` writes `ON CONFLICT` or `ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`) cover what MySQL does differently; `std/orm` and `std/sql/migrate` read the one a connection carries. `rolled_back` rolls a transaction back after an error and reports the rollback's own error when it fails. |
| `std/sql/driver` | What the drivers share. A `Session` holds the busy, cancelled and closed state and the notice function. Every operation of a connection runs as a call on the session, which fails with `Busy` or `Closed` and abandons the connection on an error the driver calls fatal. `establish` runs the login under it. `collect` buffers a call's events into its last result set, `rows` turns an `each` visitor into an event callback and `ignore_event` discards events. `report` passes a diagnostic and `setting` a session setting to the notice function, and `listener` makes the `on_notice` that replaces it. `dial` opens the socket at the dialect's default port, and `traced` wraps a handle with `otel.instrument_sql`. `call` runs an operation that is not part of the handle, such as a ping, as a call on the session. `connection` guards, traces and adopts a driver's handle, and passes `cancel` to the driver only while a call runs and once per call, so `std/sql/postgres`, `std/sql/sqlserver`, `std/sql/mysql` and `std/sql/sqlite` do not import `std/otel`. |
| `std/sql/postgres` | A PostgreSQL driver over protocol 3.0, plain or over TLS. |
| `std/sql/sqlserver` | A Microsoft SQL Server driver over TDS 7.4, plain or over TLS, with SQL or integrated authentication, read-intent logins (`Config.read_only`) and named instances: `connect`, `connect_tls`, `connect_with` and `connect_stream`. |
| `std/sql/mysql` | A MySQL and MariaDB driver over the client/server protocol, plain or over TLS: `connect`, `connect_tls`, `connect_with`, `connect_stream`, a `State` that tracks the server, thread id, schema and transaction, `begin_with` for an isolation level, and `savepoint`, `rollback_to` and `release`. |
| `std/sql/sqlite` | SQLite through libsqlite3, linked only when a program calls it. `open` and `open_with` (read-only, create, busy timeout). `exec` runs scripts. `query`, `each`, `stream` and `execute` take `?` or `?N` parameters over cached prepared statements. `cancel` interrupts the running statements through `sqlite3_interrupt` from any green thread, and each fails with `sql.Error.Cancelled`. Also `last_insert_id`, `begin`, `begin_immediate`, `commit` and `rollback`, and `backup` to a file through the online backup API. `close` and `close_on` are idempotent; a closed `Database` fails with `sql.Error.Closed`. `connection` and `connect` present a database as an `sql.Connection`: the one `connection` returns leaves closing to the `Database`, the one `connect` returns closes it. Calls block their OS thread. |
| `std/sql/pool` | A pool of connections made by an opener. `open` takes a `Config` (`max`, `max_idle`, `idle_timeout`, `max_lifetime`, `wait_timeout`, `ping_after`, `ping`, `clock`). `acquire` and `acquire_until` (with a cancel token) park in first-in first-out order while every connection is in use. A droppable `Lease` returns its connection on release, and discards the notice function set through it. A call that fails with `Cancelled` leaves the connection in the pool. Also `with`, `discard`, `prune`, `stats` and `shutdown`. |
| `std/sql/migrate` | Ordered migrations recorded in a `schema_migrations` table with a SHA-256 checksum: `plan`, `with_table`, `status`, `up`, `up_to` and `down`, each migration in its own transaction under a lock. `from_text` and `load_dir` read `-- +up` and `-- +down` files named `NNN_name.sql`. |
| `std/toml` | TOML 1.0.0: a table tree, an encoder, and serde binding. |
| `std/yaml` | YAML 1.2.2: events, a node tree, an encoder, and serde binding. |
| `std/otel` | OpenTelemetry traces, metrics and logs with W3C propagation, OTLP/HTTP protobuf and JSON, OTLP/gRPC and console exporters, and the instrumentation used by `std/http`, `std/web`, `std/sql/driver` and `std/orm`. |
| `std/protobuf` | The protobuf wire format: an encoder with nested messages and packed fields, a field decoder, and `serialize[T]` and `deserialize[T]` for structs whose field tags carry protobuf field numbers. |
| `std/protobuf/proto` | A `.proto` parser for proto3 and proto2, an import loader over a caller-supplied reader, and a generator that writes Low structs and enums with the field tags `std/protobuf` reads. |
| `std/xml` | XML 1.0 with namespaces: a pull reader, a tree, a writer, and serde binding. |
| `std/dbus` | The D-Bus wire format and a client and service over Unix sockets: the `Value` enum and `Type` signatures with `encode`, `decode`, `encode_message` and `decode_message`; addresses, SASL authentication and the session and system buses; method calls, signals, match rules, name ownership, properties and introspection; exported objects. See `### D-Bus`. |
| `std/portal` | A client of the XDG desktop portals over `std/dbus`: file chooser dialogs, `OpenURI`, the settings portal's colour scheme, accent colour and animation preference, and notifications. See `### Desktop portals`. |
| `std/orm` | Typed queries over field references, row mapping, inserts, updates and deletes, tables created from types, and raw queries mapped onto structs, over an `sql.ConnectionHandle`. |
| `std/compress/deflate` | DEFLATE, zlib and gzip. |
| `std/compress/xz` | xz with LZMA2, and `.lzma`. |
| `std/compress/zstd` | Zstandard (RFC 8878). |
| `std/compress/lz4` | LZ4 frames and raw blocks (`block_compress`, `block_decompress`). |
| `std/compress/brotli` | Brotli (RFC 7932). |
| `std/compress/bzip2` | `compress(data, level)`, `decompress(data, limit)`, a streaming `decode` and an `Encoder`. The decoder reads multiple streams and blocks and checks both CRCs. Randomised blocks fail with `Unsupported`. |
| `std/compress/snappy` | Raw blocks (`compress_block`, `decompress_block`, `decoded_length`) and the framing format with masked CRC-32C (`compress`, `decompress`, `decode`, `Encoder`). |
| `std/compress/codec` | The parts the compressors share: a growable `Buffer` that packs bits, a `BitReader`, two-level Huffman decode `Tables`, a length-limited Huffman `Builder`, hash-chain `Chain` and `walk`, a shared LZ77 `History` window (`history`, `append`, `index_to`, `extend_back`), the one-shot `decompress` scaffold with `output_for`, `deliver` and `each_chunk`, the streaming `Encoder` shell (`encoder`, `encode`, `flush`, `finish`), `copy_match`, and LEB128 varints. |
| `std/hash` | One-shot and incremental forms of CRC-32, CRC-32C, Adler-32, CRC-64 and xxHash32/64. Also FNV-1 and FNV-1a in 32 and 64 bits; SipHash-2-4 and SipHash-1-3 with a 16-byte key in 64- and 128-bit forms; MurmurHash3 (`murmur3_32`, `murmur3_128`); wyhash final 4.2; `jump` consistent hashing; and `rendezvous` hashing. |
| `std/bits` | Word operations (`popcount`, `trailing_zeros` on the processor bit scan, `leading_zeros`, `bit_length`, `select_in_word` and the 32 and 64-bit rotations `rotl32`, `rotr32`, `rotl64` and `rotr64`) and dense bitsets. `std/hash`, `std/rand`, `std/text`, the JSON, XML and YAML readers and the compressors use these for rotations, bit counts and bit lengths. |
| `std/bits/roaring` | Roaring bitmaps with the portable serialization format. |
| `std/image` | Bitmaps, pixel operations and resizing, with PNG, JPEG, GIF, BMP, QOI and lossless WebP codecs. |
| `std/crypto` | `random`, a ChaCha20 generator keyed with 32 bytes from the operating system (`getrandom` on Linux, `ProcessPrng` on Windows, the host's `crypto.getRandomValues` through `low.entropy` for `browser/wasm32`), and `wipe`, which zeroes a byte slice. A refill runs 12 ChaCha20 blocks under the key with `aead.chacha20_blocks`, replaces the key with the first 32 bytes of the first block (fast key erasure) and keeps the other 736 bytes as a buffer. A request copies its bytes out of the buffer and overwrites them with zeros, so memory after a request holds neither the key that produced its bytes nor the bytes handed out, only keystream that no request has taken. The rounds load and store every working word in the generator's own block, so the working state is never written to the stack, and the block's working words are zeroed when the refill ends. The key is replaced from the operating system after 1 MiB of output and in a forked child. On Linux a child is recognised by a page mapped with `MADV_WIPEONFORK` (`sys.map_fork_marker`) whose first word is 1 in the parent and 0 in the child, so a request pays one load; when the page cannot be mapped the request compares the process id. Windows and `browser/wasm32` have no `fork` and check nothing. A failing operating system source panics. The generator state is the module's 1024-byte `__static_block`, shared by every green thread and OS thread and guarded by a `__lock` word in it, not kept per OS thread: a green thread moves between OS threads, so per-thread state would give no exclusion on its own. A holder of a `__lock` is never preempted and the collector does not run until it returns, so the lock is held only across loops over the block, never across an allocation or a system call: the result is allocated, the operating system source is read and the marker page is mapped before or after it. Requests over 4096 bytes are served in 4096-byte chunks, each taking the lock once. |
| `std/crypto/limb` | Fixed-width limb arithmetic the big-integer code shares: `carry` and `borrow` out of a 64-bit step, `add`, `sub` and `add_masked` over limb slices, `from_bytes` and `to_bytes` between big-endian bytes and limbs, and the constant-time `same`, `mask_of`, `blend`, `choose`, `gather`, `equal` and `is_zero`. `std/crypto/nistec`, `std/crypto/rsa` and `std/crypto/edwards25519` build on it. |
| `std/crypto/drbg` | AES-256 CTR_DRBG (NIST SP 800-90A Rev. 1 section 10.2.1): `instantiate` (entropy, nonce, personalization) with the derivation function, `instantiate_without_df` (exactly 48 bytes of entropy, personalization of at most 48) without it, `reseed`, `generate` with additional input and `uninstantiate`, on a `*CtrDrbg`. Without the derivation function, reseed entropy is exactly 48 bytes and additional input at most 48, padded with zeros; this is the generator the NIST post-quantum known-answer files are drawn from. A request is at most 65536 bytes (2^19 bits) and `generate` fails with `ReseedRequired` after 2^48 requests. The caller supplies all entropy; the module reads no entropy source. Every operation zeroes the copies of the entropy and additional input, the derived seeds, the intermediate keys and the AES key schedules it makes, so a `*CtrDrbg` holds the only secrets the generator keeps. The AES block function is `std/crypto/aead`'s. |
| `std/crypto/hash` | `Algorithm` and cloneable `Digest`, SHA-1, SHA-224, SHA-256, SHA-384, SHA-512, SHA-512/224 and SHA-512/256, HMAC (`hmac`, and `hmac_keyed` for the keyed inner and outer digests that PBKDF2 clones), HKDF and constant-time `equal`. |
| `std/crypto/sha3` | SHA3-224, SHA3-256, SHA3-384, SHA3-512, SHAKE128 and SHAKE256, with incremental `absorb` and `squeeze` for the SHAKE sponges. |
| `std/crypto/aead` | `Aead` values for AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCM, ChaCha20-Poly1305 and XChaCha20-Poly1305 (`xchacha20_poly1305`, 24-byte nonce). The AES block functions for 128-, 192- and 256-bit keys (`aes_key`, `encrypt_block_into`, `decrypt_block`, `decrypt_block_into`) and `aes_wipe`, which zeroes a key schedule. The ChaCha20 functions `chacha20_block` (the RFC 8439 block function), `chacha20_state` and `chacha20_blocks` (the block function over a 32-word state the caller owns, whose rounds load and store every working word in that state and zero it afterwards, so no word of it is held in a spilled register; `std/rand`'s `chacha` and `crypto.random` build on them), `chacha20_xor` (takes the RFC 8439 block counter, on the same rounds) and `hchacha20`. |
