| `std/crypto/md5` | MD5 as a `hash.Algorithm`, so HMAC, HKDF and PBKDF2 work over it, and `sum`. For interop and checksums, not security. |
| `std/crypto/cipher` | AES in CBC, raw (`cbc_encrypt`, `cbc_decrypt`) or with PKCS#7 padding (`cbc_seal`, `cbc_open`, `pkcs7_pad`, `pkcs7_unpad`); in CTR with a 128-bit big-endian counter (`ctr`); and in XTS with ciphertext stealing (`xts`, `xts_encrypt`, `xts_decrypt`). There is no ECB, and CBC provides no integrity: use an `Aead`. |
| `std/crypto/x25519`, `std/crypto/ed25519` | X25519 key agreement (RFC 7748) and Ed25519 signatures (RFC 8032), over `std/crypto/curve25519`, which also owns the scalar `clamp`, and `std/crypto/edwards25519`. |
| `std/crypto/edwards25519` | The Edwards curve behind Ed25519 and ristretto255: the extended `Point` with `base`, `identity`, `add`, `negate`, `double`, `multiples` and the constant-time windowed `multiply`, the RFC 8032 point `encode` and `decode`, and the scalars modulo the group order `L` (`reduce`, `mul_add`, `canonical`). `std/crypto/ed25519` and `std/crypto/ristretto255` build on it. |
| `std/crypto/ristretto255` | The prime-order group of RFC 9496. `Element` with `identity`, `generator`, `decode` (canonical encodings only, `none` for any other), `encode`, `equal`, `add`, `sub`, `neg`, the constant-time `multiply` and `multiply_base`, and `from_uniform`, the one-way map of 64 bytes. Scalars are 32 little-endian bytes below `L`: `scalar_from_bytes`, `scalar_from_wide`, `scalar_add`, `scalar_sub`, `scalar_mul`, `scalar_neg`, `scalar_invert`, `scalar_is_zero` and `scalar_random`. |
| `std/crypto/ecdsa` | ECDH and ECDSA on P-256 and P-384, over `std/crypto/nistec`, with RFC 6979 nonces and SEC1 private keys. It owns the curve sizes (`scalar_size`), the named-curve lookup (`curve_by_oid`) and the uncompressed point encoding (`uncompressed`, `coordinates`) that `std/crypto/x509`, `std/crypto/jose` and `std/webauthn` use. |
| `std/crypto/nistec` | The NIST prime curves P-256 and P-384 that `std/crypto/ecdsa`, `std/crypto/hashtocurve` and `std/crypto/voprf` share. `Field` is Montgomery arithmetic modulo an odd prime (`add`, `sub`, `mul`, `to_mont`, `from_mont`, `power`, `invert`, `reduce_wide`). `Group` holds the curve (`p256`, `p384`) and has the complete projective `point_add`, `negate`, the constant-time `scalar_mul`, `affine`, the uncompressed and compressed SEC1 encodings (`encode_point`, `compress`, `decompress`) and `point_at` with its on-curve check. The scalars modulo the group order are big-endian bytes with `order_from_bytes`, `order_reduce`, `order_add`, `order_sub`, `order_mul`, `order_neg`, `order_invert` and `order_random`. |
| `std/crypto/hashtocurve` | RFC 9380 hashing: `expand_message_xmd` over any `hash.Algorithm`, `hash_to_field` with `L = 48`, and the constant-time `P256_XMD:SHA-256_SSWU_RO_` suite (`map_to_curve_p256`, `hash_to_curve_p256`). The forms RFC 9497 uses are `hash_to_scalar_p256`, `hash_to_ristretto255` (appendix B of RFC 9380) and `hash_to_ristretto255_scalar`. |
| `std/crypto/voprf` | The oblivious PRF of RFC 9497 in its three modes (`Mode`: `Oprf`, `Voprf`, `Poprf`) for `ristretto255-SHA512` and `P256-SHA256` (`Suite`). `derive_key_pair`, `generate_key_pair` and `public_key` make keys. A `Client` calls `blind` and `finalize`, and a `Server` calls `blind_evaluate` and `evaluate`; each batch of elements shares one DLEQ proof, which `finalize` verifies. `blind_with` and `blind_evaluate_with` take the blind and the proof scalar, for known-answer tests. Elements and scalars are serialized as the suites define, and `Error` names the RFC failures (`InvalidInput`, `Inverse`, `Verify`, `Deserialize`, `DeriveKeyPair`). |
| `std/crypto/rsa` | RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 and PSS signing and verification, RSAES-OAEP encryption and decryption, and PKCS#1 keys and SubjectPublicKeyInfo. `integer` strips the leading zeros that key integers from JWK and COSE carry. `mod_pow` is constant-time modular exponentiation over big-endian bytes for odd moduli of up to 8192 bits, which the SSH Diffie-Hellman groups use. |
| `std/crypto/blake3` | BLAKE3: `sum`, `xof`, `keyed_hash`, `derive_key`, an incremental `Hasher` with `finalize` and seekable `finalize_at`, and `algorithm()`, a `hash.Algorithm` for HMAC. |
| `std/crypto/otp` | HOTP (RFC 4226) and TOTP (RFC 6238) over SHA-1, SHA-256 and SHA-512: `hotp`, `totp`, `verify_totp` with a window, `verify_hotp`, `generate_secret`, base32 secrets, and `otpauth://` `uri` and `parse_uri`. |
| `std/crypto/shamir` | Shamir secret sharing over GF(2^8) with the AES reduction polynomial `0x11b`: `split` cuts a secret into `n` `Share`s (x coordinates `1..=n`, one y byte per secret byte) of which any `k` recover it, with coefficients from `std/crypto`, and `split_with` takes the `(k - 1) * secret.len` coefficient bytes as an argument. `combine` interpolates at zero and fails with `TooFewShares`, `ZeroX`, `LengthMismatch` or `DuplicateX`; `split` and `split_with` fail with `BadThreshold`, `BadShareCount`, `EmptySecret` or `BadRandomness`. Field multiplication is a fixed eight-step shift and mask loop and inversion is the fixed power `a^254`, so no table is indexed by, and no branch depends on, a secret byte, a coefficient or a share value; only the x coordinates, which are public, are checked and compared. Shares carry no check value, so fewer than `k` shares or a corrupted share decode to bytes unrelated to the secret without an error. |
| `std/crypto/mlkem` | ML-KEM-512, -768 and -1024 (FIPS 203). |
| `std/crypto/hpke` | Hybrid public key encryption (RFC 9180) with HKDF-SHA-256 and the AEADs AES-256-GCM, ChaCha20-Poly1305 and export-only. KEMs: DHKEM(X25519, HKDF-SHA256) (`Kem.DhkemX25519`, id 0x0020), which supports the base, PSK, auth and auth-PSK modes, and MLKEM768-X25519 (`Kem.Mlkem768X25519`, id 0x647a), as `draft-ietf-hpke-pq-05` defines it over the X-Wing construction of `draft-irtf-cfrg-concrete-hybrid-kems-03` (ML-KEM-768 and X25519 combined with SHA3-256, a 32-byte private seed expanded with SHAKE256, 1216-byte public keys and 1120-byte encapsulated keys), whose `DeriveKeyPair` is the `LabeledDerive` of `draft-ietf-hpke-hpke-03`. The hybrid supports the base and PSK modes and fails the authenticated ones with `Error.Unsupported`; an all-zero X25519 output takes part in the combiner as zero bytes, as the draft's group definition has it, while DHKEM rejects it with `Error.Encap` or `Error.Decap`. `Suite` pairs a `Kem` with an `AeadId`. `derive_key_pair`, `generate_key_pair`, `serialize_public_key` and `deserialize_public_key` handle keys as `KeyPair` byte strings. `Credentials` (`base`, `psk`, `auth`, `auth_psk`) selects the mode from which inputs are present, and `setup_sender`, `setup_sender_seeded` (caller-supplied ephemeral randomness, for known-answer tests) and `setup_recipient` return a `*Context` with `seal`, `open` and `export`. A context uses the sequence number as the nonce and fails with `Error.Limit` when it is exhausted, a failed `open` does not advance it, `seal` is for sender contexts and `open` for recipient contexts, and an export-only context has only `export`. `seal_single` and `open_single` do one message. Failures are `Error` values. Pre-shared keys are not required to be 32 bytes. |
| `std/crypto/prehash` | The `Hash` enum of the functions FIPS 204 and FIPS 205 accept for pre-hash signing (SHA-224, SHA-256, SHA-384, SHA-512, SHA-512/224, SHA-512/256, SHA3-224, SHA3-256, SHA3-384, SHA3-512, SHAKE128 with 32 bytes of output and SHAKE256 with 64), `digest`, `oid`, the DER object identifier that HashML-DSA and HashSLH-DSA put in the signed message, and `pure_message` and `prehashed_message`, which frame a message and context string (at most 255 bytes) as `0 || len(ctx) || ctx || message` and `1 || len(ctx) || ctx || oid || digest`. |
| `std/crypto/mldsa` | ML-DSA-44, -65 and -87 (FIPS 204). `generate` and `generate_seeded` return `Keys`: the 32-byte seed, the public key and the expanded private key. `sign`, `sign_prehash`, `sign_mu` and `sign_internal` take a `Randomness`: `Hedged` (32 bytes from `crypto.random`), `Deterministic` (32 zero bytes) or `Given` (a 32-byte value for known-answer tests). `verify`, `verify_prehash`, `verify_mu` and `verify_internal` return `false` for every malformed key, signature, context or hint encoding. The context string is at most 255 bytes. `message_representative` computes the external `mu`. Signing branches on no private value and indexes no table with one; the number of rejection-sampling rounds is the only thing it reveals. Its fixtures hold NIST ACVP key generation, signature generation and signature verification vectors and vectors generated with OpenSSL. |
| `std/crypto/slhdsa` | All twelve SLH-DSA parameter sets (FIPS 205): SHA2 and SHAKE, security levels 128, 192 and 256, `s` (small signatures, slow signing) and `f` (fast signing). `generate` and `generate_seeded` return `Keys`: the `3n`-byte seed (`SK.seed`, `SK.prf`, `PK.seed`), the `2n`-byte public key and the `4n`-byte private key. `sign`, `sign_prehash` and `sign_internal` take a `Randomness`: `Hedged` (`n` bytes from `crypto.random`), `Deterministic` (`PK.seed`) or `Given` (`n` bytes for known-answer tests). `verify`, `verify_prehash` and `verify_internal` return `false` for a key, signature or context of the wrong length and for every signature whose recomputed hypertree root differs. The context string is at most 255 bytes. WOTS+, XMSS, the hypertree and FORS are module-private. Secret seeds only enter hash inputs, so signing branches on no secret and indexes no table with one. An `s` set signs in a few seconds and key generation costs a full top-layer tree. Its fixtures hold NIST ACVP key generation, signature generation and signature verification vectors and vectors generated with OpenSSL. |
| `std/crypto/falcon` | Falcon-512 and Falcon-1024 key generation, signing and signature verification as specified in the NIST round 3 submission (Falcon specification version 1.2); FIPS 206 is not implemented, and its hashing and encodings may differ. `verify(level, public, message, signature)` takes the public key (header byte `0x09` or `0x0a`, then `h` in 14-bit coefficients) and the signature (header byte `0x39` or `0x3a`, the 40-byte nonce and the compressed `s2`), unpadded or padded with zeros to `signature_size(level)` bytes, and returns `false` for every non-canonical encoding: a wrong length or header, a public key coefficient of 12289 or more, a compressed coefficient beyond 2047, `-0`, non-zero trailing bits, partial or non-zero padding. The uncompressed signature format and the key-recovery mode are not supported. `sign(level, private, message, randomness)` takes the private key of the known-answer files (header byte `0x59` or `0x5a`, `f` and `g` in 6-bit or 5-bit coefficients, `F` in 8-bit coefficients; `G` is recomputed) and returns a signature padded to `signature_size(level)`, sampling again with the same nonce when the compressed `s2` does not fit, as `falcon_sign_dyn` of the reference implementation does; `randomness` is `Hedged`, 48 bytes from `crypto.random`, or `Given` 48 bytes, from which SHAKE256 draws the nonce and then each attempt's sampler seed. `sign_internal(level, private, message, nonce, seed)` is the round-3 NIST API path, with the 40-byte nonce and 48-byte sampler seed the known-answer harness draws, and returns the signature at its natural length. Signing is the reference implementation's dynamic-tree variant: the FFT-domain LDL tree is rebuilt for each attempt, ffSampling runs over it and SamplerZ combines the RCDT base sampler with BerExp over a ChaCha20 stream. It computes in native `f64`, which on amd64 is SSE2 and on `browser/wasm32` WebAssembly `f64`, both IEEE 754 binary64 with round-to-nearest-even and no fused multiply-add, the arithmetic the reference implementation's native build relies on and the integer emulation reproduces; as in the reference implementation, the secret key reaches a branch only in the key's validity checks and each attempt's norm test, and no table index. `generate_seeded(level, seed)` derives `Keys` (the seed, the public key and the private key) from 48 bytes through SHAKE256, as the known-answer files do, and `generate(level)` draws the seed from `crypto.random`; `F` and `G` come from the reference implementation's NTRU solver, which descends through field norms with big integers held in residue number system form over 31-bit primes and lifts back with Babai reduction in the FFT domain. Its fixtures check the key pair and the full `sm` of a subset of round-3 known-answer entries, including the 1281-byte Falcon-1024 signature, and the padded signatures against `falcon_sign_dyn` of Falcon-impl-20211101. |
| `std/crypto/jose` | JSON Web Tokens: compact JWS with HS256/384/512, RS256/384/512, PS256/384/512, ES256, ES384 and EdDSA, JWK sets, and claim checks. `asymmetric_algorithms` lists the public key algorithms and `token_kid` reads the key id from a token header. `thumbprint` returns the RFC 7638 SHA-256 thumbprint of a `Jwk`. `sign_flattened` and `verify_flattened` write and check the flattened JSON serialization of RFC 7515 section 7.2.2 for a `Protected` header (`alg`, `kid`, `nonce`, `url` and an embedded `jwk`), as ACME uses it. |
| `std/crypto/password` | Password hashes in PHC strings: Argon2id, scrypt (RFC 7914, with a memory limit checked before allocating) and PBKDF2. bcrypt (`$2b$`; passwords over 72 bytes are rejected), and `bcrypt_pbkdf`, the OpenBSD key derivation of OpenSSH private keys. `verify` handles all of them and `$2a$` and `$2y$` bcrypt strings. BLAKE2b. |
| `std/crypto/sasl` | The client side of SCRAM (RFC 5802, RFC 7677) over any `hash.Algorithm`: `start` takes the user, the prepared secret and a nonce, `first` returns the client-first-message, `final` turns the server-first-message into the client-final-message with its proof, and `verify` checks the server signature in constant time. Failures are `Malformed`, `Unsupported` (a mandatory extension) and `Rejected`. `std/sql/postgres` runs SCRAM-SHA-256 and `std/mongo` SCRAM-SHA-256 and SCRAM-SHA-1 through it; the caller prepares the secret. |
| `std/crypto/der` | A DER cursor and the ASN.1 values the other crypto modules share. Reading: `reader`, `read`, `peek`, `done`, `expect`, `enter`, `finish`, `whole`, with `unsigned`, `small_int`, `boolean`, `bits`, `named_bits`, `oid` and `time` for the primitive types; it rejects indefinite and non-minimal lengths. Writing: `encode`, `integer`, `sequence`, `set`, `octet_string`, `bit_string`, `utf8_string`, `object_identifier` (from dotted text) and `time_value` (a `UTCTime` for 1950 to 2049 and a `GeneralizedTime` for other years in 0001 to 9999); `trim_zeros` drops the leading zero bytes of a big-endian magnitude. `prime256v1` and `secp384r1` return the named-curve OIDs that `std/crypto/ecdsa` and `std/crypto/x509` share. `std/crypto/x509`, `std/crypto/rsa` and `std/crypto/ecdsa` build on it. |
| `std/crypto/x509` | PEM (`pem_blocks`, `pem_certificates`, `pem_private_key`), certificates, PKCS#8 private keys, the system roots, chain validation and the TLS signature scheme table (`signature_schemes`, `signing_schemes`, `authentication`, `verify_signature`, `sign`). Certificate status: `Certificate` carries `subject_key_id`, `ocsp_urls`, `issuer_urls`, `crl_urls`, `ocsp_no_check` and `must_staple`; `parse_crl`, `crl_lookup`, `verify_crl` and `crl_status` read revocation lists; `ocsp_request`, `parse_ocsp` and `ocsp_status` build and check OCSP exchanges; `verify_chain` applies a `Revocation` policy. See Certificate status. Writing: `generate_private_key` makes P-256, P-384 or Ed25519 keys (`KeyType`), `public_key_info`, `private_key_der`, `sec1_private_key_der`, `private_key_pem`, `certificate_pem` and `pem_encode` encode keys and certificates, `create_certificate` and `create_self_signed` write a v3 certificate from a `Template` with `Extension`s, and `create_request`, `parse_request` and `check_request_signature` write and check PKCS#10 requests. `Certificate.authority_key_id` holds the authority key identifier. |
| `std/acme` | An ACME client (RFC 8555). `connect` reads a directory and returns the `Client`; `new_account`, `find_account`, `refresh_account`, `update_account`, `change_key` and `deactivate_account` manage an `Account`; `new_order`, `get_order`, `get_authorization`, `respond`, `wait_authorization`, `wait_order`, `finalize` and `download_chain` are the steps of an order; `obtain` runs them with `Solvers` and returns the `Issued` certificate, and `csr_for` builds its request; `revoke` and `revoke_with_key` revoke. `renewal_info` and `renewal_id` implement RFC 9773, and `manager` with `start`, `stop`, `run_once`, `renew_now`, `status` and `source` keeps certificates renewed in a `Store` (`memory_store`, `directory_store`). The challenge solvers are `challenges` with `put_token`, `drop_token`, `put_certificate` and `drop_certificate`, `http01_handler`, `serve_http01` and `tls_alpn_source`, `DnsRecords`, and the helpers `key_authorization`, `dns01_name`, `dns01_value`, `http01_path`, `tls_alpn_server_name` and `tls_alpn_credentials`. The constants `alpn_protocol` and `acme_identifier_oid` name the TLS-ALPN-01 protocol and extension. `describe` writes an `Error` as text; see ACME. |
| `std/crypto/x509/fetch` | The HTTP transport for revocation data, over `std/http`. `ocsp` sends a request with GET when it is under 255 bytes and POST otherwise, `crl` downloads a list, `fetcher` returns the `x509.Fetcher` that `Revocation.fetcher` takes and `client` returns the `*http.Client` they run on. It is a module of its own because `std/http` imports `std/tls`, which imports `std/crypto/x509`. |
| `std/time` | `now` (Unix seconds), `now_millis` and `now_nanos` (the wall clock at finer resolution), `monotonic` (nanoseconds), `sleep_for` (rounds up to whole milliseconds), `after`, `Timer` and `Ticker` (their channels work in `select`), and `Stopwatch`. Calendar and zone types live in the submodules below, one module per type, so `d.plus(span.days(3))` resolves to `date.plus`. |
| `std/time/fault` | `Fault` (`OutOfRange`, `Invalid`, `Syntax(at)`, `Overflow`, `UnknownZone`, `Malformed`, `Unsupported`, `Skipped`, `Ambiguous`, `OffsetConflict`), the error every fallible time function returns, and `to_string`. |
| `std/time/duration` | `Duration`, an exact signed elapsed time with private seconds and nanoseconds. Constructors from `nanoseconds` to `hours`, and `from_seconds_f64`. Arithmetic: `plus`, `minus`, `times`, `divide`, `ratio`, `negate`, `abs`, with `try_` forms returning an optional. Plain forms panic on overflow. Also `round` and `truncate` with a `Rounding`, comparison, `to_string` (ISO 8601 `PT1H30M`), `friendly` (`1h 30m`) and `parse`, which reads both forms and fails at the offset of the first bad character. |
| `std/time/timestamp` | `Timestamp`, a point on the UTC timeline from 0001-01-01 to 9999-12-31 with nanosecond precision: `now`, `from_unix` in seconds, milliseconds or nanoseconds, `plus` and `minus` by `Duration`, `until` and `since`, `round`, `truncate`, RFC 3339 `to_string` and `parse` (offset required, `:60` read as `:59`), and `to_http_date` and `parse_http_date` (IMF-fixdate, RFC 850 and asctime). |
| `std/time/clock` | `Clock`, closures for the wall reading, the monotonic reading and a cancellable `sleep`, with `system`, `fixed` and `manual`. A `Manual` clock moves with `advance` and `set`, and its sleep records the wait in `sleeps` and advances the clock without waiting, so code that asks for the time and waits is tested without sleeping. |
| `std/time/span` | `Span`, a calendar amount of years, months, weeks, days and time units with one sign. `Unit`, `Diff` (`largest`, `smallest`, `increment`, `mode`), `to_duration` and `from_duration`, `of_calendar` (calendar counts and a time duration), `of_elapsed` and `step_of` (rounding an exact elapsed time by a `Diff`), `round_fraction` (the rounding every difference shares), ISO 8601 `to_string` (`P1Y2M3DT4H`), `friendly` and `parse`. |
| `std/time/rounding` | `Rounding` (`Trunc`, `Ceil`, `Floor`, `Expand` and the five `Half` modes) and the helpers the other time modules share. |
| `std/time/calendar` | The proleptic Gregorian calendar the date types share: `leap_year`, `days_in_month`, `days_from_civil` and `civil_from_days` from the Unix epoch, `weekday_index`, and the English month and weekday names, full and short. |
| `std/time/scan` | `Scan`, the read position the time parsers share, with `more`, `at_digit`, `at_byte`, `accept`, `expect`, `digits`, `take_whole` and `take_fraction`, and the fraction text the formatters share. |
| `std/time/date` | `Date` with private fields and validated constructors (`of`, `clamped`, `from_epoch_days`, `from_ordinal`, `from_iso_week`, `from_weekday_in_month`). `Month`, `Weekday` and `IsoWeek`. `plus` and `minus` by `Span` with month-end clamping, `until` and `since` with a `Diff`, `next_weekday` and `on_or_next`, and `range`. ISO 8601 `to_string` and `parse` (calendar, ordinal and week dates). `format` and `parse_with` over a pattern. |
| `std/time/timeofday` | `TimeOfDay` to the nanosecond. `plus` fails past midnight and `plus_wrapping` wraps. Also `until`, `round`, `truncate`, `to_string`, `parse`, and pattern `format` and `parse_with`. |
| `std/time/datetime` | `DateTime`, a civil date and time of day with no zone: `plus` by `Span` or `Duration`, `until` and `since` with a `Diff`, `round`, `truncate`, `to_string`, `parse` (an offset is rejected), and pattern `format` and `parse_with`. |
| `std/time/offset` | `Offset`, a UTC offset in whole seconds within ±25:59:59, with `utc`, `hours`, `hm`, `to_string`, `to_string_z`, `parse`, and `read`, which reads an offset out of the middle of a string and reports where it ended. |
| `std/time/zone` | IANA zones. `Zone` is a shared value decoded once, from `named` (the host database, honouring `ZONEINFO`), `load_from` (any `fn(str) -> u8[]?`), `local` (`TZ`, `/etc/localtime`, `/etc/timezone`, then UTC), `from_posix`, `decode`, `utc` or `fixed`. `fixed_offset` returns the offset of a zone `fixed` made. Queries: `info_at`, `candidates` (`One`, `Overlap`, `Gap` for a civil time), `next_transition`, `previous_transition` and `transitions`. |
| `std/time/tzdata` | An embedded copy of the database for hosts without one: `source` (for `zone.load_from`), `names` and `version`. The data is linked only when `source` or `names` is reachable. |
| `std/time/zoned` | `Zoned`, a `Timestamp` seen through a `Zone` whose offset is always the zone's own. Built from a civil time with a `Disambiguation` (`Compatible`, `Earlier`, `Later`, `Reject`) or `strict`. `plus` by `Span` moves calendar units on civil dates and time units by elapsed time; `plus_duration` is exact. Also `until` and `since` with a `Diff`, `start_of_day`, `hours_in_day`, `series` and `round`. RFC 9557 `to_string` (`2026-11-11T10:00:00+00:00[Europe/London]`), `to_rfc3339`, `to_rfc2822`, and `parse` with an `OffsetPolicy` for a written offset that disagrees with the zone. Pattern `format` and `parse_with`. |
| `std/time/pattern` | Compiled strftime and strptime patterns shared by the types above: `compile`, `Names` for other languages, `render` and `scan`. `compile_in` takes `Layouts` and so accepts `%x`, `%X` and `%c`, which `datefmt.layouts` fills from the standard patterns of a locale; `compile` rejects those three with `Unsupported`. Verbs: `%Y %y %C %m %d %e %j %b %B %a %A %u %w %U %W %V %G %H %I %M %S %f %N %p %P %z %:z %::z %Z %s %F %T %R %D %% %n %t`. Flags: `-`, `_`, `0` and `^`. Parsing is strict, and every error carries a byte offset. |
| `std/time/humanize` | `ago`, `relative`, `in_words`, `compact` and `round_to` over a `Words` value; English by default. `ago_in` and `relative_in` take a `locale.Locale` and write the relative-time patterns of that locale. |
| `std/locale` | CLDR 48 locale identifiers. `parse` reads a BCP 47 tag, canonicalises its aliases, keeps the `-u-` keys `nu`, `ca`, `co`, `hc` and `cu` as the `numbering`, `calendar`, `collation`, `hour_cycle` and `currency` fields of `Locale`, and loads the data of the locale and its parents. `chain` lists the identifiers that serve a locale, most specific first and ending in `root` (`es-MX`, `es-419`, `es`, `root`; CLDR parent overrides such as `en-AU` to `en-001` apply). `parse_with` takes the blobs of a generated `data.chain_<id>()` instead, `root`, `tag`, `maximize` and `minimize` (likely subtags) complete the module, and `Error` is `Empty` or `Malformed(subtag)`. |
| `std/locale/data` | Generated by `compiler/tablegen cldr` and `compiler/tablegen collate`. One blob per CLDR locale (1122 identifiers), the alias, likely-subtag, parent, numbering-system, currency, day-period and week tables, the plural rules, and the collation tables, each `embed`ded. `blob_by_id` reaches every blob; `chain_<id>()` (one per identifier) reaches only the blobs of one chain. A program is linked with the data its calls reach and no more. |
| `std/locale/decimal` | `Decimal`, an exact sign, digit string and exponent with `from_int`, `from_f64` (shortest round-trip), `parse`, `shift`, `strip_zeros`, `pad`, and the rounding every formatter shares: `round_places`, `round_significant` and `round_increment` under a `Mode` (ceil, floor, expand, trunc and the five half modes). |
| `std/locale/plural` | CLDR plural rules. `Operands` (`i`, `v`, `f`, `w`, `t`, `e`) built by `operands_of_int`, `operands_of_text` or `operands`, then `cardinal`, `ordinal`, `category_of` and `range` (the category of a number range). `Category` is `Zero`, `One`, `Two`, `Few`, `Many` or `Other`, and `name` is its CLDR keyword. |
| `std/locale/numfmt` | Numbers by locale: `format`, `format_int` and `format_f64` under `Options`. `Style` is decimal, percent or currency; `Notation` is standard, scientific, engineering or compact (`CompactDisplay` short or long); `currency` is an ISO 4217 code shown by `CurrencyDisplay` (symbol, narrow symbol, code, plural name) in a `CurrencySign` (standard or accounting) with `CurrencyUsage` (standard or cash digits); `SignDisplay` is auto, always, never, except-zero or negative; `Grouping` is auto, always, min2 or off; digit options are `minimum_integer`, `minimum_fraction`, `maximum_fraction`, `minimum_significant`, `maximum_significant`, `rounding_mode` (a `decimal.Mode`), `rounding_increment` and `trailing_zeros`. The numbering system comes from the `-u-nu` key of the locale. `localize_digits` and `decimal_separator` expose the digit and separator choices. Failures are `MissingCurrency`, `UnknownCurrency` and `InvalidDigits`. |
| `std/locale/datefmt` | Gregorian dates and times by locale; other calendars are not provided. `Moment` is a civil date, a time of day and an optional offset and zone id, built by `from_date`, `from_time`, `from_datetime` and `from_zoned`. `Options` takes `date_style` and `time_style` (`Length` full, long, medium or short), a `skeleton` matched against the available formats of the locale, and `hour_cycle` (`Auto` follows the `-u-hc` key and then the locale). `format` writes one value, `format_range` writes an interval, `pattern` returns the CLDR pattern, and `layouts` and `names` feed `pattern.compile_in`. `Error` is `EmptyRequest`, `MissingDate`, `MissingTime`, `MissingZone`, `InvalidSkeleton` or `NoLayout`. |
| `std/locale/list` | `format` of a list as `And`, `Or` or `Unit` at `Wide`, `Short` and `Narrow` widths. |
| `std/locale/reltime` | `format` and `format_int` of a relative amount in seconds, minutes, hours, days, weeks, months, quarters or years, `Numeric` always or auto (`yesterday`), at `Long`, `Short` and `Narrow` widths, with the plural category chosen by the locale. |
| `std/locale/collate` | UTS #10 collation. `Options` (`Alternate` non-ignorable or shifted, `Strength` primary to identical, `SecondaryOrder` for backwards secondary weights, `CaseFirst`, `numeric`), `root` and `for_locale` build a `Collator` from the root table and the CLDR tailoring of the locale. `compare`, `equal`, `compare_runes`, `sort_key`, `sort_key_runes` and `sort`. |
| `std/simd` | Vector reads and writes, `splat`, `mask`, `blend`, `min`, `max`, lane access, byte shuffles and `find_byte`. |
| `std/text` | Parsing and formatting: integer parsing in any base from 2 to 36; correctly rounded float parsing, which allocates only to expand a decimal when digits beyond the 19th decide the rounding; `parse_bool`, `radix`, `hex`, hex encoding and `digit` and `hex_digit`, the value of one digit byte in a base or in hex; `append_hex` and `parse_hex_at`, which write and read a fixed number of hex digits; `fixed(x, places)`, which rounds half to even on the exact binary value. Trimming and splitting: `trim` and its start, end, prefix and suffix forms; `split` on a byte; `split_str` on a string (an empty separator splits into code points); `fields`; `lines` (drops a final empty line and `\r` before `\n`); `chars`; `join`. Searching: `index`, `last_index`, `contains`, `count`, `starts_with`, `ends_with`. Rewriting: `repeat`, `replace`, ASCII `upper` and `lower` (return their argument when no byte changes), and case-insensitive `equal_fold` and `starts_with_fold` (allocate nothing). Widths: `char_count`, `from_char`, and `pad_left` and `pad_right` to a width in code points. `append` and `append_char` onto a `u8[]`; `char_len` and `put_char` encode one code point into a buffer, and are the only UTF-8 encoder. Comma-separated token lists. `cut` and `last_cut` return a `Cut` of `before`, `after` and `found`. `strip_prefix` and `strip_suffix` return `str?`. Also `split_n`, `title`, `quote` and `unquote` (backslash, `\x`, `\u` and `\U` escapes, failing with `QuoteError`), `edit_distance` over code points, `wrap` to a width, `is_ascii`, `is_digits` and `reverse`. `is_scalar` tells whether a `u32` is a Unicode scalar value, `lead_len` gives the length of the UTF-8 sequence a lead byte starts, and `trailing_backslashes` counts the backslashes that end a line. `parse_u64` and `parse_uint` parse unsigned digits, `digit_char` writes one digit, and `position(s, at)` turns a byte offset into a `Position` of line and column counted from 1. `float32_bits` and `float32_from_bits` convert between an `f32` and its bits, `float_decimal` writes a float in the decimal form that TOML and YAML share, `float_diagnostic` in the form of CBOR's diagnostic notation, and `quote_ascii` writes a string as a double-quoted ASCII literal. |
| `std/text/diff` | Myers line diff: `diff`, `lines`, `stats`, `distance`, `similarity`, GNU-compatible `unified` and `hunks`, `patch` and `patch_within` over a unified diff, and word and character level `inline`, `inline_chars` and `inline_markup`. |
| `std/text/table` | `Table` with per-column `Align`, width limits, truncation and wrapping, and `Border` styles `None`, `Ascii`, `Unicode`, `Markdown` and `Tabs`. Widths use `unicode.text_width` and ignore ANSI sequences. `align` and `align_tabbed` align tab-separated cells like Go's `tabwriter`. |
| `std/text/naming` | `words`, `snake`, `kebab`, `dotted`, `screaming_snake`, `camel`, `pascal`, `title`, `sentence`, the matching `is_` predicates, and `slug` and `slug_with` (transliterate Latin letters and cut at a word boundary). |
| `std/text/number` | `format_bytes` and `parse_bytes` in decimal and binary units, `grouped`, `ordinal`, `percent`, `compact`, `si`, English `words`, and `roman` with `parse_roman`. Arithmetic is exact. `grouped_in`, `percent_in`, `compact_in` and `currency_in` take a `locale.Locale` and write the separators, digits, percent sign, compact words and currency patterns of that locale. |
| `std/uri` | RFC 3986 percent coding and references. `escape` leaves the unreserved bytes, `escape_form` writes a space as `+`, `escape_target` encodes only the bytes a request target cannot hold and keeps existing escapes, and `escape_where` takes the set to keep. `unescape` returns `str?` and is `none` on a bad escape; `unescape_lossy` leaves a bad escape in place and `unescape_form_lossy` also reads `+` as a space. `encode_form` writes a list of `Field`s as `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, and `parse_query` reads one into a `[str: str]` where the last value of a name wins. `scheme_end`, `without_fragment`, `remove_dots` and `resolve` follow RFC 3986 section 5, working on strings. |
| `std/builder` | A `Builder` over a growing byte buffer, appended in place through `mut`: `write`, `write_bytes`, `write_byte`, `write_char`, `write_i64`, `write_f64`, `write_line`, `write_repeat`, `write_join`, `len`, `capacity`, `reserve`, `truncate`, `reset`, `to_str` and `to_bytes`. |
| `std/regex` | Linear-time regular expressions in the RE2 style: `compile` and `compile_with` with `Flags`, `generated` (a pattern compiled into specialised code at build time), `is_match`, `find`, `full_match`, `find_all`, `replace_all` (with a closure receiving each `Match`), `split` and `split_n`. A match offers `start`, `end`, `text`, `group` and `span`, with byte offsets. `(?i)`, `\w` and `\b` follow Unicode, and `(?m)` ends a line at `\n`, `\r` or `\r\n`. |
| `std/unicode` | Normalization (Unicode 16.0.0): `nfd`, `nfkd`, `nfc` and `nfkc`; `combining_class`; full case folding with `casefold`; `search_key`, which case-folds, applies NFKD and drops combining marks in one pass. Properties: `category`, `is_letter`, `is_upper`, `is_lower`, `is_digit`, `is_space`, `is_punct`, `is_identifier_start` and the other predicates; `east_asian_width`, `display_width` and `text_width`; `script`. Case mapping: `to_upper`, `to_lower` and `to_title`, and `upper` and `lower` with SpecialCasing but without final-sigma or locale rules. Segmentation: UAX #29 `graphemes`, `grapheme_count` and `grapheme_end`; `words`, `word_count` and `word_end`; `sentences`, `sentence_count` and `sentence_end`; and UAX #14 `line_breaks`, which lists the positions where a line may end and marks the mandatory ones, and `line_segments`. UTF-8 helpers: `decode_rune`, `is_invalid`, `encode_rune`, `utf8_valid`, `utf8_to_valid`. `Order` is the byte order of UTF-16 and UTF-32, and `bom_order` reads the byte order mark of either width. Category, width and identifier data are Unicode 16.0.0; script, grapheme break and case data come from 17.0.0 restricted to characters assigned in 16.0.0; word, sentence and line break data are 17.0.0. The tables are generated by `compiler/tablegen` and linked only when a function that reads them is reachable. |
| `std/unicode/utf16`, `std/unicode/utf32` | Encoding and decoding of code points, `u16[]` and `u32[]`, and byte forms in either order with BOM handling, strict or lossy, with surrogate validation. `join` combines a surrogate pair, `units` reads a byte form into code units, and `decode_escape` in `utf16` decodes a `\uXXXX` escape with its trailing pair. Their byte forms take `unicode.Order`. |
| `std/charset` | `decode`, `decode_lossy`, `encode`, `encode_replace`, `canonical` (WHATWG labels) and `detect_bom` for UTF-8, UTF-16, UTF-32, ISO-8859-1, -2 and -15, windows-874, windows-1250 to -1258, KOI8-R, IBM437, IBM850, Shift_JIS, EUC-KR, Big5, GBK and gb18030, and `windows_1252` for the code point of one windows-1252 byte. The encodings other than UTF-8, UTF-16 and UTF-32 follow the WHATWG Encoding Standard's decoders, encoders and indexes; a byte a single-byte index leaves unassigned is `Malformed` to `decode` and U+FFFD to `decode_lossy`. `compiler/tablegen charset <index directory> std/charset` writes `tables.bin` and `tables.txt` from the single-byte `index-<name>.txt` files and one `<name>.bin` per multi-byte index (`jis0208`, `euc-kr`, `big5`, `gb18030` and `gb18030-ranges`): a pointer count, an entry width, the code point of each pointer and the pointers sorted by code point, which the encoders binary-search. The index directory holds the WHATWG `index-*.txt` files of 2026-11-16, named by the canonical encoding name (`index-ISO-8859-2.txt`, `index-KOI8-R.txt`), and `index-IBM437.txt` and `index-IBM850.txt` in the same format, which WHATWG does not publish. The tables total 344,528 bytes and are linked with any program that reaches `decode` or `encode`, since the label picks the table at run time. EUC-JP, ISO-2022-JP and the other WHATWG single-byte encodings are not provided. |
| `std/html` | An HTML parser following the WHATWG standard: a pull `Tokenizer` returning `StartTag`, `EndTag`, `Text`, `Comment`, `Instruction`, `Doctype` and `End` tokens, `parse`, `parse_fragment` and `parse_body` building a `Node` tree, tree navigation and editing, `inner_html`, `outer_html` and `reparsable_html`, CSS selector queries (`compile`, `query`, `query_all`, `matches`, `closest`), an allow-list sanitizer (`sanitize`, `clean`, `default_policy`), `unescape` for character references and `escape` for text and attribute values. |
| `std/markdown` | GitHub Flavored Markdown. `parse` returns a public `Block[]` tree of headings, paragraphs, fenced and indented code, raw HTML blocks, lists with a tight flag, tables with alignment, block quotes and thematic breaks, and `parse_inline` returns the `Inline[]` spans of a string. `plain_text` extracts text from spans. A `Renderer` carries hooks for code blocks, code spans, headings, HTML blocks, inline HTML and tables that can call back into `render` and `render_inline`. `render` and `to_html` produce the HTML `marked` writes with its default options. |
| `std/math` | libm's trigonometric, hyperbolic, exponential and rounding functions, `pi`, `tau`, `e`, `infinity`, `nan`, `is_nan`, `is_inf`, generic `abs`, `min`, `max`, `clamp` and `compare`, and integer `ipow`, `gcd`, `floor_div` and `floor_mod`. |
| `std/math/stats` | Statistics over `f64[]` that fail with `Error` (`Empty`, `TooShort`, `Mismatch`, `NotFinite`, `Range`, `Bins`, `Edges`, `ZeroVariance`) instead of returning `nan`: `sum` and `mean` (Neumaier compensated summation), `median`, `min`, `max`, sample and population `variance` and `stddev`, `quantile`, `percentile` and `quantiles` (linear interpolation between closest ranks, the default of `numpy.quantile`), `histogram` and `histogram_edges` (binning as `numpy.histogram`, last bin closed), and sample and population `covariance` and Pearson `correlation`. |
| `std/math/complex` | `Complex` of two `f64` with `complex`, `add`, `sub`, `mul`, `div` (Smith's method), `neg`, `scale`, `conj`, `abs` (`hypot`), `arg`, `polar` and `rect`, `cexp`, `clog`, `csqrt`, `cpow`, `pow_int`, `format` and `format_fixed`. The transcendental functions carry a `c` prefix because `exp` and `sqrt` are builtins. |
| `std/math/matrix` | A dense row-major `Matrix` of `f64`: `zeros`, `identity`, `from_data`, `from_rows`, `clone`, `get`, `set`, `transpose`, `add`, `sub`, `scale`, `multiply` and `multiply_vector`. `lu` (partial pivoting) gives an `LU` with `lower`, `upper`, `permutation`, `lu_determinant` and `lu_solve`; `determinant`, `solve` and `inverse` build on it. `qr` (Householder reflections) gives a thin `QR`, and `least_squares` solves by it. A singular matrix is `Error.Singular`; a shape mismatch panics. |
| `std/math/fft` | Discrete Fourier transforms of `Complex[]` of any length: `forward`, `inverse` (scaled by `1 / n`), and for real signals `forward_real` and `inverse_real`, which use the `n / 2 + 1` bins of `numpy.fft.rfft`. Power-of-two lengths use iterative radix-2 and other lengths Bluestein's algorithm. |
| `std/slices` | Queries and transforms: `contains`, `index`, `clone`, `concat`, `reverse`, `map`, `filter`, `fold`, `scan`, `any`, `all`, `sum`, `min` and `max` (as optionals), `sorted`. Sorting: `sort` and `sort_by` are unstable and in place, a quicksort with median-of-three pivots that recurses into the smaller side and finishes runs of 16 or fewer with insertion sort. `sort_stable` and `sort_stable_by` are a top-down merge sort; `sort_by_key` is stable. Also `reduce`, `count`, `find`, `find_index`, `first`, `last`, `equal`, `compare`, `starts_with`, `ends_with`, `unique` (adjacent duplicates), `flat_map`, `flatten`, `repeat`, `lower_bound`, `binary_search` (the first match) and `binary_search_by`. `take`, `skip`, `chunks` and `windows` share the source's elements. `partition` and `zip` work over `Pair[A, B]`. `fill`, `swap`, `rotate`, `insert` and `remove` are in place. They chain as calls on the slice: `xs.sort()`, `words.map(fn(w) w.len).sum()`.\|> slices.sort()`. |
| `std/maps` | `keys`, `values`, `sorted_keys`, `entries`, `from_pairs`, `clone`, `merge` (the right map wins), `filter`, `map_values`, `has`, `get_or`, `invert`, `count_by` and `group_by`. |
| `std/os` | Process state: `args` and `environment` (from the argument and environment vectors the program started with), `env` as `str?`, `umask` (sets the creation mask and returns the previous one), `pid` and `exit`. Queries: `getcwd`, `chdir`, `hostname`, `uid`, `gid`, `euid`, `egid`, `ppid`, `executable`, `uptime`, `cpu_count`, `error_message` (the system's message text for an error number, a `strerror` table on Linux and `FormatMessageW` on Windows), `error_code` (the error number behind an `io.Error`) and `user_name`. Accounts read `/etc/passwd` and `/etc/group` and return `none`, or an empty slice, on Windows: `lookup_user` and `lookup_user_name`, `lookup_group` and `lookup_group_name` and `supplementary_groups` (the groups that list a login name), over `parse_passwd` and `parse_group`, which skip blank lines, comments and malformed lines. Limits: `getrlimit` and `setrlimit` take a `Resource` (the sixteen Linux `RLIMIT_` resources) and an `Rlimit` of `soft` and `hard` values where `none` is unlimited, use `prlimit64`, and fail with `Unsupported` on Windows. `cgroup_limits` gives the `CgroupLimits` of the process: `cpu` (a `CpuLimit` of `quota`, `period` and `cpus`), `memory_max`, `memory_high` and `memory_current`, where `none` is no limit and a process outside a cgroup v2 hierarchy fails with `Unsupported`. On Linux it finds the group with `locate_cgroup` from the `0::` line of `/proc/self/cgroup` and the `cgroup2` mount that `parse_mountinfo` reads from `/proc/self/mountinfo` (decoding `\040`-style escapes and a mount root that is not `/`), then `cgroup_limits_of` reads `cpu.max`, `memory.max` and `memory.high` from the nearest group that sets a limit, walking up to the mount's top group and passing over a missing file or `max`, and `memory.current` from the group itself, with `parse_cpu_max` and `parse_memory_limit` rejecting other text as `Malformed`. On Windows the same call reads the job object of the process: a hard CPU rate cap as `cpu`, the smaller of the process and job memory limits as `memory_max`, and a process in no job fails with `Unsupported`. `set_env` and `unset_env` are seen by `env`, `environment`, `process.find` and the environment `process.run` passes on. Directories: `home_dir`, `cache_dir`, `config_dir`, `data_dir` and `state_dir` (XDG variables when absolute, otherwise under `$HOME`), and `temp_dir_path`. |
| `std/stdio` | `stdin`, `stdout` and `stderr` as `io.Stream`s, and `exit`. For `browser/wasm32` the host supplies the streams through `low.read` and `low.write`, and `exit` ends the run through `low.halt`, so a browser program reads input and sets its exit status without `std/os`. |
| `std/os/signal` | `Signal` (`Hangup`, `Interrupt`, `Quit`, `IllegalInstruction`, `Trap`, `Abort`, `BusError`, `FloatingPoint`, `Kill`, `User1`, `SegmentationFault`, `User2`, `Pipe`, `Alarm`, `Terminate`, `Child`, `Continue`, `Stop`, `TerminalStop`, `TerminalInput`, `TerminalOutput`, `UrgentData`, `CpuLimit`, `FileSizeLimit`, `VirtualAlarm`, `ProfilingAlarm`, `WindowChange`, `IoReady`, `PowerFailure`, `BadSystemCall` and `Other(number)` for any other number), the one signal vocabulary of `std/process`, with `number`, `name`, `from_number` and `catchable`. `notify` returns a droppable `Watch` (copyable `WatchHandle`) whose `signals` channel delivers the caught signals, with `wait_for`, `wait_timeout`, `wait_any` and `stop`. Also `ignore`, `reset`, `send` and `raise`. `Kill` and `Stop` cannot be caught and fail with `Invalid`. |
| `std/process` | `Command` with arguments, an optional replacement environment, a working directory, `Inherit`, `Piped` or `Null` for each standard stream, an optional new process group, which is a job object on Windows and ends its child when the parent ends (`PR_SET_PDEATHSIG` with `SIGKILL` on Linux, `JOB_OBJECT_LIMIT_KILL_ON_JOB_CLOSE` on Windows), and `windowless`, which starts a Windows console program with no console window. `start` returns a droppable `Child` that kills and reaps an unwaited process and closes its pipes when released. `start_handle`, `adopt`, `wait`, `kill` (which takes a `std/os/signal` `Signal`; an `Exit` is `Exited(code)` or `Signaled(signal)` with the same type), and `wait_on`, `wait_exit_on` (waits without reaping, so the process group can still be signalled) and `kill_on` on the shared `ChildHandle`. `run` feeds input and collects both outputs. `find` searches `PATH`. `success`. |
| `std/build` | The declarations of a project's `build.low` (see [Projects](#projects)): `title`, `version`, `dep(source, range)`, `local(dir)`, `modules(dir)`, `program(dir)`, `gui(dir)`, `pre_build(program, args)` and `post_build(program, args)`. The compiler reads them from the source; run as a program, each prints its declaration. |
| `std/buildinfo` | What the compiler knew when it built the program: `revision`, the commit `git rev-parse HEAD` prints in the program's project directory, empty when that fails, and `time`, the build time in seconds since the Unix epoch, read from `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH` when it holds an integer and from the clock otherwise. The compiler writes both constants when the program imports the module, so a program that does not import it runs no `git` and does not depend on the time it was built. `low lsp` and `low doc std` see the empty placeholders. |
| `std/cdp` | The Chrome DevTools Protocol. `launch` starts a headless Chromium with a disposable profile, connects to it and returns a droppable `Browser` that shuts it down when released. Its `handle` is the shared `*BrowserHandle`; `launch_handle`, `adopt`, `shutdown_on` and `open_page_on` take it. `call` sends a command to the browser or a session, and each `subscribe` gets every other message as events. `open_page` opens a page in its own browser context, which `close_page` disposes, and every request of the page, including those of cross-site frames and workers, passes through `Handlers`. Also `navigate` and `shutdown`. |
| `std/filepath` | The operating system's paths, as `std/path` handles slash-separated ones: `clean`, `join`, `base`, `dir`, `ext`, `stem` and `is_absolute`, plus `volume` and `is_separator`. On Linux and the browser it behaves as `std/path`. On Windows `\` and `/` both separate and results are written with `\`; a drive (`C:`), share (`\\server\share`) or device (`\\?\C:`, `\\?\UNC\server\share`) volume is kept by `clean` and `dir`; a share, a device path or a drive followed by a separator is absolute, while `C:x` and `\x` are not; `join` adds no separator after a bare drive and drops the leading separators of later parts, so two parts never form a share. The volume rules are the private `volume_length` in `volume_windows.low`; `volume_linux.low` and `volume_browser.low` give none. |
| `std/path` | Slash-separated path manipulation for module paths, URLs and other paths that are not the operating system's: `clean` (lexical, resolving `.` and `..`), `join`, `base`, `dir`, `ext`, `stem` and `is_absolute`. `matches` matches shell globs with `*`, `?`, `[...]` classes (ranges, `!` or `^` negation) and `\` escapes; no wildcard crosses a `/`. |
| `std/sync` | `Mutex` with `lock`, `try_lock`, `unlock` and `locked`. `WaitGroup` with `add`, `done` and `wait`. `Once` with `run`. `RwLock`, first in first out and writer-fair, with `read_lock`, `read_unlock`, `write_lock`, `write_unlock`, `try_read_lock`, `try_write_lock`, `reading` and `writing`. `Semaphore` with `acquire`, `try_acquire`, `release` and `available`. `Cond` with `wait_cond`, `signal` and `broadcast`. `Barrier` with `wait_barrier`. `OnceValue[T]`. `ErrorGroup[E]`, whose `go` blocks while `limit` tasks run and whose `join` returns the first error. All are built on channels, so a waiting green thread parks instead of spinning. `lock_thread` and `unlock_thread` pin the calling green thread to its OS thread, for C libraries whose state belongs to one thread; they nest. |
| `std/retry` | `Policy` with attempts, base, multiplier, maximum delay, `Jitter` (`None`, `Full`, `Equal`, `Decorrelated`), a deadline, a seeded `Rng` and a cancel token. `delay`; a loop driver `start` with `next` (`reserve` then `commit`) and `at_least`; `run` over a `Step` (`Done`, `Retry`, `RetryAfter`, `Fatal`) giving an `Outcome`. |
| `std/ratelimit` | `Bucket` (token bucket in exact nanosecond units) with `allow`, `allow_n`, `reserve`, `wait` and `set_rate`. `Window`, a sliding log. `Keyed`, a bounded per-key set of buckets with idle eviction. All take a `clock.Clock`. |
| `std/singleflight` | `Group[K, V]` with `do`, `do_shared`, `forget` and `in_flight`, which run one call per key while the others wait for its result. `Expiring[V]`, a value refreshed at most once per interval. |
| `std/atomic` | `Int`, `Bool` and `Flag` built on compare-and-swap, with `load_int`, `store_int`, `add_int`, `swap_int`, `compare_and_swap_int` and `update_int`, and the same forms for `Bool`. Every load is a locked operation, so a loop cannot hoist it. `Value[T]` swaps a heap pointer under a lock: `load_value`, `store_value`, `swap_value`, `update_value`, `snapshot_value` and `compare_and_swap_value` (compares pointer identity). |
| `std/rand` | A seedable, non-cryptographic xoshiro256** generator: `seeded`, `rng` (all 256 bits of state from `std/crypto`), `next`, unbiased `below` and `between`, `float`, `chance`, `shuffle` and `pick`. `pcg32`, `pcg64` and `chacha` (seeded from 32 bytes, producing the keystream of `std/crypto/aead`'s `chacha20_block`, computed by `chacha20_state`; `chacha_rng` seeds from entropy) produce an `Rng` that every function accepts, and `next32` draws 32 bits. Distributions: `normal`, `exponential`, `gamma`, `beta`, `poisson`, `binomial`, `geometric`, `zipf`, `weighted_pick`, `cumulative` with `pick_cumulative`, `sample` and `permutation`. Use `std/crypto` for secrets. |
| `std/uuid` | Random v4 and time-ordered v7 UUIDs, `format`, `parse` (reports the offset of the first bad character), `version`, `timestamp` for v7, `equal` and `nil`. |
| `std/csv` | RFC 4180 records with quoted fields, doubled quotes, embedded newlines and CRLF or LF line ends. `parse` and `parse_with` a separator fail with the line of an unterminated or bare quote. `format`, `format_with` and `append_record` quote only fields that need it. |
| `std/dotenv` | `.env` files. `parse` and `read` return a map from `NAME=value` lines. They allow `export`, blank lines, `#` comments (inline after whitespace in unquoted values), single quotes taken literally, and double quotes with `\n`, `\t` and `\r` escapes that may span lines. Errors carry the line. |
| `std/flag` | Command-line flags from `Spec`s with help text, booleans and defaults: `--name value`, `--name=value`, single-dash forms and `--`. `get`, `enabled`, `number`, the remaining arguments, and `usage`. |
| `std/log` | Leveled line logging (`debug`, `info`, `warn`, `error`) to a descriptor (stderr by default) as `level prefix: message`, without timestamps so journald and other collectors add their own. `with_prefix` nests a prefix. A `Logger` also carries attributes (`str_attr`, `int_attr`, `float_attr`, `bool_attr`, `with_attrs`, and the `_with` level forms), a `Format` of `Text` (`key=value`, quoting what needs it) or `Json` (one object per line), a `Sink` (a descriptor, an `io.Stream`, a line closure, a `Record` handler, or a `Destination` function that can fail, set with `with_destination`), optional RFC 3339 timestamps, and a level from `parse_level` or the `LOG_LEVEL` variable. Each record is one `write`, so lines from green threads do not interleave. A failed write or failed `Destination` is passed to `Logger.on_error` (set with `with_error_handler`), which ignores it by default. `std/log/syslog` and `std/log/journald` attach as destinations. |
| `std/log/syslog` | RFC 5424 syslog. `format` writes a `Message` (a `Facility`, a `Severity`, an optional timestamp, hostname, app name, process id, message id, structured data as `Element` and `Param` values and the text) as `<PRI>1 TIMESTAMP HOSTNAME APP-NAME PROCID MSGID STRUCTURED-DATA MSG`, with an RFC 3339 UTC timestamp, header fields cut to the lengths of section 6 and to printable ASCII, `-` for an empty field, structured-data values escaped as section 6.3.3 says and the UTF-8 byte order mark before text that is not ASCII. `message` makes a `Message` from a `log.Record`, putting the attributes in one structured-data element, and `severity_of` maps a level to a severity. `open` returns a droppable `Syslog` for a `Transport`: `UnixDatagram` (the default, `/dev/log`), `UnixStream`, `Udp` (RFC 5426), `Tcp` and `Tls` (RFC 6587 and RFC 5425, each message framed by octet counting, which `octet_counted` writes). The connection is made by the first `send` and again by the first one after a failure; a failed send returns the error and the message is lost; sends from green threads are serialised. `send`, `send_record`, `close` and `attach`, which returns a `log.Logger` that sends every record through the `Syslog`. |
| `std/log/journald` | The native protocol of systemd's journal. `encode` writes `Field` values as datagram payload: `NAME=value` and a newline, or for a value with a newline the name, a newline, the length as a 64-bit little-endian integer, the value and a newline; a field whose name is not valid (`valid_name`) is left out. `fields` makes the fields of a `log.Record`: `PRIORITY` (`priority_of`), `MESSAGE`, `SYSLOG_IDENTIFIER` and one field for each attribute, named by `field_name`, so an attribute `code_file` or `code_line` becomes `CODE_FILE` or `CODE_LINE`. `open` returns a droppable `Journal` that sends to `/run/systemd/journal/socket` by default over a Unix datagram socket, opened by the first `send` and again after a failure; an entry too large for a datagram goes as a sealed in-memory file passed with `SCM_RIGHTS`, as the protocol describes, and fails with `Unsupported` on Windows. `send`, `send_record`, `close` and `attach`. |
| `std/term` | `is_tty`, `size` and `set_size`. `enter_raw` returns a droppable `Raw` that restores the terminal when released. `Key`, `read_key` (a lone escape resolves after 50 ms) and `parse_keys`. ANSI helpers: `fg`, `bg` with 16, 256 and RGB colours, `bold`, `dim`, `underline`, cursor movement, `clear_screen`, the alternate screen, `strip` and `visible_width`. `colour_enabled` honours `NO_COLOR`, `CLICOLOR_FORCE` and `TERM=dumb`, and on Windows switches a console to virtual terminal processing so the escape sequences print as colour. `open_pty`. |
| `std/mime` | Media types by file extension and back (`by_extension`, `extension`) for web, image, font, archive, audio, video and subtitle formats. `sniff` recognises content by its magic bytes or its leading text and falls back to plain text or `application/octet-stream`. |
| `std/semver` | Semantic Versioning 2.0.0: `parse` (with an optional `v`, failing at the offset of the first bad part), `format`, and `compare` and `less` with prerelease precedence, ignoring build metadata. |
| `std/container/heap` | A binary `Heap[T]` ordered by a `less` function: `insert`, `pop`, `peek` and `size`. |
| `std/container/lru` | A least-recently-used `Cache[K, V]` of fixed capacity: `get` (refreshes the entry), `put` (returns the evicted key), `contains`, `size` and `keys` from most to least recent. All run in constant time over index-linked entries. |
| `std/container/deque` | A ring-buffer `Deque[T]`: `push_front`, `push_back`, `pop_front`, `pop_back`, `front`, `back`, `at` and `size`, doubling when full. |
| `std/container/set` | `Set[T]` over a map, in insertion order: `set`, `from_slice`, `add` and `remove` (both return whether the set changed), `has`, `size`, `items`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference` and `is_subset`. |
| `std/container/ordered` | An AVL `Tree[K, V]` for ordered keys: `tree`, `put`, `get`, `remove`, `contains`, `size`, `height`, `is_valid`, `first`, `last`, `floor_entry`, `ceiling_entry`, `each`, `each_range`, `keys`, `values`, `entries` and the half-open `range(lo, hi)`. |
| `std/container/sorted` | `Sorted[T]`, an ordered set over `ordered.Tree`: `sorted`, `from_slice`, `add`, `remove`, `has`, `size`, `items`, `first`, `last`, `floor_item`, `ceiling_item` and `range`. |
| `std/archive` | The filesystem layer shared by `std/archive/tar`, `std/archive/cpio` and `std/archive/zip`, built on `std/file` and `std/sys`. Names are cleaned and rejected when absolute, containing `..` or NUL, or passing through a symbolic link below the destination. Files are created exclusively without following links. The extractors share its `Stamp` and `apply_stamps` for directory times, `write_from` for file bodies and `walk` for the sorted tree walk of `create`, and the tar and cpio readers share its `Input`, which counts every byte against the total limit. |
| `std/archive/tar` | ustar, GNU and pax archives. A streaming `Reader` (`next`, `read_body`, `read_entry`, `list`) with limits on header, entry and total size. A `Writer` (`add_file`, `add_dir`, `add_symlink`, `add_hardlink`, `finish`) that uses pax records for long or non-ASCII names. gzip through `read_gzip` and `write_gzip`. `extract`, `extract_gzip`, `create` and `create_gzip` over the filesystem strip setuid, setgid and sticky bits. |
| `std/archive/cpio` | newc, CRC and odc archives, detected per entry on read: a `Reader`, a `Writer` (`add_file`, `add_dir`, `add_symlink`, `add_links`, `finish`), and `extract` and `create` over the filesystem with the safe extraction of `std/archive`. Hard links store their data with the last link in newc and CRC. The binary format fails with `Unsupported`. |
| `std/archive/zip` | Zip archives read through the central directory, with zip64, stored and deflate entries, CRC-32 checks and limits on entries, sizes and compression ratio. Written with `add_file`, `add_dir`, `add_symlink` and `finish`, which emits zip64 records when a count or size needs them. `begin_entry` returns an `EntryWriter` that takes one file entry in pieces through `write_entry` and completes it with `end_entry`: the data goes through the streaming raw deflate encoder or is stored as it arrives, so the writer keeps only the encoder state, a running CRC-32 and the byte counts, and a `stream_writer` sink receives it before the entry ends. The local header sets general purpose flag bit 3 with the CRC-32 and sizes zero and a data descriptor follows the data, so the descriptor width is fixed when the entry begins: 8-byte sizes with a zip64 extra field in the local header when `force_zip64` was called first, otherwise 4-byte sizes and `write_entry` fails with `TooLarge` at `0xFFFFFFFF` bytes. While an entry is open `add`, `begin_entry` and `finish` fail with `Invalid`. `extract` works over the filesystem. Encrypted entries and other methods fail with `Unsupported`. |
| `std/testing` | `T`, passed to a test `fn(*T)`, with `log`, `error`, `fatal`, `skip`, `require` and `sub`. Assertions `equal`, `not_equal`, `equal_slice`, `is_true`, `is_false`, `is_some`, `is_none`, `contains`, `contains_item`, `close_to`, `unexpected`, `must` and `expect_error` render values of any type. `table` and `table_of`. `run` and `run_with` run each test on its own green thread with a timeout, filter with `-run`, print with `-v` and exit with status 1 on failure. `Settings.timeout_ms` of `0` sets no limit, for a parent process that enforces the timeout. `Test` carries the `file` and `line` of its function, which a generated list fills. `run` with `-list` prints `name`, `file` and `line` separated by tabs for each test and exits with status 0 without running anything; with `-one <name>` it runs only the top-level test of that exact name, prints its report without a summary and exits with status 0 when it passed, 1 when it failed and `skipped_status` (3) when it was skipped, or prints `testing: no test named <name>` and exits with status 2 when no test has that name; `low test` runs a test program through these two flags, one process per test. `-inputs <file>` with `-one` creates the file and starts `sys.record_inputs` on it just before the test runs, so the runner's own reads are not logged; a file that cannot be created leaves the test running without a log. `bench`, `bench_for` and `blackhole`. `golden` rewrites its file when `Settings.update_golden` is set, which `-update` does. `check_property`, `check_shrunk` and `check_ints` print the seed and shrink integers. |
| `std/base32` | RFC 4648 base32 with the standard and extended-hex alphabets: `encode`, `encode_unpadded`, `decode` (case-insensitive, padding optional, failing at the first bad character or on an impossible length), `encode_hex` and `decode_hex`. |
| `std/base58` | The Bitcoin alphabet (`encode`, `decode`), the Flickr alphabet (`encode_flickr`, `decode_flickr`) and Base58Check (`encode_check`, `decode_check`), failing at the first bad character. |
| `std/ascii85` | `encode`, `encode_adobe` (with `<~ ~>`), `decode`, and Z85 (`encode_z85`, `decode_z85`). |
| `std/msgpack` | MessagePack: the `Value` model with timestamps and extensions, a decoder with depth and length limits that reports the offset of an error, the smallest-form `encode`, and `serialize[T]` and `deserialize[T]`. |
| `std/arrow` | Apache Arrow arrays and the IPC stream and file formats. `Array` holds a type, a length, a validity bitmap, value buffers, children and a dictionary for null, bool, 8 to 64-bit integers, float16, float32, float64, decimal128, date, time, timestamp, duration, interval, binary, utf8 and their large forms, fixed-size binary, list, large list, fixed-size list, struct, map, dense and sparse union and dictionary types; `Schema` and `Field` carry key and value metadata. Decimal256, view types, run-end encoding, big-endian data and the version 4 union layout are not supported, and reading them fails with `Unsupported`. Buffers are `u8[]`, so an array in memory is not 64-byte aligned; the writer aligns the metadata and every body buffer to `WriterOptions.alignment` (8, 16, 32 or 64 bytes, default 8) and the reader accepts any alignment. `builder` with `append_*`, `child`, `end_list`, `end_struct`, `append_union` and `finish` builds arrays (a dictionary builder interns text, bytes and integers) and `finish` validates them; `validate` checks offsets, lengths, null counts and type ids of any array. `int_at`, `uint_at`, `bool_at`, `float_at` (half floats widen to `f64`), `bytes_at`, `str_at`, `decimal_text`, `list_range`, `union_slot` and `dictionary_index` read values and `diagnostic` prints an array on one line. `stream_reader`, `stream_bytes`, `next_batch`, `read_batches`, `open_file`, `open_file_bytes`, `read_batch` and `read_file_batches` read the IPC stream and file formats, including the continuation marker, the pre-0.15 framing, dictionary batches and deltas, and LZ4 frame and Zstandard compressed bodies; `ReaderOptions` caps the metadata and body sizes, every offset and length in the hand-read Flatbuffers metadata is checked against its buffer, nesting is limited to 64 levels, and truncated or corrupt input returns an `Error` (`Invalid`, `Truncated`, `TooLarge`, `Unsupported`, `Mismatch` or `Io`) and does not panic or loop. `stream_writer`, `file_writer`, `write_batch`, `close`, `write_stream` and `write_file` write both formats, with `Compression` of `Uncompressed`, `Lz4` or `Zstd` per buffer (a buffer that does not shrink is stored as it is); a stream writer resends a dictionary whose values changed, a file writer fails with `Mismatch` because the file format cannot replace one, and dictionary deltas are read but never written. |
| `std/avro` | Apache Avro. `parse` reads a schema (primitives, records, enums, arrays, maps, unions, fixed, named references, namespaces, aliases, defaults and the logical types decimal, uuid, date, time-millis, time-micros, the timestamp and local-timestamp types and duration) into a `*Schema` graph; `canonical`, `fingerprint` (CRC-64-AVRO), `fingerprint_sha256` and `fingerprint_md5` give the parsing canonical form and its fingerprints, and `to_json` writes a schema back. `Value` is the dynamic datum, with a logical variant for each logical type; `decode` and `decode_with` (`Options` limit the length, element count and depth the input may declare, so a hostile length or a zero-byte element count cannot allocate or loop) read binary data and `encode` writes it, failing with `Mismatch` and the path of a value that does not fit. `decode_resolved` and `resolve` apply schema resolution: promotion, aliases, defaults, enum defaults and union matching. `serialize[T]`, `deserialize[T]`, `to_value` and `from_value` go through `std/serde`. `file_writer` and `open` write and read object container files as a stream over `std/io` with the codecs null, deflate, snappy (with its CRC-32), zstandard, bzip2 and xz, `append` or `write_value` adding values, `finish` closing the last block, and `next`, `read_all` or `read_values[T]` yielding them; a writer takes a fixed sync marker for reproducible files, and a reader checks the magic, sync markers, block sizes and checksums. `encode_single`, `decode_single` and `SchemaStore` implement single-object encoding. Every decoding failure is an `Error` carrying the byte offset; `diagnostic` prints a value on one line. |
| `std/parquet` | Apache Parquet files. `open_bytes` and `open` read the footer into `Metadata` (schema, row groups, column chunks, encodings, statistics, key and value metadata); `open` reads a file by seeking to each column chunk and keeps no file open. `read_row_group`, `read_batches` and `read_column` return Arrow arrays, `columns` and `arrow_schema` describe the leaf columns and the Arrow schema (`PARQUET:field_id` becomes field metadata, an unknown logical type reads as `null`), `read_rows` returns `Value` rows and `diagnostic` prints a `Value`; a field list projects columns and reads only their chunks, and `select_row_groups` keeps the row groups whose integer statistics can hold a range. Physical types boolean, int32, int64, int96, float, double, byte array and fixed-length byte array; logical types string, enum, UUID, JSON, BSON, float16, date, integers, decimals, times and timestamps; lists (the three-level form and the older repeated forms), maps and structs of any nesting through repetition and definition levels. Encodings PLAIN, the RLE and bit-packed hybrid, PLAIN_DICTIONARY and RLE_DICTIONARY, DELTA_BINARY_PACKED, DELTA_LENGTH_BYTE_ARRAY, DELTA_BYTE_ARRAY, BYTE_STREAM_SPLIT and RLE booleans, in data pages v1 and v2 and dictionary pages; codecs UNCOMPRESSED, SNAPPY, GZIP, ZSTD, BROTLI, LZ4_RAW and the Hadoop-framed LZ4 (read only). A page CRC is checked unless `ReaderOptions.verify_checksums` is off; statistics are trusted when the file declares a type-defined column order, or for the older fields only on boolean, signed integer and float columns. Encrypted files and LZO fail with `Unsupported`; bloom filters and page indexes are ignored. `ReaderOptions` caps the footer, page and value count, the hand-written Thrift compact reader limits nesting and every length, and damaged input returns an `Error` (`Invalid`, `Truncated`, `TooLarge`, `Unsupported`, `Mismatch`, `Checksum` or `Io`) and does not panic or loop. `write_bytes`, `write` and `write_rows` write a file from Arrow batches or `Value` rows: `WriterOptions` sets the codec and level (SNAPPY by default; the Hadoop LZ4 and LZO are not written), dictionary encoding with a fallback to PLAIN when the dictionary page would exceed `dictionary_page_size`, data page version 1 or 2, the page size, rows per row group, statistics, page CRCs and an encoding per column (PLAIN, RLE, DELTA_BINARY_PACKED, DELTA_LENGTH_BYTE_ARRAY, DELTA_BYTE_ARRAY, BYTE_STREAM_SPLIT or RLE_DICTIONARY); the footer declares type-defined column orders and the statistics hold the minimum and maximum (NaN excluded, unsigned and decimal orders respected) and the null count. Arrow dates in milliseconds, times and timestamps in seconds, durations, intervals and unions are not written (`Unsupported`), a null in a non-nullable field is `Mismatch`. |
| `std/bson` | BSON documents as `Field[]` with ObjectId, datetime, binary, regex, int32, int64, double and Decimal128 (as bytes): `decode`, `encode`, `get`, `diagnostic`, `new_object_id` and `parse_object_id`. |
| `std/mongo` | A MongoDB driver over `OP_MSG` with `std/bson` documents, plain or over TLS. `connect` takes a `mongodb://` string and `connect_with` an `Options`; both return a droppable `Client` whose copyable view is `ClientHandle`. `database` and `collection` give the handles that `find`, `find_one`, `aggregate`, `count_documents`, `estimated_document_count`, `distinct`, `insert_one`, `insert_many`, `update_one`, `update_many`, `replace_one`, `delete_one`, `delete_many`, `find_one_and_delete`, `find_one_and_replace`, `find_one_and_update`, `bulk_write`, `create_index`, `create_indexes`, `drop_index`, `list_indexes`, `create_collection`, `drop_collection`, `drop_database`, `list_collection_names`, `list_database_names` and `run_command` take. A `Cursor` reads with `next` and `all` and is released with `close_cursor`. A `Session` comes from `start_session` and carries `start_transaction`, `commit_transaction`, `abort_transaction` and `with_transaction`. `text_of`, `int_of`, `document_of` and the other builders and `get_text`, `get_int` and the other getters work on `bson.Field[]`. `Error` separates connection, authentication, server (with its error labels), write and write-concern failures. |
| `std/ini` | INI files with sections, `=` or `:`, `;` and `#` comments, quoted values, continuations and duplicate keys (last wins, or all with `collect`): `parse`, `get`, `get_all`, `sections`, `set`, `format`. |
| `std/properties` | Java `.properties`: `parse`, `get`, `set`, `format`, with `\u` escapes and surrogate pairs. |
| `std/big` | `Int` over 32-bit limbs. Parsing and printing in bases 2 to 36 (printing divides the limbs, packed into 64-bit words, by the largest power of the base below 2^64 with a precomputed reciprocal). `add`, `sub`, `mul` (Karatsuba), `divmod`, `divmod_floor`, `divmod_euclid`, `pow`, `modpow` (Montgomery for odd moduli), `mod_inverse`, `gcd`, `lcm`, `isqrt`. Shifts and bitwise operators with two's complement semantics. `is_probable_prime`, `next_prime`, `random_below`, `to_f64`, `from_bytes` and `to_bytes`. Division by zero is an error. Not constant time. |
| `std/big/rat` | Normalised rationals over `big.Int`: `ratio`, `parse` (`a/b`, decimals, exponents), the four operations, `inv`, `cmp`, `to_fixed`, `to_f64`. |
| `std/big/decimal` | A coefficient and an `i32` exponent: exact `add`, `sub` and `mul`, and `div` and `rescale` with `HalfEven`, `HalfUp`, `Down`, `Up`, `Floor` or `Ceiling` rounding to a target exponent. |
| `std/redis` | RESP2 and RESP3 over TCP or TLS: a droppable `Client` with a `ClientHandle`, `connect` with AUTH, SELECT and HELLO 3, `command`, typed helpers for strings, hashes, lists, sets, sorted sets and `publish`, pipelining, `transaction`, pub/sub through a channel usable in `select`, command timeouts, and limits on bulk size and nesting. |
| `std/nats` | A NATS client over TCP or TLS: a droppable `Client` with a `ClientHandle`, `connect` with a token, a user and password or an NKey seed that signs the server nonce, the TLS upgrade when the server sends `tls_required`, `connect_tls` for a server that handshakes first, `connect_stream`, `publish` and `publish_with` (reply subject and headers), `subscribe` and `queue_subscribe` through a channel usable in `select`, `unsubscribe` and `unsubscribe_after`, `request` over one shared inbox with a timeout and `NoResponders`, `flush`, `drain`, answers to the server `PING` and a client ping interval with a missed-pong limit, subject and header validation, `max_payload` enforcement and limits on control lines and message size. Reconnection with server lists, JetStream, key/value and object stores, the WebSocket transport and JWT credential files are not included. |
| `std/mqtt` | An MQTT 3.1.1 and 5.0 client over TCP or TLS: a droppable `Client` with a copyable `ClientHandle`, `connect`, `connect_tls` and `connect_stream` taking an `Options` that picks the version, credentials, will, session, keep alive, `receive_maximum`, `max_packet_size` and topic aliases. `publish` and `publish_async` with `wait_publication` run QoS 0, 1 and 2 in both directions; `subscribe`, `subscribe_with` and `unsubscribe` take `Filter`s with the MQTT 5 options; incoming messages arrive on a channel usable in `select` that holds back the connection's reads while it is full, and the end of a connection on `disconnected`. `reconnect` resumes a session that outlives the connection (`clean_start` false in MQTT 3.1.1, a session expiry above 0 in MQTT 5) and resends unacknowledged publishes with dup, completing them with `SessionLost` when there is no session to resume. MQTT 5 properties, server DISCONNECT and AUTH through an `Options.authenticate` callback are supported. `encode`, `decode` and `read_packet` expose the codec, and `valid_topic_name`, `valid_filter` and `matches` validate and match topics. `close_client` is explicit and idempotent from `Client` and `ClientHandle`, and a connection whose broker stops reading ends through `Options.timeout` or the keep alive. WebSocket, automatic reconnect loops and client-initiated re-authentication are not provided. |
| `std/smtp` | An SMTP client: `connect`, `connect_starttls` and `connect_tls`, EHLO extensions, AUTH PLAIN, LOGIN, XOAUTH2 and CRAM-MD5 (plaintext authentication is refused unless `insecure_auth`), `send` with per-recipient results, dot-stuffing, pipelining when advertised, and errors separating 4xx from 5xx. |
| `std/mail` | Message building: `Message` with text and HTML alternatives and attachments, RFC 2047 encoded words, quoted-printable, base64 wrapped at 76, RFC 5322 dates, and rejection of CR and LF in any header. |
| `std/template` | A text template engine over a `Data` model (`to_data[T]` through serde): `{{ expr }}`, `if`, `for`, `include`, `block` and `extends`, `let`, trim markers, filters (built in and registered), HTML autoescaping, depth and output limits, and errors with line and column. A `Set` is read-only while rendering, so green threads share it. |
| `std/multipart` | multipart parsing and writing (RFC 7578, RFC 2046): a streaming `Reader` with limits and a spill sink for large parts, RFC 2231 and 5987 parameters, `Writer` (`write_field`, `write_file` and `write_form_part`) and `Form`; `std/http` reads and writes them with `multipart_reader`, `multipart_form`, `set_multipart` and `with_multipart`. |
| `std/base64` | One encoder, `encode_with(data, alphabet, padded)`, and one decoder, `decode_with(s, alphabet, padding)`, over an alphabet (`standard`, `url_safe`) and a `Padding` rule (`Required`, `Forbidden`, `Optional`). `encode`, `encode_unpadded` and `encode_url`, and `decode`, `decode_unpadded` and `decode_url`, fix the alphabet and padding. Every decoder rejects non-zero trailing bits and a byte outside the alphabet. |

### Resource ownership

Every standard library resource that must be disposed of is a droppable owner bound with `using`. `defer` is for work that is not disposal: unlocking a lock, signalling a channel, restoring ambient state and logging.

- An owner keeps the resource's name (`file.File`, `net.Conn`, `net.Listener`, `net.Socket`, `dbus.Connection`, `tls.Session`, `http.Connection`, `websocket.Socket`, `process.Child`, `cancel.Registration`, `pipe.Reader`, `pipe.Writer`, `sqlite.Database`, `sql.Connection`, `cdp.Browser`, `otel.Sdk`) and holds its view in a public `handle` field. Its `drop` closes through the handle.
- A view is named `<Owner>Handle`: `FileHandle`, `ConnHandle`, `ListenerHandle`, `SocketHandle`, `SessionHandle`, `ChildHandle`, `RegistrationHandle`, `ReaderHandle`, `WriterHandle`, `DatabaseHandle`, `ConnectionHandle`, `BrowserHandle` and `SdkHandle`. `SocketHandle` names the view in both `net` and `websocket`. A view is a copyable struct, or a pointer to the shared state when that state is mutable, so closures capture it, slices, channels, optionals and pointer-held structs store it, and `spawn` takes it. A view never closes anything on its own.
- Operations a caller uses directly take the owner and borrow it: `net.read(c, buf)`. Operations that run after ownership has moved elsewhere (green threads, `io.Stream` closures, pools, callbacks) take the view and end in `_on`: `net.read_on(h, buf)`, `net.stream_on(h)`, `net.close_on(h)`. A function that returns a view without an owner ends in `_handle`: `net.accept_handle`, `tls.client_handle`, `cdp.launch_handle`. `adopt(h)` makes an owner from a view. `detach(mut owner)` gives up an owner's responsibility so a function that opened a resource and wrapped it can return the wrapper.
- Closing is explicit and idempotent from the owner, the view and the stream, and the drop after an explicit close does nothing. The closed state lives in the shared view. Descriptor-based views share an `io.Guard`: every operation enters it, `retire` marks it closed and wakes blocked operations, and once the wake has returned the last operation to leave closes the descriptors. A view used after its owner closed fails with `NotConnected` and never reaches a reused descriptor. The SQL drivers fail with `sql.Error.Closed` and cdp with `cdp.Error.Closed`.
- `io.Stream` is a view whose `close` closes everything beneath it. In `tls.client(net.stream(c), config)`, closing the session closes the connection, and the connection's own drop does nothing.
- A droppable cannot be stored in a pointer, slice, map, channel, optional or enum variant, captured by a closure or passed to `spawn`, so long-lived and shared state holds views. The HTTP client's idle connections, its HTTP/2 connections and the reaper's deadlines hold `ConnHandle` and `io.Stream` views and close them explicitly with the `_on` operations. A green thread that should own a resource receives its view and binds the owner itself: `http.serve` accepts with `net.accept_handle`, and each spawned connection thread runs `using c = net.adopt(h)`. Pump threads that only use a resource take its view and leave it to its owner. A scope that opens a resource for a wrapper it returns binds it with `using var` and calls `detach`, as `tls.dial` does.
- A `file.Mapping` unmaps its region when released and has no view: its `data` slice is valid while the `Mapping` is held, and a copy used after the release faults.
- A `process.Child` that was not waited for is killed and reaped when released. A `pipe.Reader` or `pipe.Writer` completes when released, so a peer never waits on an abandoned end.

### Networking

- Sockets are non-blocking. An operation that would block waits with `sys.wait_socket`, so a green thread waiting on the network never holds an OS thread the collector must wait for. Every connection writes through a second descriptor from `sys.write_view`, so one green thread can wait to read while another waits to write.
- A timeout spawns a green thread that sleeps and shuts the socket down if the operation is still waiting, entering the guard first so a socket already closed is left alone. The waiting operation then fails with `TimedOut`.
