- A connection counts the reads and writes in progress. Closing it shuts it down, which wakes any green thread still waiting while the descriptor stays registered. The last operation to finish closes the descriptors, so a descriptor number is never reused under a waiting operation. Reads and writes after `close` fail with `NotConnected`, and closing twice does nothing.
- A UDP receive that waits re-enters the socket's guard after each wake, so closing the socket from another green thread fails the waiting receive with `NotConnected`.
- `serve` runs each connection on its own green thread.
- A resolver query tries a host in this order: IP literal, hosts file, `localhost`, then DNS over each name of the search list. Every attempt opens a new UDP socket, so the kernel picks the source port, and writes a query with a random id and an EDNS(0) payload of 1232. Only a reply from the server's address and port with that id, the query's question and QR set counts; anything else is ignored until the attempt's timeout. A truncated reply repeats the query over TCP. Servers are tried in order (rotated with `rotate`) for `attempts` rounds. An NXDOMAIN reply that is authoritative or has recursion available ends the search, and one that is neither moves to the next server. A and AAAA are asked for each candidate name, and the search continues to the next candidate only on NODATA. A `FORMERR` reply to an EDNS query is repeated without EDNS, and `use-vc` sends every query over TCP. CNAME chains are followed within a reply and across queries up to eight links, and a loop fails `Io(Malformed)`.
- A resolver with a token registers a callback that closes the socket or connection of the attempt in progress, and the lookup then fails with `Io(Aborted)`.
- Cancellation is cooperative. A token runs its callbacks and closes its channel once, so code stops blocking work by closing what it waits on: `net.close` wakes a waiting read or write, `process.kill` ends a child, and `select` takes `<-cancel.done(t)`.
- An HTTP/1.1 handler without an `Upgrade` header runs on the connection's green thread while one spawned watcher reads ahead into the request reader. End of input or a read error cancels the request's token, and the response is still written. Requests read ahead are served in order afterwards. An HTTP/2 or HTTP/3 stream's token is cancelled when the client resets it or the connection ends. Every request's token is cancelled once its handler returns.
- A client request with a token arms its deadline even without a timeout. Cancelling the token fires the deadline, which shuts down the connection the request is using and wakes the request from waiting on a pooled connection or an HTTP/2 stream.
- A server speaks HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 on the same port. A connection that opens with the HTTP/2 preface is served as HTTP/2; any other is HTTP/1.1 with keep-alive, pipelining, chunked bodies and `Expect: 100-continue`. Over TLS, ALPN offers `h2` and `http/1.1` and the same detection applies. The `h2c` upgrade from HTTP/1.1, deprecated by RFC 9113, is not offered.
- HTTP/2 covers framing, HPACK with its dynamic table and Huffman decoding (`std/http/huffman`), stream multiplexing, flow control in both directions, SETTINGS, PING, RST_STREAM, GOAWAY and CONTINUATION, and the request validation of RFC 9113 section 8. A reader green thread feeds frames to the connection, each request's handler runs on its own green thread, and the connection selects over frames and finished responses, so every write happens on one green thread. The encoder uses the static table and literals without Huffman coding. It passes h2spec except the test sending a garbage preface, which the shared port reads as an HTTP/1.1 request. A handler's `Response.trailers` are sent as a final HEADERS frame over HTTP/2; HTTP/1.1 responses carry none.
- HTTP/3 (RFC 9114) is served by `serve_quic` and `serve_quic_on` over a `quic.Listener` whose ALPN is `h3`; a connection that negotiated another protocol is closed with `H3_GENERAL_PROTOCOL_ERROR`. Each connection opens a control stream that carries SETTINGS (QPACK table capacity 4096, 16 blocked streams, field section size 65536) and a QPACK decoder stream, and the encoder is static-only so no encoder stream is opened. A green thread per peer unidirectional stream reads the control, QPACK encoder and QPACK decoder streams, at most 16 at a time; a duplicate or a push stream is `H3_STREAM_CREATION_ERROR`, a closed critical stream `H3_CLOSED_CRITICAL_STREAM`, a first control frame that is not SETTINGS `H3_MISSING_SETTINGS`, and an unknown stream type is stopped and ignored. A green thread per request stream reads HEADERS, DATA and an optional trailing HEADERS, runs the handler and writes the response; a connection runs at most the listener's `max_streams` of them at a time and does not accept the next request stream until one has finished, so a client that stops its streams cannot push the handler count past that. One `qpack.Decoder` per connection sits behind a mutex; a section that needs table entries the encoder stream has not delivered waits for them, bounded by the life of the connection, and every `decode`, `cancel_stream` and `on_encoder_stream` writes the decoder stream bytes it owes, taking and writing them under one lock so the instructions reach the peer in the order they were produced. A write to the decoder stream that fails because the peer sent STOP_SENDING closes the connection with `H3_CLOSED_CRITICAL_STREAM`. Frame sizes are checked before anything is read or allocated: a HEADERS frame over 64 KiB or a control frame over 16 KiB is `H3_EXCESSIVE_LOAD`, and no buffer is sized from a declared length, since a field section is read into a buffer that grows with the bytes that arrive, unknown frame types are read and discarded in chunks of at most 4 KiB, DATA is read in chunks of at most 16 KiB, each chunk no larger than what is left of the frame, and a body over 64 MiB resets the stream with `H3_EXCESSIVE_LOAD`. A frame out of order, such as DATA before HEADERS or HTTP/2-only frame types, is `H3_FRAME_UNEXPECTED`. A request with an uppercase field name, a connection-specific field, a missing or repeated pseudo-header, a pseudo-header in the trailers or a `content-length` that differs from the DATA total resets and stops its stream with `H3_MESSAGE_ERROR` and the handler is not called. A field section over the size limit is answered with `431`. A response is HEADERS, DATA, an optional trailing HEADERS for `Response.trailers`, then FIN; a streaming `Response.stream` sends one DATA frame per chunk. `Request.token` is cancelled when the response is complete, when a write of the response fails because the client stopped or reset the stream, and when the connection ends; a stop or reset that arrives while the handler runs is not noticed before the response is written. A client that resets its request stream before it is complete has its response reset with `H3_REQUEST_CANCELLED`. Server push, extended CONNECT and HTTP datagrams are not supported.
- A handler that returns status 101 owns the connection: the server writes nothing more and closes it once the handler returns. `http.upgrade(req, headers)` writes the `101` and returns the connection's reader, and `http.upgraded()` is the response returned after a WebSocket session.
- `Request.connection` is the buffered reader of an HTTP/1.1 connection. `websocket.accept` writes the handshake to it and reads frames from it. A WebSocket server requires masked client frames, answers pings, reassembles fragments, checks UTF-8 in text messages, limits messages to 16 MB and echoes a close frame.
- `http.client()` returns a `Builder[Client]` value whose `with_*` functions return updated copies. `build` makes a `*Client` that any number of green threads share. `c.request(method, url)` copies the client's settings into a `Builder[Response]`, so headers, authentication, timeouts, redirects, retries, version and decompression can be overridden per request. The type parameter only selects whether `build` or `send` applies.
- HTTP/1.1 client connections: a request takes an idle keep-alive connection from a pool keyed by scheme, host and port, dials a new one while the host is under its limit, or queues for one. A sweeper green thread expires idle connections. A pooled connection that fails before any response byte is retried once on a fresh one.
- HTTP/2 client connections: over TLS the client offers `h2` and `http/1.1` by ALPN and keeps one HTTP/2 connection per host. A reader green thread decodes frames into per-stream queues, request green threads wait on their stream, flow control credits windows as bodies are consumed, and SETTINGS, PING, RST_STREAM, GOAWAY and the peer's stream limit are honoured. `with_prior_knowledge(true)` speaks HTTP/2 to `http://` URLs without negotiation (h2c), keeping one connection per host as over TLS.
- HTTP/3 client connections: `with_version(Http3)` sends `https` requests over QUIC and keeps `http` requests, requests through a proxy and requests of a client with a dialer on HTTP/1.1. There is no fallback: a QUIC connection that cannot be made fails the request with `Connect`, `Tls` or `Timeout`. The host holds one connection, shared by concurrent requests, next to its HTTP/1.1 and HTTP/2 state; it is replaced once it has ended or the server sent GOAWAY, and `close_idle` and the sweeper close it when idle. The TLS options of the client apply with ALPN `h3` and the URL host as server name, `with_resolver` supplies the addresses, which are tried in turn when one times out or is refused, `with_connect_timeout` becomes `quic.Config.handshake_timeout` and `with_interface` fails with `Connect(Unsupported)`. The handshake runs on its own green thread, so a cancelled token or the request's deadline abandons the wait at once. The deadline of the request that dials never becomes the handshake timeout, because the connection outlives that request: a server whose HANDSHAKE_DONE arrives after the deadline still leaves a connection the next request reuses. The client shares the connection code of the server: it opens the control stream with SETTINGS and the QPACK decoder stream, reads the server's control, QPACK encoder and decoder streams under the same limits (at most 16 at a time, duplicates and unknown critical streams rejected), and decodes field sections through one mutex-guarded `qpack.Decoder` that waits for encoder stream instructions. A push stream is `H3_ID_ERROR` because the client never sends MAX_PUSH_ID, and a MAX_PUSH_ID from the server is `H3_FRAME_UNEXPECTED`. QUIC is configured with no server-initiated bidirectional streams. A request opens a bidirectional stream, writes HEADERS (`:method`, `:scheme`, `:authority`, `:path`, then the lowercased request headers without connection-specific ones, `host` and a `te` other than `trailers`), DATA frames for the body, streamed from a body reader in 16 KiB frames, and FIN. The response is up to 16 interim `1xx` HEADERS that are discarded, then a final HEADERS checked by `valid_h3_response`, DATA frames read as the body stream, and an optional trailing HEADERS checked by `valid_h3_trailers` that fills `Response.trailers`; `Response.version` is `Http3`. A malformed response, which is an invalid section, a `content-length` that differs from the DATA total, DATA on a bodiless response, or too many interim responses, resets and stops the stream with `H3_MESSAGE_ERROR` and fails as `Protocol(Malformed)`; a frame out of order closes the connection with `H3_FRAME_UNEXPECTED`; a section over 64 KiB resets the stream with `H3_EXCESSIVE_LOAD`. One green thread per request watches the deadline and the token and, when either fires, resets and stops that stream with `H3_REQUEST_CANCELLED` and wakes a blocked QPACK wait, leaving the connection open. Opening a stream also races the deadline, because it parks at the peer's stream limit, and so does opening the control and QPACK decoder streams right after the handshake, which parks when the server allows fewer than two unidirectional streams: that setup runs on its own green thread under the deadline, the token and the connect timeout (10 seconds when none is set), and closes the connection when one of them fires, so the host's dial flag is always cleared. A stream the server reset before the response head, a stream the server refused with GOAWAY, a request on a connection that ended, and a request that finds the connection draining count as stale, and a replayable request is sent once more on a new connection, as for HTTP/2. Decompression, redirects, cookies, retries and the 64 MB body limit sit above the transport and apply as for HTTP/2.
- One reaper green thread per client enforces whole-request deadlines by shutting down an expired HTTP/1.1 exchange. HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 waits select on the deadline and reset the stream.
- Redirects switch 301, 302 and 303 to GET without a body, keep the method and body on 307 and 308, resolve relative locations and drop `Authorization` and `Cookie` across origins.
- Responses are decoded from gzip, deflate (zlib or raw), Brotli and Zstandard as they stream, and chunked bodies are read and sent. An optional RFC 6265 cookie jar is shared by the client.
- Retries apply to connection failures and, for idempotent methods, to transport errors and 429, 502, 503 and 504, through `std/retry` with exponential backoff, where `Retry-After` raises the planned wait.
- Servers set `TCP_NODELAY` so separate HEADERS and DATA writes are not held back by delayed acknowledgements.
- `Response.trailers` holds the trailer fields of chunked HTTP/1.1 bodies and of HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 responses.
- Request and response bodies are limited to 64 MB.
- The duplex server and the client's call API (`std/http/call.low`) are the HTTP/2 surface `std/grpc` stands on. A duplex handler runs when the request headers arrive and reads the body through `Request.upload`: `receive_body` returns the next chunk and `none` at the end of the request or once the request token is cancelled, and the window of received data is returned to the peer as chunks are taken, so a handler that stops reading stalls the sender. `Response.finish` supplies the trailers after the last chunk of `stream`. A reset from the peer or a closed connection cancels `Request.token`. Closing the drain channel of a duplex connection sends `GOAWAY`, refuses later streams and lets the streams in progress finish. The connection then reads and discards frames until the peer closes it or one second passes, and only then closes, so the peer reads the `GOAWAY` before the close; a socket on Windows closed with unread input resets the connection, and the peer loses the data it has not read. `open_call` returns a `*Call` once the request headers are sent; `call_send` writes one DATA frame and waits for the peer's window, and `call_receive` returns `Head`, `Data`, `Trailers`, `Reset(code)`, `Lost` or `End` and returns window as events are taken. `call_close(call, complete)` releases the stream, resetting it with `CANCEL` when it is not complete. Cancelling the token of `open_call` resets the stream with `CANCEL` at once, and `call_send` and `call_receive` fail with `TimedOut` from then on, without sending data or returning events that arrived before.

### gRPC

- `std/grpc` speaks the gRPC protocol over HTTP/2 prior knowledge (`host:port` and `http://` targets) and over TLS with ALPN `h2` (`https://` targets). Messages are `std/protobuf` structs; `*_bytes` functions carry raw message bytes. Every function that can fail returns `grpc.Error`, an enum with the one variant `Failed(status)`; `status_of(e)` returns its `Status` and `error(code, message)` builds one.
- `Code` has the 17 gRPC codes with their numbers; `code_name` gives the upper-case name. `Status` holds the code, the message, the `grpc-status-details-bin` bytes and the trailers that came with it. `grpc-message` is percent-encoded on the wire by `encode_message` and `decode_message`, and a message that does not decode is kept as received. `code_of_http` maps an HTTP status without gRPC trailers (400 `Internal`, 401 `Unauthenticated`, 403 `PermissionDenied`, 404 `Unimplemented`, 429, 502, 503 and 504 `Unavailable`, any other `Unknown`) and `status_of_reset` maps an `RST_STREAM` code (`REFUSED_STREAM` `Unavailable`, `CANCEL` `Cancelled`, `ENHANCE_YOUR_CALM` `ResourceExhausted`, `INADEQUATE_SECURITY` `PermissionDenied`, every other code `Internal`).
- A frame is a compressed-flag byte, a four-byte big-endian length and the message. `MessageReader` reassembles frames from arbitrary chunks. `grpc-timeout` is at most eight digits with one of `H M S m u n`; `encode_timeout` picks the finest unit that fits in eight digits, rounding up, and `parse_timeout` rejects anything else. Compression is `Identity`, `Gzip` or `Deflate`, negotiated by `grpc-encoding` and `grpc-accept-encoding`; a request in an encoding the server does not have fails with `Unimplemented` and the server's accepted list in `grpc-accept-encoding`. A message longer than `max_receive` after decompression (4 MiB by default) fails with `ResourceExhausted`, as does a message longer than `max_send` before it is sent.
- `Metadata` is an ordered multi-map of lower-case keys. `add` and `set` take ASCII values, `add_bin` and the `-bin` keys carry bytes, base64 on the wire. Keys outside lower-case letters, digits, `-`, `_` and `.`, keys that start with `grpc-` or `:` or are `content-type`, `te`, `content-length`, `host`, `connection`, `keep-alive`, `transfer-encoding`, `upgrade` or `proxy-connection`, an ASCII value outside printable ASCII, and `add` with a `-bin` key or `add_bin` with another key fail with a `MetadataError`. Both sides send metadata as headers first and trailers last.
- A `Channel` made by `dial` connects on the first call, shares one HTTP/2 connection per server between all calls and green threads, queues calls beyond the server's stream limit and reconnects when the connection ends. `unary` and `unary_bytes` return a `Reply`; `server_streaming`, `client_streaming` and `bidi_streaming` return a `Stream` read with `receive`, written with `send` and `close_send`, finished with `finish` and released with `close_stream`. `send` blocks while the stream's window is closed. `CallOptions.timeout` is the whole call's deadline sent as `grpc-timeout`, and a call past it fails with `DeadlineExceeded` and resets the stream with `CANCEL`; `CallOptions.token` and `cancel_stream` cancel the call with `Cancelled` and reset the stream at once; a `receive` after the cancel fails with `Cancelled` without returning messages that arrived before, unless the server had already ended the call. A unary call that fails with `Unavailable` before any response header arrived is retried under `ChannelOptions.retry`, and `retry: false` turns that off; no other failure is retried.
- A `Server` is a table from `/package.Service/Method` to a handler. `handle_unary`, `handle_server_streaming`, `handle_client_streaming` and `handle_bidi_streaming` register one with message types, `handle_bytes` one over raw bytes. A handler receives a `*Context` with the method, the request metadata, `peer`, `token`, `response_headers` and `response_trailers`, and reads and writes through `read` and `write`; `flush_headers` sends the headers early. A response that fails before any message is a Trailers-Only response. A handler that returns an error ends the call with its status; `Cancelled` from a call whose deadline passed becomes `DeadlineExceeded`. A method that is not registered fails with `Unimplemented`, an HTTP/1 request with `505`. `remaining(ctx)` is what is left of the client's deadline and `outgoing(ctx)` is the `CallOptions` for a call made on behalf of the handler, with that deadline and `ctx.token`, so a cancelled or expired call stops its downstream calls. `serve`, `serve_on`, `serve_tls` and `serve_tls_on` accept connections until `shutdown(s, grace)` closes the listeners, sends `GOAWAY` and waits for the calls to end; after `grace` milliseconds the remaining calls are cancelled with `Unavailable`. Methods are registered before serving starts.
- `health()` is the state of `grpc.health.v1.Health`, `register_health` serves it, `set_health` and `set_all_not_serving` change it, and `check_health` and `watch_health` are the client calls. `Check` of a service never set fails with `NotFound`; `Watch` sends the current state, `ServiceUnknown` for a service never set, and each change after it.
- There is no server reflection, no interceptor chain, no load balancing across addresses, no service config, no hedging, and no conversion of a handler panic into `Internal`.

### Processes

- `process.start` builds the path, arguments, environment and pipes before `fork`, so the child runs only straight-line syscalls: `setpgid` for a new group, `dup2` onto descriptors 0 to 2, `chdir` and `execve`. Every descriptor the standard library opens is close-on-exec, so the child inherits only its three streams. A child's pipes are streams over guarded descriptors, so closing one twice or using it after it closed fails with `NotConnected` and never reaches a reused descriptor.
- A failed `chdir` or `execve` writes its errno to a close-on-exec pipe, which the parent reads before returning. The read sees end of file once `execve` succeeds; otherwise the parent reaps the child and fails with that errno.
- The parent's pipe ends are non-blocking and wait through the scheduler, as sockets do; the child's ends stay blocking. On Windows the parent's ends are overlapped pipes, as `## Compiler` describes. `wait` waits for the process's pidfd to become readable, then reaps it with `waitid` and remembers the result. `kill` signals through the pidfd, or the whole group when the command asked for one.
- `signal.notify` installs a handler with `rt_sigaction`, or on Windows registers a console control handler with `SetConsoleCtrlHandler`. The handler is a short block of machine code in a mapped page: it writes the signal number to a non-blocking pipe and returns through `rt_sigreturn`. The Windows handler writes through an overlapped `WriteFile` whose address `handler_code` writes into the code, maps Ctrl+C to `Interrupt`, Ctrl+Break to `Quit`, a closed console and logoff to `Hangup` and shutdown to `Terminate`, and runs on a thread the system starts. It returns `1` only for the events of the signals some `notify` asked for and `0` for the rest, so an event no watch asked for reaches the default handler and ends the process, as Ctrl+Break does in a program that watches only `Interrupt`. A `Watch` forwards only the signals it asked for. On Windows `notify` of any other signal fails with `Unsupported`. The runtime gives every OS thread an alternate signal stack, so the handler runs on whichever thread receives the signal. A forwarding green thread reads the pipe with `wait_readable` and sends to the `signals` channel, which holds 16 and drops repeats while full. Another `notify` for the same signal replaces the handler. Dropping a `Watch` restores the saved dispositions and unmaps the page, except that a handler another `notify` has replaced is silenced and its page stays mapped. `raise` signals the calling thread, so its handler has run when `raise` returns; `send` is process-directed and unordered against other threads.
- `watch.next` reads every event an inotify read returns and queues them. With `recursive` set, new and moved-in directories are watched before the batch that reports them is returned. `stop` wakes a parked `next` by adding and removing a watch on `/`, which makes the descriptor readable. The kernel merges identical consecutive events it has not yet delivered.

### Resilience

- `retry.delay` is the capped exponential backoff and `jittered` applies the chosen jitter to it. `reserve` plans the wait and stops at the attempt limit, the deadline or a cancelled token; `commit` calls the observer, waits through the clock's `sleep`, which a cancelled token ends early, and starts the next attempt; `next` is `reserve` then `commit`.
- A `Bucket` keeps its balance as an integer in units of one token over `per` nanoseconds, so refills are exact and a wait is the ceiling of the deficit over the rate. `reserve` consumes and returns the wait. `wait` sleeps that long and refunds if cancelled.
- `Group.do` runs the function outside its lock, removes the key and stores the result before waking the waiters. It matches the key by call identity, so a `forget` followed by a new call is not disturbed.

### Numerics

- `std/math/stats`, `std/math/complex`, `std/math/matrix` and `std/math/fft` are written in Low and use no operating system service. Only `complex` and `fft` reach libm, through `std/math`, for `hypot`, `atan2`, `log`, `cos` and `sin`, so a program that uses only `stats` or `matrix` stays static. All four build for `browser/wasm32`.
- `stats` rejects a `nan` or infinite input, and a `nan` or infinite result such as an overflowing sum, with `NotFinite`, so a statistic is never a silent `nan`. `variance` and `covariance` sum deviations from the mean with compensation and subtract the squared sum of the deviations, so a large common offset does not cost digits. `quantile` sorts a copy and takes position `q * (n - 1)`; `quantiles` sorts once for several. `histogram` takes its range from the data, widened by 0.5 on each side when every value is equal. `histogram_edges` ignores values outside the edges and finds the bin by binary search.
- `complex.div` scales by the larger part of the divisor. A zero divisor divides each part of the dividend by zero. `csqrt` returns the root with a non-negative real part, and the sign of the imaginary part of a negative real argument follows the sign bit of the imaginary part, so the branch cut agrees with C99 `csqrt`.
- `matrix` panics with a `matrix:` message when shapes do not fit, as `std/slices` panics on misuse; `fft.inverse_real` does the same with an `fft:` message. `lu` fails with `Singular` only when the best pivot is exactly zero, which is the criterion of LAPACK, so a matrix that is singular up to rounding factorises. `determinant` of a singular matrix is `0`. `least_squares` fails with `Singular` when a diagonal entry of `R` is at most `max(rows, cols) * epsilon` times the largest one, since rank deficiency from rounding shows as a small entry, not a zero.
- `qr` takes `alpha = -sign(a[k][k]) * norm` for each reflection and leaves a column that is already zero below the diagonal alone, which gives the signs of LAPACK and `numpy.linalg.qr`.
- `fft` computes twiddle factors with `cos` and `sin`, not by recurrence. Bluestein's algorithm reduces `k * k` modulo `2 * n` before forming the chirp angle and convolves with a power-of-two transform of length at least `2 * n - 1`. `inverse` is the conjugate of the forward transform of the conjugate, divided by `n`.
- The fixtures `testdata/run/std_math_stats`, `std_math_complex`, `std_math_matrix` and `std_math_fft` print every value rounded to six digits and normalise `-0.000000`, so the last bits of a libm do not change the output; their `.out` files come from `numpy`, `cmath`, `math.fsum` and exact `fractions` arithmetic.

### Text

- `diff.unified` merges hunks whose gap is at most twice the context, omits a count of 1 in a hunk header and writes `\ No newline at end of file`, as GNU `diff -u` does. The edit script is minimal.
- `number.format_bytes` shows one decimal below 10 and rounds half to even on exact integers. `parse_bytes` reads `k` and `kb` as 1000 and `ki` and `kib` as 1024, and fails with `OutOfRange` on overflow or `TooPrecise` beyond 18 fraction digits. `si` rounds on the exact decimal expansion of the `f64`.
- `naming.words` splits at case, acronym and digit boundaries and keeps combining marks attached to their letter, so `HTTPServerID2` is `http server id 2`.

### Time

- Each concept is its own type: `Timestamp` is a point on the UTC timeline; `Date`, `TimeOfDay` and `DateTime` are civil values with no zone; `Zoned` is a timestamp seen through a zone; `Duration` is exact elapsed time; `Span` is a calendar amount. Types never convert silently.
- Fields are private and constructors validate, so a `Date` cannot hold February 30. Months are 1 to 12, and `Month` and `Weekday` are enums. Nothing rolls over: `date.of(2026, 2, 30)` fails with `Invalid`, and the clamping forms are named (`clamped`, `plus_months`).
- Calendar arithmetic and elapsed arithmetic are separate. A `Duration` moves a `Timestamp` or `Zoned` by elapsed time. A `Span` moves a `Date`, `DateTime` or `Zoned` by calendar units: a month after January 31 is the last day of February, and a day after a spring-forward midnight is the next civil day although it is 23 hours long. `until` and `since` take a `Diff`, add whole years, then months, to the start without passing the end, then count the rest.
- Only `zone.local` reads the host zone. A civil time in a daylight saving gap or overlap needs a `Disambiguation`. In an overlap, `Compatible` and `Earlier` pick the first occurrence and `Later` the second. In a gap, `Compatible` and `Later` move forward by the gap and `Earlier` back. `Reject` fails with `Skipped` or `Ambiguous`. `zoned.start_of_day` is the first instant of the civil day, correct where midnight does not exist.
- Zones come from TZif versions 1 to 4, using the 64-bit body from version 2, with the footer's POSIX TZ rule applying after the last transition. Before the first transition the first standard-time type applies. The rules cover `Jn`, zero-based `n` and `Mm.w.d` days and transition times from -167 to 167 hours, so negative daylight saving (Dublin, Casablanca), half-hour shifts (Lord Howe) and skipped days (Apia) work.
- Parsing is strict ISO 8601, RFC 3339 and RFC 9557: `T`, `t` or a space separates date and time, a fraction has 1 to 9 digits, `:60` reads as `:59`, and a civil type rejects an offset. A written offset that disagrees with a bracketed zone is `OffsetConflict` unless an `OffsetPolicy` says which wins. Every parse error is `Syntax(at)` with the byte offset of the first bad character, or a value error naming the field.
- The supported range is 0001-01-01 to 9999-12-31 for a `Timestamp` and years -9999 to 9999 for a `Date`. Leap seconds are not modelled. Plain arithmetic panics on overflow; the `try_` forms return an optional.
- Month and weekday names are English. `pattern.Names` and `humanize.Words` carry other languages, and `datefmt.names`, `pattern.compile_in` and `humanize.ago_in` fill them from CLDR for a `locale.Locale`.

### Browser automation

- `cdp.launch` finds `chromium`, `chromium-browser`, `google-chrome-stable` or `google-chrome` on `PATH` unless given an executable, and creates a profile with `file.temp_dir`. It starts the browser headless in its own process group with `--remote-debugging-port=0`, standard input and output discarded and standard error piped. A green thread reads standard error until the `DevTools listening on` line, which gives the WebSocket address, and keeps draining it so the browser never blocks on a full pipe. Another waits with `process.wait_exit_on` for the browser to end, so a browser that dies before the line fails the launch at once, as `Launch(Aborted)`, even while a helper it started, such as the crash reporter, keeps standard error open. Standard error closing with the browser still running is `Launch(Eof)` and the startup deadline `Launch(TimedOut)`. `shutdown` closes the WebSocket, kills the process group, waits for that thread, reaps the browser and removes the profile.
- Commands carry an id and, for a session, its `sessionId`. A pump green thread routes replies to the waiting call and sends every other message to each subscription. A message that is not JSON fails every waiting call with `Protocol`. Sends are serialised and a call waits at most 30 seconds. Once the socket closes, every waiting call fails with `Closed` and the subscription channels close.
- `open_page` creates a browser context that is disposed on detach, denies downloads in it, and turns on auto-attach with `waitForDebuggerOnStart` and flat sessions, so every page, frame and worker target pauses on creation. Each page subscribes to the events and acts only on those of its own context and the sessions attached in it. The first page in the context is the opened page; any other page in it, such as a popup, is closed before it runs. Every other target in the context gets `Fetch.enable` for all URLs, `Network.enable`, auto-attach for its own children and, for pages, `Network.setBypassServiceWorker`, and only then resumes, so no request escapes interception; a target that cannot be prepared is closed and, for the opened page, fails `open_page`. `close_page` ends the subscription and disposes the context. Each paused request is passed to `Handlers.request` on its own green thread and continued or failed with its verdict. Responses and failed loads are reported with their URL.
- Cancellation is the caller's: `let h = b.handle` and `cancel.on_cancel(t, fn() { cdp.shutdown_on(h) })` end everything the browser was doing.

### TLS

- TLS 1.3 (RFC 8446) and TLS 1.2 (RFC 5246), client and server. `Config.min_version` and `Config.max_version`, written with `tls12` and `tls13`, bound the versions and default to 1.2 through 1.3. A client offers its range through `supported_versions`, and a server answers with the highest version both support. `Session.handle.version` holds the result.
- Downgrade protection: a server that supports 1.3 ends a 1.2 ServerHello random with the RFC 8446 downgrade sentinel; a client that offered 1.3 aborts with `illegal_parameter` when it sees either sentinel; a server refuses `TLS_FALLBACK_SCSV` below its highest version with `inappropriate_fallback`.
- `Config.cipher_suites` lists suites in preference order, and a server takes the first of its own list that the client offers. `Session.handle.cipher_suite` holds the IANA code and `Session.handle.suite` the AEAD and hash.
- TLS 1.3 has TLS_AES_128_GCM_SHA256, TLS_CHACHA20_POLY1305_SHA256 and TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384. TLS 1.2 has ECDHE with ECDSA or RSA authentication and AES-128-GCM with SHA-256, ChaCha20-Poly1305 with SHA-256 or AES-256-GCM with SHA-384, AEAD only: GCM records carry the 64-bit sequence number as their explicit nonce, and ChaCha20-Poly1305 records use the RFC 7905 nonce.
- TLS 1.3 key exchange offers the hybrid post-quantum group X25519MLKEM768 first, then X25519, P-256 and P-384. A client sends key shares for X25519MLKEM768 and X25519. A server picks the first group of its own order that the client supports, answering with a HelloRetryRequest when the client sent no share for it, so a client that supports X25519MLKEM768 always gets it. TLS 1.2 uses the first of X25519, P-256 and P-384 in the server's order that the client lists, and a server with an ECDSA certificate also requires the client to list the certificate's curve (RFC 8422).
- Servers sign with ECDSA P-256 or P-384, RSA-PSS, RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 in TLS 1.2, or Ed25519, with keys loaded from PEM (`x509.pem_certificates`, `x509.pem_private_key`). `std/crypto/x509` owns the one table of signature scheme codes: the client offer, the codes a key signs with and the key exchange authentication of a code all come from it. TLS 1.2 ECDSA signature schemes name only the hash, so either curve signs with SHA-256 or SHA-384 (`x509.SignatureRules.Tls12`); in TLS 1.3 a code names its curve. Clients verify the chain against `Config.roots`, or the system roots when that is empty, and check the host name against the certificate's subject alternative names. They verify CertificateVerify or ServerKeyExchange with any offered scheme the certificate allows and, in TLS 1.2, the suite's authentication.
- TLS 1.2 requires the extended master secret (RFC 7627) on both sides. A client requires the secure renegotiation indication (RFC 5746), a server echoes it when the client sends it, and both refuse renegotiation with a `no_renegotiation` warning.
- ALPN, SNI, KeyUpdate in TLS 1.3 and `close_notify` are supported. Session tickets are ignored, so there is no resumption or 0-RTT. Servers do not request client certificates; a client answers a CertificateRequest with an empty Certificate.
- Records hold at most 2^14 bytes of plaintext, and a peer's ciphertext may add 256 bytes in TLS 1.3 or 2048 in TLS 1.2. A record or handshake error sends its fatal alert, such as `bad_record_mac`, `record_overflow`, `unexpected_message` or `decode_error`, and leaves the session unusable.
- A session's read and write sides are independent, with a write lock, so different green threads can read and write it.
- QUIC mode (`quic_client`, `quic_server`) runs the TLS 1.3 handshake without records: handshake messages go to the transport per level, and the traffic secrets are handed over as the schedule derives them. It offers TLS 1.3 only, carries the transport parameters in extension 0x39, requires ALPN and an empty `legacy_session_id`, rejects handshake data at the wrong level and bytes left over when a key changes, and caps a handshake message at 64 KiB.
- A client with `Config.revocation.mode` other than `Off` sends the `status_request` extension (RFC 6066 section 8, `ocsp` with empty responder and request extensions) and checks what the server staples as Certificate status describes. A TLS 1.2 server answers with an empty `status_request` in its ServerHello and a `CertificateStatus` message after its Certificate; a TLS 1.3 server puts the response in the `status_request` extension of the certificate entry it belongs to. A server staples only when the client asked, from `Config.staples`: each entry in TLS 1.3, the first entry alone in TLS 1.2. A revoked certificate sends `certificate_revoked`, and any other failure of the chain, including a status that cannot be established, `bad_certificate`. `status_request_v2` is not offered or accepted.
- A server with `Config.credentials` calls its `choose` once per connection with the server name and ALPN protocols of the first ClientHello and presents the `Credentials` it returns instead of `Config.certificates`, `Config.key` and `Config.staples`. A selected `Credentials.protocol` the client did not offer fails the handshake after a `no_application_protocol` alert, and credentials without a certificate or a key refuse it with `unrecognized_name`. `std/acme` uses this hook to serve a certificate that renews and to answer TLS-ALPN-01.

### Certificate status

- `x509.verify_chain` takes a `Revocation` with a `mode`, a list of stapled `staples` and an optional `fetcher`. `Off`, the default, checks nothing and does not require must-staple certificates to be stapled. `SoftFail` and `HardFail` check every certificate of the path below the trust anchor against the next certificate of the path, skipping one with the `id-pkix-ocsp-nocheck` extension. The anchor is never checked.
- A certificate's status comes from the first source that gives an answer: the stapled responses, then, with a `fetcher`, an OCSP request to each of its `ocsp_urls`, then each of its `crl_urls`. A response answers for the certificate its `CertID` names, whatever its place in the list. `Revoked` fails the check in every mode, with `Refused`. A status that cannot be established, because no source answered, the response is `Unknown`, unusable, unsigned by an acceptable responder or out of date, or the fetch failed, passes under `SoftFail` and fails with `Invalid` under `HardFail`. An `Unknown` OCSP answer falls through to the next source.
- A certificate with the TLS feature extension `status_request` (must-staple, RFC 7633) needs a stapled `Good` response whenever the mode is not `Off`; a response from the fetcher does not satisfy it, and without one the check fails with `Invalid` in both modes.
- Times are checked against the `now` the caller passes. A list or response counts as issued up to `clock_skew` (300 seconds) after `now` and as current up to `clock_skew` after its `nextUpdate`. A CRL and a single OCSP response without `nextUpdate` are not accepted.
- `ocsp_request` writes an unsigned request with a SHA-1 or SHA-256 `CertID` and, for a nonce of 1 to 32 bytes, the RFC 8954 nonce extension. A fetched request carries a random 16 byte nonce; `ocsp_status` rejects a response whose nonce differs and accepts one with none, as responders that serve precomputed responses do, and ignores the nonce of a stapled response.
- `ocsp_status` accepts a response signed by the issuer of the certificate, named by name or by key hash, or by a certificate carried in the response that the issuer issued, that is valid at `now`, has no unhandled critical extension and has `id-kp-OCSPSigning`. It recomputes the `CertID` with the hash the response names (SHA-1, SHA-256, SHA-384 or SHA-512) and verifies the signature with the algorithms of `check_signature`.
- `parse_crl` reads v1 and v2 lists as strict DER, including every revoked entry, and keeps the entries encoded: `crl_lookup` scans them for one serial number and builds a value only for the match. `verify_crl` needs the list's issuer name to equal the issuer certificate's subject, `cRLSign` in its key usage when it has one, the authority key identifier of the list to equal the subject key identifier of the issuer when both exist, and a valid signature. `crl_status` also needs the list to cover the certificate: an issuing distribution point may be limited to user or CA certificates, or name a distribution point that must appear in the certificate's `crl_urls`. A list that is indirect, limited to some reasons or to attribute certificates, a delta list, or that has another critical extension or critical entry extension, fails to parse with `Unsupported`, since it would be incomplete for the certificates it is used for.
- Fetching is a pair of function values, `Fetcher.ocsp` and `Fetcher.crl`, so tests and callers with their own transport substitute them. `std/crypto/x509/fetch` implements them over `std/http`. Nothing caches a response or a list; a caller that wants a cache wraps the functions.

### ACME

- `acme.connect` fetches the directory (an `https` URL) of RFC 8555 and returns a `Client` that any number of green threads may share. Every signed request is a flattened JWS (`jose.sign_flattened`) with `url` and `nonce` in its protected header, `kid` set to the account URL, or an embedded `jwk` for `new_account` and `revoke_with_key`; a request that reads a resource is POST-as-GET with an empty payload. The client keeps the `Replay-Nonce` values the server sends in a pool and fetches one from `newNonce` when the pool is empty. A `badNonce` answer is repeated with a fresh nonce up to `Options.nonce_retries` times, and `rejected_nonces` counts those answers. An account key signs with ES256 (P-256), ES384 (P-384), EdDSA (Ed25519) or RS256 (RSA); `x509.generate_private_key` makes the first three and `x509.pem_private_key` loads an RSA key.
- An error answer is a `Problem`: its `kind` is the suffix of the `urn:ietf:params:acme:error:` type (`Other` for any other type or a body that is not a problem document), with `type_uri`, `detail`, `status`, `instance` and the `Subproblem` list of a `compound` answer. `Error` separates `Http`, `Server`, `Protocol` (an answer the protocol does not allow), `Crypto`, `Rejected` (an authorization became `invalid`), `OrderFailed`, `Timeout`, `Cancelled`, `Unsupported`, `Solver` and `Store`; `describe` writes any of them as one line.
- Accounts: `new_account` creates an account or returns the one the server holds for the key, with contacts, terms-of-service agreement and an external account binding (`Binding`, an HMAC SHA-256 key of at least 32 bytes). `find_account` never creates one and fails with kind `AccountDoesNotExist`. `change_key` signs the rollover with both keys and `deactivate_account` ends the account.
- Orders: `new_order` takes `Identifier`s from `identifier(name)`, which makes an `ip` identifier (RFC 8738) of an IP address in its canonical text and a `dns` identifier of anything else, with an optional `profile` and the `replaces` identifier of RFC 9773. `wait_authorization` and `wait_order` poll on `Options.clock`, waiting for `Retry-After` (a number of seconds or an HTTP date) or `Options.poll_interval`, and fail with `Timeout` after `Options.poll_timeout` and with `Cancelled` when `Options.token` is cancelled. `download_chain` reads the PEM chain and lists the `alternate` links of the response in `Chain.alternates`.
- `obtain` runs a whole order with `Solvers`: for each authorization that is not `valid` it takes the first of `Solvers.methods` that the server offers and a solver exists for, puts the response in place, answers the challenge, waits for the authorization and takes the response away again, also when it fails. A wildcard name needs `dns-01`. `csr_for` writes the PKCS#10 request with the first DNS name as its subject when it is at most 64 bytes and every name in the subject alternative name. `revoke` signs with the account and `revoke_with_key` with the certificate's own key.
- Solvers: `http01_handler` answers `GET` and `HEAD` of `/.well-known/acme-challenge/<token>` from a `Challenges` table with the key authorization (404 for an unknown token) and passes every other request on; `tls_alpn_source` presents the certificate `put_certificate` holds for the server name to a ClientHello that offers `acme-tls/1` and passes every other ClientHello to the source it wraps. `tls_alpn_credentials` writes that certificate: valid from an hour before `now` for 7 days, with the critical `id-pe-acmeIdentifier` extension holding the SHA-256 digest of the key authorization. For an IP identifier the SNI name is the `in-addr.arpa` or `ip6.arpa` name of the address. `DnsRecords` is a pair of functions that publish and remove the TXT record `dns01_name` and `dns01_value` compute; the module never contacts a DNS server.
- `renewal_id` builds the certificate identifier of RFC 9773 from the authority key identifier and the serial number (`Certificate.authority_key_id`, `Certificate.serial`), and `renewal_info` returns the suggested `RenewalWindow` with the `Retry-After` of the answer.
- `manager` keeps one certificate per entry of `ManagerConfig.names` obtained and renewed. It reads `account.pem` from the `Store`, finds the account for that key or, when the server does not know it, creates one with the same key, and writes a new key there. A certificate saved as `<names joined with +>.pem` (with `*` and `:` written `_`) is used again when its key matches its leaf and its names cover the entry; anything else counts as absent. Each issuance is retried under `ManagerConfig.backoff` with `std/retry`, only the first attempt of a renewal carries `replaces`, and a certificate is saved and served only after its leaf is checked against the key and the names. A run that gives up leaves the certificate alone for `failure_pause`; `renew_now` clears that pause.
- A renewal is due at `renew_fraction` of the lifetime, or, when the directory has `renewalInfo` and `use_ari` is set, at a random time inside the window the server suggests. The window is asked for again after its `Retry-After` or `check_interval`; a failed request keeps the earlier window, and a window that ends before the certificate's validity begins is ignored. `run_once` makes one pass and returns the wait until the next; `start` runs passes on a green thread that `stop` or the cancelled `ManagerConfig.token` ends. Every issuance reports an `Event` (`Obtained` with the reason `initial`, `scheduled`, `suggested` or `requested`, or `Failed`) to `ManagerConfig.observer`.
- `source` returns the `tls.CredentialSource` that presents the current certificate of the entry whose name covers the SNI name (exact match without regard to case, or `*.` for one label); an empty name gets the first certificate held, and an unknown name or nothing held yet refuses the handshake. It also answers TLS-ALPN-01 when `Solvers.challenges` is set. A renewed certificate reaches connections that start after the swap.
- `memory_store` keeps the bytes in a map. `directory_store` creates its directory with mode `0o700`, writes each file to a temporary file with mode `0o600` in that directory, flushes it and moves it onto the name, and refuses a name that is empty, starts with `.` or contains a path separator with `Invalid`.
- Not supported: pre-authorization, listing the orders of an account and generating RSA keys.
### SSH
- The transport follows RFC 4253 with the binary packet protocol, strict key exchange (`kex-strict-c-v00@openssh.com`, `kex-strict-s-v00@openssh.com`) and `ext-info` (RFC 8308). Key exchanges are `mlkem768x25519-sha256`, `curve25519-sha256` (and its `@libssh.org` name), `ecdh-sha2-nistp256`, `ecdh-sha2-nistp384`, `diffie-hellman-group16-sha512` and `diffie-hellman-group14-sha256`. Host keys are `ssh-ed25519`, `ecdsa-sha2-nistp256`, `ecdsa-sha2-nistp384`, `rsa-sha2-256` and `rsa-sha2-512`; `ssh-rsa` with SHA-1 is not offered. Ciphers are `chacha20-poly1305@openssh.com`, `aes256-gcm@openssh.com`, `aes128-gcm@openssh.com`, `aes256-ctr` and `aes128-ctr`, the CTR ciphers with `hmac-sha2-512-etm@openssh.com` or `hmac-sha2-256-etm@openssh.com`. Compression is `none`. `Algorithms` restricts each list; `default_kex`, `default_host_keys`, `default_ciphers` and `default_macs` return the defaults in preference order.
- A packet carries at most 262144 bytes, and a larger length fails with `TooLarge` before anything is read. MACs and tags are compared in constant time. Sequence numbers run for the life of the connection. Under strict key exchange a message other than key exchange messages before `NEWKEYS`, or a first packet that is not `KEXINIT`, fails with `StrictKex`, and sequence numbers reset at each `NEWKEYS`.
- A key exchange starts after `rekey_bytes` (default 1 GiB) or `rekey_seconds` (default one hour) under one set of keys, or when the peer starts one. `key_exchanges` counts them. The `diffie-hellman` groups use `rsa.mod_pow`, so the private exponent never selects a branch or a table index.
- A client verifies the server's host key through `verify_host_key`, which `accept_any_host_key`, `key.known_hosts_verifier` and `key.trust_on_first_use` make; the signature over the exchange hash is checked after it. A server signs with the host key whose algorithm the client offers.
- User authentication (RFC 4252, RFC 4256) tries the `AuthMethod`s in order: `NoAuth`, `Password`, `PublicKey` (a query, then a signature over the session identifier), where a `Signer` may be a certified key or come from `std/ssh/agent`, and `KeyboardInteractive`. The server calls `ServerAuth` callbacks, can require several methods in sequence (`required`, answered with partial success), shows a banner, and ends the connection with `Disconnected` after `max_attempts` failures (default 6). Queries and `none` requests do not count as failures. A client that exhausts its methods fails with `AuthFailed`.
- `ClientConfig.handshake_timeout` (milliseconds, 0 or less for none) bounds the TCP connect of `dial` and the version exchange, key exchange and authentication of `dial` and `client` together; `dial` gives the handshake what the connect left. It is a timer green thread that closes the stream when it fires and stops once authentication succeeds, so later channel IO is not bound by it. `dial_through` bounds only the handshake. A failure of the transport in a stage, a timeout, an end of stream or a stream error, is `Handshake(phase, cause)` with `Phase` `Connect`, `VersionExchange`, `KeyExchange` or `Authentication`, and `cause` `TimedOut` for the timeout. The failures the protocol itself reports keep their variants, which name their stage: `BadVersion` for the version exchange, `NoCommonAlgorithm`, `KeyExchange`, `BadSignature` and `StrictKex` for the key exchange, `HostKeyRejected` for the host key and `AuthFailed` for authentication.
- Channels are multiplexed with a 2 MiB window per channel, adjusted after half is consumed. A write waits while the peer's window is empty, so a slow reader stops the writer and the sender never overruns it. At most 1024 channels are open. `close_write_on` sends EOF, and a channel closes once both sides have sent `CLOSE`. Extended data type 1 is stderr. A server handler called for each `Incoming` calls `accept` or `reject`; by default every channel is refused.
- A session client reads `wait_exit_on` for the `exit-status` or `exit-signal` the server sent. `exec` collects stdout, stderr and the exit status.
- Forwarding: `listen_local` binds a local port and opens a `direct-tcpip` channel for each connection; `listen_remote` sends `tcpip-forward` and `accept_forward` returns each `forwarded-tcpip` channel with the origin address; `direct_streamlocal` reaches a Unix socket. `serve_direct_tcpip` and `forward_global` give a server the matching handlers.
- `key.parse_private_key` reads unencrypted and `aes256-ctr` plus bcrypt encrypted `openssh-key-v1` keys, with the check integers verified before the keys are returned, and PEM keys. A wrong passphrase is `WrongPassphrase`. A certificate checks the signature of its authority over the certificate body, its validity window, its type and the principal that logs in.
- SFTP version 3 requests are pipelined: reads run ahead by 8 requests and writes are sent without waiting for each reply, with a failure reported by a later write, `flush` or `close`. The sizes come from `limits@openssh.com` when the server sends it, capped at 65536. The server resolves every path from its root: `..` stops at the root, symbolic links are expanded in the process and an absolute link target is taken relative to the root, and more than 40 links fail. On Windows a backslash separates components in client paths and link targets, a drive prefix of a link target is dropped before the target is taken relative to the root, and a component with a character Windows rejects (`<>:"|?*`, control characters), a trailing dot or space, or a device name (`CON`, `NUL`, `COM1` and the others) is `NotFound`. A failing `lstat` other than `NotFound` ends the request. The root itself cannot be removed, renamed or replaced. Each operation resolves its path again, so a directory replaced by a link while a request runs is not protected against by the server.
- `std/ssh/agent` keeps one `io.Stream` per `Session`, made by `dial` in `agent_linux.low` (a Unix socket through `std/net`) or `agent_windows.low`, which opens a path that starts with `\\.\pipe\` through `sys.open_pipe` and `sys.stream_on`, so reads and writes park the green thread, and sends any other path to the Unix socket as on Linux. `connect_env` uses `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` and, when it is unset or empty, the OpenSSH agent service pipe `\\.\pipe\openssh-ssh-agent` on Windows and `NoAgent` elsewhere. `connect_pageant` finds the window of class `Pageant`, and each request creates a file mapping named `PageantRequest` and sixteen hex digits, copies the framed request into it, sends `WM_COPYDATA` with `dwData` `0x804e50ba` and the mapping's name through `SendMessageTimeoutW` (a minute, abort if hung) and reads the framed reply back from the mapping, which has the default security descriptor and holds at most 262144 bytes. `agent/pageant.low` wraps that exchange in an `io.Stream`: a write collects one frame and calls the exchange, and reads return the reply. `connect_any` tries `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` when it is set, then on Windows the pipe and Pageant, and fails with the first failure. `agent/pageant_test.low` runs the framing against a fake exchange on Linux, and Pageant itself is not exercised by any test. `testdata/windows/ssh_agent_pipe.low` serves a fake agent on a named pipe from a child process and `std/ssh/agent/agent_test_windows.low` checks the default pipe, a missing pipe and a missing Pageant window.
- The OpenSSH interoperation tests and the permission-bit tests of `std/ssh/sftp` run on Linux only (`interop_test_linux.low`, `sftp_test_linux.low`); `sftp_test_windows.low` holds the Windows path and link containment tests.
- Interoperation is checked against OpenSSH 10.6p1: the `ssh`, `sftp` and `scp` clients run against the server and the client runs against an unprivileged `sshd`, over every cipher, MAC, key exchange and host key above.

### QUIC

- An endpoint is one UDP socket and one reader green thread. A datagram over 1500 bytes is dropped, and the rest are routed by destination connection ID, at most 4096 routes. Each connection is a loop green thread that owns all of its state; the reader, the TLS handshake green thread and user calls reach it through channels, and every send to it also waits on a channel that closes when the loop exits, so nothing blocks on a connection that has ended.
- A server answers an unsupported version with Version Negotiation only for datagrams of at least 1200 bytes. It holds at most 256 connections whose handshake is incomplete, at most 8 of them per client IP address; an Initial beyond either limit is dropped. From 128 of them, or always when `Config.retry` is set, a new Initial gets a Retry whose token is an HMAC-SHA256 over the client address, an expiry 10 seconds ahead, the original connection ID and the source connection ID of the Retry, compared in constant time. The token is accepted only on an Initial whose destination connection ID is that source connection ID, so one token starts one connection: later Initials with that ID are routed to it. Before the client address is validated the server sends at most three times the bytes received. A client accepts one Retry and ignores Retry and Version Negotiation once it has processed another packet. A short-header datagram of at least 42 bytes for no connection gets a smaller stateless reset whose token is an HMAC of the connection ID.
- The handshake runs `tls.quic_client` or `tls.quic_server` on its own green thread. Received CRYPTO data is reassembled to at most 64 KiB in at most 64 pieces per level, beyond which the connection fails with `CRYPTO_BUFFER_EXCEEDED`, and at most 16 packets wait for keys not yet installed. CRYPTO data that arrives after the handshake, such as session tickets, is discarded. Initial keys are discarded when Handshake packets flow and Handshake keys when the handshake is confirmed. The handshake timeout runs until the handshake is confirmed, which for a client is the arrival of HANDSHAKE_DONE, so a client whose server abandoned the handshake after the client finished its side ends with `TimedOut` within that timeout. Each side checks the peer's transport parameters: the connection IDs they name, including a Retry's, must match the ones seen on the wire, and a client must not send the parameters only a server sends.
- Packets carry ACKs for at most 32 received ranges, sent within 5 ms or at once after two ack-eliciting packets. Loss recovery is `std/quic/recovery`. The sender skips a packet number every 128 to 384 packets, and an ACK of a skipped or unsent number is `PROTOCOL_VIOLATION`; only the first 256 ranges of an ACK frame are processed. Lost frames are sent again from what the packet carried, so stream data, flow control updates and control frames are rebuilt rather than copied.
- 1-RTT keys are updated after 2^23 packets or when `update_keys` asks, and a peer's key update is followed. Every change of the sending keys sends a PING, so the acknowledgement that allows the next update arrives even when there is no data to send. A connection closes with `AEAD_LIMIT_REACHED` once more packets fail authentication than the integrity limit of its AEAD: 2^52, or 2^36 for ChaCha20-Poly1305.
- Connection IDs: the endpoint issues 8-byte IDs and stores at most 2 of the peer's. RETIRE_CONNECTION_ID frames owed to the peer are capped at 8, beyond which the connection fails with `CONNECTION_ID_LIMIT_ERROR`. Only the latest PATH_CHALLENGE is answered. Connection migration is not supported.
- Streams: flow control applies per stream and per connection in both directions, and the receive windows are credited as the application reads. A stream reassembles into one receive buffer that spans the read position to the highest offset received and grows by doubling, never past the flow control window, with each byte copied once into place; bytes that were already received are not overwritten by a retransmission. The buffer is described by a sorted list of received ranges, at most 256, beyond which the connection fails with `PROTOCOL_VIOLATION`, and data past the flow control limit, a changed final size and an offset plus length over 2^62-1 fail the connection with the matching error. A peer opens at most the advertised number of streams of each type, at most 4096 are tracked, and the allowance of a peer-opened stream returns (MAX_STREAMS) only when the stream is complete and the application has accepted it, so streams the application never accepts stay bounded by the advertised number. `write` parks while the stream's 1 MiB send buffer or the peer's credit is exhausted.
- Closing: `close_on` sends CONNECTION_CLOSE and the connection stays closing for three probe timeouts, answering with the same frame. While a stream holds data the peer has not acknowledged, `close_on` holds the CONNECTION_CLOSE back for at most 5 seconds and never past the idle timeout. `close_listener` asks every connection to close before it waits for any, so it takes as long as the slowest one, not the sum. A CONNECTION_CLOSE from the peer drains it. The idle timeout, the handshake timeout and a stateless reset end it. A reason phrase is truncated to 1024 bytes and never interpreted.
- Not supported: 0-RTT, session resumption, connection migration, datagrams (RFC 9221) and versions other than 1.

### Derived serialization

`std/serde` defines a `Writer` and a `Reader`, structs of closures through which values are written and read. For every concrete type the compiler derives a function that writes a value to a `Writer`, a function that reads it from a `Reader`, and a `Schema` describing it:

- Booleans, integers, floats and strings map to their primitives; `u8[]` to binary; slices to sequences; maps to maps with keys written as text; optionals to null or the value; pointers to their target, a shared pointer in full each time. A pointer reached again while its target is being written is a cycle and panics.
- A struct is a map from field names to values, and every field takes part, private ones included. A reader opens it with `begin_map` and steps through it with `next_field`, which a format may spell differently from `next_key`, the step through the entries of a map. Reading skips unknown fields, uses a field's default when it is missing, treats a missing optional field as `none`, and fails with `Missing` otherwise.
- An enum variant without fields is its name. One with labelled fields is a single-key map from its name to a map of its fields, and one with positional fields maps its name to a sequence.
- A schema records each type's kind, name, fields with whether they are required and their tag, and variants with their index (the position when the enum declares no indexes). A struct or enum reached again through a pointer while it is being described becomes a `Ref` to its name.

A `Reader` reports `at` in an `Error` as its `position`, whose unit is the format's: a byte offset for JSON, a line for YAML, the count of values read for CBOR, MessagePack and TOML, a column index for `std/orm` and `0` for XML. CBOR, MessagePack, TOML, YAML, `std/avro` and `std/template` build a `serde.Tree` from a value and read a value from one, so the variant shape, the text of a map key and the range of a `u64` are defined once, in `std/serde`.

`std/json`, `std/orm` and `std/web` are built on these. `T.field`, where `T` is a struct type, is a `serde.Field[T, F]` holding the field's name and index, so a function can take a field of a given struct with a given type.

### Data

- `json.Reader` tokenizes UTF-8 bytes like .NET's `Utf8JsonReader`, strictly per RFC 8259, with a depth limit of 64. It returns strings without copying unless they contain escapes. Numbers are parsed as integers or correctly rounded floats.
- `json.serialize_with` and `json.deserialize_with` take `Options` for indentation, naming (`Unchanged`, `CamelCase`, `SnakeCase`, `PascalCase`), maximum depth and `omit_none`. Naming applies to struct field names and to variant names in both directions, and not to the keys of a map; a variant name read back is matched in PascalCase. With `omit_none`, a field whose value is `none` is left out instead of written as `null`. Binary data is base64; non-finite floats are written as `null`.
- `std/cbor` decodes RFC 8949 items into `Value`s: every major type, indefinite-length strings, arrays and maps, tags, simple values, and half, single and double floats, which become `f64`. Indefinite-length items arrive as their definite equivalents. Text must be valid UTF-8 and map keys must be distinct. These fail with `Malformed`: truncated input, reserved additional information, a stray break, indefinite integers or tags, mixed or nested string chunks, a two-byte simple value below 32, data after the item, and a declared length that exceeds the remaining input (checked before anything is allocated). Nesting deeper than `Options.max_depth`, or a string, array or map longer than `max_length`, fails with `TooLarge`. `decode_first` returns the first item and its length so a caller can continue after it. `encode` writes the deterministic encoding of §4.2.1: shortest heads, definite lengths, map entries sorted by the bytes of their encoded keys, and floats in the shortest of half, single or double that keeps the value, with NaN as `f97e00`. `diagnostic` writes the notation of §8. `serialize` and `deserialize` bind types through serde with `u8[]` as byte strings, structs and variants as maps with text keys, and `none` as null; reading looks through tags and accepts integer map keys as their decimal text.
- `std/webauthn` produces options JSON in the shape py_webauthn's `options_to_json` writes, with base64url challenges, user ids and credential ids, `pubKeyCredParams` defaulting to `supported_algorithms()` of the COSE parser (EdDSA, ES256 and RS256), and attestation `none`.
  - `verify_registration` takes the credential JSON a browser's `PublicKeyCredential` gives in base64url, with or without padding. It checks that `id` is the base64url of `rawId`, that `type` is `public-key`, that clientDataJSON has type `webauthn.create`, the expected challenge (compared in constant time) and the origin. It then decodes the attestation object and checks the RP ID hash, user presence, user verification when required, and attested credential data. It parses the COSE key, accepts only the `none` format with an empty statement, and rejects a backup state without backup eligibility. It returns the credential id, the COSE key bytes exactly as the authenticator wrote them, and the sign count.
  - `verify_authentication` makes the same client data and authenticator data checks for `webauthn.get`. It rejects a sign count that is not greater than the stored one unless both are zero. It verifies the signature over authenticatorData followed by SHA-256 of clientDataJSON with the stored key: ES256 as DER ECDSA on P-256, EdDSA as Ed25519 and RS256 as PKCS#1 v1.5 with SHA-256.
  - Each failure is its own `Error` variant.
- `std/orm` builds queries from conditions over field references, such as `orm.filter(orm.from(users), orm.ge(User.age, 18))`, and type-checks them against the field. It renders them for the connection's `Dialect`, maps rows onto structs, stores nested values as JSON columns, creates tables from types with an identity key named `id`, and returns generated keys from inserts: through `RETURNING` on PostgreSQL and MariaDB, `OUTPUT` on SQL Server and `LAST_INSERT_ID()` on MySQL.
- `sql.Value` holds dates as days since 1970-01-01, times as microseconds since midnight, timestamps as microseconds since 1970-01-01 UTC, infinite dates and timestamps as the `i64` maximum and minimum, and UUIDs as 16 bytes. A connection serves one green thread at a time; a call made while another is running fails with `Busy`. `orm.transaction` rolls back on any error from its body and fails with that error, or with the rollback's error when the rollback fails too.
- `std/sql/postgres` authenticates with SCRAM-SHA-256, MD5 or cleartext. It uses the simple protocol for statements without parameters and the extended protocol with a 64-entry prepared statement cache otherwise, streams `each` in batches of 512 rows, reads results as text decoded by type OID, and keeps a connection usable after a server error. A `Null` or `Text` parameter is declared with OID 0, so the server infers its type. Every other `Value` is a typed parameter: the driver declares it with OID 0 as well, sends Parse, Describe statement and Sync for the unnamed statement, and reads the ParameterDescription. A typed parameter that the server inferred as anything but `text` or `unknown` keeps the inferred type, so `Int(21)` binds into the `int4` of `twice($1)` and a `Float` into a `real`; one the server could not infer (`SELECT $1`, both sides of `$1 < $2`) is declared with its own type (`Int` as `int8`, `Float` as `float8`, `Decimal` as `numeric`, and so on), and the statement is parsed again with those OIDs. Bind sends every value in text format, so the server parses the text into whichever type it chose. This round trip happens once per statement text and value types, on the call that first sees them; the later calls reuse the declared OIDs and the prepared statement cache, and a statement without a typed parameter keeps its single round trip. `cancel` sends a CancelRequest over a new connection once the request is sent and keeps the connection; the call fails with `Cancelled` when the server stops it with SQLSTATE `57014`, and `each` also stops between batches. A timeout sends a CancelRequest and closes the connection. Every field of an ErrorResponse fills the `Diagnostic`. An ErrorResponse or NoticeResponse reaches `on_notice` as a `Message` and a NotificationResponse as a `Notification`, in order with the rows, while a call reads the connection. `stream` sends a statement without parameters through the simple protocol, so every result set of a string of statements arrives in order. `stream_prepared` always sends Parse, Bind, Describe portal and Execute, with or without parameters, so the server refuses text holding more than one command with SQLSTATE `42601`; `describe` sends Parse and Describe statement for the unnamed statement and reads the ParameterDescription and the RowDescription or NoData, leaving the prepared statement cache as it is. A `Column` takes `table` and `number` from the table OID and attribute number of its RowDescription field, `number` being negative for a system column. A ParameterStatus after startup reaches `on_notice` as a `Setting`. A `Column` takes its `precision`, `scale` and `length` from the RowDescription type modifier: `numeric(p, s)`, the length of `varchar` and `bpchar` (-1 without one) and the fractional digits of `time`, `timetz`, `timestamp`, `timestamptz` and `interval` (6 when undeclared). A CommandComplete tag gives `Done` a count for SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, MERGE, COPY, FETCH and MOVE, and `none` for every other command. With `Options.server_text` every non-null value is `Text` of the server's own text, and the startup message leaves out `DateStyle` so the server's setting stands. `connect_tls` upgrades with SSLRequest and `std/tls`.
- `std/sql/sqlserver` speaks TDS 7.4 with SQL authentication, or on Windows with integrated authentication when `connect_with` gets `Config.integrated`: LOGIN7 carries an SSPI Negotiate token for `MSSQLSvc/host:port` and the server's SSPI tokens are answered until the login completes. `Config.read_only` sets the read-only intent bit of the LOGIN7 type flags. A host written `host\instance` with no port is resolved through the SQL Server Browser on UDP port 1434; `.` and `(local)` name `localhost`.
  - Prelogin offers no encryption to `connect` and full encryption to `connect_tls`. `connect_tls` runs the `std/tls` handshake inside PRELOGIN packets and unwraps the packets the server keeps sending around TLS records. LOGIN7 announces UTF-8 support.
  - Statements without parameters go as SQL batches. With parameters they go to `sp_executesql` as `@p1..@pN` declared as `bigint`, `float`, `bit`, `decimal(38, s)`, `nvarchar`, `varbinary`, `uniqueidentifier`, `date`, `time(7)`, `datetime2(7)` or `datetimeoffset(7)`, with NULL sent as `nvarchar(4000)`. Every request carries the transaction descriptor, and `begin`, `commit` and `rollback` use transaction manager requests.
  - Results decode every fixed, nullable, decimal, money, date and time, GUID, character, binary, long-value, text, image, XML, `sql_variant` and user-defined type. GUIDs become RFC 4122 byte order. `char`, `varchar` and `text` decode as UTF-8 when the collation's UTF-8 flag is set and otherwise through `std/charset` in the collation's code page: a SQL collation's sort id picks it, and a Windows collation's LCID does, by locale where a language spans two code pages and by language otherwise. Text that is all ASCII is returned as it stands; other text whose collation has no code page, or that the code page does not map, fails with `Conversion`. Row counts sum the DONE and DONEINPROC counts that carry the count bit. A `Column` takes `precision`, `scale` and `length` from COLMETADATA (`length` is the declared size, in characters for `nchar` and `nvarchar` and in bytes for the rest, and -1 for `max`, `xml`, `text`, `ntext` and `image`; `scale` is also the fractional-second digits of `time`, `datetime2` and `datetimeoffset`). With `Options.server_text`, `time`, `datetime2` and `datetimeoffset` (and `sql_variant` holding them) are `Text` as `yyyy-mm-dd hh:mm:ss` with exactly `scale` fractional digits, a `datetimeoffset` in its local time followed by ` +hh:mm`. An ENVCHANGE of the database or language after the login reaches `on_notice` as a `Setting` named `database` or `language`.
  - `each` streams rows as they arrive, and stopping early sends an ATTENTION and reads until the server acknowledges it. A server error below severity 20 leaves the connection usable. A per-connection watchdog sends an ATTENTION and closes the stream when one read waits longer than the timeout, and the call fails with `Timeout`. `cancel` sends an ATTENTION once the request is sent, and the call fails with `Cancelled` when the server acknowledges it; finishing a response and sending an ATTENTION take one lock, so no ATTENTION follows a finished response.
  - Every ERROR and INFO token becomes a `Diagnostic` with the number as its code, the state, the class, the server, the procedure and the line: class 10 and below is `Info`, 11 to 19 `Failure` and 20 and above `Fatal`. Each one reaches `on_notice`, and the ERROR tokens of a call make one `Server` error in order. In `stream`, DONE and DONEINPROC end a result set with their count, or `none` without the count bit, and DONEPROC ends none.
- `std/sql/mysql` speaks the MySQL client/server protocol, version 10 greeting and protocol 4.1; the greeting tells MySQL from MariaDB, and the connection carries `sql.mysql()` or `sql.mariadb()`.
  - Packets are a three-byte length and a sequence number; a payload of `0xFFFFFF` bytes or more is split, with an empty packet closing an exact multiple, and the sequence numbers are checked on every packet. The login negotiates protocol 4.1, `DEPRECATE_EOF`, session tracking, multiple statements and result sets, and `utf8mb4`; on MariaDB it asks for extended column metadata, which names `JSON` columns. `connect_tls` sends the SSL request and runs the `std/tls` handshake before the login, and fails with `Unsupported` when the server offers no TLS. A login after `connect_stream` uses the stream as given.
  - Authentication follows the plugin the server names, and an auth switch changes it: `mysql_native_password`, `caching_sha2_password`, `sha256_password`, MariaDB's `client_ed25519` and `mysql_clear_password`. `caching_sha2_password` answers the fast path, and the full path sends the password in the clear over TLS and RSA-OAEP (SHA-1) encrypted under the server's public key over a plain connection, which the driver asks for with a one-byte request and which seals the NUL-terminated password XORed with the nonce; `sha256_password` does the same. `mysql_clear_password` is refused without TLS. An empty password sends an empty response. Any other plugin fails with `Unsupported`.
  - Statements without parameters go as `COM_QUERY` and read text rows. With parameters they go through `COM_STMT_PREPARE` and `COM_STMT_EXECUTE`, read binary rows, and keep a cache of 64 prepared statements that closes the oldest when full. A text or byte parameter of 1 MiB or more is sent in 1 MiB `COM_STMT_SEND_LONG_DATA` chunks. `stream` passes every result set of a statement in order, each ending with `Done` and the number of its rows, and a statement without a result set gives `Done` with its affected rows; `query` returns the last set. A `Column` takes `scale` from the decimals byte of its definition (also the fractional-second digits of temporal types), `precision` from the display length of a decimal, and `length` in bytes for `char`, `varchar`, `binary` and `varbinary`. `Options.server_text` has no effect and no `Setting` is sent. An ERR packet becomes a `Diagnostic` whose code is the SQLSTATE and whose message ends with the error number. A `LOCAL INFILE` request from the server is refused with `Unsupported` and the connection stays usable.
  - Values decode every column type: integers with their signedness, with `BIGINT UNSIGNED` as `Decimal`, `DECIMAL` and `NEWDECIMAL` as `Decimal`, floats, `DATE`, `TIME` (negative and past 24 hours), `DATETIME`, `TIMESTAMP`, `YEAR`, `BIT` as bytes, `ENUM` and `SET` as text, `JSON` as text, binary and character columns told apart by character set, and geometry as bytes. After the login the session selects the UTC time zone.
  - `State` reports `server`, `mariadb`, `connection_id`, `schema` and `in_transaction`, from the greeting, the status flags and the session-tracking packets, and carries `ping` and `reset`, which send `COM_PING` and `COM_RESET_CONNECTION`. `begin_with` takes an `Isolation` and a read-only flag and starts the transaction; `savepoint`, `rollback_to` and `release` manage nested ones.
  - A timeout opens a second connection with the same options, runs `KILL QUERY` for the thread id, and closes the first; the call fails with `Timeout`. `cancel` runs the same `KILL QUERY` once the statement is sent and keeps the connection, and the call fails with `Cancelled` when the server reports error 1317. A malformed packet abandons the connection with `Protocol`. Dropping the connection sends `COM_QUIT`.
  - MySQL commits implicitly before and after a DDL statement, so a migration that creates or alters a table cannot be rolled back with the rest of its transaction. The connection reports `in_transaction` from the server's flags, so a caller can see it.
- `std/mongo` speaks `OP_MSG` and `OP_COMPRESSED` over TCP, or over `std/tls` when the connection string or `Options.tls` asks for it.
  - A message carries a kind 0 body and kind 1 document sequences, and `insert`, `update` and `delete` send their documents as sequences, batched by the server's `maxWriteBatchSize` and `maxMessageSizeBytes`. A reply's CRC-32C is verified when the flag is set. A reply that sets `moreToCome` or an unknown flag bit, a frame over 96 MiB and a frame that does not parse fail with `Protocol`.
