  - Signed powers of two add `(x >> 63) >>> (64 - k)` before the arithmetic shift so the result truncates toward zero. A negative divisor negates the quotient.
  - Any other divisor multiplies by a magic number with the high-multiply instruction, signed (`imul`) or unsigned (`mul`), adds the operand back or halves and re-adds it when the magic number needs 65 bits, shifts, and for signed values adds the dividend's sign bit.
  - A remainder is the dividend minus the quotient times the divisor.
  - Signed division by the minimum 64-bit value stays an instruction with no checks.
- Common subexpressions are removed within the dominator tree for add, subtract, multiply, and, or, xor, shifts, conversions, function addresses and `check`. Comparisons, constants and loads are not merged: a load may observe a store, and a comparison that stays single-use can fuse into its branch.
- Bounds-check elimination:
  - A `check [i, n]` is removed when an identical check dominates it, or when `i < n` is known on the path and `i` is known to be non-negative.
  - A branch on a single-predecessor block's comparison of integers establishes `i < n` on the true edge of `i < n`, on the true edge of `n > i`, on the false edge of `i >= n` and on the false edge of `n <= i`.
  - `i` is non-negative when it is one of:
    - a non-negative constant;
    - an unsigned value;
    - a value a dominating signed comparison bounds below by a constant of at least 0 (or by -1 strictly);
    - a product of non-negative values that cannot overflow;
    - an induction variable that starts non-negative and only grows by a non-negative step without overflowing.
  - A variable stepping by one cannot overflow below a bound it is compared against. Any other step cannot overflow when the upper bounds of the variable and the step, added, stay within the type's range.
  - An upper bound is derived for a value from a constant, from the width of a narrow unsigned type, from a dominating signed comparison against a value that has an upper bound (strict comparisons subtract one), and for a sum or product of two non-negative values from their upper bounds when the result stays within the type's range. Only comparisons whose block dominates the use contribute.
  - A check `[i, n]` with a constant `n` is removed when `i` is non-negative and its upper bound is below `n`.
  - Branch facts also record `i <= n` on the false edge of `i < n` and the true edge of `i >= n`. These never remove a check and never prove a step cannot overflow.
  - A length that is a phi satisfies `n >= L` when every input satisfies it on its incoming edge, by being `L`, a constant at least `L`, a phi that does, or a value that a fact on the input's dominators bounds below by `L`. When `i < L` is known and `n >= L` holds, `i` non-negative removes the check, and otherwise the check compares against `L` instead of `n`, which keeps the same panics.
  - A slice filled by a counted `push` loop from 0 to `L` has length `L` when `L > 0` and 0 otherwise, so indexing it by a counter below `L` needs no check.
- Dead-value removal:
  - Values nothing reads are removed, together with groups of values that only read each other, such as a phi and the add feeding it.
  - Only these operations are removed: constants, strings, embeds, undefined values, phis, add, subtract, multiply, and, or, xor, shifts, negation, `not`, complement, conversions, `sqrt`, `floor`, comparisons, function addresses, high multiplies, trailing-zero counts, loads, float division, and integer division or remainder by a constant other than 0 and -1.
  - Every other instruction stays, including calls, stores, checks, vector instructions, frame slots and divisions that can panic.
  - An unread load through a reference, a slice element or a frame slot is removed, since `*T` values are never null and an index is checked before its element is loaded. A `load` through a `ptr[T]` stays, so a null raw pointer traps even when the value is unused.
- Float arithmetic, negation, `sqrt`, `floor` and conversions between integers, `f32` and `f64` are folded on constants with the result the SSE instruction produces.
  - `f32` operations round to single precision.
  - A float to integer conversion of a NaN or an out-of-range value gives the minimum 64-bit value.
  - Unsigned 64-bit conversions are left to the instruction.
  - A result that is NaN is never folded, so the sign and payload of a NaN come from the hardware.
- Loop-invariant code motion:
  - It works on natural loops found from back edges whose header dominates the latch, innermost first, in loops with a single outside predecessor that ends in a plain jump. That predecessor is the preheader.
  - Only values whose operands are defined outside the loop or already hoisted move: multiply, and, or, xor, shifts, negation, `not`, complement, conversions, `sqrt`, `floor`, function addresses, high multiplies, trailing-zero counts, float division, and add, subtract or shift that is not the constant-offset address arithmetic an addressing mode absorbs.
  - Loads, checks, calls, stores and integer division stay where they are, so a loop that runs zero times executes nothing it would not have executed.
  - A constant a hoisted value reads is copied into the preheader, because the code generator defines a constant that is not an immediate at its first use.
- Strength reduction replaces `i * c` by an add induction variable when `i` is a basic induction variable of a loop with a constant step, and replaces `i * c + d` derived from it the same way. The new variable starts at the start value multiplied by `c` in the preheader and steps by `step * c` on the back edge, with 64-bit wrapping arithmetic, so the result equals the product for every start, step and trip count, including overflow and negative steps.
- Devirtualisation and specialisation:
  - A call through a function value whose code address is a function address becomes a direct call that keeps the environment argument.
  - Before inlining, a function loses its function-address and environment parameter words when it is never called through a function value, is not `main` or a callback, and receives the same function address and the same constant environment from every direct call site. The parameters become the constants, the arguments leave every call site, and the function and its callers are simplified again. Calls from the function to itself that pass its own parameters unchanged count as agreeing. This runs for up to four rounds, so a function value handed down through several calls reaches the call that uses it.
  - A function of at most 150 values that calls a function-value parameter, or hands it to another call, is cloned per function address when its direct call sites pass two or more different function addresses for that parameter, or one function address with different environments.
    - Each clone takes the call sites of one function address and is named `name..withN.target`, where `N` is the first parameter word.
    - The clone has that word as the constant, drops the environment word too when every one of those call sites passes the same constant, and is simplified, so its call through the function value becomes direct.
    - Calls from the clone to the original that pass the parameters unchanged call the clone. The original stays for the remaining call sites.
    - A function gets at most 4 clones, and clones add at most 4000 values to the program.
  - The same conditions let an integer parameter that receives one and the same constant from every direct call site become that constant, but only when it pays: the function is simplified as a copy with the constants substituted and as a copy without, and the substitution is kept when the copy with it has fewer checks, blocks or values besides constants and parameters.
- Inlining:
  - Release builds inline direct calls bottom-up over the strongly connected components of the call graph, one level at a time: a component's level is one more than the highest level among the components it calls. The functions of a level find their sites and expand them in parallel; the program budget is charged between those steps in the level's discovery order, so the output does not depend on scheduling.
  - A callee is eligible when it:
    - is not part of a cycle;
    - is not a callback;
    - has at most 48 values besides constants, parameters and results, or at most 150 when the site is its only reference;
    - has at most one loop and an entry block no jump returns to;
    - uses no vector instruction.
  - Frame slots, `ccall`s and runtime calls are cloned with the body, and the callee's slots are appended to the caller's.
  - A function's references are its call sites and address uses in the program, plus one for `main` and each runtime root. They are counted before inlining, and an inlined site gives its reference to the references the callee's body holds. The original of a callee inlined at its only reference becomes unreachable and is dropped. After inlining, the program keeps the functions reachable from its roots and empties the body of every other one.
  - A callee of at most 14 values, or one inlined at its only reference, is free. Any other site is charged to the caller, which grows by at most 240 values, and to the program, whose budget is a fifth of its value count before inlining, at least 6000 and at most 40000. The last quarter of the program budget is kept for call sites inside loops.
  - A caller inlines up to four rounds. Each round takes its sites inside the deepest loops first, then the smaller callees, then the earlier values; splits the call's block; clones the callee's blocks with parameters bound to the arguments; forwards a single return's values or joins several returns in phis; and simplifies the caller. Calls that an inlined body or a devirtualised call exposes are inlined in the next round.
  - Loop back edges keep their marks, and checks and divisions keep their panics.
  - Dev builds do not inline, specialise or hoist literals, so hot reload patches see every function and every literal's data.
  - A `compiler.Profile` chooses the target, whether the IR is optimised and whether code generation is hot-reloadable. `release(target)` optimises and generates plain code. `dev` skips the optimiser and generates `fn_table` code.
- Literal hoisting:
  - A release build hoists a non-empty slice or map literal whose elements are all constants: integer, float, `bool` and `str` literals, negated numbers, `none`, enum variants without payload and non-C struct literals of constants.
  - The checker marks a literal read-only when it is the operand of an index, a `for` or `.len`, or is bound by a `let` whose every use is such an operand. An assignment, a `mut` argument or any other use anywhere in the target path of an element or field makes the binding non-read-only.
  - Each hoisted literal owns a word of `rt_cells`. The code loads the word, and when it is 0 builds the literal as usual and stores the pointer, so the first evaluation builds it and a racing thread at most builds a second equal copy. The miss path calls `rt_region_suspend` before it builds the literal and `rt_region_resume` after it stores the pointer, so the literal is on the heap even when the first evaluation is inside a `region` block.
  - A read-only literal uses the cell's pointer, as a slice of its length for a slice.
  - Any other use copies it: a slice with one grow call that moves the template's bytes, a map with the prelude's `__map_clone`, which copies the entry array and the index. A literal below the copy thresholds of the Slices and Maps sections is built each time in that case.
- Block merging and jump threading:
  - A block that ends in a jump to a block with no other predecessor and no phis is merged into it.
  - A block that branches on a phi, or on its negation, and holds nothing else but constants that only feed its successors' phis, is skipped by a predecessor that passes a constant: the predecessor jumps to the successor the constant selects, and takes a copy of each constant that successor's phis read from the block. Blocks are visited successors first, so a chain of such blocks is threaded in one pass.
  - The phi that joins `a && b` takes `false` from the edge where `a` is false, and the phi that joins `a || b` takes `true` from the edge where `a` is true, so a condition built from them threads into branches on its operands.
  - The backend threads a block the same way when a predecessor copies a constant, or a value its own predecessor branched on, into the register the block branches on.

### Code generation

- `generate` produces the functions on one green thread per CPU. Unless the build is hot-reloadable, the functions run in at most 128 consecutive batches (`ir.batches`). For `low asm` each batch runs inside one `region` block whose value is the assembly text, data chunks and stack maps of its functions. Outside a hot-reloadable build, each function is generated and assembled inside its own `region` block whose value is its `asm.Chunk`, data chunks and stack maps, so its text never leaves the region. Inside it, generation runs in a nested `region` whose value is the function's text, data chunks and stack maps, and assembly in another whose value is the chunk, so the memory of instruction selection and register allocation is freed before assembly starts. That memory is freed while it is still in the cache, does not count toward the collector's trigger, and a collection scans only the regions in progress. A function assembled after data cannot know so until the function before it is generated, so the first function of each batch is assembled as if after code, keeps its text, and is assembled again after the batches finish when the last function of the batch before ends in data. The stack maps become a `.rodata` chunk built from the maps' return labels. The chunks merge in function order through `asm.assemble_pieces`, together with the runtime, data and start text. A hot-reloadable build generates and assembles sequentially outside regions as text, because the function table keeps the symbol names it is given.
- Phis and layout:
  - Critical edges are split and each phi becomes a parallel copy at the end of every predecessor, emitted with cycles broken through `r11`.
  - An argument whose live range ends at that copy prefers the phi's register, and the phi prefers the registers of its arguments, so a loop-carried value usually needs no move.
- Block layout:
  - Blocks are laid out in reverse post-order, visiting the false successor first so the true successor follows its branch.
  - Inside a loop, the true arm of an integer `==` comparison against a constant is treated as rare when it holds a loop, a call, a return or an unreachable end. The blocks between the branch and its false successor move after the last block of the innermost loop, so the common path falls through to the back edge. A light arm stays in line, because the extra taken branches would cost more than the arm when it is the common path.
  - A jump or branch to an empty block goes to that block's final target, a jump to the next block in layout is omitted, and a conditional branch whose true target is next is inverted. A block holding phi copies is never empty.
- Loop latches:
  - A loop's latch whose header holds only a fused compare and branch ends with that compare and a conditional branch to the body, after the poll check when the block has one, instead of a jump to the header.
  - A header with up to 12 instructions, calls included, is copied after the latch instead, so the back edge tests the condition without a jump and the first test still runs at the original header.
- A comparison used only by the branch that ends its block is fused into a `cmp` and conditional jump.
- Addressing:
  - A load or store addresses `[base + index*scale + disp]` at every width.
  - A 64-bit `add` read only by addresses folds into them, as does a `shl` or `mul` by 1, 2, 4 or 8 feeding the index. A constant operand becomes the displacement. Other strides keep one multiply and fold the add and the displacement.
  - The folded base and index are uses of the load or store, so they stay live to it, across blocks where the definition dominates. A base or index in a spilled slot is reloaded into `r10` or `r11` immediately before the instruction. An instruction with a memory operand takes any other temporary from `rax`.
- Load folding:
  - A load is folded into its only consumer as a memory operand when both are in one block and no store, call, runtime call, vector operation or poll lies between them.
  - Integer arithmetic, logic, bounds checks and compares fold a 64-bit load.
  - A narrow load is never folded into a 64-bit operation. It folds only into a compare of the same type against a constant the type holds, as `cmp` of a sized memory operand with an immediate.
  - Scalar SSE arithmetic and compares fold a load of the operation's own width, `f64` or `f32`.
- A float multiplication by 2.0 is an addition of the operand to itself, which is exact for every input including NaN, infinities and signed zeros. Other float constants keep their multiply.
- Boolean branches:
  - A branch on `not x` swaps its successors instead of computing the complement, which is exact for float comparisons as well.
  - A comparison read only by `not` is emitted with the inverted condition.
  - A block that only branches on a boolean phi is threaded: a predecessor that passes a constant jumps to the successor the constant selects, a predecessor that passes the condition of its own branch jumps to the matching successor, and any other predecessor branches on the value it passes.
  - Evaluation order and short-circuiting of `&&` and `||` operands are unchanged.
  - A branch on the result of a `not` or a comparison defined in its own block uses that operand directly, so the `not` and the materialised boolean are not emitted.
- Spilling:
  - A value's spill weight is the sum over its uses and definitions of 8 raised to the block's loop depth, capped at depth 4. Loop depth counts the natural loops containing the block, found with the dominator tree the optimiser also uses; a cycle with more than one entry is not a loop.
  - When no register is free the allocator spills the conflicting value with the lowest spill cost, breaking ties by the later end of its live range.
  - The allocator runs three times per function, with the spill cost set to the weight, to the weight divided by the length of the live range and to the weight divided by its square root. It keeps the assignment whose spilled values have the lowest total weight plus 2 for each callee-saved register it uses. The weight alone wins ties.
- Constants that fit in 32 bits are used as immediates and take no register. An undefined value used as an operand or stored to memory is the immediate 0, so a variant's unused payload words never hold stale pointers.
- Narrow integer results:
  - Results of narrow integer operations that can leave the type's range are re-extended with `movsxd`, `movzx` or a 32-bit `mov`.
  - A `u32` add, subtract, multiply, negation, complement and constant left shift uses the 32-bit form of the instruction, whose write zero-extends and needs no re-extension.
  - A conversion to an integer type that holds every value of the source type is a register copy. A right shift of an extended value needs no re-extension.
  - Unsigned comparisons and branches use the unsigned condition codes, and unsigned division uses `div`.
  - A division by a constant other than 0 and -1 that reaches the code generator, which is signed division by the minimum 64-bit value, emits no zero or -1 test.
- Float registers:
  - Float values live in `xmm2` to `xmm15`; `xmm0` and `xmm1` are scratch.
  - A value is a float register value when it is produced or consumed by float arithmetic, a float comparison, a conversion, `sqrt` or `floor`. A float that only passes through moves, calls, stores and phis stays in a general register.
  - Scalar SSE instructions (`addsd`, `subss`, `sqrtsd`, `roundsd`, `cvttsd2si` and so on) work on registers directly, with a spilled operand as a memory operand.
  - An `f32` in an `xmm` register keeps bits 32 to 63 zero, so `movq` moves it to a general register or the frame unchanged. `movq` is used only where a float meets an integer word: call, return and parameter words, stores and loads, bit casts, and C argument registers.
- A float constant that does not fit in 32 bits is a `.rodata` quadword, shared by value and used as a memory operand. Zero is `xorps`.
- A float comparison used only by the branch that ends its block is fused into `ucomisd` or `ucomiss` and a conditional jump. `<` and `<=` swap their operands and use `ja` and `jae`, so an unordered result is false. `==` and `!=` also test the parity flag.
- Shifts and rotates:
  - Variable shift counts go through `cl`, with `rcx` saved in `r11` around the shift. Counts above 63 take a separate path.
  - The pair `(x << k) | (x >> (w - k))` is one `rol` when it meets all of these: both shifts act on one `u32` or `u64` value `x` of width `w`, `k` is a constant between 1 and `w - 1`, the shifts are read only by the `or`, and they appear in either order. No other combination is a rotate.
- `exp` is a runtime routine: range reduction by `ln 2` split into high and low parts, a degree-13 polynomial, and scaling by two powers of two so subnormal results stay in range.
- A comparison of a `str` against a literal of up to 16 bytes is inline: a length compare, then one compare of the bytes at the pointer against an immediate of 1, 2, 4 or 8 bytes, or two when the length is none of these, ending in `sete`. The literal's address and length are never materialised and the comparison is not a call. Any other `str` equality moves the arguments into the argument registers, returns 0 without calling `rt_str_eq` when the lengths differ and calls it otherwise.
- Register allocation:
  - Registers are assigned by linear scan over each value's live segments, so a value does not occupy a register where it is dead and a register's holes hold other values. Each machine register value has a number, block liveness is a bitset over those numbers, the instruction selector counts uses and definitions in slices indexed by them, and the allocator keeps one list of assigned values per register; the three spill-cost orders stop at the first allocation that costs nothing.
  - `rcx`, `rsi`, `rdi`, `r8` and `r9` hold values that are not live across a call. `rbx` and `r12` to `r15` hold values that are, and are saved in the function's frame when used. Values that find no register are spilled to the frame. `rax`, `rdx`, `r10` and `r11` are scratch.
  - A growth call, `rt_grow_scan` or `rt_grow_noscan` according to whether the new buffer may hold pointers, takes the old pointer, the used bytes and the new size in `rax`, `rdx` and `r10` and returns the new pointer in `rax`. It preserves every other general register, so a value that cannot hold a pointer stays in `rcx`, `rsi`, `rdi`, `r8` or `r9` across it. A value that may hold a pointer is treated as live across a call: the routine saves the preserved registers below the scanned part of the stack, where the collector does not read them. The routine records the caller's return address and frame pointer like any runtime entry, and the old buffer stays reachable through the words it keeps in the scanned region.
  - Float register values are allocated from `xmm2` to `xmm15` by the same scan. No `xmm` register survives a call, so a float live across a call is spilled, and spilled floats are not in the stack maps. A function that uses vectors gets no float registers, so its float operations go through `xmm0` and `xmm1`.
- Register preferences:
  - A value used as a call argument prefers that argument's register, a parameter prefers the register it arrives in, and a copy and its source prefer each other's registers.
  - The result of a two-operand add, subtract, multiply, and, or, xor, shift or float divide prefers the register of its first operand, and for a commutative operation of its second, so the instruction can work in place. Where it cannot share, it avoids the register of the operand it still has to read.
- Wrapped saves:
  - A function whose calls all lie below one block, the nearest common dominator of its call blocks, saves the callee-saved registers it uses at the head of that block instead of in the prologue and restores them only on returns reached through it.
  - The paths that skip the block use no callee-saved register, have no calls or polls and are at most 8 instructions, copies included, where they rejoin the shared tail, which is duplicated for them.
  - A value defined on a skipping path and live into the block is copied at the end of that path into a fresh value, and the block's head copies it into the original. The original lives only below the block, so a loop counter initialised on the light path stays in a caller-saved register.
  - A value defined in the region below the block and live into it that crosses a call is given a fresh value there.
  - Save slots keep their frame positions, and the stack maps name every save slot at every safepoint.
  - A vector function is not wrapped. The block and the tail are found with the same dominator tree that the optimiser uses.
- Frame modes: a function has one of three.
  - A full frame is built in the prologue.
  - A function with a wrapped region builds its frame, `push rbp`, `mov rbp, rsp` and `sub rsp`, at the head of the block that holds the saves, and the paths that skip the block return with a bare `ret`. This needs every skipping block to be free of anything that reads or writes the frame: no spilled value, frame address, slot access, parameter beyond the sixth, poll, panic or return wider than two words.
  - A function with no calls, polls, panicking checks, frame slots or spilled values and no callee-saved register has no frame at all and contains no safepoint.
- A frame slot's address is not held in a register: a load or store through it addresses `[rbp+offset]` directly and any other use computes the address with `lea` into a temporary where it is needed. A spilled value copied from another value whose live range it does not overlap shares that value's slot, so splitting a value at the wrapped block adds no slot-to-slot copy.
- A call result in `rax` or `rdx` that is read once, by the instruction directly after the call, stays there and is not moved. The immediate consumers are integer arithmetic and logic, a store of the value, call arguments, the return, a branch condition and a phi copy. A result word nothing reads is not moved at all.
- A block that only returns, with at most four predecessors that end in a jump, is copied into them, so each returns without a jump.
- A return restores with `leave`, or `pop rbp` in a frame of size 0. The last instruction of a returning block writes `rax` directly when it defines the first result and nothing else reads it. Up to two results are placed with a parallel move.
- Instruction selection:
  - An add or subtract of a constant or a register into a different register is `lea`.
  - A multiply by a constant is the three-operand `imul`.
  - A commutative operation takes whichever operand already sits in the destination.
  - A compare with 0 is `test`. A compare of a memory operand with an immediate reads the memory directly.
  - The constant 0 is `xor r32, r32` where the flags are dead.
  - A 32-bit add or subtract into a different register is a `lea` with a 32-bit destination and 64-bit address registers.
  - A move that undoes the move before it is dropped.
- The poll check is the `TLS_POLL` macro, which compares the thread's preemption flag with 0, and `jne` to an out-of-line stub that calls `rt_poll` and jumps back to a label placed directly after the `jne`. On Linux `TLS_POLL` is `cmp byte ptr fs:[-8], 0`; on Windows it reads the flag through `rt_tls_slot` and `gs`.
- Alignment:
  - Every function and every C callback thunk starts on a 32-byte boundary.
  - A program for Linux is assembled with branch alignment, which pads so that no jump, and no fused compare and jump, crosses or ends at a 32-byte boundary. Hot patches are assembled without it.
  - A loop that is a single back-edge block, ends in its own conditional jump and has at most four instructions and phi copies besides the poll, if any, and the compare starts on a 32-byte boundary, so the whole loop sits in one window and needs no padding inside.

Calling convention:

- The first six argument words go in `rdi`, `rsi`, `rdx`, `rcx`, `r8` and `r9`. The rest go in an outgoing area at the bottom of the caller's frame, so `rsp` does not move inside a function.
- Up to two result words return in `rax` and `rdx`. Wider results are written to the outgoing area after the stack arguments.
- Argument and parameter registers, general and `xmm`, are filled with a parallel move that breaks cycles through `r11`.

### Vectors

- A vector local lives in a frame slot, like a variable passed as `mut`, and a vector expression evaluates to the address of a slot holding its result.
- A vector operation is one IR instruction, `vector`, taking the destination and operand addresses, or `vectorscalar` for `mask`.
- At calls, returns, fields and other aggregates a vector is its words, loaded from or stored to a slot. A `let` of a computed vector adopts the slot of the result instead of copying it.
- A function containing vector instructions is compiled twice. Its symbol is a stub that jumps to the AVX2 version when the CPU and the operating system support AVX2, and to the SSE2 version otherwise. `rt_cpu_init` checks `cpuid` and `xgetbv` at startup.
- `LOW_SIMD=sse2` in the environment selects the SSE2 versions, which the fixture runner uses to test both.
- The AVX2 version uses three-operand VEX instructions on `ymm` registers. An addition, subtraction, multiplication, minimum, maximum, floating-point division, bitwise operation, byte shuffle or integer equality is one instruction on the allocated registers, and its second operand may be the slot in memory. A splat broadcasts into the destination register. `vzeroupper` runs before every call and return of a vector function.
- A vector slot whose uses are all vector instructions, vector loads and stores through the slot, and lane addresses (the slot plus an index) is a register slot. The register allocator treats it as one value whose home is the frame slot and gives it one of `ymm8` to `ymm15` (`xmm8` to `xmm15` for a 128-bit vector) with the same linear scan as the other values. A register slot that gets no register is read from and written to its home. Any other vector slot stays in memory.
- A register slot is live in its register across blocks and loops. A pass after lowering inserts the transfers: a spill (`vmovdqu` to the home) before a word load from the slot and before a word store that writes only part of it, a fill (`vmovdqu` from the home) before the next read through the register, and a spill of every live register slot before a call, runtime call, C call or a syscall that is not emitted inline, with a fill after it where the value is read again. A run of word stores that writes the whole slot needs no spill. A block ends with every live-out register slot in its register. No `ymm` register survives a call.
- The poll stub of a block saves the register slots live out of the block to their homes, runs `vzeroupper`, calls `rt_poll`, loads them back and jumps to the block's continuation.
- The SSE2 version splits 256-bit operations into two 128-bit halves and keeps every vector in memory.
- An operation without an instruction in the version being generated runs as a scalar loop over the lanes in a scratch area of the frame. These operations are `pshufb` on SSE2, 64-bit multiplication, minimum, maximum and comparison where unavailable, byte shifts and arithmetic 64-bit shifts. Unsigned comparisons flip the sign bit of both operands and compare signed.

### WebAssembly

- `compiler/wasm` turns each SSA function into a WebAssembly function whose parameters and results are its words as `i64`. Engines refuse a function type of more than 1000 parameters or results, so a function whose parameters or results come to more than 256 words passes that side through linear memory. The rule reads only the word counts of the function type, so every direct and indirect call site and the callee agree. Parameters above the limit are one `i64` pointer to the words, which the caller stores in an area of its frame and the callee loads into locals before anything else runs. Results above the limit are a leading `i64` pointer to a caller-owned slot in the same area, which the callee fills at `return` and the caller loads from after the call. The area is part of the caller's zeroed frame, so the collector's scan of the stack sees any pointer it holds, and the callee's pointer parameters get their root words as usual. Every SSA value is an `i64` local, and values whose live ranges do not overlap share one, coloured along the dominator tree.
- Blocks nest as `block`, `loop` and `if` following the dominator tree, as in Ramsey's "Beyond Relooper", built with an explicit task stack. A graph is reducible when every edge to a block that is not later in reverse post-order goes to a block that dominates its source; `ir.is_reducible` in `compiler/ir/dominators.low` is that test. The back end fails the build with `irreducible control flow in <function>` when it is false, and the lowering has no node splitting. `compiler/tests/ir` requires every function to be reducible after optimisation (see Checks). Phi copies run at the end of the predecessor, which is always a jump because the IR splits every other edge into a block with phis. A switch is a `br_table`.
- Results match the amd64 back end: narrow integers are sign-extended or masked, division by zero panics, a shift by more than 63 fills, and a float converted to an integer out of range or NaN gives the minimum `i64`.
- There is one linear memory. A Low address is the WebAssembly address plus 64 KiB, so a null pointer and small offsets from it lie outside the memory and trap. From the bottom: the 16 bytes of hash seeds, one 1024-byte static block for each module that calls `__static_block`, the hoisted-literal cells, the strings and embedded files, an 8 MiB stack for frame slots that grows down from its top through the global `sp`, then the heap, which starts at the next 64 KiB boundary and is managed by the runtime described below. A prologue that would move `sp` below the stack panics with `stack overflow`. A `region` block allocates on the collected heap, so memory in a region is reclaimed when it becomes unreachable, not at the end of the block, and a use after the block does not fault.
- A memory scan cannot see a local, so a frame also holds root words. Before a call that can collect (a direct or indirect call, `rt_alloc`, `rt_grow` or a scheduler routine, which allocates thread records and switches stacks), each local whose value is live across the call and not scalar is stored to its root word, and the prologue stores the parameters that are not scalar. A function with no such call has no root words. Objects do not move, so nothing is reloaded after the call.
- String equality, printing, panics, `memmove`, the high half of a 64-bit multiplication and the float-to-integer conversions are WebAssembly functions the generator writes into every module.
- A `vector` instruction is straight-line scalar code over its lanes in `compiler/wasm/vector.low`, with no SIMD instructions. Each lane is loaded from the operand addresses at its width, computed as an `i64`, `f32` or `f64` and stored to the destination before the next lane is read, so a destination that is also an operand is safe. `shuffle` builds its result in locals before storing it, `copy` is `memory.copy`, and bitwise operations and `select` run on 8-byte words. `vectorscalar` (`mask`) ors the top bit of each lane into the result.
  - Lanes match the amd64 versions: integer lanes wrap, comparisons give all ones or all zeros, and a float `min` or `max` returns its second operand when either is NaN or both are zero.
  - A shift count at or above the lane width gives zero, or the sign fill for a signed right shift. A count on 8-bit lanes and on a signed 64-bit right shift is taken modulo 64.
- `compiler/compiler/prelude/browser.low` is the runtime of a target without an operating system. The compiler parses it with the prelude's privileges and joins it to every program, and a function named `__rt_*` in privileged source is a root beside `main`. The back end calls `__rt_alloc`, `__rt_grow`, `__rt_pin`, `__rt_unpin`, `__rt_lock`, `__rt_unlock`, `__rt_cas`, `__rt_context`, `__rt_set_context`, `__rt_has_context`, `__rt_lock_thread`, `__rt_unlock_thread`, `__rt_lock_all`, `__rt_unlock_all`, `__rt_spawn`, `__rt_current`, `__rt_park`, `__rt_ready`, `__rt_sleep`, `__rt_timer_add` and `__rt_timer_del` by name for the runtime value of the same symbol, with the words `compiler/ir/runtime.low` gives it. The thread entry calls `__rt_finish`. A held lock panics `every green thread is blocked forever`, because channel code never parks while it holds one.
- A runtime function uses no `new`, slice, map, run-time string, closure or interpolation and has no stack slot, so it has no frame, and it keeps addresses in `i64` so that no local of it is a root. It allocates only by calling `__rt_alloc` or `__rt_grow`, and stores the object it gets in a word the collector reads before it allocates again. `compiler/tests/wasm` checks that no `__rt_` function has a stack slot, contains `rt_alloc` or `rt_grow`, or calls a function outside the `__rt_` names.
- The runtime reaches the machine through `extern "wasm"`, accepted only in privileged source and lowered inline with no import. All of them take and return `i64`, and an address is a Low address:
  - `stack_pointer()`, `stack_top()`, `cells_start()`, `cells_end()`, `heap_start()` and `memory_end()` give the shadow stack bounds, the hoisted-literal cells, the heap's first address and the end of the memory.
  - `memory_grow(pages)` grows the memory by 64 KiB pages and returns the previous page count, or -1 when the host's page limit or the address space refuses.
  - `fill(at, byte, n)` and `copy(to, from, n)` are `memory.fill` and `memory.copy`.
  - `stress_words()` is the host's stress interval, or 0.
  - `stack_floor()` gives the lowest address a frame may use, and `set_stack(sp, top, floor)` sets `sp`, `top` and `floor` together.
  - `clock(id)` is `low.clock`.
  - `switch(from, to)` and `wait(ms)` call the host imports of the same names. They exist only in a module that imports them, and trap otherwise.
- The heap is a table of 65536 page entries, set up on the first allocation in the first pages of the heap, followed by the pages the allocator hands out. An entry names the page's kind (free, small, large head, large tail or runtime table), whether its objects hold pointers, and the allocated and mark bits of a large object.
  - An object of up to 16 KiB comes from a page of one size class and one scan kind: 16, 32, 48, 64, 96, 128, 192, 256, 384, 512, 768, 1024, 1536, 2048, 3072, 4096, 8192 or 16384 bytes. The page starts with an allocated bitmap and a mark bitmap, and the free objects of a (class, scan kind) are linked through their first word.
  - A larger object takes a run of whole pages, found first-fit. A tail entry names its head.
  - When no run fits, the memory grows. When it cannot, the runtime collects once and retries, then panics `out of memory`.
  - Returned memory is zeroed, at least one word and 8-byte aligned. `rt_grow` returns a block of the new size holding the first `used` bytes of the old one, which is a root while the new block is allocated.
- A collection runs when the bytes allocated since the last one exceed the bytes that survived it, with a floor of 4 MiB. It is conservative and non-moving:
  - The roots are every word of the shadow stack from `sp` to its top, of the hoisted-literal cells and of the pin table, plus the context word and the scheduler's state. Every green thread that is not running is a root with its stack from its saved `sp` to its top. A word that points anywhere inside an allocated object, including past its first byte, marks it.
  - Marking pushes objects that hold pointers on a stack kept in pages from the page allocator, which doubles when full. An object that holds none is marked and not scanned.
  - Sweeping rebuilds the free lists, returns empty small pages and unmarked large runs to the free pages and clears the marks. `unpin` removes one occurrence of the word.
- The host asks for stress with `host.stress`, which `host.mjs` sets from `LOW_GC=stress` when run by `node`; the module reads it through `low.gc_stress()`. The runtime then collects every 8192 words allocated, or every eighth of the live words when that is more, and sweep fills each freed object after its link word and each freed large run with `0xa5`, so a use of an unrooted object reads a pattern instead of stale data. `LOW_WASM_MAX_PAGES=n` in the same environment sets `host.maxPages`, which caps the memory at `n` pages above its initial size; `memory_grow` consults `low.page_limit()`.
- The module imports `low.write(fd, ptr, len)`, `low.exit(status)`, `low.clock(id)`, which returns nanoseconds as `i64` from the monotonic clock for id 1 and from the wall clock since the Unix epoch otherwise, `low.entropy(ptr, len)`, `low.gc_stress()` and `low.page_limit()`, plus `m.exp` and each `f64` function an `extern "m"` block declares, which must be one of the functions `wasm.host_math` lists and `host.mjs` provides. A module of a program that uses the scheduler routines also imports `low.switch(from, to)` and `low.wait(ms)`, and exports `run_thread`. It exports `memory` and `_start`, which fills the hash seeds, calls `main` and exits with status 1 and `error: Variant` when `main` fails.
- `extern "low"` in standard library source imports `low.<symbol>` from the host. A pointer parameter (`ptr`, `ptr[T]` or a reference argument) is an `i32` holding the WebAssembly address, another integer parameter is an `i32` wrapped from the value, and an `f64` stays `f64`. A result is nothing, an integer, which is extended to `i64` by the result type's signedness, or an `f64`. `std/crypto` declares `entropy(buf: ptr, n: i64)` this way, and `std/stdio` declares `read(fd, buf, n)`, which returns the count read, `write(fd, buf, n)` and `halt(status)`.
- A target without an operating system (`target.has_os`) rejects, while the loader reads the program, anything that reaches one:
  - An import of `std/sys` fails at the import with `browser/wasm32 has no operating system, so std/sys cannot be imported: main -> std/os -> std/sys`, the chain being the shortest from `main`, because the loader is breadth first and records each module's first importer.
  - An `extern` block whose library is not `m` fails at the block with `browser/wasm32 has no library "c"; a browser program can declare only extern "m"`. Libraries `low` and `wasm` are allowed only in privileged source, the standard library and the runtime.
  - A call to `__syscall` fails at the call with `browser/wasm32 has no system calls, so __syscall cannot be called`; the checker decides it for every target without Linux system calls, `windows/amd64` included.
- `wait_readable`, `wait_writable` and calls through C function pointers fail the build with a `browser/wasm32:` message naming them.
- Green threads run on one host thread. A thread switches away when it parks, sleeps or finishes, and a loop that never does so is preempted at its back-edges.
  - A module that uses the scheduler routines has a mutable `i64` global, the budget, which starts at 1000. At the end of each block that closes a loop (`b.poll`), before the phi copies, the block decrements the budget; at 0 it resets it and calls `__rt_yield`. Other modules have neither the global nor the polls, and `__rt_` functions never poll.
  - The poll is a collecting point: `colors.low` records, keyed by the block, the pointer locals busy at the end of the block (the values live out of it and its control value), and the poll stores them to the frame's root words before the call, as a call does.
  - `__rt_yield` returns at once while the held-lock count, a word of the heap's table page that `__rt_lock`, `__rt_unlock`, `__rt_lock_thread`, `__rt_unlock_thread` and through them `__rt_lock_all` and `__rt_unlock_all` keep, is not 0. Otherwise it runs the expired timers and returns when the run queue is empty, and else marks the thread runnable, queues it and switches to the head of the queue as `park` does.
  - A thread is a heap record holding its state (runnable, running, parked or dead), a wake token, the closure's code and env words, its stack block, its top and saved `sp` and its context word. `spawn` copies the spawner's context into the record and queues it without running it. The main thread's record is made on the first scheduler call and uses the static stack.
  - The scheduler's state hangs from a word of the heap's table page: the table of live threads, the FIFO run queue, the timers, the running thread and the free stacks.
  - `ready` queues a parked thread and sets the wake token of any other. `park` consumes a set token and returns, and otherwise marks the thread parked and switches away.
  - Switching away picks a thread in a loop: it runs the expired timers, takes the head of the run queue, and with an empty queue waits in the host with `low.wait` until the earliest timer or, with no timer, panics `every green thread is blocked forever`. When the pick is the thread itself it continues; otherwise it saves its `sp` and context, makes the pick current, calls `low.switch(from, to)` and, once resumed, restores `sp`, `top` and `floor` with `set_stack` before any function with a frame runs.
  - A timer is a deadline, the thread to ready, a waiter, the arm index and an id. Firing one with a waiter claims the waiter's `claimed` word from 0 to 1, stores the arm index and 0 in the waiter and readies the thread; a timer with no waiter readies the thread directly, and a claimed waiter's timer is dropped. `sleep` adds a timer without a waiter and parks. Timers use the monotonic clock.
  - A thread's stack is a 256 KiB block from `__rt_alloc` of the kind that holds no pointers, which the collector scans from the thread's saved `sp` to its top, taken when the thread first runs, from the free list when it holds one, with the floor at its base so that overflowing it panics `stack overflow`. The exported `run_thread(g)` sets `sp`, `top` and `floor` from the record, calls the closure through the table with its env as the only argument, calls `__rt_finish`, which marks the thread dead, removes it from the table, picks the next thread as above and then frees the stack, and returns the thread to run next. The stack stays referenced while the pick runs because a collection there still runs on it.
- `compiler/wasm/host.mjs` is the host. `run(program, host)` takes the bytes of a module or a compiled `WebAssembly.Module`, instantiates it with the imports above, runs it and returns its exit status. `host.read(fd, bytes)` answers `low.read` with the count read, 0 at the end of the input, or -1 when the host has no input, and `low.halt` ends the run as `low.exit` does; a trap prints `panic: invalid memory access` or `panic: stack overflow` and returns 2. The host uses WebAssembly JavaScript Promise Integration only for a module that imports `low.switch`; any other module runs `_start` synchronously, because a promising call runs on a secondary stack of under 1 MB. For a module that imports it, `low.switch` and `low.wait` are `WebAssembly.Suspending` imports and `_start` and `run_thread` are called through `WebAssembly.promising`:
  - `switch(from, to)` stores a promise as the resumer of `from` and schedules `go(to)` with `queueMicrotask`, never running WebAssembly inside the import. `go(g)` resolves the stored resumer of `g` when there is one, and otherwise calls `run_thread(g)` and then `go` on the thread it returns.
  - `wait(ms)` returns a promise that `setTimeout` resolves.
  - `_start` returning ends the run with status 0. A rejection of `_start` or of any `run_thread` call ends it the way a trap does: `low.exit` gives its status and any other error prints `panic:` and its message and gives 2. The run clears its pending timeouts and settles once.
  - Without `WebAssembly.Suspending`, a module that imports `low.switch` prints `panic: green threads need WebAssembly JavaScript Promise Integration` and exits with 2; a module that does not import it needs no Promise Integration.

  Run by `node` with a module path, it sets `--experimental-wasm-jspi` through `v8.setFlagsFromString` when `WebAssembly.Suspending` is missing, reads input from the process's descriptors when the module asks and runs the module on a worker with a 256 MB stack. The worker's exit without a status prints `panic:` and gives 2, so a program whose threads all wait for a wakeup that never comes cannot exit with status 0. `low run --target browser/wasm32` writes the script beside the module and runs it that way, and `compiler/tests/wasm` builds run fixtures for the host and for the browser, once normally and once under stress, and checks both print the same and exit with the same status. The fixtures include channel, `select`, `sleep` and `std/sync` programs, spinning threads that only back-edge preemption lets run, and the deadlock panic. A test also runs a threaded module with `WebAssembly.Suspending` deleted and checks the panic above. It also runs a program that allocates 2 GiB in total with at most 1 MiB live under a 64 MiB page limit.

## Hot reload

`low dev app.low` or `low dev app/` builds a dev binary for the host, runs it, and watches every source file of every loaded module, every embedded file, and the file lists of their directories. The watcher polls the content of those files every 100 ms on every host. On save:

- A file that fails to compile is reported and the running program is left alone.
- The program restarts when any of these changes:
  - the set of linked libraries;
  - the set of called C functions;
  - the set of modules that call `__static_block`;
  - a struct or enum layout;
  - an existing function's signature;
  - a closure's captured variables;
  - `main`;
  - more functions than `fn_table` has entries.

  A running frame of old code may still call any function, so it must keep the old calling convention and data layout. `main` never returns to pick up new code.
- Otherwise every function whose generated assembly differs from the running version is recompiled, including callers whose code changed only because a callee did, every instance of an edited generic function, and new functions. Heap, stack, open regions and all live values are kept.

Mechanism:

- Dev builds call every function through `fn_table`, a writable table of code addresses, sized at build to the first power of two that is at least 4096 and at least twice the number of functions, with `call [rip+fn_table+8*i]`. Release builds call directly.
- In dev builds a function value's code word is the address of its `fn_table` entry and calls through it use `call [r10]`, so a stored function value or closure runs the newest code. Callback thunks call through the table too.
- `compiler/amd64` owns this protocol: `generate` with a `FuncTable` and `generate_patch`, the `fn_table` size, the patch signal and entry size, the mailbox and handler symbol names, and `hot_base`, `hot_size` and `hot_runtime` for each `amd64.Platform`.
- At start, before the collector reserves the heap, the dev runtime reserves a 64 MB read-execute region, close enough to the binary for 32-bit relative references. On Linux it installs a `SIGUSR1` handler and maps the region at `0x40000000`. On Windows `rt_hot_init` calls `VirtualAlloc` for the region at `0x180000000` and panics when the address is taken. A dev build for Windows is linked by `link.pe_fixed` at the image base `0x140000000` with relocations stripped and no `DYNAMIC_BASE`, so the image and the region stay a fixed distance apart.
- A patch is assembled and linked in process, by `Built.Patch`, at the next free address in that region, resolving its references against the symbols of the running binary that the linker returned with its image. It holds only the changed functions and their strings. Patched functions get a fresh symbol suffix so they never collide with the originals.
- `low dev` writes the machine code into the region and the `(index, address)` pairs and count into a mailbox in the program, then runs the program's `rt_hot_handler`, which stores each address into `fn_table` with an aligned 8-byte write and clears the count, which `low dev` waits for. On Linux the host writes through `/proc/<pid>/mem` and sends `SIGUSR1`. On Windows it opens the process with `OpenProcess`, writes with `WriteProcessMemory`, flushes the instruction cache and starts a thread at `rt_hot_handler` with `CreateRemoteThread`.
- Frames already running keep executing their old code; the next call through the table enters the new code. Old code is never unmapped.
- On Windows, once the program has cleared the count, `low dev` posts the registered window message `LowHotReload` to every top-level window of the program. A `std/ui/platform` window answers it with a `Resized` of its current size, so a `std/ui` window draws a frame with the patched code. On Linux a window draws patched code at its next frame.
- `low dev` starts the program as a process group, which is a job object on Windows. `Ctrl+C` is caught through `signal.notify`, which stops the session, kills the program's group and removes the build directory.

## UI

`std/ui` holds the modules that draw a window's user interface with Vulkan, on Wayland on Linux and on Win32 on Windows: windows and input, a flexbox layout engine, fonts and text, a scene of GPU primitives, a renderer that draws a scene into an offscreen target or a window, and on top of them an element tree with signals, hit testing and focus that a program runs as an application.

### UI modules

| Module | Contents |
|---|---|
| `std/ui` | `Element` and its builders, `Signal`, `Task`, `Context`, `App` and `run`. |
| `std/ui/geometry` | `Point`, `Size`, `Rect`, `Edges`, `Corners` and `Color`, all in `f32`. |
| `std/ui/layout` | The flexbox engine over a tree of styled nodes. |
| `std/ui/font` | TrueType and OpenType CFF parsing, glyph outlines, the rasteriser and the embedded fallback font. |
| `std/ui/dwrite` | DirectWrite shaping, rasterisation and rendering parameters on Windows, and a stub that fails with `Unavailable` elsewhere. |
| `std/ui/svg` | An SVG document of strokes and fills, parsed and rasterised to a coverage mask. |
| `std/ui/text` | Shaping, line breaking, measurement, the glyph atlas and icon masks. |
| `std/ui/scene` | Quads, shadows, underlines, sprites and filled or stroked vector paths with their clip rectangles and draw order, and batching by kind and atlas. |
| `std/ui/gpu` | The Vulkan renderer, its shaders, offscreen targets and swapchains, and the generated Vulkan bindings. |
| `std/ui/platform` | Windows, input events and the waiting loop: Wayland, xkbcommon and the generated Wayland bindings on Linux, user32 and kernel32 on Windows. |

- `std/ui/geometry`, `std/ui/layout`, `std/ui/font`, `std/ui/dwrite`, `std/ui/svg`, `std/ui/text`, `std/ui/scene` and, apart from `open` and `run`, `std/ui` only compute, so they run on Linux and Windows with no library besides `dwrite.dll` on Windows. All of them but `std/ui` also build for `browser/wasm32`, where `std/ui/dwrite` is the stub.
- `std/ui/gpu` and `std/ui/platform` hold one file per target for what differs, `surface_linux.low` and `surface_windows.low`, `wayland_linux.low` and `win32_windows.low`, behind one API (see Modules under Syntax). `std/ui/platform/window_browser.low` imports `std/sys`, so the loader rejects a `browser/wasm32` build of `std/ui/platform` and of every module that imports it, `std/ui` among them, before checking it.
- The generated bindings are private to the module that uses them. `std/ui/gpu/vulkan.low` holds the types and constants, and `vulkan_linux.low` and `vulkan_windows.low` the commands, linked from `libvulkan.so` and `vulkan-1.dll`. `std/ui/platform/wayland_linux.low` holds the Wayland bindings.
- A Linux program that opens a window links `libvulkan.so.1`, `libwayland-client.so.0`, `libxkbcommon.so.0` and libc. A Windows one imports `vulkan-1.dll`, `user32.dll`, `kernel32.dll` and `dwmapi.dll`.

### Generators

- `compiler/wlgen/protocols` holds the Wayland protocol XML: `wayland.xml`, `xdg-shell.xml`, `cursor-shape-v1.xml` and `text-input-unstable-v3.xml`. `low run compiler/wlgen -o std/ui/platform/wayland_linux.low compiler/wlgen/protocols/wayland.xml compiler/wlgen/protocols/xdg-shell.xml compiler/wlgen/protocols/cursor-shape-v1.xml compiler/wlgen/protocols/text-input-unstable-v3.xml` generates the Wayland bindings.
  - Every interface given in one run shares an `Interfaces` record of `wl_interface` tables that `interfaces()` builds in C memory. The record keeps every block it allocates, listener copies included, and `release_interfaces(ifs)` frees them once the connection is closed. An interface that a request or event creates must be declared by a given protocol, or generation fails. An object argument of an undeclared interface, such as the `zwp_tablet_tool_v2` that `cursor-shape-v1.xml` names, gets a null entry in its message type table.
  - A request becomes `interface_request(...)` over `wl_proxy_marshal_flags`, taking the `Interfaces` record when it creates an object of a known interface. `wl_registry_bind` takes the interface table and version.
  - Destructor requests pass `WL_MARSHAL_FLAG_DESTROY`, and an interface without a `destroy` request gets one that calls `wl_proxy_destroy`.
  - Objects are `ptr`, strings `ptr[u8]`, `fixed` an `i32`.
  - Events become an `extern struct` listener of `extern fn` fields, each defaulting to a generated function that ignores the event, so a listener names only the events it handles. `interface_add_listener(ifs, proxy, listener, data)` copies it into the record's C memory. Callbacks receive the listener's `data` pointer, a pinned Low object.
  - Enum entries are constants such as `wl_shm_format_argb8888: u32`. Names that clash with Low keywords or builtins gain a trailing `_`.
- `compiler/vkgen/registry` holds the Vulkan registry, `vk.xml.gz` from Vulkan-Docs v1.4.363, and `vulkan.list`, which selects extensions, commands, extra types such as `pNext` structures, and the library each target loads.
  - `low run compiler/vkgen -registry compiler/vkgen/registry/vk.xml.gz -select compiler/vkgen/registry/vulkan.list -o std/ui/gpu` writes `vulkan.low` and one `vulkan_<os>.low` per target. A `command` line that ends with a target name, such as `command vkCreateWaylandSurfaceKHR linux`, goes only to that target's file, and `library windows vulkan-1` names the library its `extern` block links.
  - Commands and every type they reach are generated, skipping Vulkan SC variants. Enum values come from the core enums, every Vulkan feature level and the selected extensions.
  - A handle is a one-field `extern struct`, `ptr` for dispatchable handles and `i64` for the others, so `VkBuffer {}` is the null handle and handles of different kinds do not mix.
  - Structures and unions become `extern struct`s whose fields all have defaults: zero, `null`, the empty handle, or for `sType` the structure's own value, so a literal names only what it sets. A union takes the layout of its widest member.
  - A fixed C array becomes numbered fields, `name_0` to `name_N`, with an accessor `Struct_name(s)` that returns them as a slice, or as a `str` for a character array.
  - Vulkan types map to `u32` (`uint32_t`, `VkBool32`, `VkFlags`, 32-bit flag bits), `i32` (`int32_t`, enums), `i64` (`uint64_t`, `VkDeviceSize`, `VkFlags64`, `size_t`), `u16`, `u8`, `f32` and `f64`. Platform types such as `HWND` and `wl_display*` are `ptr`.
  - Pointers to known types become `ptr[T]`; `void*`, function pointers and pointers to pointers become `ptr`. A command's single input or output through a pointer is a `mut` parameter; an array or optional pointer is a `ptr[T]`.
  - Enum entries and API constants are constants such as `VK_FORMAT_R8G8B8A8_UNORM: i32`, and `vk_make_api_version` builds version numbers.
- Both generators emit declarations without `pub`. `compiler/tests/vkgen` and `compiler/tests/wlgen` fail when the checked-in files differ from what the generators produce.
- `compiler/protogen` is the library behind `low protogen`, the driver of `std/protobuf/proto`: `low protogen -I testdata/run/std_protobuf_generated/protos -o testdata/run/std_protobuf_generated all.proto rich.proto items.proto kinds.proto` writes the modules that `testdata/run/std_protobuf_generated` compiles and compares with the generator's output. `testdata/run/std_grpc_generated` holds the generated client and server code of two services that its program serves and calls over `std/grpc`, and compares it with the generator's output. `compiler/tests/low` runs `low protogen` on those protos and requires the files it writes to equal the checked-in modules. `compiler/tests/protogen` checks that `proto.keywords` lists the lexer's keywords and builds the generated code of a proto whose names are keywords.
- `std/ui/gpu/shaders` holds the GLSL and its SPIR-V. `low run compiler/shadergen std/ui/gpu/shaders` compiles each stage with `glslc` to a `.spv` and records the SHA-256 sums of the sources and the `.spv` files in `shaders.sum`.

### Platform

`std/ui/platform` gives both targets one API:

- `open(Options) -> Window ! Error` creates a window of a logical size with a title. `Window` is droppable; releasing it destroys the window and closes the connection. A window belongs to the green thread that opened it. With `Options.hidden` the window is created and sized as usual but stays invisible until `show(w)`, which does nothing to a window that is already visible.
- `next(w, timeout_ms) -> Event[] ! Error` returns the events that arrived, waiting until at least one arrives, a `Waker` fires or the timeout passes. A negative timeout waits without limit. The first `next` after `open` returns a `Resized`.
- `waker(w) -> Waker` is a copyable handle; `wake(k)` makes the waiting `next`, or the next one, return a `Woken`. Each `wake` gives one `Woken`.
- `native(w) -> Native` gives the handles Vulkan needs to make a surface: the `wl_display` and `wl_surface` on Linux, the `HINSTANCE` and `HWND` on Windows.
- `Error` is `NoDisplay(detail)` when there is no compositor or no desktop, `Unsupported(detail)` when the windowing system lacks something the window needs, `Failed(call, code)` with the call's error code, `Disconnected`, and `BadIcon(detail)` when an icon frame is not a PNG of the size it states or the system cannot make an icon from it.
- `Event` is `Resized(width, height, scale)` with `f32` logical pixels and scale, `CloseRequested`, `PointerMoved(x, y)`, `PointerLeft`, `PointerDown(ButtonEvent)`, `PointerUp(ButtonEvent)`, `Scrolled(dx, dy, modifiers)`, `Key(KeyEvent)`, `Preedit(text, cursor)`, `Commit(text)`, `Maximized(bool)`, `FocusChanged(bool)`, `FileDragEntered(x, y, paths)`, `FileDragMoved(x, y)`, `FileDropped(x, y)`, `FileDragExited` and `Woken`. Pointer positions are logical pixels from the top left of the window. `Maximized` arrives only when the maximised state changes.
- A `ButtonEvent` carries the `Button` (`Left`, `Right`, `Middle`, `Navigate(Back)` and `Navigate(Forward)` for the side buttons, as gpui's `MouseButton`, and `Other(code)`), the position, the `Modifiers` and `clicks`, how many presses of that button in a row the press makes, counted by the platform as gpui's `ClickState` counts them (`platform/windows/window.rs:1013-1083`): a press of the same button within the double-click time of the last press and within the double-click distance of it on each axis counts on, any other starts again at one, and a release carries the count of its press. On Windows the time is `GetDoubleClickTime`, the distance `SM_CXDOUBLECLK` and `SM_CYDOUBLECLK` device pixels, read at each press, and the press time `GetMessageTime`; Shift and Control come from the message's key flags and Alt and Super from `GetKeyState`. On Wayland the time is 400 milliseconds and the distance 5 logical pixels, as gpui's `DOUBLE_CLICK_INTERVAL` and `DOUBLE_CLICK_DISTANCE`, measured from the button event's timestamp, and `BTN_SIDE` and `BTN_EXTRA` are back and forward.
- `clipboard(w) -> Clipboard` is a copyable handle to the system clipboard through the window. `write_clipboard(c, text) ! Error` replaces the clipboard's contents with `text`, and `read_clipboard(c) -> str? ! Error` returns its text, or `none` when it holds none.
- `set_text_input(w, caret: Caret?)` turns the system's input method on, with its candidate window beside `caret` (logical pixels from the top left of the content, so it can follow the text being edited), or off for `none`. It starts off, and setting it again moves the caret. While it is on, the input method's composition arrives as `Preedit(text, cursor)`, `cursor` being a `TextRange` of byte offsets into `text` or `none`, and empty text ending it, and its result as `Commit(text)`, which replaces the composition. Neither arrives while it is off. Keys the input method handles give no `Key` event; other keys still give `Key` events with their text.
- `set_cursor(w, Cursor)` sets the pointer's image while it is over the window's content. A window starts with `Cursor.Arrow`; `Hidden` shows no image.
- `Options.titlebar` is a `TitlebarOptions`, as gpui's `TitlebarOptions` is (`platform.rs:1248`); its `appears_transparent` hides the system title bar so the program draws its own, and it defaults to `false`. The platform draws no caption buttons.
- `Options.owner` is a `WindowHandle?`, the copyable view that `Window.handle` holds, because a `Window` is droppable and cannot sit in an optional; opening fails with `Unsupported` when the owner has closed or belongs to another `Display`. `Options.min_width` and `Options.min_height` are the smallest content size in logical pixels, zero for no limit. `Options.placement` is a `Placement`: `System` leaves the position to the system, `CentreOnOwner` centres on the owner and keeps the window inside the work area of the owner's monitor, and `CentreOnScreen` centres on the work area of the monitor that holds the first window of the window's `Display` that is still open; with no owner `CentreOnOwner` centres on that window, and the first window of a display is centred on the primary monitor's work area. The size that is centred is the size after the minimum. `Options.taskbar` is `true` by default and gives the window its own taskbar button. `Options.activate` is `true` by default; `false` shows the window with `SW_SHOWNOACTIVATE`, so it takes neither the keyboard focus nor the foreground. `Options.border` is a `u32?`, the colour of the window's one-pixel border as `0xRRGGBB`, and `none` keeps the system's; it is a plain integer because `platform` imports no geometry. `Options.icon` is an `IconFrame[]?`, the frames of the window's icon, and `none` keeps the system's. An `IconFrame` is a square picture at one pixel size: `size`, its width and height in pixels, and `png`, the bytes of a PNG file as a `str`, which is what `embed` returns, so a program writes `IconFrame { size: 32, png: embed("icons/32.png") }` for each of its sizes. `set_icon(w, frames) ! Error` changes the icon while the window is open and `set_title(w, title)` changes the title. The Wayland backend ignores `owner`, `min_width`, `min_height`, `placement`, `taskbar`, `activate`, `border` and `icon`.
- `set_hit_test(w, f)` sets `f(x, y) -> WindowControlArea?`, which answers what lies at a point of the content in logical pixels. `WindowControlArea` is `Drag`, `Close`, `Max` or `Min`, as gpui's is (`window.rs:479`). The window asks it where the system asks for a hit test, so the program marks the parts of its own title bar. A window with no `f` has no areas.
- `prefers_reduced_motion() -> bool` needs no window: it reports `SPI_GETCLIENTAREAANIMATION` turned off on Windows, and on Linux the GNOME `enable-animations` setting turned off, read with `portal.animations_enabled` from `std/portal` with a 200 millisecond limit, and `false` where the system does not say.
- `controls(w) -> Controls` is a copyable handle for code that does not own the window. `is_maximized(c)` reports the maximised state, `minimize(c)` minimises the window and `toggle_maximize(c)` maximises it or restores it, followed by a `Maximized` event.
- A `KeyEvent` carries a `Key` (`Named(NamedKey)` for Enter, Escape, Tab, Backspace, Delete, the arrows, Home, End, Page Up, Page Down, Insert, Pause, Break, Menu and F1 to F12, or `Character(str)`, the key's text with at most Shift applied), the text it produces with the modifiers held, the `Modifiers` (`shift`, `control`, `alt`, `super`), whether it is a press or a release, and whether it repeats. Control+A is `Character("a")` with empty text. Modifier keys, and keys with neither a name nor text, give no event.
- The scale is the Wayland surface's preferred buffer scale, a whole number, or the window's DPI divided by 96 on Windows, which is 1.25 at 125 percent and 2.25 at 216 DPI. A window's logical size is its client size in device pixels divided by the scale, so it is fractional where the scale does not divide the pixel size.
- A `Gpu` made with `gpu.open_for` draws into the window, so it is released before the `Window`.

Wayland:

- `open` connects to the display, binds `wl_compositor`, `xdg_wm_base` and `wl_seat` from the registry, and also `wl_data_device_manager` (up to version 3), `wp_cursor_shape_manager_v1` and `zwp_text_input_manager_v3` when the compositor offers them. It makes a surface with an `xdg_toplevel` and returns once the first `configure` has been acknowledged, failing with `Unsupported` when none arrives within five seconds. The surface has no buffer until the first `present`, which is when the compositor maps it, so `Options.hidden` and `show` change nothing. The window has no decorations, so `TitlebarOptions` changes nothing, `set_hit_test` is never asked and the program moves and closes the window only through what it draws and handles itself.
- Listener callbacks run during `wl_display_dispatch_pending` on the green thread that called `next` and append to the window's event queue, a pinned Low object. A failure inside a callback is kept and returned by the next `next`.
- `xdg_wm_base.ping` is answered inside the callback, `xdg_toplevel.close` becomes `CloseRequested`, a `configure` with a non-zero size becomes `Resized`, and `wl_surface.preferred_buffer_scale` sets the scale. A `configure` also carries the toplevel's states, and a change of whether they hold `maximized` becomes `Maximized` when the `configure` is applied. `minimize(c)` is `xdg_toplevel.set_minimized` and `toggle_maximize(c)` is `set_maximized` or `unset_maximized`, each flushed at once.
- `set_cursor` keeps the cursor in the window and, while the pointer is over the surface, shows it through `wp_cursor_shape_device_v1.set_shape` with the serial of the last pointer `enter`. Every `Cursor` is one shape: `Arrow` is `default`, `Text` `text`, `Pointer` `pointer`, `Crosshair` `crosshair`, `Grab` `grab`, `Grabbing` `grabbing`, `NotAllowed` `not_allowed`, `Move` `move`, `Wait` `wait`, `Progress` `progress`, `Help` `help`, `ResizeLeftRight` `ew_resize`, `ResizeUpDown` `ns_resize`, `ResizeUpLeftDownRight` `nwse_resize` and `ResizeUpRightDownLeft` `nesw_resize`. `Hidden` is `wl_pointer.set_cursor` with no surface. The cursor starts as `Arrow`, and the current one is shown on every `enter`, so a cursor set while the pointer is outside takes effect on the next one. Without `wp_cursor_shape_manager_v1` only `Hidden` takes effect.
- The clipboard is the `wl_data_device` selection of the seat. `write_clipboard` makes a `wl_data_source` that offers `text/plain;charset=utf-8` and `text/plain`, sets it as the selection with the serial of the latest key, button or keyboard `enter`, and keeps the text; each `send` event writes the text to the descriptor it carries and closes it, and `cancelled` drops the source. `read_clipboard` returns the kept text while the window owns the selection. Otherwise it asks the selection offer, which prefers `text/plain;charset=utf-8` over `text/plain`, to `receive` into a pipe and reads until end of input, stopping after a second without data; it gives `none` when the text is empty, the selection offers neither type or the window has not received one, which a compositor sends only to the focused client. `write_clipboard` fails with `Unsupported` without `wl_data_device_manager`, and both fail with `Disconnected` once the window is closed.
- `set_text_input` uses `zwp_text_input_v3`. The caret is kept, and while the surface has text-input focus (`enter`) a caret enables the input method with the normal content type and its cursor rectangle in a `commit`, and `none` disables it. `preedit_string` and `commit_string` fill pending state that `done` turns into events: `Commit` first when there is committed text, then `Preedit` when the composition is not empty or ended one, with the cursor range from `cursor_begin` and `cursor_end` or `none` when either is negative. Text input focus lost, or turned off with a composition showing, gives `Preedit("", none)`. A `done` while text input is off gives no event. Without `zwp_text_input_manager_v3` text input does nothing.
- Files dragged from another program are not supported: the data device's drag offers are not handled, so no `FileDrag*` event is sent. A drag started by `on_drag` inside the window works.
- The keymap arrives as a file descriptor, which is read with `std/sys` and compiled with `xkb_keymap_new_from_string`. Keys are translated with `xkb_state_key_get_one_sym` and `xkb_state_key_get_utf8`, and modifiers follow `xkb_state_update_mask`; a pointer button event and a `Scrolled` event carry the modifiers the last `wl_keyboard.modifiers` reported. Control with Pause gives the `Break` keysym, which is `Break`, and the `Menu` keysym is `Menu`. libxkbcommon is bound by hand in `xkb_linux.low`.
- Key repeat follows the compositor's `repeat_info`: a held key that produces text or is a named key repeats from a timer inside `next`.
- `next` waits on the display's file descriptor without blocking an OS thread. A green thread started by `open` waits with `wait_readable(fd)` each time `next` arms it and reports readiness on a channel; `next` prepares the read, then `select`s on that channel, the waker's channel and an `after` arm for the timeout, the next key repeat or 60 seconds, cancelling the read when anything else wins. Only one green thread may wait on a descriptor, so a flush that would block retries every millisecond for up to five seconds.
- A `Display` on Wayland holds the windows opened in it, one channel that their reading threads and wakers signal, and the waker that `waker_of` returns; each window keeps its own connection. `wait` drops the windows that have closed, then flushes and dispatches each open window and returns when one has events, a wake or a readable descriptor. Otherwise it arms the reading thread of every window and `select`s on the display's channel, its waker's channel and an `after` arm for the timeout, which is negative to wait without limit. `waker_of(d)` gives no `Woken` to any window, so its wake ends `wait` whether or not any window is open; a wake that arrives while no one waits ends the next `wait`, and wakes that arrive together end one. Releasing the display closes none of its windows, and its `open_in_on` and `wait_on` then fail with `Unsupported`.
- A broken or reset connection gives `Disconnected`. The generated bindings' C memory is freed when the window is released.

Win32:

- On Windows a `Display` holds what belongs to the connection and not to one window: `open_display` locks the green thread to its OS thread with `sync.lock_thread`, because a window's messages reach only the thread that created it, sets per-monitor DPI awareness, registers the `LowDisplay` class of its message-only window, calls `OleInitialize` and creates the message-only window (`HWND_MESSAGE`). Each window registers the `LowWindow` class before it is created. Releasing the display destroys the message-only window, attempts `UnregisterClassW` for both classes, calls `OleUninitialize` and unlocks the thread once; the unregistration fails and is ignored while a window of the class exists, so releasing one display never stops another from opening a window. `Display` and `Window` are droppable, so each has a public copyable `handle` and an `_on` form of every operation, as `std/file` and `std/net` have; `DisplayHandle` is the view of a `Display`, and once the display is released its `open_in_on` and `wait_on` fail with `Unsupported` and a waker from `waker_of_on` does nothing. `open_in(d, options)` opens a window in `d` and releasing it leaves `d` open; a window must be released before its display. `open(options)` makes a display, opens one window in it and gives the window the display, so releasing the window closes both. `wait(d, timeout_ms) ! Error` dispatches the thread's messages with `PeekMessageW` and returns when a window of `d` has events or a fault for `next`, when a wake of `waker_of(d)` has arrived, or after the timeout, which is negative to wait without limit; it waits in `MsgWaitForMultipleObjectsEx` and takes no event out of any window. `waker_of(d) -> Waker` posts `WM_APP` to the message-only window, whose procedure counts the wake on the display and gives no `Woken` to any window, so the wake ends `wait` whether or not any window is open; a wake that arrives while no one waits ends the next `wait`, and wakes that arrive together end one. `next` pumps through the window's display, and each pump clears the pending key of every window of the display.
- `open` creates the window with `CreateWindowExW`, its title converted to UTF-16. A creation error of 0 or access denied gives `NoDisplay`. A window opened with `hidden` is cloaked with `DwmSetWindowAttribute(hwnd, DWMWA_CLOAK, TRUE)` before `ShowWindow`, so the system keeps a sized and composed window that no one sees and a Vulkan swapchain made for it works as for any window; `show` clears the attribute. If cloaking fails, `open` leaves `ShowWindow` to `show`. A failed uncloak is returned by the next `next`. `DwmGetWindowAttribute` with `DWMWA_CLOAKED` reports the cloak as `DWM_CLOAKED_APP`. A window opened with `Options.border` is given `DwmSetWindowAttribute(hwnd, DWMWA_BORDER_COLOR, &colorref, 4)` right after `CreateWindowExW` returns, `colorref` being the `0xRRGGBB` colour with its red and blue bytes swapped into `0x00BBGGRR`; Windows 11 draws it as the window's border, Windows 10 has no border colour and fails the call, and `open` ignores the result either way. Every frame given to `Options.icon` or `set_icon` is checked first, on every platform, in `icon.low`: `size` must be positive, `png` must be at least 24 bytes that start with the eight-byte PNG signature and a chunk named `IHDR`, and the width and the height in that header must both equal `size`; the first frame that is not fails the call with `BadIcon(detail)` naming its index, and changes nothing.
