On Windows `set_icon` takes `GetSystemMetricsForDpi(SM_CXICON, dpi)` and `GetSystemMetricsForDpi(SM_CXSMICON, dpi)` for the window's `GetDpiForWindow`, picks for each the smallest frame at least that size, the first of equals, or the biggest frame when none is (`pick_icon_frame`), and makes an `HICON` of each picked frame with `CreateIconFromResourceEx(png, len, TRUE, 0x00030000, size, size, 0)`, which accepts PNG data; when it returns null, which it does for a PNG whose body is damaged, the call destroys any icon it made, fails with `BadIcon` and leaves the window's icons alone. Otherwise it sends `WM_SETICON` with `ICON_BIG` and then `ICON_SMALL` and destroys the two icons the window had, so the window never has an icon that is destroyed. The window keeps its frames and the index it picked for each size, and `WM_DPICHANGED` picks again and replaces the icons only when an index changes, ignoring a failure. No frames send `WM_SETICON` with a null icon for both sizes, which restores the system's icon. `Options.icon` is applied right after `CreateWindowExW` returns and its failure is ignored, as the border's is. The window destroys the icons it owns when it closes, whether by `close_on`, by being released or by the system destroying it with its owner, and `set_icon` fails with `Unsupported` once it has closed. The Wayland backend checks the frames and ignores them.
- The window procedure is a Low callback. It reaches the window's state through `GWLP_USERDATA`, set from `lpCreateParams` in `WM_NCCREATE`, and appends events for `WM_SIZE`, `WM_DPICHANGED`, `WM_CLOSE`, `WM_MOUSEMOVE`, `WM_MOUSELEAVE`, the button messages, `WM_MOUSEWHEEL`, `WM_MOUSEHWHEEL`, `WM_KEYDOWN`, `WM_KEYUP`, `WM_SYSKEYDOWN`, `WM_SYSKEYUP`, `WM_CHAR`, `WM_SYSCHAR`, `WM_SETFOCUS` and `WM_KILLFOCUS`. A failed call inside it is kept and returned by the next `next`.
- `open` queues a `Resized` for the first `next`, and a `WM_SIZE` that repeats the last size or minimises the window gives none. A `WM_SIZE` queues `Maximized` when `IsZoomed` differs from the state kept.
- A window with an owner is created with the owner's `HWND` as `hWndParent` and `WS_OVERLAPPEDWINDOW`, which makes an owned top-level window: it stays above its owner, minimises with it and is destroyed with it, and no window ever calls `EnableWindow` on its owner. `taskbar: true` sets `WS_EX_APPWINDOW`, so an owned window has its own taskbar button; `taskbar: false` sets `WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW` on a window with no owner and nothing on an owned one. The extended style is kept on the window and passed to every `AdjustWindowRectExForDpi`, because a tool window's caption is smaller. An owner that has closed fails `open_in` with `Unsupported`. When the window is placed, it is created at the top left of the target work area, so it starts on the monitor it will be centred on and reads that monitor's DPI, and `fit_window` then sets its size and the centred position in one `SetWindowPos`, clamped into the work area with the left and top edge first. The owner's `GetWindowRect` gives the centre for `CentreOnOwner` unless the owner is minimised, which centres on the work area.
- `WM_GETMINMAXINFO` sets `ptMinTrackSize` to the minimum content size times the window's scale, plus the frame from `AdjustWindowRectExForDpi` for the dpi of that scale, or plus `frame_insets` with `appears_transparent`, for each of `min_width` and `min_height` that is above zero. A change of scale from `WM_DPICHANGED` takes effect at the next message. `set_title` calls `SetWindowTextW`, and a failure is returned by the next `next`. `WM_NCDESTROY` clears the window's handle, so a window that the system destroys with its owner is released without a `DestroyWindow` call, and its anchor is unpinned.
- With `appears_transparent`, the window is created with the usual overlapped style and `fit_window` adds the frame insets to the requested size, so the content is the requested size, calling `SetWindowPos` with `SWP_FRAMECHANGED` instead of `AdjustWindowRectExForDpi`. `WM_NCCALCSIZE` insets the client area as gpui's `handle_calc_client_size` does (`events.rs:699-718`, insets from `get_client_area_insets`, `1494-1534`): a restored window loses the resize frame, `GetSystemMetricsForDpi(SM_CXSIZEFRAME)` or `SM_CYSIZEFRAME` plus `SM_CXPADDEDBORDER`, from its left, right and bottom, and nothing from its top on Windows 10 or one device pixel on Windows 11 (build 22000 or later, read once with `RtlGetVersion`), the one row the system paints as the top border at every scale, so the resize borders lie in the invisible frame outside the content. A maximised window loses the frame on all four sides, the part of it the system hangs off the monitor. A window with the system title bar leaves `WM_NCCALCSIZE` to `DefWindowProcW`.
- `WM_NCHITTEST` converts the screen point with `ScreenToClient` and answers in this order, as gpui's `handle_hit_test_msg` does (`events.rs:856-890`): the area `set_hit_test` gives, `Drag` as `HTCAPTION`, `Close` as `HTCLOSE`, `Max` as `HTMAXBUTTON` and `Min` as `HTMINBUTTON`; then `DefWindowProcW`, whose resize border answers (`HTLEFT`, `HTTOPLEFT` and the like, or `HTNOWHERE`) are returned as they are; then `HTTOP` within `SM_CYSIZEFRAME` of the top of the content when the window is not maximised; then `HTCLIENT`. An area therefore takes priority over the resize borders that lie in the content, as it does in gpui. `HTMAXBUTTON` is what makes Windows 11 show its snap layouts over the `Max` area. A window with the system title bar returns the area where one answers and otherwise what `DefWindowProcW` returns.
- `WM_NCLBUTTONDOWN` and `WM_NCLBUTTONDBLCLK` over `HTCLOSE`, `HTMAXBUTTON` and `HTMINBUTTON` are taken and remembered when the window has no system title bar, or when the last hit test answered that code from an area, and `WM_NCLBUTTONUP` over the same code runs the action, `WM_CLOSE` posted, `toggle_maximize` or `minimize` through `ShowWindowAsync`, as gpui's handlers do (`events.rs:63`, `949-998`, `1000-1065`); a release over another code, or none pressed, runs nothing. The press is forgotten by `WM_LBUTTONUP`, `WM_NCMOUSELEAVE`, `WM_CAPTURECHANGED` and the release itself, so a release elsewhere followed by an unpaired `WM_NCLBUTTONUP` closes nothing. Other messages go to `DefWindowProcW`, so `HTCAPTION` drags and double-clicks as the system does, and the system's own caption buttons of a titled window keep their default handling. With the title bar hidden, `WM_NCMOUSEMOVE` gives `PointerMoved` in content coordinates, requesting leave tracking with `TME_LEAVE | TME_NONCLIENT` where `WM_MOUSEMOVE` requests `TME_LEAVE`, and `WM_NCMOUSELEAVE` gives `PointerLeft`, and also reaches `DefWindowProcW` when the window has its system title bar; the caption buttons themselves give no pointer event for a click. A `WM_SIZE` that minimises the window leaves the maximised state as it was.
- Pointer leave is requested with `TrackMouseEvent` on the first move, and `PointerLeft` comes only when it was requested. A press captures the pointer until every button is up. The extra buttons are `Other(4)` and `Other(5)`. One wheel notch scrolls 48 logical pixels. A `Scrolled` event takes its Shift and Control from the wheel message's key flags and its Alt and Super from `GetKeyState`, as a button event does.
- Virtual-key codes map to `Key`, with `ToUnicode` giving the character with only Shift applied. Control with Pause arrives as `VK_CANCEL`, which is `Break`, and `VK_APPS` is `Menu`. The text of a press comes from the `WM_CHAR` or `WM_SYSCHAR` that follows it in the same drain, with surrogate pairs joined.
- `next` drains the thread's message queue with `PeekMessageW`, which dispatches the messages of every window on the thread to its own window's queue, and when nothing is pending waits in `MsgWaitForMultipleObjectsEx` with the timeout. The OS thread is in a C call while it waits, so other green threads keep running. A `Waker` of a window posts `WM_APP` to the window, and `WM_NCDESTROY` and releasing the window detach it; a window destroyed by the system clears its handle, so releasing it destroys nothing twice.
- The clipboard holds `CF_UNICODETEXT`. `write_clipboard` converts the text to UTF-16, with a surrogate pair for each code point above U+FFFF and a terminating zero, into a `GMEM_MOVEABLE` block and opens the clipboard with the window as its owner, trying every 5 ms up to 20 times before failing with `Failed("OpenClipboard", code)`. It empties the clipboard and gives the block to `SetClipboardData`, which then owns it; the block is freed when any step fails, and the clipboard is closed on every path. A text that holds a zero ends there. `read_clipboard` converts the block back to UTF-8 up to its first zero within `GlobalSize`; a clipboard with no `CF_UNICODETEXT`, which Windows also synthesises from `CF_TEXT`, gives `none`. A released window gives `Disconnected`.
- Text input is off by default: `open` detaches the input context with `ImmAssociateContextEx(hwnd, null, 0)`. `set_text_input` with a caret attaches the default context with `IACE_DEFAULT`, and `none` detaches it again and, when a composition was showing, queues `Preedit("", none)`. Each time the caret is set, and at `WM_IME_STARTCOMPOSITION`, `WM_SETFOCUS` and a DPI change, it is placed in device pixels: `ImmSetCompositionWindow` puts the composition at the caret's bottom left (`CFS_POINT`) and `ImmSetCandidateWindow` keeps the candidate window off the caret's rectangle (`CFS_EXCLUDE`).
  - `WM_IME_STARTCOMPOSITION`, `WM_IME_COMPOSITION` and `WM_IME_ENDCOMPOSITION` are handled without `DefWindowProcW`, so the system draws no composition window and does not turn the result into `WM_IME_CHAR` and `WM_CHAR`; `WM_IME_NOTIFY` still reaches it, so the candidate window shows. All three are ignored while text input is off.
  - `GCS_RESULTSTR` becomes `Commit`, which also clears the composition. `GCS_COMPSTR` becomes `Preedit`, with a collapsed `TextRange` at the `GCS_CURSORPOS` offset converted from UTF-16 units to UTF-8 bytes, or `none` when the input method gives no position. An empty composition string and `WM_IME_ENDCOMPOSITION` give `Preedit("", none)` once.
  - `WM_CHAR` never gives a `Commit`: its text joins the press before it as `KeyEvent.text`, with or without text input. A key the input method handles arrives as `VK_PROCESSKEY`, which gives no `Key` event, so its `WM_CHAR` has no press to join and is dropped.
- `set_cursor` maps each `Cursor` to a stock cursor from `LoadCursorW`: `Arrow` to `IDC_ARROW`, `Text` to `IDC_IBEAM`, `Pointer`, `Grab` and `Grabbing` to `IDC_HAND` (Windows has no open or closed hand), `Crosshair` to `IDC_CROSS`, `NotAllowed` to `IDC_NO`, `Move` to `IDC_SIZEALL`, `Wait` to `IDC_WAIT`, `Progress` to `IDC_APPSTARTING`, `Help` to `IDC_HELP`, `ResizeLeftRight` to `IDC_SIZEWE`, `ResizeUpDown` to `IDC_SIZENS`, `ResizeUpLeftDownRight` to `IDC_SIZENWSE` and `ResizeUpRightDownLeft` to `IDC_SIZENESW`; `Hidden` is `SetCursor(null)`.
  - `WM_SETCURSOR` applies the window's cursor and returns `TRUE` when the hit test is the client area, and leaves the others to `DefWindowProcW`. `set_cursor` also calls `SetCursor` at once while pointer-leave tracking is active, which is from the first `WM_MOUSEMOVE` until `WM_MOUSELEAVE`; otherwise the next `WM_SETCURSOR` applies it.
- Files dragged from the shell arrive through an `IDropTarget` that a new window registers with `RegisterDragDrop` (`platform/windows/window.rs:1237-1249`) after its display's `OleInitialize` (`platform/windows/platform.rs:93`), and releasing the window revokes it with `RevokeDragDrop` (`window.rs:510`) before its display's `OleUninitialize`. The target is a Low object whose vtable holds callbacks, written as the `IDropTarget` of `window.rs:880-1010` is; the window's state is reached through the object. Its `QueryInterface` answers `IUnknown` and `IDropTarget`, comparing all 16 bytes of the interface id, and returns `E_POINTER` for a null out pointer. `DragEnter` asks the data object for `CF_HDROP` with `QueryGetData` and `GetData`, reads the paths with `DragQueryFileW`, releases the medium with `ReleaseStgMedium` and gives `FileDragEntered`; `DragOver` gives `FileDragMoved`, `Drop` gives `FileDropped` and `DragLeave` gives `FileDragExited`. The point each call receives is in screen pixels, which `ScreenToClient` and the window's scale turn into logical pixels from the top left of the window. Each call is also forwarded to an `IDropTargetHelper` made with `CoCreateInstance` of `CLSID_DragDropHelper` (`platform.rs:143`, `window.rs:931-934`, `948-951`, `971`, `990-993`) and released on revoke, so the shell's drag image shows over the window; a window whose helper cannot be made works without the image. A drag with files answers the first of `DROPEFFECT_COPY`, `DROPEFFECT_MOVE` and `DROPEFFECT_LINK` that the source allows in the `*pdweffect` it passes in, where gpui always answers copy; with none allowed, or with no files, it answers `DROPEFFECT_NONE`, and a drag without files gives no event. A drag whose mouse capture is lost without a release (`WM_CAPTURECHANGED`) is not ended: the platform gives no event for it, and the next release ends it.

### Renderer

- `gpu.open() -> Gpu ! Error` creates a Vulkan 1.3 instance and device on the first physical device that offers dynamic rendering, synchronization2 and a graphics queue. `gpu.open_for(Surface) -> Gpu ! Error` also needs `VK_KHR_swapchain` and a queue that presents to the window, and owns the `VkSurfaceKHR` it makes from the `Surface`'s display and window handles. `gpu.adapter(Gpu) -> str` names the physical device it chose. `Gpu` is droppable: it waits for the device to go idle and destroys everything it made. A `Target` made from it is droppable too and must be released first, which `using` does when it is bound after the `Gpu`.
- A `Gpu` is one `Device` with one `Presenter`. `gpu.open_device(first: Surface) -> Device ! Error` opens the first Vulkan 1.3 device that offers `VK_KHR_swapchain` and a graphics queue that presents to `first`, then destroys the probe surface it made, so the `Device` keeps no window. `gpu.presenter(d, Surface) -> Presenter ! Error` makes the `VkSurfaceKHR` and swapchain state of one window and fails with `NoDevice` when the queue family of `d` cannot present to that surface. `gpu.present_to(p, scene, mut atlases)` draws into the next image of `p`'s swapchain on the `Device` that made `p`. A `Device` shares one frame ring, one set of pipelines keyed by surface format and one texture per atlas between its presenters and targets, so rows uploaded by `present_to` with one presenter are sampled by the next draw with any other. A `Device` keeps its live presenters and targets. Releasing a `Presenter` waits for the device to go idle and destroys its swapchain and surface, and must come before its window is released; a window takes one `Presenter`, and on Windows the `present_to` of a second one fails with `VK_ERROR_NATIVE_WINDOW_IN_USE_KHR`. Releasing the `Device` first destroys every presenter's swapchain and surface and every target's image, so a later `Presenter` or `Target` release does nothing and `present_to` with a `Presenter` fails with `NoDevice`. Releasing a `Gpu` releases its presenter and then its device, and every `Target` still alive with it. A `Target` drawn to or read through a different `Gpu` or `Device` fails with `WrongDevice`.
- `gpu.Error` is `NoDevice`, `Vulkan(call, result)` for a failed call and its `VkResult`, `NoMemoryType(allowed, flags)` when no memory type matches, `InvalidSize(width, height)` for a target smaller than one pixel, and `NoSurface` for `present` on a `Gpu` without a window. `describe(e)` gives it as text.
- A `Gpu` or `Device` keeps one texture for each `scene.Atlas` of the list it was last given, which mirrors that atlas, and makes a texture again when its atlas changes size and drops the textures past the end of the list. A sprite names the atlas it samples by its index in the list, and a sprite whose index is negative or not in the list fails the draw with `InvalidTexture` before anything is written. Each atlas uploads its own dirty rows, and `render` and `present` clean every atlas of the list once the frame is submitted.
- It holds seven pipelines, one per primitive kind, in the order of `scene.kinds()`, and an eighth that composites rasterised paths, sharing one descriptor set layout (the instance storage buffer at binding 0, an atlas texture and its linear, edge-clamped sampler at binding 1) and one pipeline layout whose push constants are the target size in pixels at offset 0 and the scene's `TextParams` at offset 16, 48 bytes in all. The subpixel pipeline needs dual-source blending, which `supports_subpixel(g)` reports; without it the subpixel kind draws with the sprite shader. Each frame in flight, two of them, owns a command buffer, a fence, its semaphores, one host-visible instance buffer per kind that grows by doubling and one more for the path sprites, each with a descriptor set for every atlas texture the kind samples, and one for the kinds that sample none; the path sprites' set binds the resolved path image at binding 1. The sets come from a list of descriptor pools that grows by one pool when the last is full.
- Drawing a scene writes each kind's instances into its buffer in draw order, uploads each atlas's dirty rows through a staging buffer, and records one `vkCmdDraw` of six vertices per batch, with `firstInstance` at the batch's first instance and the descriptor set of the batch's texture bound, inside one dynamic rendering pass that clears to the scene's background. A batch of paths ends that pass, rasterises its triangles and composites them, then resumes the pass with the target's contents loaded: the `Paths` buffer holds the vertices of every path, three to a triangle, and the batch is drawn with `vkCmdDraw` over its range of them into a four-sample `R8G8B8A8_UNORM` image whose render area is the batch's clipped bounds rounded out to whole pixels; the samples are averaged into a single-sample image, which a barrier makes readable and which the main pass then copies into the target through one `vkCmdDraw` of six vertices per path sprite, a rectangle that is read with `texelFetch` at the fragment's pixel. When every path of the batch has one draw order the sprites are the paths' own clipped bounds, which are disjoint; otherwise there is one sprite over their union, so a pixel several paths cover is composited once. Each `Device` makes the two path images on the first batch, grows them when a batch needs more, to a multiple of 256 pixels on each side, after waiting for the device to go idle, and keeps them until it is released, so a path drawn into a target or window of any size reuses them.
- `target(g, width, height)` makes an offscreen `Target`, an `R8G8B8A8_UNORM` image. `render(g, t, scene, mut atlases)` submits a frame and returns, and `read(g, t)` waits for the queue to go idle and returns the pixels as RGBA bytes.
- `present(g, scene, mut atlases)` draws into the next image of the `Gpu`'s swapchain and queues it. `present_to` does the same for one `Presenter` of a `Device`; presents run one after another on one queue, each waiting up to thirty seconds to acquire an image, so a window that stops consuming frames stalls the others. A draw that fails after its image is acquired submits an empty batch that waits on the acquire semaphore and marks the swapchain for rebuilding, so the frame slot can be reused. The swapchain has a `B8G8R8A8_UNORM` or `R8G8B8A8_UNORM` format and FIFO presentation. It is made on the first call at the scene's size in device pixels, or at the surface's current extent when the surface fixes one, and made again when that size changes or acquiring or presenting reports `VK_ERROR_OUT_OF_DATE_KHR` or `VK_SUBOPTIMAL_KHR`. Each frame in flight has its own acquire semaphore and each image its own render-finished semaphore. A scene of no pixels, or a minimised window, presents nothing.
- Colours are sRGB-encoded and blended in that space with premultiplied-free `SRC_ALPHA, ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA` blending. The path pipelines write premultiplied colour and blend with `ONE, ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA`, both into the rasterised image and from the resolved image into the target, as gpui's path pipelines do.
- C structures that Vulkan reads through a pointer are pinned heap values kept by a droppable `Pins` list for the duration of the call or the frame.
- The shaders:
  - Quads have per-corner radii and per-edge border widths. The fragment shader reads the pixel centre from `gl_FragCoord`, returns the background where no border or corner reaches it, and otherwise computes the signed distance to the outer edge and an approximate one to the inner edge, a circle where the two border widths of a corner agree and a quarter ellipse where they differ. It blends the border colour over the background with the straight-alpha `over` operator, mixes by the inner distance, and multiplies the alpha by the outer distance covering one pixel of anti-aliasing.
  - Shadows are a rounded rectangle blurred by a Gaussian of the given sigma, integrated in closed form along x with a polynomial approximation of the error function and sampled four times along y, and drawn over the rectangle grown by three sigma. A shadow without blur is the rounded rectangle's signed distance covering one pixel.
  - Underlines are the rectangle of their bounds in one colour; a wavy one is drawn as gpui's `underline_fragment` does (`shaders.hlsl:1108-1130`). With the height of the bounds as the unit, the stroke follows `sin(x * frequency) * amplitude` about the middle row, where `frequency` is `PI * 2 * thickness / height` and `amplitude` is `thickness * 0.8 / height`, and the alpha is `saturate(0.5 - max(-d_bottom, d_top))` for the distances to the stroke's two borders, measured along the normal of the curve and converted to pixels.
  - Sprites sample the first byte of the `R8G8B8A8` atlas as coverage for a monochrome glyph tinted with the sprite's colour. When the scene's `TextParams` are not all zero the coverage first goes through DirectWrite's gamma-ratio correction with the grey contrast, as the reference renderer does for grey glyphs.
  - Subpixel sprites sample the first three bytes as one coverage per colour stripe, in blue to red order when `TextParams.bgr` is set. The fragment shader returns the colour with the correction applied per stripe, using the subpixel contrast and the colour's own brightness, as the second blend source, and the blend is `SRC1_COLOR, ONE_MINUS_SRC1_COLOR` over the target's red, green and blue channels.
  - Polychrome sprites sample all four bytes of the `R8G8B8A8` atlas as a colour with straight alpha, with the sampler's linear filtering, as gpui's `polychrome_sprite_fragment` does (`shaders.hlsl:1210-1270`). The fragment shader optionally replaces the colour with its luminance (`0.2126, 0.7152, 0.0722`), multiplies the alpha by the sprite's opacity and by `saturate(0.5 - quad_sdf)` over the sprite's rounded bounds, and the blend is the same `SRC_ALPHA, ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA` as for the other sprites and gpui's polychrome blend state (`directx_renderer.rs:1512-1527`); gpui's decoded images are straight alpha too (`img.rs:678-712`).
  - Paths are rasterised from triangles whose vertices carry a position, the curve coordinates `st`, the path's colour and a clip rectangle, the path's bounds cut to the clip it was pushed under, 48 bytes in the renderer and 16 in the scene. A solid triangle has `st` of `(0, 1)` and every pixel inside it is covered. A curve triangle has `st` of `(0, 0)`, `(0.5, 0)` and `(1, 1)`, and the fragment shader computes `f = s * s - t`, the screen-space gradient of `f` with `dFdx` and `dFdy`, and the alpha `saturate(0.5 - f / length(gradient))`, the Loop-Blinn coverage of gpui's `path_rasterization_fragment` (`shaders.wgsl`); a triangle whose `s` changes by less than `0.001` per pixel is solid. The fragment shader outputs the colour multiplied by that alpha and by the path's own alpha, premultiplied, and gives zero alpha to a fragment whose pixel centre is outside the vertex's clip rectangle, which it receives as four distances to the sides interpolated across the triangle. The path sprite fragment shader returns the resolved texel at `gl_FragCoord`.
  - Every other instance carries a clip rectangle, and fragments outside it are discarded by alpha.

### Scene

- A `Scene` holds `Quad`, `Shadow`, `Path`, `Underline`, `Sprite`, `SubpixelSprite` and `PolychromeSprite` instances in device pixels, each with its clip rectangle and its draw order, and each sprite with the index of the atlas it samples, and the `TextParams` the glyph shaders read: four gamma coefficients, the grey and subpixel contrasts, and the stripe order. All zero is the identity correction.
- `push_clip` and `pop_clip` keep a stack of clip rectangles; each primitive takes the intersection of the stack.
- A primitive's draw order is one more than the greatest draw order among the earlier primitives whose bounds, cut to the clip, it overlaps, or 1 when it overlaps none; a primitive whose cut bounds are empty is dropped. Touching edges do not overlap. A shadow's bounds are those before blurring. A grid of 64-pixel cells holds the placed bounds, so a push tests only the primitives in the cells it covers. A quad with a transparent background is split into the four strips around its interior (the quad inset by one pixel and by the larger of each border width and corner radius, taking the larger of the two bands that leave the corners out), and each strip is placed on its own, so a primitive pushed inside the border draws below it; a quad whose interior is empty stays whole.
- Each list is kept sorted by draw order, a primitive going after those of equal draw order. `grouped(s)` returns the scene with each sprite list sorted by draw order and then by atlas, in one stable sort that takes `O(n log n)` time however the atlases interleave and moves nothing when the lists are already in that order, so sprites of one draw order and one atlas keep the order pushed where gpui breaks the tie by tile. Sprites of one draw order never overlap, so gathering them by atlas changes no pixel. The renderer draws `grouped(s)`. `push_sprite`, `push_subpixel_sprite` and `push_polychrome_sprite` sample atlas 0 and `push_sprite_from`, `push_subpixel_sprite_from` and `push_polychrome_sprite_from` take the atlas index. `batches` merges the lists into runs of one kind and, for sprites, one atlas, taking at each step the lowest draw order and, among equal ones, the kind that comes first in `kinds()`: shadows, quads, paths, underlines, sprites, subpixel sprites, polychrome sprites, the order of gpui's `PrimitiveKind`. A `Batch` carries its kind, its first instance, its count and its `texture`, the atlas its sprites sample, which is 0 for every other kind. `Kind.index` is the position in `kinds()`, and the renderer keeps one pipeline and instance buffer per index and one descriptor set per index and texture. A `scene.Atlas` holds four bytes per pixel: `write` fills the first byte with coverage, `write_stripes` the first three and `write_colors` all four, and `extend_edges` copies a tile's outermost pixels into the one-pixel border around it. A `PolychromeSprite` carries its bounds, clip, atlas tile, four corner radii, a grayscale flag and an opacity, 80 bytes. An `Underline` carries its bounds, clip, colour, a thickness and a wavy flag as a float, 64 bytes; `push_underline` places it by its bounds as any other primitive.
- A `Path` is a list of `PathVertex` (a position and the curve coordinates `st`, 16 bytes), three to a triangle, with the colour it fills with, the bounds that hold its start and every vertex, the clip `push_path` gives it and its draw order. `path(start, color)` starts one, and its methods follow gpui's `Path`: `move_to` starts a contour, `line_to` adds the triangle from the contour's first point to the last point and the new one once a contour has two edges, `curve_to(to, ctrl)` adds a curve triangle from the current point through the control point to `to` after the same fan triangle, and `push_triangle` adds one. A `line_to` straight after a `move_to` adds a triangle with two equal corners, as gpui's does. `push_path` places a path by its bounds, as every primitive is placed, and a path whose bounds are empty once clipped is dropped. No fill rule exists: a path covers the union of its triangles, which is the interior of a convex or fan-visible outline and not that of a concave or self-intersecting one, and a translucent colour darkens a pixel once for every triangle that covers it. `path_copies(s, b)` gives the rectangles of a batch of paths that the renderer composites, per path when the batch has one draw order and as one union otherwise.
- `stroke_polyline(points, width, color)` builds a path for a line of `width` device pixels, a strip of quads with butt caps. A corner shares one mitred vertex pair between the two segments, and is bevelled when the mitre is longer than four half-widths; where a segment is shorter than the reach of its corners the corner uses overlapping quads, so the stroke has no gap or spike, and a translucent stroke is darker where they overlap. `stroke_quad` and `stroke_cubic` stroke the polyline from `flatten_quad` and `flatten_cubic`, whose points are within a tenth of a pixel of the curve. Round and square caps, joins other than mitre and bevel, and dashes are not drawn.

### Layout

- A `layout.Tree` is made by `tree(scale)` for a display of `scale` device pixels per logical pixel and holds nodes in a slice; `node(mut t, style, children)` and `leaf(mut t, style, measure)` add one and return its index, and `compute(mut t, root, window)` places every node in a window of `window` logical pixels. `rect(t, node)` is its border box in logical pixels relative to the window and `content(t, node)` its content box.
- The engine is the flexbox algorithm of taffy 0.15 with the pixel snapping of gpui-fast 0.1.12 (`taffy.rs` in that crate):
  - A node's authored lengths (sizes, minimums, maximums, `basis`, margins, insets, padding, gaps) are multiplied by the scale and rounded to whole device pixels, a tie toward zero. A border width is `0` when zero and otherwise at least one device pixel. A percentage is kept as a fraction.
  - A leaf's measure function is called with logical widths and its answer is multiplied by the scale and rounded up to whole device pixels.
  - The tree is laid out in device pixels. Each node's border box has its origin and far corner, summed unrounded from the root, rounded to whole device pixels with a tie toward zero, and `rect` divides them by the scale, so edges that meet in the window round alike.
  - A root with an `Auto` width or height is as large as the window on that axis, rounded as a length is; where the window is `unbounded` it is as large as its content.
- A `Style` has:
  - `display` (`Flex`, or `None` for a node that takes no space), `position` (`Relative`, or `Absolute` with an `Inset` of one `Dimension` per side);
  - `direction` (`Row` or `Column`), `wrap` (`Single` or `Multi`), `justify` (`Start`, `End`, `Center`, `SpaceBetween`, `SpaceAround`, `SpaceEvenly`), `align_items` (`Start`, `End`, `Center`, `Stretch`), `align_content` (`Start`, `End`, `Center`, `Stretch`, `SpaceBetween`, `SpaceAround`, `SpaceEvenly`) and `align_self` (those or `Auto`, which follows `align_items`);
  - `grow`, `shrink` and `basis`; `width`, `height` and their minimums and maximums as `Auto`, `Px(f32)` or `Percent(f32)`, where `Percent(50.0)` is half of the parent's inner size, or of its padding box for an absolute node;
  - `margin` as a `Margin` of one `Dimension` per side, where `Auto` absorbs free space and a negative length pulls the node over its neighbours; `padding` and `border` edges in pixels; `column_gap` and `row_gap`;
  - `overflow_x` and `overflow_y`, each `Visible`, `Hidden` or `Scroll`.
- Sizes, minimums, maximums and `basis` are border-box lengths, and a box is never smaller than its padding and border.
- An `Auto` minimum along the parent's main axis is the automatic minimum of a flex item: the smaller of its specified size and its content's narrowest size, never above its maximum, and zero when the node's overflow on that axis is `Hidden` or `Scroll`. The narrowest width of a leaf is its measure function's answer for an `unbounded` width, so text does not wrap when it is asked for its narrowest size, and of a container the sum or maximum of its children's, and its narrowest height is its height at the width it is laid out at. An `Auto` minimum on the cross axis is zero.
- The algorithm follows the CSS flexbox steps as taffy 0.15 orders them, including lines, `align_content`, auto margins, relative insets and the cache of measured sizes. It leaves out block and grid display, right-to-left and reversed directions, `wrap-reverse`, baseline alignment, `align-content: balance`, aspect ratios, sizing keywords and scrollbar gutters.
- Absolute children are placed against the padding box of their parent by their insets and do not take part in the flow. One with no inset on an axis is placed on it by the parent's `justify` or `align_items`. Every node is a containing block, so an absolute child is laid out by its immediate parent.
- A leaf's measure function takes the width available to it, `unbounded` when unconstrained (`is_bounded` tells them apart), and returns its size.
- A node with `display: None` and every node under it get an empty rectangle at the top left of the node's parent.
- `testdata/ui_layout/` holds trees and the rectangles taffy 0.15 gives them under gpui-fast's rounding at scales 1, 1.25, 1.5 and 1.75 in a 640 by 480 window; `std/ui/layout/oracle_test.low` lays out each tree with `layout` and requires every rectangle to equal the stored one. A tree with a reversed wrapping container is left out. `testdata/ui_layout/README.md` describes the file format.

### Text

- `font.parse(data) -> Font ! Error` reads a TrueType font with `glyf` outlines, or an OpenType font with the `OTTO` signature and `CFF ` outlines, from the `head`, `hhea`, `maxp`, `cmap` and `hmtx` tables, the `loca` and `glyf` tables or the `CFF ` table, and the optional `kern` table. It uses a `cmap` subtable of format 12 or 4, preferring a Unicode one that covers every plane, and the first horizontal `kern` subtable of format 0. A `maxp` of version 0.5 is read.
  - It fails with `Unsupported(table)` for CFF2 outlines, a font collection, and `CFF ` outlines in a font without the `OTTO` signature, and with `Malformed(table)` for a table directory entry, `loca` offset or `CFF ` structure that runs past the data or disagrees with itself.
  - A `CFF ` table is checked in full when the font is parsed: the header, the Name, Top DICT, String and Global Subr INDEXes, the Top DICT's `CharStrings`, `Private`, `FontMatrix`, `ROS`, `FDArray` and `FDSelect` operators, each Private DICT with its `Subrs` INDEX, and the `FDSelect` of format 0 or 3. An `FDArray` holds at most 256 font dictionaries, the most an `FDSelect` can name. The `CharStrings` INDEX must hold as many charstrings as `maxp` has glyphs and the `CharstringType` must be 2. A CID-keyed font takes each glyph's Private DICT, local subroutines and `FontMatrix` from the font dictionary its `FDSelect` names, and a `FontMatrix` in both the Top DICT and a font dictionary is composed. The matrix maps the charstring's units to `units_per_em`, so the default `0.001` leaves a 1000-unit font unscaled.
  - Glyph data and lookup tables are read when used, and a malformed one gives glyph 0, an empty outline or a zero metric.
  - `Font` metrics (`units_per_em`, `ascent`, `descent`, `line_gap`, `glyph_count`) are `i64` font units with y up. `scale(f, px)` converts them to pixels, and `glyph_index`, `advance` and `kern` look up one glyph or pair.
- `Font` also carries `underline_position`, `underline_thickness`, `strikeout_position` and `strikeout_thickness` in font units, from the `post` table (offsets 8 and 10) and the `OS/2` table (offsets 28 and 26), or a tenth of an em below the baseline, a third of an em above it and a twentieth of an em thick when a table is missing or short; a position is the distance of the stroke's top from the baseline, negative below it.
- `font.fallback()` is Inter Variable 4.1 at its default instance (weight 400, optical size 14), embedded under the SIL Open Font License in `std/ui/font/LICENSE`; `font.fallback_file()` returns the file. The parser reads the default outlines and ignores the variation tables.
- `outline(f, glyph)` gives a glyph's contours of on- and off-curve points, with composite glyphs expanded through their offsets and scales, at most 6 deep and 64 components in all. `rasterize(f, glyph, px)` gives a `Bitmap` of coverage bytes with its offset from the pen, and `rasterize_slanted(f, glyph, px, slant)` shears the outline right by `slant` times its height first.
  - A `glyf` contour is quadratic. A `CFF ` glyph runs its Type 2 charstring, which draws every path operator, takes the hint, mask and width operators without drawing them, calls local and global subroutines with their bias, and evaluates the arithmetic and storage operators, and it gives cubic contours (`Contour.cubic`): the first point is on the curve, each later on-curve point is a line, and each two off-curve points and the on-curve point after them are one cubic curve.
  - A charstring with more than 48 values on the stack, subroutines nested deeper than 10, a subroutine that does not exist, more than 200000 operations, a `seac` form of `endchar`, no `endchar` or any other malformed operator gives an outline with no contours.
- The rasteriser scales the outline to pixels, flattens each quadratic curve into lines whose count grows with the curve's size, flattens each cubic curve by halving it until the larger second difference of its control points is within 4/3 of 0.05 pixel, so the lines stay within 0.05 pixel of the curve, and accumulates each line's signed area and cover into an `f32` buffer. A running sum along each row gives the coverage, clamped to 0..1 and stored as one byte per pixel.
- `text.System` holds the fonts, in order of preference, the glyph atlases (`atlases`, which the renderer is given, a sprite naming its atlas by index) and the caches. `system(fonts)` makes one, `default_system()` one with only the fallback font, and `system_with_atlas(fonts, size)` one with a single smaller atlas that never grows or gains a second. A code point is drawn with the first font that maps it, else with the first font's missing glyph.
- Shaping maps each code point to one glyph and places glyphs by their advances plus the `kern` pair adjustment when both come from one font. There are no ligatures, no `GPOS`, no bidirectional reordering and no complex scripts.
- `text.wrap(mut sys, s, style, width) -> Layout` breaks lines after spaces and at `\n`, and breaks a word longer than the width between glyphs; spaces at the end of a line hang past it. A `Style` has the em size, the family, the weight, the `FontStyle` (`Normal`, `Oblique` or `Italic`) and the line height. With the built-in fonts every line has the first font's ascent, descent and line gap, centred in the line height when there is one, and the family and weight are ignored; the embedded Inter Variable has no italic face, so a style other than `Normal` rasterises each glyph with its outline sheared by 0.2126 of its height (`font.rasterize_slanted`), keeping the advances, and the sheared glyphs are packed apart from the upright ones. A `Line` has `width`, the end of its last glyph that is not a space, and `end`, the pen after its last glyph, where the next glyph would go; a glyph's `x` and `end` place a caret. `measure` gives a layout's size. Layouts of the same string, size and width are kept until `end_frame(mut sys)`, and `cached(sys)` counts them.
- `Style.decoration` is `None`, `Underline` or `Strikethrough`; a `Layout` carries it with a `Stroke` (`top` above the baseline, `thickness`, both in layout pixels) from the first font's metrics, or from `dwrite.strokes`, DirectWrite's `DWRITE_FONT_METRICS` of the face, when the layout is native, and `paint` pushes one `scene.Underline` per line with an edge snapped to device pixels and a height of at least one device pixel. DirectWrite's own underline and strikethrough callbacks are not used: the layout sets no decoration, so `renderer_decoration` is never called. `set_subpixel(mut sys, subpixel)` switches an installed-fonts system between subpixel and grey glyphs and forgets every packed glyph.
- `use_system_fonts(mut sys, subpixel)` switches a system to the installed fonts through `std/ui/dwrite` and returns whether it could; a system that could not keeps the built-in fonts. It adds the embedded Inter Variable, the default family, so the default family draws whether or not it is installed. With the installed fonts, `wrap` finds the face of the style's family nearest its weight and `FontStyle`, else the default family, with `CreateTextFormat` given the matching `DWRITE_FONT_STYLE` so that a family with no italic or oblique face is drawn with DirectWrite's synthesised oblique, and shapes each paragraph as one line with ligatures and contextual alternates. Lines are `line_height` apart, or the face's ascent plus descent when that is zero, with the ascent and descent centred in the line. Lines break where gpui's `compute_wrap_boundaries` breaks them: a break is allowed between two characters by the rules of its `line_wrapper` (inside a word, after a space, around CJK text, never before closing punctuation), a line breaks when its width past the last break exceeds the width, and a word wider than the width breaks between glyphs. The width of a laid-out text is the smaller of the width it wraps at and its unwrapped width, with trailing spaces counted. A `Style` with `wrap` false never breaks; with `truncate` `End`, `Start` or `Middle` and a width, a line wider than the width is cut one character at a time as gpui's `LineWrapper::truncate_line` cuts it, trailing spaces and punctuation are dropped, and U+2026 marks the cut. `align` `Center` or `Right` moves each line within the width the text was laid out at, by the line's advance with its trailing spaces. A `Glyph` carries the UTF-16 unit where its cluster starts, so a break and a cut map glyphs back to characters. `add_font(mut sys, data)` adds the font file in `data` (a `str`, as `embed` gives it) to a system with the installed fonts through an in-memory DirectWrite font collection: a family the file holds is picked before an installed one of the same name, and a context's `add_font(cx, data)` does the same for its text. `params(sys)` returns the `TextParams` for the scene: the identity for the built-in fonts, and for the installed ones the gamma coefficients, contrasts and stripe order that DirectWrite's rendering parameters give.
- `paint(mut sys, mut scene, layout, origin, color, scale)` pushes one sprite per visible glyph. Built-in glyph origins are snapped to whole device pixels. An installed font's glyph origin is split into whole device pixels, a quarter-pixel step across and a whole pixel down, each rounded with ties toward zero and carried into the whole part; the glyph is rasterised at that step, with `GetRecommendedRenderingMode` picking the mode, and pushed as a subpixel sprite, or as a sprite when `subpixel` is off.
- `add_image(mut sys, width, height, rgba)` keeps a picture of red, green, blue and alpha bytes with straight alpha and returns an `Image`; a picture larger than the first atlas, up to 4096 pixels, makes the next `end_frame` grow it to hold it, and a larger one is left out. `paint_image(mut sys, mut scene, image, fit, bounds, corners, grayscale, opacity, scale)` follows gpui's `Window::paint_image` (`window.rs:5121-5215`): `fit_bounds` places the picture in `bounds` by `ObjectFit::get_bounds` (`style.rs:42-139`); the visible part is the overlap of the two, snapped with `geometry.snap`; the tile is cut to the visible part by gpui's rounded ratios when it is smaller than the placed picture; and the corner radii are limited to half the shorter visible side before they are scaled. A picture is packed into the atlas once with a one-pixel border that repeats its edge pixels (`scene.extend_edges`), where gpui's atlas packs tiles without one, so a magnified sprite's edge texels blend with the picture's own edge and not with transparent black or a neighbouring picture.
- `paint_icon(mut sys, mut scene, source, bounds, color, scale)` pushes one sprite that draws the SVG document `source` in `color`. `bounds`, in logical pixels, is snapped to device pixels with `geometry.snap`; the document is rasterised by `svg.render` at the snapped width times two, with the height from the document's proportions, into one atlas tile per source and size; and the sprite is that tile's size halved and rounded up, centred on the snapped bounds with its origin rounded to a whole pixel, so the linear sampler averages each pixel's 2 by 2 texels. A document that does not parse, or has a snapped size under one device pixel, draws nothing.
- `svg.parse(source) -> Icon ! Error` reads `path`, `line`, `polyline`, `polygon`, `rect`, `circle` and `ellipse` under `svg` and `g`, with `viewBox`, `width` and `height`, and the inherited `fill`, `stroke`, `stroke-width`, `stroke-linecap` and `stroke-linejoin`. Shapes are built as `usvg` builds them. Path data is read in double precision as absolute coordinates and cast to single precision per point, with the reflection rules of `S` and `T`, an implicit move after a close, and quadratic curves kept quadratic. An elliptical arc, and the corners of a rounded rectangle and the sides of a circle or ellipse, become cubic curves by `kurbo`'s method: the centre parameterisation of the SVG specification, and a split into `ceil(max(3.999999, (1.1163 * r / 0.1) ^ (1/6)) * |sweep| / 2 pi)` equal cubics with arm length `4/3 * tan(step / 4)`. An arc whose radius is under `1e-5` or whose ends coincide is a line. A rectangle without a positive size, a circle or ellipse without a positive radius, a polyline or polygon with fewer than two points, and a path with fewer than two segments are dropped. A `transform`, `fill-rule="evenodd"`, any other element, and a stroke whose cap or join is not round fail with `Unsupported`. `svg.render(icon, width) -> Mask` computes the pixmap size and scale in single precision as gpui does for `resvg` (`scale = width / document width`, pixmap size `trunc(document size * scale)`, transform `scale(size / document size)`). Each shape is filled and then stroked in document order onto one coverage buffer. The fill is a port of tiny-skia's anti-aliased path filler, which supersamples four sub-scanlines and four sub-pixels per pixel, clips and chops curves into edges in fixed point, and composites source-over. The stroke is a port of tiny-skia's path stroker with round caps and joins, called with `res_scale = max(|sx|, |sy|)` of the transform, whose outline the filler then draws; the path is transformed point by point in single precision as `x * sx + tx`. A stroke one device pixel wide or thinner is drawn as a filled outline, where tiny-skia draws a modulated hairline. Colours and opacities are ignored because only coverage is kept. The cosine, arc cosine and power the stroker needs are the single-precision `cosf`, `acosf` and `powf` of the C library on Linux and Windows, and the double-precision functions rounded to single precision for `browser/wasm32`, which no test compares with `resvg`.
- An atlas is a `R8G8B8A8` image packed in shelves, and a `text.System` starts with one of 1024 by 1024. A glyph is rasterised at its pixel size, rounded to 1/64 pixel, the first time it is drawn, with a one-pixel border, and goes into the open atlas, the newest one. A glyph, icon or picture larger than the first atlas, or one that does not fit in the room it has left, is left out together with everything packed after it in the frame; `end_frame` then replaces the first atlas with one twice as large, as often as needed up to 4096 by 4096, the smallest `maxImageDimension2D` Vulkan allows, and the next frame packs its own text and pictures, `frame` marking the context due so that frame is drawn. Once the first atlas is 4096 by 4096 and full, `paint` adds an atlas of that size and packs into it, so what the frame drew stays drawn, up to eight atlases in all; the sprites name their atlas, `scene.batches` splits a sprite run where the atlas changes, and the renderer binds each batch's texture. A tile that needs more than 4096 pixels across or down with its border is left out on its own, because 4096 is the largest image every Vulkan device accepts; gpui gives such a sprite a texture of its own (`min_size.max(&DEFAULT_ATLAS_SIZE)`). Each 4096 by 4096 atlas holds 64 MiB of pixels in memory and the same again in its texture, and the frame that first draws from it stages up to 64 MiB more, so eight atlases cost up to 512 MiB of each. When the eight are full, `end_frame` clears the first atlas, drops the others and the frame after packs from the start. `system_with_atlas` keeps one atlas that neither grows nor gains another, and clears it when it fills.

### Elements

`std/ui` builds an interface from values and draws it with the modules above; a program imports `std/ui` and nothing else for a window.

- An `Element` is a box or a text run: `div()` and `text(s)` make one and each builder returns a changed copy, so an element is built by chaining and `children()` takes a trailing block of elements. The builders are `row`, `column`, `gap`, `padding`, `padding_xy`, `padding_top`, `padding_right`, `padding_bottom`, `padding_left`, `margin`, `margin_xy`, `margin_top`, `margin_right`, `margin_bottom`, `margin_left`, `margin_auto`, `margin_x_auto`, `margin_y_auto`, `margin_top_auto`, `margin_right_auto`, `margin_bottom_auto`, `margin_left_auto`, `size`, `width`, `height`, `width_percent`, `height_percent`, `w_full`, `h_full`, `size_full`, `min_width`, `min_height`, `max_width`, `max_height`, `grow`, `shrink`, `flex_1`, `flex_auto`, `flex_initial`, `flex_none`, `basis`, `basis_percent`, `flex_wrap`, `absolute`, `relative`, `top`, `right`, `bottom`, `left`, `inset`, `overflow_hidden`, `overflow_x_hidden`, `overflow_y_hidden`, `overflow_scroll`, `overflow_x_scroll`, `overflow_y_scroll`, `items_start`, `items_center`, `items_end`, `justify_center`, `justify_end`, `justify_between`, `background`, `border`, `border_top`, `border_right`, `border_bottom`, `border_left`, `border_color`, `rounded`, `shadow`, `opacity`, `invisible`, `visible`, `group`, `group_hover`, `group_active`, `text_color`, `text_size`, `font_style`, `italic`, `text_decoration`, `underline`, `strikethrough`, `nowrap`, `wrapping`, `ellipsis`, `ellipsis_start`, `ellipsis_middle`, `truncate`, `text_left`, `text_center`, `text_right`, `placeholder`, `placeholder_color`, `id`, `track_scroll`, `window_control_area`, `child` and `children`. They set a `layout.Style` and a `Visual`, the colours, corners, shadows and text style, which are what `layout` and `scene` take. The handler builders `on_drag`, `on_drag_move`, `on_drop`, `can_drop` and `drag_over` add a listener or a condition and are described under hit testing and focus. `opacity` multiplies the alpha of each colour an element and its children draw (background, border, shadow, text, caret, selection and an icon's colour) and the opacity of an image by its factor, and factors multiply down the tree, as gpui's `with_element_opacity` and `element_opacity` do (`window.rs:4287-4301`, `4380`; `div.rs:2627`): it is applied once per primitive and not to a group, so overlapping children show through one another. `flex_1` sets `grow` and `shrink` to one and a basis of zero percent, as gpui's `flex_1` does (`styled.rs:181-187`), so in a parent without a definite main size it takes the content size. `width_percent` and `height_percent` set the width or height to a percentage of the parent's inner size on that axis, where `50.0` is half, and `w_full`, `h_full` and `size_full` are `100.0` for the width, the height or both, as gpui's `relative(1.)` sets them.
- `nowrap` stops a text from breaking, `ellipsis`, `ellipsis_start` and `ellipsis_middle` cut a line wider than its box with U+2026 at the end, the start or the middle, `truncate` is `overflow_hidden`, `nowrap` and `ellipsis`, and `text_left`, `text_center` and `text_right` align lines in the box; each is inherited with the text style.
- `text_color`, `text_size`, `font_family`, `font_weight`, `font_style` and `line_height` are inherited by an element's children; a text run is black at one rem in the default family at weight 400 and upright until they are set. `font_style(e, uitext.FontStyle)` takes `Normal`, `Oblique` or `Italic`, and `italic(e)` is `font_style` with `Italic`. `text_decoration(e, uitext.Decoration)` takes `None`, `Underline` or `Strikethrough`, inherited with the text style, and `underline(e)` and `strikethrough(e)` are `text_decoration` with those; a text draws one `scene.Underline` per line in its colour from the line's first glyph to its last that is not a space, at least one device pixel thick, positioned from the font's metrics (see Text). Its line height is the size times 1.618034 until `line_height` sets it, and either is rounded to a whole device pixel. A text run's width is rounded up to a whole logical pixel.
- A `Signal[T]` is a value that belongs to the application, not to one window. `signal(cx, v)` makes one with any window's context, `get(s)` reads it, `set(s, v)` and `update(s, f)` change it and make a frame due in every window of the application. Copies of a signal share the value. The tree is a function of its signals, so a program holds its state in signals and returns a new tree from the function it gives `run`.
- A `Context` is the user interface of one window: what the pointer and the keyboard are on, and its frames. The signals, the text system, the keymap, the clipboard, the posted closures and the clock belong to the application's shared state, which every window's context reaches, and a signal change or a drained post makes each window due by advancing a generation count. It is a handle: copies share the state. `headless(background)` makes one with no window and its own shared state, `frame(cx, render, size, scale)` returns the scene of the tree that `render` returns, and `dispatch(cx, event)` applies a `platform.Event`, so a program or a test drives an interface with no window and no GPU, and `draw(cx, g, t, scene)` draws that scene into an offscreen target. `window_size(cx)` returns the client size in logical pixels that the frame being built is laid out in, `frame_due` reports whether a frame is due, `closed(cx)` whether the window has closed or `quit` was called, and `quit(cx)` ends `run` whichever window it is called with.
- `open_window(cx, o, render) -> Window ! Error` opens a window beside the application's others, and `close_window(w)`, `is_open(w)`, `window_context(w)` and `main_window(cx)` close it, report it, return its context and return the first window. `activate_window(w)` brings a window to the front through `platform.activate`, which on Windows restores a minimised window with `ShowWindow(SW_RESTORE)` and then calls `SetForegroundWindow`; a headless or closed window has no system window, so it does nothing. `WindowOptions.owner` names the window a new one belongs to, `closed` runs with a `CloseReason` once the window has closed, and `close_window` closes the windows a window owns first. Only a context from `headless` opens windows, each with its own state and the application's shared state, driven with `frame` and `dispatch` on `window_context(w)`; a context from `open` and a call made while a frame is being built fail with `Platform(Unsupported(detail))`.
- `open(Options) -> App ! Error` opens a window, a `Gpu` that draws into it and a `Context` whose waker is the window's, and `run(app, render) ! Error` loops until the window is closed: it waits for events, dispatches them, and when a frame is due builds the tree, lays it out, paints it and presents it. `open` opens the window with `platform.Options.hidden`, so it is invisible while the GPU and the fonts load, and `run` calls `platform.show` once, right after the first `present` returns without error, whether or not that call had anything to present. A window with no frame to draw, because its content area is empty, is shown 500 milliseconds after `run` starts, the loop waiting no longer than that; a first `present` that fails ends `run` with the error and the window stays hidden. `app.cx` is the context, so `app.cx.signal(0)` makes a signal before `run`. `Options` has a title, a size, a background, a `border` colour and a `titlebar`, whose `appears_transparent` hides the system title bar; `border` is a `geometry.Color?` that `open` converts to the `0xRRGGBB` of `platform.Options.border` with `geometry.rgb_of`, which ignores alpha, and `WindowOptions.border` is the same for `open_window`, while a headless context ignores it; `Options.icon` and `WindowOptions.icon` are `IconFrame[]?` of `std/ui`, the same `size` and `png` as `platform.IconFrame`, that `open` and `open_window` convert and give the platform, and `set_window_icon(w, frames) ! Error` changes a window's icon through `platform.set_icon_on`, failing with `Platform(BadIcon(detail))` for a frame that is not a PNG of its `size` and doing nothing for a headless or closed window; `std/ui` draws no caption buttons itself, so a program that hides it draws its own title bar and its minimise, maximise and close buttons from elements marked with `window_control_area`. `open` gives the platform a hit test that answers from the areas of the last frame. On Windows `open` switches the context's text to the installed fonts, drawn as subpixel text when the GPU can blend it and the system uses ClearType; a headless context keeps the built-in font. `set_text_smoothing(cx, mode)` changes that choice afterwards: `TextSmoothing.System` is the choice `open` made, `Subpixel` is subpixel when the GPU can blend it, and `Greyscale` is one coverage value per pixel. It clears the cached glyphs through `uitext.set_subpixel` and makes every window due for a frame; a headless context draws built-in glyphs whatever the mode.
- `handle(cx)` returns a copyable `Handle`, and `post(h, f)` queues a closure that runs on the context's green thread before the next frame and wakes the window, so another green thread changes signals through it.
- `background_spawn(cx, work, done) -> Task` runs `work` on a new green thread and posts `done` with its result to the context, as gpui's `background_spawn` hands its result back to the foreground; `spawn_into(s, work) -> Task` does the same and sets the signal `s` to the result. A `Task` is a handle read and changed only on the context's green thread: `cancel(t)` drops the result when it arrives, and `finished(t)` is `true` once the result has arrived, whether or not it was dropped. The work itself always runs to its end.
- A frame runs in order: the posted closures, `render`, building a layout tree from the elements (a text run is a leaf whose measure wraps the text), `layout.compute`, recording the hitboxes, hit testing, then painting. Painting pushes, per element in tree order, its shadows, its quad and its text sprites, so a child draws over its parent. An element with `display: None` and its children take no space and draw nothing.
- Painting turns logical rectangles into device pixels with `geometry.snap`, `geometry.cover` and `geometry.snap_stroke`. A quad has each edge rounded to the nearest device pixel, a tie going toward zero, and each border width rounded the same way but never under one device pixel unless it is zero; corner radii are limited to half the shorter side of the border box in logical pixels, as `Corners::clamp_radii_for_quad_size` does (`geometry.rs:2479`, called by `Style::paint` at `style.rs:707-710`), and then scaled. An element paints as `Style::paint` does: its shadows, then its background in a quad with no border, then its children, then its border in a quad with a transparent background, so the border covers whatever a child draws under it. A shadow takes the smallest device-pixel rectangle that holds its box moved by its offset and grown by its spread, keeps the element's corner radii, and reads `blur` as the standard deviation of the Gaussian. A clip is the smallest device-pixel rectangle that holds its box.

- A rem is 16 logical pixels until `set_rem_size(cx, size)`; `rems(cx, count)` is `count * rem_size(cx)`, which the builders then take as logical pixels, so a 13 pixel rem makes `rems(cx, 0.5)` 6.5 pixels and layout rounds that to device pixels like any authored length.

Hit testing and focus:

- An element has a hitbox when it has a click, key, mouse or scroll handler, a `hover`, `active`, `group_hover`, `group_active` or `focus` style, a `cursor`, a `group` name, a `window_control_area`, a tooltip, menu or popover, is `focusable`, or is made with `occlude` or `block_mouse_except_scroll`. A frame records one per such element, the rectangle of its border box in logical pixels, in tree order, and keeps for each the hitbox of the nearest ancestor that has one.
- An element's key is its `id` when it has one, else its position in the tree, such as `"0/2/1"`. Focus, and the press a click needs, are kept by key from one frame to the next, so an element keeps focus when the tree moves it only if it has an id.
- The pointer is over every hitbox that contains it, so the elements of a nested path are all hovered, until an `occlude`d hitbox: the hitboxes are walked from the last recorded to the first, and one made with `occlude` stops the walk after itself, so nothing under it is hovered, pressed, clicked or scrolled; one made with `block_mouse_except_scroll` stops hover and clicks the same way but lets the wheel reach what is under it, as gpui's `hit_test` (`window.rs:775-795`) and `should_handle_scroll` (`window.rs:514`). `on_mouse_down_out` asks whether the pointer is inside an element's rectangle, as gpui's `contains`, and `on_mouse_up_out` whether the element is hovered. `hover(e, f)` restyles `e` with `f` while the pointer is over it or over an element inside it, `active(e, f)` from a press of the primary button over it until the release, and `focus(e, f)` while it is focused; the conditions apply in the order the builders were called. A change of what is hovered or focused makes a frame due.
- `group(e, name)` names `e` a group, and a group always has a hitbox (`div.rs:1697`). `group_hover(e, name, f)` applies `f` to `e` while the pointer is over the hitbox of the innermost group called `name` that holds `e`, that is over the group's clipped bounds and not only over `e`, and `group_active(e, name, f)` applies it from a press of the primary button over that group until the release (`div.rs:2330-2348`, `2497-2504`, `2548-2552`). Groups are looked up from the elements inside them, nearest first, as gpui's `GroupHitboxes` stack does (`div.rs:2876-2900`); a group is pushed after its own style is computed, so an element does not find its own group, and a name with no enclosing group never applies. A frame resolves each name to a hitbox during the walk that records the hitboxes. A group under an `occlude`d hitbox is not hovered, so its `group_hover` styles do not apply there. The group conditions apply in builder order with `hover`, `active` and `focus`, and a change of which groups are hovered makes a frame due like any other hover.
- `invisible(e)` and `visible(e)` set `Visual.visible`, which `with_visible(v, b)` sets in a style function; `with_opacity(v, f)` sets the opacity there. A hidden element keeps its place in the layout and its hitbox, and paints nothing: as `Interactivity::paint` returns before painting the element, its children, its listeners and its cursor when `style.visibility` is `Hidden` (`div.rs:1809-1811`, set by `visibility_style_methods!` at `styled.rs:26`, `style.rs:296-298`), so neither it nor an element inside it draws, runs a click, key or wheel handler, shows a cursor or takes focus, and its hitboxes take no part in hit testing once the frame that hides it is painted, while the styles of that frame still see the hover the hitboxes had before it. The visibility is the one the conditions leave, so `group_hover` can make a hidden element visible, as a row's delete button that appears while the pointer is over the row.
- `on_click(e, f)` runs `f` with a `ClickEvent` when the primary button is pressed and released over `e`. A click goes to the innermost element with a click handler that both the press and the release were over, and to no other. A press elsewhere, a release elsewhere and any other button give no click, as gpui's `Interactivity` records a pending press only for `MouseButton::Left` (`div.rs:2133-2144`). The event carries the release's position, modifiers and click count, so a double click runs the handler twice, the second time with `click_count` two, and a handler tells a single from a double or triple click by the count as gpui's `ClickEvent::click_count` does. A click from Enter or Space has `keyboard` set, a count of one and the bottom left corner of the element as its position, as gpui's `KeyboardClickEvent` (`interactive.rs:180-194`).
- `on_mouse_down(e, button, f)`, `on_any_mouse_down(e, f)` and `on_mouse_up(e, button, f)` run `f` with a `MouseDownEvent` or `MouseUpEvent` (button, position, modifiers, click count) for a press or release of that button, or any button, over `e`, and `on_mouse_move(e, f)` runs `f` with a `MouseMoveEvent` (position, the last pressed button still held, and the modifiers of the last button or key event) for each move over `e`; a press or release that also moves the pointer gives no move. Each goes to the elements under the pointer from the innermost outward, every one of them, until a handler calls `stop_propagation(cx)`, as gpui's bubble phase does. `on_mouse_down_out(e, f)` and `on_mouse_up_out(e, button, f)` run for a press or release that is not over `e`, before the handlers of the elements under the pointer, as gpui's capture-phase `on_mouse_down_out` does; a menu closes on a click elsewhere through them. A press of the primary button focuses before its handlers run, so a handler that moves focus keeps it.
- `on_drag(e, kind, value, preview)` starts a drag when the primary button is pressed over `e` and the pointer then moves more than two logical pixels from the press, as gpui's `DRAG_THRESHOLD` and the check in `div.rs:2159` (`elements/div.rs:47`). The press that began it gives no click on `e` or on any element around it, and the innermost element under the press with an `on_drag` starts it. The drag carries a `DragPayload` of `kind` and `value`; `preview(value)` builds the element drawn over everything else, with its top left at the pointer minus the distance the pointer had from the top left of `e` at the move that crossed the threshold, as `div.rs:2163` takes the offset there and not at the press, and `window.rs:2055` draws the preview where the tooltip would be. An element has one `on_drag`; with several the first applies. While the drag is active the pointer shows the `cursor` of `e`, or the arrow when `e` sets none, whatever is under it (`div.rs:2082`, `2538`). A preview is laid out with no limit on its width or height, where gpui uses `AvailableSpace::min_size()` (`window.rs:2058`) and `layout.compute` has no minimum-content measure for a root, so its text does not wrap. A preview is drawn with its ids, handlers, hover, active and focus styles, scrolling and fields stripped, so it takes no part in hit testing or focus and records no hitbox.
- Where gpui picks the handlers a drag reaches by the Rust type of its value, Low has no runtime type identity, so a drag carries a `kind` string the application chooses and a `value` string, and `on_drag_move`, `on_drop` and `drag_over` take the `kind` they accept. An application keeps larger values in its own state and drags a key to them. The drag of files from another program has the kind `external_paths` and its `paths`, as gpui's `ExternalPaths`.
- `on_drag_move(e, kind, f)` runs `f` with a `DragMoveEvent` (position, the border box of `e`, modifiers, payload) for every move of the pointer while a drag of `kind` is active, wherever the pointer is, as gpui's `on_drag_move`; the move that starts a drag does not run it, and a handler that calls `stop_propagation(cx)` stops the move reaching the others. `on_drop(e, kind, f)` runs `f` with a `DropEvent` (position, modifiers, payload) when a drag of `kind` is released over `e`, whether or not it started there. The release is offered to every element the pointer is over, from the last recorded to the first, as each drop listener runs in bubble order when its hitbox is hovered (`div.rs:2095`, `window.rs:3705`), so an element under a sibling that overlaps it without occluding it still takes it, as `drag_over` decides it does. The first with an `on_drop` of that kind takes the drag before `can_drop` is asked, as `div.rs:2100-2113` does, so a refusal runs nothing and offers it to no other element; an element that occludes `e` takes the pointer away from it. The drop is registered after the element's `on_mouse_up` (`div.rs:2089`, `2047`), so in bubble order it runs before the element's own mouse up handlers, and an accepted one stops propagation, which skips them and those of every element further out. `can_drop(e, f)` refuses the payloads `f` returns `false` for, for `on_drop` and for `drag_over`; an element has one predicate and with several the last applies, as each gpui call replaces the one before. `drag_over(e, kind, f)` restyles `e` with `f` while a drag of `kind` that `can_drop` accepts is over it or over an element inside it.
- While a drag is active no element is hovered or shown as active, none is clicked and no tooltip shows or is started, as `div.rs:2498` suppresses hover while dragging and `window.rs:2055` prepares the preview where it would prepare the tooltip, so `std/ui` hides the tooltip during a drag. Any release of any button ends the drag (`window.rs:3716-3724`), whether or not an element took it; Escape does not, as in gpui. `dragging(cx)` reports whether one is active, `drag_payload(cx)` returns what it carries, and `cancel_drag(cx)` ends it without a drop, as `stop_active_drag` (`app.rs:1949`). gpui's `group_drag_over` has no counterpart because `std/ui` has no groups.
- A drag of files from another program arrives as the platform events `FileDragEntered(x, y, paths)`, `FileDragMoved(x, y)`, `FileDropped(x, y)` and `FileDragExited`, which `dispatch` turns into the mouse events gpui turns them into (`window.rs:3620-3660`): entering makes a drag of kind `external_paths` and a move of the primary button held, a move moves the pointer, dropping is a release of the primary button with a click count of one, and leaving ends the drag with no drop, takes the pointer out of the window and refreshes hover and tooltips as `PointerLeft` does. A `FileDragMoved` to the position the pointer already has gives no move, as `PointerMoved` does not. Such a drag has no preview, because the system draws one.
- `masked(e)` makes a field draw a bullet (U+2022) for each grapheme of the text it shows, preedit included, while the text stays the field's value. A masked field refuses copy and cut, which are consumed, and treats the whole text as one word, so Control with an arrow key or Backspace moves or deletes one grapheme and a double click selects all of it; selection, deletion, paste and the input method work as in any field.
- A field selects the word under the pointer on a press with a click count of two, by `unicode.word_end` segments, and the whole text on a count of three; a drag after either does not extend it.
- A press focuses the innermost `focusable` element under the pointer, or its nearest focusable ancestor, and a press on none removes focus. `set_focus(cx, key)`, `blur(cx)` and `focused(cx)` change and read it. Tab and Shift+Tab move focus to the next and previous `focusable` element in tree order, wrapping at the ends, or to the first and last when none is focused.
- `on_key(e, f)` runs `f` for each key press, repeat and release while `e` or an element inside it is focused. A press reaches it only when no key binding took the press. A key goes to the focused element and then outward until a handler returns `true`; Tab and Enter and Space act only when no handler took the key. Enter and Space click the focused element when it has a click handler.
- `dispatch` tests against the hitboxes of the last frame, so the pointer is compared with what was drawn.

Title bar:

- `window_control_area(e, area)` marks `e` with a `platform.WindowControlArea`, as gpui's `window_control_area` does (`div.rs:581`, `1004`) and records its hitbox as `insert_window_control_hitbox` does (`window.rs:3324`, called from `div.rs:1849`). `window_control_area_at(cx, x, y) -> platform.WindowControlArea?` answers from the hitboxes of the last frame, by a rule of `std/ui`'s own: it starts at the last hitbox in tree order that contains the point and walks outward through its ancestors, the first element marked with an area answers with it, and an element that has a handler, a text field or is `focusable` answers `none` when it is met first, so a menu button on a `Drag` bar is a button and not part of the caption, and the window answers the system `HTCLIENT` there so the button gets its clicks as ordinary pointer events. gpui answers from the first window-control hitbox, in the order they were recorded, whose id lies among the hitboxes under the last mouse move (`window.rs:1133-1146`), and delivers a click on a button over a `Drag` bar through non-client mouse dispatch (`events.rs:949-990`). It gives `none` where no marked element lies, and before the first frame. Areas clip like hitboxes do. `open` makes it the platform's `set_hit_test`.
- A `Close`, `Max` or `Min` area acts on a press and release by the system, not through `on_click`; a program that wants a click handler on one adds the handler and the area to different elements.
- `is_maximized(cx)` follows the platform's `Maximized` events, and `dispatch` of one makes a frame due only when the state changed, so a title bar can restyle for the maximised window. `minimize(cx)` and `toggle_maximize(cx)` run the window's commands; a headless context has no window, so `toggle_maximize` flips the state it keeps and makes a frame due, and `minimize` does nothing.

Key bindings and actions:

- An action is a name, such as `"editor.save"`. `on_action(e, name, f)` runs `f` when the action is dispatched while `e` or an element inside it is focused, and `capture_action(e, name, f)` runs `f` before any `on_action` handler, as gpui's `on_action` and `capture_action` (`elements/div.rs:842-866`). The dispatch path is the focused element and the elements around it, or the root element when none is focused, as gpui's `focus_node_id_in_rendered_frame` falls back to the root (`window.rs:3982-3990`). Capture handlers run from the outermost element inward and let the action continue unless one calls `stop_propagation`; `on_action` handlers then run from the focused element outward and the first stops the action unless it calls `propagate(cx)`, as gpui's `dispatch_action_on_node` sets `propagate_event` to false before each bubble handler (`window.rs:3992-4080`). `dispatch_action(cx, name)` dispatches an action from code and returns whether a handler stopped it.
- `key_binding(keystrokes, action, context)` reads a binding as gpui's `KeyBinding::load`: keystrokes separated by spaces, each modifiers and a key joined by `-` (`ctrl`, `alt`, `shift`, `super`, also written `cmd` or `win`, and `secondary`, which is `ctrl`), a single upper case letter meaning that letter with `shift` and `ctrl--` meaning Control with the minus key, as `Keystroke::parse` (`platform/keystroke.rs:120-200`). The context is a predicate over key contexts that joins identifiers and `key == value` tests with `&&`, `||`, `!`, `!=`, `>` and parentheses, with gpui's precedences and its meanings for `!` (no context on the path matches) and `>` (a context inside another), as `KeyBindingContextPredicate` (`keymap/context.rs:180-470`). Text that cannot be read fails with `KeymapError`. `bind_keys(cx, bindings)` adds bindings and `clear_key_bindings(cx)` removes them all. An empty action name unbinds its keystrokes where its predicate holds, as gpui's `NoAction`.
- `key_context(e, context)` adds identifiers and `key = value` pairs, such as `"Editor mode = full"`, to the contexts of the path, as gpui's `key_context` (`elements/div.rs:658`). An element with a key context or an action handler takes part in dispatch.
- A key press goes to the keymap before any `on_key` handler or text field. The bindings whose predicate matches the path's contexts and whose keystrokes match the presses so far are ordered by how deep in the path their predicate matched and then by which was added later, and a binding that is a prefix of a longer one makes the presses wait, as gpui's `Keymap::bindings_for_input` (`keymap.rs:142-206`). A matched binding dispatches its action; when no handler stops it, the next binding is tried and then the press reaches `on_key` handlers and the focused field as if it were unbound. A chord such as `ctrl-k ctrl-o` holds its first keystroke and types nothing until the second arrives; a press that ends no binding replays the longest bound prefix and then the rest as ordinary presses, a chord left waiting one second replays its keystrokes the same way, and moving focus drops a waiting chord, as gpui's `dispatch_key`, `replay_prefix` and `flush_dispatch` (`key_dispatch.rs:465-548`, `window.rs:3800-3830`). Releases never match. `pending_keystrokes(cx)` returns the keystrokes of a waiting chord.
- A key matches a keystroke by the text it produces: a letter typed with Shift matches `shift-k`, and a character that needs Shift, such as `!`, matches a binding that names the character with or without `shift`, as gpui's `should_match` on Windows (`platform/keystroke.rs:82-115`). The keyboard reports the produced character, so `shift-1` matches nothing; a binding names `!`. Space is `space`, and the named keys are `enter`, `escape`, `tab`, `backspace`, `delete`, `left`, `right`, `up`, `down`, `home`, `end`, `pageup`, `pagedown`, `insert`, `pause`, `break`, `menu` and `f1` to `f12`, so Control with Pause matches `ctrl-break` and the context-menu key matches `menu`.
- `bindings_for_action(cx, name)` returns the bindings that would dispatch `name` from the focused element, leaving out those another binding of the same keystrokes takes over there, and `bindings_for_action_in(cx, name, key)` does so for any element, as gpui's `bindings_for_action_in` lets a menu show the shortcuts of what was focused before it opened. `keystroke_text_for(cx, name)` returns the keystrokes of the last of them, such as `ctrl-k ctrl-o`, or the action's name when none, `keystroke_text` and `binding_text` write keystrokes in gpui's modifier order, `ctrl`, `alt`, `super`, `shift`, and `is_action_available(cx, name)` and `available_actions(cx)` report the actions the focused path handles.
- A context has a clock in milliseconds that timers such as the chord timeout and the tooltip delay run on. `run` reads the monotonic clock and waits for events at most until the next timer; a context from `headless` starts at zero and moves only when `advance_clock(cx, ms)` moves it, which also runs the timers of every open window that fall due.
