- `now(cx)` returns that clock, held at the time the frame started while a frame is being built. `after(cx, ms, f)` runs `f` once on the context's green thread `ms` milliseconds from now and `every(cx, ms, f)` runs it every `ms` milliseconds, at least one; each returns an id for `cancel_timer(cx, id)`, which `f` may call on its own id. The ticks of `every` keep their phase, and a clock that moves past several at once runs `f` once and waits for the tick after it. A timer that ran makes a frame due. `animate(cx, f) -> i64` calls `f(now)` at the start of each frame, before `render`, until it returns `false`, and keeps a frame due while any animation runs, so a value computed from `now` follows the clock whatever else changes; it returns an id for `cancel_timer`. All of them run on the clock `advance_clock` moves, so a test steps an animation with `advance_clock` and `frame`.
- `prefers_reduced_motion(cx)` returns whether the system asks for no animation, read from `platform.prefers_reduced_motion()` at most once a second: `SPI_GETCLIENTAREAANIMATION` on Windows, and on Linux the GNOME `enable-animations` setting, read with `portal.animations_enabled` from `std/portal` on the session bus with a 200 millisecond limit, which is `false` when there is no portal or it fails. A context from `headless` reads `false`. `set_reduced_motion(cx, on)` makes the answer `on` in every window of the application, and `none` hands it back to the system.

Overlays, tooltips, menus and dialogs:

- `deferred(e)` draws `e` after the rest of the tree, outside every clip of its ancestors, and records its hitboxes after theirs, so it is on top and takes the pointer first, as gpui's `deferred` and `defer_draw` (`elements/deferred.rs:7-60`, `window.rs:2157`, `2756`). It is laid out in its place in the tree and keeps its ancestors for hover, actions and focus. `priority(e, n)` orders deferred elements: a higher priority draws later, equal priorities draw in tree order, and a deferred element inside another draws after it, as `with_priority`.
- `anchored()` is an absolutely positioned box that places its children at a point and keeps them inside the window, as gpui's `anchored` (`elements/anchored.rs:122-230`). `anchor(e, corner)` names the `Corner` of the box that goes at the point, top left by default; `position(e, p)` sets the point, which is in window coordinates in the default `PositionMode.Window` and measured from the box's own place in `Local`; without a position the point is the box's own place. `attach_to(e, key, corner)` takes the point from a corner of the element with that key, in the frame being built or else the last one. `offset(e, p)` moves the box after the point is chosen. The default `FitMode.SwitchAnchor` moves a box that would cross the window's right or bottom edge to the other side of the point on that axis when that fits, then pushes it back inside; `snap_to_window` only pushes it back, and `snap_to_window_with_margin(e, edges)` pushes it back to the margin from the edge it crossed. The flip is measured from the point without the offset, and the final shift is rounded to whole logical pixels, as gpui does. `bounds_of(cx, key)` returns the rectangle an element with that key was laid out at in the last frame, for any element with an id or a hitbox.
- `occlude(e)` and `block_mouse_except_scroll(e)` give `e` a hitbox that blocks the pointer, as above.
- `tooltip(e, build)` shows the element `build` returns 500 milliseconds after the pointer comes to rest over `e`, as gpui's `TOOLTIP_SHOW_DELAY` (`elements/div.rs:48`, `2650-2860`). It is placed one pixel right of and below the pointer where the pointer was when it appeared; one that would cross the right or bottom edge goes to the other side of the pointer, or to zero when that side has no room, as gpui's `prepaint_tooltip` (`window.rs:2088-2156`). It is drawn over everything else and hides when the pointer leaves `e`, a button is pressed or the wheel turns. `hoverable_tooltip(e, build)` keeps it through presses and wheels and while the pointer is over it, and hides it 500 milliseconds after the pointer has left both `e` and the tooltip, as `HOVERABLE_TOOLTIP_HIDE_DELAY`. A tooltip whose owner leaves the tree hides.
- `context_menu(e, id, items)` opens a menu at the pointer when the secondary button is pressed over `e` or an element inside it, kept 8 pixels inside the window, and `dropdown_menu(e, id, items)` opens one under `e`, its left edge on the left edge of `e`, when `e` is clicked, and closes it when `e` is clicked again; with no room below it opens above `e`, and with no room on the right it ends at the right edge of `e`. `items` is called each frame the menu is open and returns `MenuItem`s: `menu_entry(label, f)` runs `f`, `menu_action(label, action)` dispatches `action` to the element focused before the menu opened and shows its key binding there, as gpui's `bindings_for_action_in`, `menu_separator()` draws a line, `submenu(label, items)` opens a menu to the right of its row, or to the left of it when there is no room on the right, and `disabled(item)` greys an item out and skips it. An action no element on that path handles is disabled. A menu takes focus while it is open: Up and Down move the highlight past separators and disabled items, wrapping, Home and End go to the ends, Enter and Space choose, Right opens a submenu and Left closes it, and Escape closes the menu ahead of any key binding. Moving the pointer over an item highlights it and over a submenu's row opens it. Choosing closes the menu and gives focus back before the item runs.
- `popover(e, id, content)` opens the element `content` returns where a dropdown menu would open when `e` is clicked and closes it on a second click. A menu or popover closes on a press outside it and its owner, and that press then reaches what is under the pointer; Escape closes the topmost, after the focused element and its parents inside the layer have had it through their `on_key` handlers, so an element inside a dialog or popover that handles Escape keeps the layer open. A menu or popover whose owner leaves the tree closes.
- `open_dialog(cx, id, content)` shows `content` as given, centred on a translucent black backdrop that covers the window and occludes it, and moves focus to the first focusable element inside. While a dialog is open, Tab and Shift+Tab and presses move focus only among its elements and a press outside its content does nothing; Escape, unless the focused element or one of its parents handles it with `on_key`, closes it and gives focus back to the element focused before it opened. Menus and popovers opened inside a dialog work as elsewhere.
- `open_dialog_with(cx, id, backdrop, content, closed)` opens a dialog as `open_dialog` does with `backdrop` as the colour over the window. `closed` runs once after the dialog closes by any route: Escape, `close_overlay` on it or on a layer under it, or a layer under it whose owner left the tree. Focus has been given back by then, and `closed` runs as a closure posted with `post` does, before the next frame, never inside layout or prepaint. Opening an id that is open closes the first dialog and notifies it.
- `close_overlay(cx, id)` closes a menu, popover or dialog and those opened after it, and `overlay_open(cx, id)` reports whether it is open. Menus, popovers, dialogs and tooltips are laid out each frame in trees of their own after the deferred elements, menus and popovers at their natural size and a dialog against the window, and drawn after them in the order they opened. A popover or dialog draws only the element its `content` returns, so its background, border, corners, padding and shadow are the application's; the backdrop of a dialog is the one thing `std/ui` colours. Menus draw the application's `MenuStyle`: `set_menu_style(cx, style)` sets, for every window, the panel's `background`, `border`, `radius` and `shadow` and the `text`, `disabled`, `hint`, `highlight` and `separator` colours from the next frame on, and `MenuStyle {}` is the light panel menus draw until then.

Scrolling and clipping:

- `overflow_hidden`, `overflow_x_hidden` and `overflow_y_hidden` clip an element's children to its padding box on the axes named. `overflow_scroll`, `overflow_x_scroll` and `overflow_y_scroll` clip them and scroll them with the wheel. A scrolling element has a hitbox and so a key; give it an `id` to keep its offset when the tree moves it.
- Children that would not fit a scrolling element's main axis give up space like any flex item, down to their automatic minimum, so a child that must keep its size in a scrolling column or row takes `shrink(0.0)`.
- The scroll range of an axis is how far the children's far edge lies beyond the padding box's inner edge, never below zero. The offset is kept per key, shifts the children and their hitboxes, and is clamped to the range by each frame; `scroll_to(cx, key, point)` sets it and `scroll_offset(cx, key)` reads it.
- `scroll_metrics(cx, key) -> ScrollMetrics?` returns what the last frame recorded for the scrolling element under `key`: `viewport`, the size of its box inside border and padding, and `content`, the viewport grown by the scroll range, so `content` less `viewport` is the farthest `scroll_offset` on each axis and an axis with nothing to scroll has equal sizes. A scrolling element, a `uniform_list` and a `list` record it while they are prepainted and a `text_area` while it is painted; a tree being built reads the frame before. A `text_area`'s content is every line at its line height by the widest line in view, so its content width changes as lines of other widths scroll in.
- `scrollbar(e, ScrollbarOptions)` draws a bar over the padding box of a scrolling element, `e`, on each axis that scrolls and overflows: a groove in `track`, `width` thick, and a thumb in `thumb` whose length is `min_thumb` at least and otherwise the track's length times viewport over content, and whose place follows the element's offset. A second bar leaves the corner between them empty. A press on the thumb drags it, keeping the point grabbed under the pointer, even once the pointer leaves the bar and the element; a press on the groove scrolls one viewport toward the press; neither reaches the element's click handlers, and the wheel scrolls as before. The thumb is `hot` under the pointer and while dragged, is drawn `inset` in from each long side of the bar, or `hot_inset` while hot, with the whole width of the bar still taking the pointer, `margin` keeps the groove and the thumb's travel short of each end of the edge, and `autohide` draws the bar only while the pointer is over the element or a drag is on. A `list` is dragged with `list_scroll_by`, a `text_area` draws both bars without an overflow style and a press on one neither moves its caret nor gives it focus, and the bar of an element that does not scroll draws nothing.
- A clip applies to painting and to hit testing, so a child scrolled out of view is not hovered, pressed or scrolled. The hitbox of a clipped element is its rectangle intersected with its ancestors' clips.
- A `Scrolled` event goes to the hitboxes under the pointer from the innermost outward. Each runs its `on_scroll_wheel` handler with a `ScrollEvent` of the pointer position, the distances as the wheel reported them and the `Modifiers` held, and the first scrolling element that moves stops the event; one already at the end of its range passes it on. A vertical-only scroll (`dx` zero) made with Shift held and no Control, Alt or Super scrolls the elements horizontally by `dy`, so Shift with the wheel turning down moves the same way as a wheel turning right; a container with no horizontal range to move passes it on like any other scroll at the end of its range, and a `list` or `uniform_list`, which scrolls only vertically, passes it on.

Virtual lists:

- `uniform_list(id, count, render)` and `list(state, render)` are scrolling elements that build only the rows in view. Each has `overflow_y: Scroll`, no size of its own, no children, and a hitbox, so `height`, `flex_1` and the like size it and its clip, hitbox and wheel routing are those of `overflow_y_scroll`. They are in `std/ui/list.low`, and `Element` carries the render closure and the handle or state in `rows`.
- The tree is laid out in one pass and a list needs its own size before it can render rows, so the rows are laid out after it. The prepaint walk reaches a list with its content box known, asks `render` for the rows in range, lays out each row as its own `layout.Tree` root (`layout.compute` with the content width and, for `list` and for the measured item of `uniform_list`, an `unbounded` height; a `uniform_list` row gets the item height), and prepaints it as a child of the list: shifted to its position, clipped to the list's content box, so the list's padding and border stay still while the rows scroll, and with the list's hitbox as its parent. The row trees stay in the context until painting draws them inside the list's clip, in the order they were prepainted. `Walk` carries the inherited text style and the scale for this, since a row is built where only the walk is known. A row without an `id` has the key `"<list key>/<index>"`, so hover, press, focus and a field's caret stay with the row as it scrolls. Paint draws a list's rows and skips the children of the list element, as prepaint does. Rows are kept per list element and frame, by the index of the list's hitbox, so two lists with one `id` each draw their own rows. A row's laid out height is never below zero.
- A `uniform_list` follows `uniform_list.rs`. A frame calls `render(0, 1)` and lays that row out at the content width, which gives the height of every item (`measure_item`, `uniform_list.rs:650-672`, which lays it out at the minimum content width; `layout` has no minimum-content root), then calls `render(start, end)` for the visible range `floor(offset / height)` to `min(ceil((offset + viewport) / height), count)` (`uniform_list.rs:450-457`). The scroll range is `count * height` less the content box's height, the offset is kept under the list's `id` in the same map `scroll_offset` and `scroll_to` use, a row sits at `index * height` less the offset, and the wheel scrolls it like `overflow_y_scroll`. The visible range and the row positions are computed in `f64` from the index and the offset; the offset itself is an `f32` as in gpui, so its step doubles at each power of two past 16777216 pixels. A list with no items or a zero-high item draws nothing, and a list with no items leaves its stored offset alone.
- `uniform_list_scroll_handle(cx)` makes a `UniformListScrollHandle`, a handle whose copies share state, and `track_scroll(e, h)` attaches it to a list. `scroll_to_item(h, index, strategy)` sets a request that the next frame applies in prepaint as `uniform_list.rs:393-448` does: the stored offset is first kept within `0` and the scroll range, as `uniform_list.rs:378-384` does; nothing moves when the item is fully in view, else the offset is the item's top for `ScrollStrategy.Top`, the item centred in the viewport for `Center` and the item's bottom at the viewport's bottom for `Bottom`, kept within `0` and the scroll range. gpui 0.2.2 has no `Nearest`. `scroll_to_item_strict` applies the strategy even when the item is in view, and the `_with_offset` forms shorten the viewport by whole items from the top for `Top` and `Center` and from the bottom for `Bottom`. `logical_scroll_top_index(h)` returns the requested item while a request is pending and else the first item at the offset, `visible_range(h)` the range `render` was asked for, `item_height(h)` the measured height and `is_scrollable(h)` whether the items are taller than the viewport.
- `list` follows `list.rs`. `list_state(cx, count, alignment, overdraw)` makes a `ListState`, a handle that keeps the item count, each item's measured height and the scroll position as a `ListOffset` of an item index and an offset into it, so the position stays on its item when heights above it change. `ListAlignment.Top` starts at the first item and `Bottom` rests on the last, and a `Bottom` list shorter than its viewport sits at the bottom of it (`list.rs:539-551`, `list.rs:700-717`). A frame follows `layout_items` (`list.rs:604-779`): from the scroll position it lays out items forward until the viewport and `overdraw` are filled, lays items out above when they do not fill the viewport and moves the position up, then measures items in the leading overdraw. Only the items in the viewport are drawn; an item outside it is laid out once and its height kept. A row that has been measured is laid out again at each frame in which it is drawn. Items that have not been laid out count as zero high (`list.rs:1117-1123`), so the scroll range covers the items laid out and a list whose rows exactly fill the viewport with no `overdraw` has nothing to scroll. A change of the content width drops every height (`list.rs:1025-1038`).
- The wheel on a `list` is `StateInner::scroll` (`list.rs:487-537`): it moves the position from the one the last frame resolved by the wheel's distance within `0` and the heights laid out less the viewport, a `Bottom` list that reaches the end returns to resting on the last item, and events are dropped between `list_reset` and the next frame. `list_scroll_to(s, offset)`, `list_scroll_by(s, distance)` and `scroll_to_reveal_item(s, index)` set the position as `list.rs:348-389` do, `logical_scroll_top(s)` reads it, `bounds_for_item(s, index)` returns where a laid out row was drawn at the last frame, unlike gpui, which computes it from the logical scroll top, and `item_count(s)` the count. `list_reset(s, count)` and `list_splice(s, start, end, count)` replace items with ones not laid out and keep the position on its item as `list.rs:244-307` do. They carry the `list_` prefix because `reset`, `scroll_to` and `scroll_by` are taken in `std/ui`. The heights are an `f32` slice and a Fenwick tree of `f64` sums, so an item's offset and the item at an offset are found in logarithmic time, and a splice or reset rebuilds the sums in linear time, where gpui uses a `SumTree`.
- Not implemented from gpui: `ListState::measure_all`, the scroll handler, the scrollbar offsets, keeping a focused row that has scrolled out of view (`splice_focusable`), autoscroll requests from a row, `ListSizingBehavior::Infer`, `uniform_list` decorations, `y_flipped` and horizontal scrolling of `uniform_list`.

Canvases:

- `canvas(paint)` is a box that draws vector shapes at its place in the tree. `paint` is a `fn(Painter)` that `std/ui` calls while it paints the frame, after layout, with a `Painter` whose `width` and `height` are the size of the canvas's content box. The shapes draw above the canvas's background, in the order `paint` makes them, and under its children and border. A canvas has no size of its own and is sized, padded, bordered, faded and positioned by the builders of any box; its children, laid out in the content box, are how a chart carries text. `paint` does not run for a canvas that is `invisible` or inside an `invisible` element.
- Every point, rectangle, radius and stroke width a `Painter` method takes is in logical pixels, and a point is measured from the top left of the content box. Painting moves the point by the box's place, which includes the scroll offsets of the canvas's parents, multiplies it and the widths by the window scale, multiplies each colour's alpha by the opacity of the canvas and its parents, and pushes the shapes inside a clip of the canvas's padding box, which narrows the clips of its parents. A shape whose faded colour is transparent is left out of the scene, and a canvas that itself scrolls moves its children and not its shapes. Padding is therefore room for a stroke that runs along an edge of the content box.
- `fill_rect(p, r, color)` and `fill_circle(p, center, radius, color)` push quads: the rectangle with its edges rounded to device pixels as a background's are, the circle a square with corner radii of `radius`. `fill_triangle(p, a, b, c, color)` and `fill_polygon(p, points, color)` push a `scene.Path` that fans from the first point, so they fill a convex outline, or one where the straight line from the first point to every other point stays inside, and no other. `fill_area(p, points, baseline, color)` fills between the line through `points` and the horizontal line at `baseline` as two triangles a segment, or one on each side of the baseline for a segment that crosses it, which is the shape of an area chart and one a fan cannot fill; its triangles do not overlap while the points run one way along x.
- `stroke_line(p, a, b, width, color)`, `stroke_polyline(p, points, width, color)`, `stroke_quadratic(p, a, ctrl, b, width, color)` and `stroke_cubic(p, a, c1, c2, b, width, color)` push the path of `scene.stroke_polyline`, the curves along `quadratic_points(p, a, ctrl, b)` and `cubic_points(p, a, c1, c2, b)`, which return the points of the flattened curve in the canvas's coordinates, within a tenth of a device pixel of it, for a fill or an area with a curved side. The strokes keep the limits of the scene's: butt caps, mitred or bevelled corners, no dashes, and triangles that overlap inside a turn sharper than its segments are long, where a translucent colour is darker. Fewer points than a shape needs, or a width of zero or less, draw nothing.
- A canvas takes part in hit testing as the box it is: `on_click`, `on_mouse_move`, `hover`, `cursor` and the other handlers work on it and give it a hitbox. Events carry window positions, so `canvas_point(cx, key, position) -> Point?` returns `position` measured from the top left of the content box of the canvas under `key` as the last frame placed it, the coordinates `paint` draws in, or `none` when the last frame placed no canvas under that key. Prepaint records the content box of every canvas by its key, hidden ones included.
- The paint function and the options of `scrollbar` are held behind one pointer in the `Element`, `extras`, which a builder replaces with a changed copy. An `Element` is copied at each level of the tree while it is built, prepainted and painted, so its size sets how deep a tree fits the stack, and an option few elements use belongs behind that pointer.

Text fields, the cursor and the clipboard:

- `image(picture)` is a leaf that draws `picture`, a value of `add_image(cx, width, height, rgba)`, over its border box through `text.paint_image`, with the element's corner radii. A width or height that is not set follows gpui's `Img::request_layout` (`img.rs:277-400`): both unset take the picture's pixel size as logical pixels, and one unset follows the other's logical size in the picture's proportions. `object_fit` takes a `text.Fit` (`Contain` by default, as `img.rs:136-145`) and `grayscale` draws luminance.
- `add_png(cx, data)` decodes a PNG with `std/image`, converts it to `Rgba8` with straight alpha and keeps it as `add_image` does, and `add_png_file(cx, path)` reads the file first. Every colour type, bit depth and interlacing is accepted, palette and `tRNS` become alpha and 16-bit samples round to 8 bits. Gamma and colour profile chunks are not applied. Both fail with the `io.Error` of `std/file` and `image.decode_png`. `testdata/ui_png` holds the PngSuite (Willem van Schaik, free to use), and `suite.golden` lists each file's size and the CRC of its 8-bit pixels, equal to ImageMagick's for every decodable file; the 14 corrupt `x*` files fail with `Malformed` or `Unsupported`.
- `icon(source)` is a leaf 16 by 16 logical pixels that draws the SVG document `source` through `text.paint_icon` over its border box in the inherited text colour; `size`, `width` and `height` change the box.
- `field(s)` is a single-line text field showing `s`: a leaf 200 logical pixels wide that is `focusable` and shows the text cursor, changed by the box builders and by `text_color` and `text_size`. It keeps no text. `on_change(e, f)` runs `f` with the text after each edit, and the application stores it in a signal that the next frame passes to `field` again; a field whose application ignores `f` shows the same text. A field with an `id` keeps its caret, selection and horizontal scroll from one frame to the next wherever the tree puts it.
- The caret and the anchor of the selection are byte offsets, kept per key and clamped to the text each frame. `field_selection(cx, key)` returns them as a `FieldSelection` as of the last event, `set_field_selection(cx, key, anchor, caret)` selects between two offsets and `select_field_text(cx, key)` selects all of the text with the caret at its end, as `text_area_selection` and `set_text_area_selection` do for a text area. A setter keeps its request on the field's state, which it creates when the key has none, and resolves it when the field is next painted against the text it shows then, so a call made in the event that first adds the field to the tree, or together with a change to the text, applies to the next frame. An offset past the end of the text moves to the end and an offset inside a grapheme cluster to the nearest cluster boundary. A setter asks for a new frame and scrolls the field to show the caret even when it is not focused; it does not move focus, and neither does focus change the selection, so `set_focus` and a setter work in either order. A key, a click, a drag or an input method event that arrives before that frame resolves the request against the text of the last frame first. Left and Right move by extended grapheme cluster, and by word segment (`unicode.word_end`, skipping spaces) with Control; Home and End move to the ends; Shift keeps the anchor and so selects; an arrow without Shift collapses a selection to its near edge. Backspace and Delete remove the selection, else a cluster, or a word with Control. Control or Super with A selects all, C copies, X cuts and V pastes.
- Typed text, a paste and an input method's `Commit` replace the selection and drop every code point below space and DEL, so a field holds one line. The text of a key press is inserted from the `Key` event alone; an input method's `Preedit` is shown at the caret, underlined, in place of the selection, and is not part of the text until a `Commit`. Key releases, shortcuts and keys that make no text insert nothing, and Tab, Enter and the other keys fall through to focus movement and click handling, so Enter runs the field's `on_click`.
- A press places the caret at the nearest cluster boundary of the pointer, and a drag with the primary button held extends the selection from there. The caret is a one device pixel quad drawn when the field is focused, the selection a translucent quad behind the text; both are clipped to the field's content box, and the text scrolls to keep the caret inside it.
- `text_input(cx) -> Caret?` is where the focused field's caret was in the last frame, in logical pixels from the top left of the window, and `none` when no field is focused. `run` hands it to `platform.set_text_input` after each frame that changed it, which turns the input method on for a field and off otherwise.
- `cursor(e, c)` sets the shape shown over `e` and over its children that set none, and `pointer_cursor(cx)` returns the shape of the innermost element under the pointer that sets one, else `Arrow`, and `Arrow` over a `scrollbar` and while its thumb is dragged, whatever the element under the bar sets; `run` hands it to `platform.set_cursor` when it changes.
- `clipboard(cx) -> str?` and `set_clipboard(cx, s)` read and write the clipboard. A context with no window keeps the text in memory; `open` makes them the window's system clipboard, falling back to the last text written when the system clipboard cannot be read.
- `read_only(e)` marks a `field` or a `text_area` as showing text the user may select and copy but not change; on any other element it does nothing. A read-only element keeps focus by click and by Tab, selection by pointer (click, drag, double and triple click) and by keyboard (Shift with the arrows, Home and End, Control variants, select all), copy, caret movement, scrolling, and the drawn caret and selection. It refuses every path that ends in a change of text: typed characters, Backspace and Delete with or without Control, cut and paste, an input method's `Preedit` and `Commit`, and in a text area also Enter, Tab, undo, redo and the built-in handlers of `text_area.backspace`, `delete`, `delete_word_left`, `delete_word_right`, `new_line`, `tab`, `cut`, `paste`, `undo` and `redo`. A refused key changes neither the text, the caret nor the selection, and `on_change` and `LineSource.edit` are never called; a preedit already showing when an element becomes read-only is dropped at the next frame. A refused key is not consumed: its action is left unhandled by the area, so `on_action` handlers of the elements around it still run, and then the key goes to `on_key` handlers outward from the focused element and on to focus movement and click handling, as a key the element does not use does. Tab therefore moves focus on from a read-only text area and Enter runs its `on_click`. `is_action_available` and `available_actions` leave the refused actions out for a focused read-only text area. `text_input(cx)` is `none` while a read-only element is focused, so the platform opens no composition for it.

Text areas:

- `text_area(source)` is a multi-line editor over a `LineSource`, a struct of function values the application provides: `line_count() -> i64`, `line_text(i) -> str` (a line without its line break), `edit(TextEdit)`, and the optional `edit_all(TextEdit[])`, `line_runs(i) -> TextRun[]` and `line_fill(i) -> Color?`, which `line_source(line_count, line_text, edit)` leaves empty. The application owns the document, and the functions read the live document and not a snapshot, since keys, clicks and an input method's text reach the area between frames. A `TextEdit` replaces the text from `start` up to `end`, each a `TextPosition` of a line and a byte offset in it, with `text`, whose line breaks are `\n`. The area works out where its caret lands from the replacement and takes the removed text before it calls `edit`. `edit` applies exactly the change it is given. After each call the area reads back the lines the edit touches and the line count, and when the document differs from what the edit describes, as when the application ignores or alters it, the area drops its undo and redo history and keeps its caret within the text the document holds. Before it reads a position, whether for a key, a click, a drag, an input method's text or `set_text_area_selection`, the area moves the caret and the anchor into the current document: the line to the last line, and the byte offset to the line's length and down to a grapheme boundary, so an edit the application made to the document between frames never leaves a position outside it. A count below one is read as one empty line.
- The element is a leaf 300 by 200 logical pixels, `focusable`, with the text cursor and the key context `TextArea`; the box builders and `text_color`, `text_size`, `font_family`, `font_weight`, `font_style` and `line_height` change it. A `key_context` added to it extends `TextArea` and does not replace it. It keeps per key, and so by `id` where the tree moves it, the caret and the anchor as `TextPosition`s, the preferred column of vertical moves as a distance in layout pixels, the scroll position, the preedit and the undo and redo history. `text_area_selection`, `set_text_area_selection`, `text_area_selections`, `set_text_area_selections`, `text_area_top_line` and `scroll_text_area_to_line` read and set the selection or selections and the first line in view. `reveal_text_area_selection(cx, key, selection)` makes the next frame scroll to the caret of `selection` on both axes in place of the primary selection's, for the application that adds a selection with `set_text_area_selections` and wants that one in view; it moves no selection, applies to one frame, and a key, click or drag before that frame drops it.
- The editing keys are actions, as in gpui's `examples/input.rs`, which declares `actions!` for each key and binds them with `KeyBinding::new` (`input.rs:13-35`, `681-693`). `text_area_bindings()` returns the default bindings in the context `TextArea` and the application passes them to `bind_keys`, so a later binding of the same keystrokes wins and an empty action unbinds. The actions are `text_area.left`, `right`, `up`, `down`, `word_left`, `word_right`, `line_start`, `line_end`, `document_start`, `document_end`, `page_up`, `page_down` and the `select_` form of each, `backspace`, `delete`, `delete_word_left`, `delete_word_right`, `new_line`, `tab`, `select_all`, `copy`, `cut`, `paste`, `undo`, `redo`, `add_cursor_above`, `add_cursor_below`, `select_next_occurrence`, `select_all_occurrences` and `collapse_selections`. Dispatching one runs the `capture_action` handlers of the elements on the path first, from the outside in, then the area's own `on_action` handlers, then its built-in handler, which stops the action, so the focused area has the first claim as the handlers of gpui's example are registered on the focused element. An application overrides an action on the area by handling it there and keeps the default by calling `propagate`, and an element around the area intercepts an editing action with `capture_action`, which stops the built-in handler when it calls `stop_propagation`; an `on_action` handler on an ancestor does not run for an action the area handles. `is_action_available` and `available_actions` list them for a focused area. Typed text is not an action: a key that makes text and is bound to nothing inserts that text at the caret, as the field does, with every code point below space except Tab dropped.
- Left and Right move by extended grapheme cluster, and by word with Control, and cross from the end of a line to the start of the next. Up and Down keep the column the caret had when the vertical run began, as the distance in layout pixels from the left edge, and land on the grapheme boundary nearest it, so a short line in between does not lose the column; Up on the first line goes to its start and Down on the last to its end. Home and End go to the start and end of the line, Control with them to the start and end of the document, and Page Up and Page Down move the caret and the view by the lines in view less one. Shift extends the selection from the anchor, and an arrow without Shift collapses a selection to its near edge, as the field does. Backspace and Delete join lines at a line's start and end; Control makes them remove a word. A selection of several lines is removed by typing, Backspace, Delete, cut or paste.
- Tab inserts a tab character through `text_area.tab`, as a code editor does. An application that wants Tab to move focus binds `tab` to the empty action; Shift+Tab is not bound, so it moves focus back. Enter inserts `\n` through `text_area.new_line`. Escape is bound to `text_area.collapse_selections`, which the area handles only while it holds several selections, so with one the key bubbles to the application as before.
- `tab_size(e, n)` sets the tab stops of a text area: a `\t` advances to the next multiple of `n` columns, counted in code points from the start of the line with earlier tabs expanded, and the line is shaped with each tab as the spaces that fill that span. `n` below `1` is `1`, which is also the default and keeps a tab one column wide. The caret, a click or drag (which lands on the nearer edge of a tab's span), the selection rectangles, the preferred column of Up and Down and the widest line all read the same expanded columns; the text, `copy`, the edit offsets and every byte offset of the `LineSource` are unchanged.
- `blank_marks(e, color, caret_line)` draws `·` over each space and `→` at the first column of each tab, in `color` and on the caret's line in `caret_line`. A no-break space and every other `White_Space` code point that is not a line break gets a `·`. The marks are drawn after the line's text from glyphs laid out in the area's own style: a `·` is centred in its blank's cell and a `→` starts at the left of the tab's first column. They are drawing only, so text, `copy`, columns and widths do not change, and only the lines in view are visited. Without it nothing is laid out or drawn for blanks.
- A press places the caret on the line under the pointer and the nearest grapheme boundary on it, at the end of the document when the pointer is below the last line or the content box, and at the start when it is above the box, as gpui's `index_for_mouse_position` returns the end of the content for a position below the bounds and the start for one above (`input.rs:166`); a press with Shift moves only the caret, as gpui's `on_mouse_down` calls `select_to` when Shift is held (`input.rs:97-108`, `184-195`). A press with a click count of two selects the word under the pointer and one of three selects the line with its break, or to the end of the last line. A drag after a single press extends the selection, also with the pointer outside the area, and a drag after a double or triple click does not. The `selection_reversed` flag of gpui's example is the order of the area's anchor and caret.
- Copy writes the selected text with `\n` between lines. Paste inserts the clipboard text with `\r\n` and `\r` turned into `\n` and every code point below space except Tab and `\n` dropped, where gpui's single-line example replaces newlines with spaces (`input.rs:131-136`).
- Undo and redo apply inverse edits through `source.edit`; gpui has no text history, so the grouping is this module's own. An undo step is a group of edits and restores the selection, or every selection, from before it, and redo restores the ones from after it. Typed characters and Tabs in a row, Backspaces in a row and Deletes in a row each form one group, a group stops growing 300 milliseconds after its last edit on the context's clock, when the caret moves by key, click or `set_text_area_selection`, or when the selection is not empty at the edit. Enter, paste, cut, a commit and removing a selection are each a group, and so is any key that edits at several selections at once. A new edit drops the redo steps, and the history keeps 1,000 groups. An edit the application makes to the document itself is not in the history. An undo or redo step whose positions do not lie in the document, or whose edit the application does not apply as given, stops there and drops the history.
- A text area holds one or more selections, each an anchor and a caret, kept in document order and never overlapping. The first is the primary: the view scrolls to it, the input method's preedit and `text_area_selection` follow it, and `set_text_area_selection` replaces all of them with one. `text_area_selections(cx, key)` returns every selection in document order and `set_text_area_selections(cx, key, selections)` replaces them; an empty slice changes nothing, and the positions are fitted to the document as `set_text_area_selection` fits one. Selections that overlap merge, an empty selection touching another merges into it, and two non-empty selections that only touch stay apart; a merged selection keeps the direction of whichever reached further. Every caret is drawn and every selection painted, and a frame finds the ones in view by binary search, so its cost follows the lines in view and the selections among them and not the document or the selection count; a single selection takes the same paint path with no allocation. Movement keys move every caret, each keeping its own preferred column, and carets that meet merge. Typed text, an input method's `Commit`, Enter, Tab, Backspace, Delete, the word deletions, cut and paste apply at every selection as one step: one undo group that restores the whole set of selections from before it on undo and from after it on redo. Copy joins the selections' texts with `\n`. Paste splits the text at `\n` and gives one line to each selection when there are as many lines as selections, and otherwise inserts the whole text at each. A step of several edits reaches the source as one `edit_all(edits)` call when the source provides it, and as one `edit` call per element otherwise. The edits are ordered last position first by `start` and then `end`, so applying them in order never moves a position a later one names; every position is in the document as it was before the step; no two edits overlap, as edits that would overlap, such as a word deletion from two carets in one word, are merged into their union first; and an insertion at the start of a deletion comes after the deletion. Undo and redo replay a group through `edit`, one change at a time. After the batch the area reads back the line count and each edit's text as for a single edit and drops its history on a difference. `text_area.add_cursor_above` and `text_area.add_cursor_below` (Control+Alt+Up and Down) add a caret one line above or below the caret of each selection, at its preferred column. `text_area.select_next_occurrence` (Control+D) selects the word at the caret when the primary selection is empty and otherwise adds the next match of the newest selection's text after it, wrapping round the document, comparing case-sensitively, skipping a match that overlaps a selection and doing nothing when every match is selected; `text_area.select_all_occurrences` (Control+Shift+D) selects every match. A press with Control adds a caret and a double or triple click with Control adds the word or line, and dragging after it extends the added selection. A drag with Alt makes a column selection, one selection per line between the pressed and the pointer's line, each between the two columns read from that line's own shaped text, so `tab_size` stops are honoured and a line shorter than the column gets a caret at its end; with Control and Alt the column selections are added to the existing ones, and a plain press collapses to one selection. A drag with the middle button makes the same column selection as a drag with Alt, from the press to the pointer, with Control keeping the existing selections as Control and Alt does; a middle press without movement leaves one caret at the press, takes focus as a left press does and pastes nothing. The drag keeps following the pointer outside the area, as the platform keeps the pointer captured for any held button on Windows, and ends at the release of the middle button. A middle press does not stop propagation, so `on_mouse_down` handlers for `Button.Middle` on the area and the elements around it still run.
- An input method's `Preedit` is shown underlined at the caret, and `Commit` inserts at the caret. A preedit that starts while the selection is not empty first removes the selection through `source.edit` as a history group of its own, so no hidden text is drawn and the lines after a multi-line selection move up; a commit with a selection still present replaces it in one group. The preedit itself is not in the history until the commit. The platform delivers the preedit's range in bytes of the preedit, and the area uses it as the field does; gpui's `impl EntityInputHandler` (`input.rs:261`) counts UTF-16 units over the whole document, and its `text_for_range`, `bounds_for_range` and `character_index_for_point` (`input.rs:262`, `352`, `373`) have no counterpart here.
- The view is a top line, as an `i64`, and the pixels of it scrolled off, below one line, so a document of a million lines keeps full precision where a pixel offset in `f32` would not. The wheel scrolls both axes and, like a scrolling box, hands the event on when it cannot move. After any caret move or edit the next frame scrolls the least that brings the caret into view on both axes, as `uniform_list`'s `scroll_to_item` does for a row (`uniform_list.rs:395-440`), and a wheel turn or `scroll_text_area_to_line` afterwards is left alone. The horizontal range is the widest line among those in view, so it follows the lines the view shows and not the whole document; lines do not wrap.
- A frame reads from the source only the lines that intersect the view, which is the visible range of `uniform_list` (`uniform_list.rs:450-457`) at a fixed line height, the text size times 1.618034 rounded to a device pixel until `line_height` sets it; nothing is read for the lines outside it, the caret's line included when it is out of view. A click, a key or a drag reads the lines it needs, a few at most apart from copying or deleting a selection.
- A line is drawn from its runs, the plain gaps between them in the element's text style: each run is shaped on its own, in the element's colour unless the run sets one and in its `weight` when the text system has a face for it, and the pieces sit one after another, so the caret, the selection and a click all read the same positions. `line_fill` fills the full width of a line's row behind its selection and text, across the area's padding on each side, and a line's gutter is not supported.
- A focused area draws, clipped to its content box, the fill of each line in view, a translucent selection rectangle on each line the selection touches, the text of each piece, and the caret as a quad one device pixel wide and a line high, as the field does. A line the selection continues past has its rectangle extended by the width of a space, so an empty line in the selection shows. A composition is underlined one device pixel high. A tab is shaped as a space, byte for byte, so offsets stay aligned and the font's missing-glyph box is not drawn. `text_input(cx)` reports the caret's place, in logical pixels from the top left of the window, while the caret is inside the content box on both axes, and `none` otherwise, as for an unfocused area.

### UI tests

- `std/ui/dwrite` and the installed-font half of `std/ui/text` are tested by `std/ui/dwrite/dwrite_test_windows.low` and `std/ui/text/text_test_windows.low`, which run on Windows against the fonts that ship with it.
- `std/ui/svg` and the built-in-font half of `std/ui/text` are tested by `std/ui/svg/svg_test.low`, `std/ui/svg/svg_mask_test.low` and `std/ui/text/text_test.low`, which check parsing, errors, the verbs `usvg` builds for quadratic curves, circles and closed subpaths, masks equal to the ones `resvg` renders for a stroked line, curves and arcs, and a filled rounded rectangle with strokes, and the sprites `paint_icon` pushes.
- `std/ui/layout`, `std/ui/font` and `std/ui/text` are tested by `testdata/run/ui_*` programs, which need no library and so run on Linux and, through the `windows` suite, on Windows. They check layouts against hand-computed rectangles, glyph metrics and rasterised coverage of the fallback font, line breaking and atlas packing. `testdata/run/ui_font_cff` parses name-keyed and CID-keyed OpenType CFF fonts built with fontTools, checks their outlines and advances against the ones fontTools' `RecordingPen` gives, probes each Type 2 operator and limit through fonts it assembles, rejects damaged CFF tables, and rasterises curved glyphs. `std/ui/geometry/geometry_test.low` and `std/ui/scene/scene_test.low` check rectangle and colour arithmetic, clipping, draw order, scene batching by atlas and the glyph atlases' dirty rows, tiles, clearing and added textures.
- `std/ui/*_test.low` runs with `low test std/ui` and drives a `Context` with no window and no GPU: it checks the scene a frame returns against hand-computed rectangles and colours, and dispatches pointer and key events to check hover, click, focus and key bubbling. `std/ui/titlebar_test.low` checks which area lies under a point for nested areas, handlers, fields, clipping and overlap, that `Maximized` events and `toggle_maximize` change `is_maximized` and make a frame due, and that `minimize` runs the window's command; `std/ui/examples/title_bar.low` runs the same headless. `std/ui/window_test.low` opens several headless windows on one application and checks that a signal change or a drained post makes each due, that the keymap and the clock are shared while chords and timers stay per window, that a task delivers through a closed window's signal, and how `close_window` and `open_window` behave. `std/ui/task_test.low` holds a task's work at a channel to check that its result reaches a signal only on the context's green thread, after the work returns, and never once the task is cancelled.
- `compiler/tests/ui` builds Low programs that import the `std/ui` modules and runs them:
  - offscreen renders of every primitive kind, clipping, draw order, frames in flight, instance buffer growth, atlas updates and sprites that sample different atlases in both orders and interleaved by draw order, compared by pixels, an element tree driven through hover, press, focus, click and scrolling, and the `parity` and `overlays` scenes, whose pixels at the spots the tests name must equal the ones gpui-fast 0.1.12 draws for the same element trees, the second covering `deferred`, `priority` and `anchored` with each fit mode; they build for the host, so they run on Linux and on Windows, and skip without libvulkan or a Vulkan 1.3 device;
  - the `virtual_lists` scene, a `uniform_list` scrolled to an item with `Center` and a `list` scrolled into an item, each cut by a clipping box, whose pixels must equal the ones gpui-fast 0.1.12 draws for the same element tree;
  - the `group_hover` scene, two groups with one under the pointer and a child outside any group that names it, whose pixels must equal the ones gpui-fast 0.1.12 draws for the same element tree with the desktop pointer moved onto the group;
  - on a Windows host, the `placeholder` scene, two empty fields drawn through DirectWrite, whose box, border and background must equal the ones gpui-fast 0.1.12 draws for the same element tree and whose placeholder ink must lie inside the content box in its colour; it skips without a display or a device;
  - on a Windows host, the `text_area` scene, a focused text area with a line fill, a selection across two lines and the caret drawn through DirectWrite, whose fill, selection rectangles and caret must equal the ones gpui-fast 0.1.12 draws for the same element tree and whose text ink must lie inside its rows; it skips without a display or a device;
  - the Wayland listener callbacks and key translation, driven directly by a program built from the platform's sources, which also covers text input composition, every cursor shape, clipboard offers and sends through pipes, and reading a pipe to its end or its timeout; key translation skips without libxkbcommon;
  - `app`, the example program: a window with a heading, a hoverable, pressable and clickable button, a focusable panel that shows the last key pressed, a text field and signal-driven text. Run by hand it is the interface to try; run with `--smoke` it focuses the button and the field through `set_focus`, activates the button with Enter, types into the field, quits and prints the signals' values, and the test compares them. It runs on the host, so it needs a Vulkan device and, on Linux, a compositor, and skips without them; the Windows build is checked to import `vulkan-1.dll`, `user32.dll`, `kernel32.dll` and `dwmapi.dll`;
  - a Wayland window that opens with `hidden`, which `show` leaves as it is, opens again after closing, writes to and reads back the clipboard, and presents frames through `open_for` and `present`, and a `Display` of two windows whose `wait` runs to its timeout with nothing pending and ends when the compositor answers a maximise of either window, on a wake from another green thread and on a wake with no window open; these skip without a running compositor;
  - `win32_twopresent` and `wayland_twopresent`, two platform windows drawn by one `Device` through two `Presenter`s with a different scene each: each window must show its own scene after a glyph atlas is uploaded through either window, after forty presents alternating between the windows and once the first window's `Presenter` is released. The Win32 fixture reads each window's pixels with `PrintWindow`. A Wayland window cannot be read back, so its fixture checks that the presents succeed, and the atlas sampling in both directions is checked by `std/ui/gpu/device_test.low`, which draws into a `Target` of the same `Device` and reads it back. A presenter refused for a surface the device was not opened for is a failure, not a skip. They skip without a Vulkan 1.3 device that presents to the first window, and the Wayland one without a running compositor;
  - on a Windows host, the Win32 platform run with posted and sent messages standing in for input, on windows disabled so the desktop's own pointer does not reach them, each opened with `activate: false` and driven only by messages posted to its handle, never by synthesised input, so they take no focus from the person running them: opening, which checks the window is neither the foreground nor the active window, `win32_hidden`, which opens a window with `hidden` and checks with `DwmGetWindowAttribute` that `DWMWA_CLOAKED` reports `DWM_CLOAKED_APP` until `show` and nothing after it, that a second `show` and a `show` on a window opened without `hidden` change nothing, and that the client area and the `Resized` event are the requested size while cloaked, `win32_border`, which on Windows 11 or later opens windows with `border: some(0x444444)`, `some(0x445566)` and `none`, makes each topmost without activating it and reads the screen along the edges of its `DWMWA_EXTENDED_FRAME_BOUNDS` with `GetPixel`, because `DwmGetWindowAttribute` has no getter for `DWMWA_BORDER_COLOR`, to check that the frame draws the colour with red and blue in place and that a window with no border colour draws neither, `win32_icon`, which opens windows with PNG frames made by `std/image`, reads each icon back with `WM_GETICON` and measures its bitmap with `GetIconInfo` and `GetObjectW` to check that the large and the small icon are the frames that cover `SM_CXICON` and `SM_CXSMICON` at the window's scale, that a replacement, a refusal (not a PNG, another size than stated, a PNG cut after its header, a bad frame after a good one) and no frames each leave the icons they should, and that the replaced icons and the icons of a closed window are destroyed, `win32_appicon`, which does the same through `ui.open`, `ui.open_window` and `ui.set_window_icon`, pairs of windows, `win32_classonce`, which opens windows in one `Display` and closes them in either order and checks with `GetClassInfoExW` that the window class and the message-only window's class stay registered until the display closes, that a display can open windows after another display or a `platform.open` window has been released, `win32_handles`, which holds a `DisplayHandle` in a closure and checks that after the display is released `open_in_on` and `wait_on` fail with `Unsupported`, and `win32_wait`, which checks that a message posted to the second window of a display and a wake of `waker_of`, also from another green thread, each end `wait`, that a wake ends it after a window has closed and with none open, that one wait takes all the wakes that arrived, that `wait` runs to its timeout less one clock tick, and that key events for two windows keep their text apart, and `win32_owned`, which checks that `GetWindow(GW_OWNER)` returns the owner, that the owner stays enabled, that `WS_EX_APPWINDOW` is set for `taskbar: true` on an owned window and not for a window with no owner, which has neither it nor `WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW`, `WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW` for `taskbar: false` without an owner, that an owner from another `Display` fails with `Unsupported`, that `WM_GETMINMAXINFO` reports the minimum at 100 and 150 percent with the frame from `AdjustWindowRectExForDpi` and answers as `DefWindowProcW` does with no minimum, that a window smaller than its minimum is centred at the minimum, that a centred window lies within a pixel of its owner's centre and inside the work area and is clamped into it when the owner sits in a corner, that `set_title` changes the window text, and that a window the system destroys with its owner has a cleared handle, returns one `CloseRequested` from `next` and then fails with `Unsupported`, reports no `DestroyWindow` failure when released and has its anchor and drop target pins free, which it shows by filling the runtime's pin table (`pin_capacity`) and releasing the owner or destroying it 40 times, and `win32_windowhandles`, which holds a `WindowHandle` in a closure and checks `detach`, `adopt`, an idempotent `close_on` and `Unsupported` from a stale handle, pointer, keys, resizing, the clipboard, input method composition, cursors, waking, dropped files, which queries the registered `IDropTarget` for its interfaces, calls it with a data object holding `CF_HDROP` that checks the format description it is asked for, with sources that allow one effect, with a point in negative screen coordinates and with a data object holding no files, and checks that the target is gone once the window closes, and the title bar: `win32_titlebar` sets a hit test on a platform window and checks that the client area is the window less the frame insets and the requested size, the hit codes of the areas, the eight borders in the invisible frame and the top edge, a close click, a press and release on different buttons, a press released over the content followed by an unpaired non-client release, double clicks on a caption button and on the drag area, `ShowWindow` maximising and restoring with the client area kept inside the monitor's work area, `toggle_maximize`, the minimise button, and minimising a maximised window, which sends no `Maximized(false)`; `win32_titled` opens a window with the system title bar and checks that its hit test falls through to the system except where an area answers, that a press and release on an area-answered close code closes it and that `WM_NCMOUSELEAVE` gives `PointerLeft`; `win32_apptitlebar` opens a `std/ui` window, disabled and found by a title that holds the process id, with the title bar hidden and checks that its areas answer through `WM_NCHITTEST`, that a click on the maximise area maximises it and that `toggle_maximize` restores it. The `Maximized` event is shown by the first and last. `win32_appreveal` opens a `std/ui` window and checks that `open` leaves it cloaked, that it is uncloaked after the first frame is presented, and that a window of no area, which never has a frame to present, is still cloaked 200 milliseconds after `run` starts and uncloaked a second after. The title bar cases need a desktop, and `win32_apptitlebar` and `win32_appreveal` also a Vulkan 1.3 device.
  - The clipboard program saves every format on the clipboard and puts them back when it ends, and skips when one is a graphics handle it cannot copy. The input method program composes through `ImmSetCompositionStringW` and sends the `WM_IME_*` messages itself when the host's input method sends none. The cursor program compares `GetCursor` with the stock cursor after `set_cursor` and after a sent `WM_SETCURSOR`.
- `compiler/tests/vkgen` and `compiler/tests/wlgen` check that the checked-in bindings match their generators. Both find their libraries through `link.system_dirs`.
  - `compiler/tests/wlgen` tests the generated signatures and checks, then builds a registry client from the vendored protocols and, when a Wayland compositor is running, runs it.
  - `compiler/tests/vkgen` tests declaration parsing, extension enum values and the selection format, checks the shape of the generated bindings, and renders a rounded, bordered quad offscreen with the quad shaders through the bindings and dynamic rendering, comparing pixels. It skips without libvulkan or a Vulkan 1.3 device.
- `compiler/tests/shaders` checks that the sources and the SPIR-V match the sums recorded in `shaders.sum` and validates the SPIR-V with `spirv-val` when installed.

## Tests

The tests are Low programs. `compiler/lowtest` is the fixture harness: `low build compiler/lowtest -o lowtest`, then `lowtest <low binary> [run|examples|fail|lint|test|windows|browser]... [-filter text]` from the repository root checks any `low` binary against the sidecar files using only its command line. Each `compiler/tests/<name>` is a `std/testing` program for `compiler/<name>`, run with `low run compiler/tests/<name>`; `low` tests `compiler/low`, `editors` the editor grammars and `ui` the `std/ui` modules. A test that needs a tool outside `low` skips when the tool is missing. The `run` and `examples` suites skip on a Windows host and the `windows` suite skips on a Linux host, so the workflow runs the `windows` and `test` suites, `low test std/...` and the Win32 tests on a Windows runner with a `low.exe` and `lowtest.exe` built for Windows. The `compiler/tests/asm` and `compiler/tests/link` oracle tests need the pinned binutils and LLVM below, which the workflow does not install, so they run by hand.

### Fixtures

A fixture program is a `.low` file or a directory with a `main.low`. Its expectations sit beside it in sidecar files named after it: `name.low` and `name/` both take `name.out`. `compiler/fixture` reads and writes the sidecars; `compiler/fuzz/corpus` writes fixtures through it and `compiler/lowtest` lists programs for the harness.

- `testdata/run` holds programs that run on Linux:
  - `.out` is the expected stdout, empty when absent.
  - `.err` is the expected stderr. A program has one exactly when it exits with a panic status.
  - `.maxrss` is a limit on peak memory in KB.
  - `.maxtime` is a limit on the wall-clock time of one run, as a duration such as `1s`. Only programs whose point is speed have one. A program with a `.maxtime` or `.maxrss` runs on its own, not alongside the others.
  - `.needed` lists the shared libraries the binary must link, one per line in sorted order. Without it the binary must be static with no interpreter. A program with one has no Windows build.
  - `.tags` declares what the runner cannot learn from the program: `simd` marks a program that compiles to vector code and runs a second time with `LOW_SIMD=sse2`, and `linux-only` marks a program that uses operating system services the Windows standard library lacks, such as signals, pseudo-terminals, inotify, Unix sockets, file modes or `/proc`, or relies on a Linux kernel guarantee, such as appends from concurrent threads landing whole, and has no Windows build, and `debug` builds the program with `-c debug`. The runner fails when `simd` and the compiled code disagree.
- `testdata/windows` holds programs that only run on Windows, with `.out`, `.err` and `.maxtime`.
- `std/<module>/examples` holds the module's examples, with `.out` and, for an example with no Windows build, a `.tags` of `linux-only`. The runner does not check `simd` for them.
- `testdata/fail` holds programs that must fail to compile with the message in `.err`, with file paths shown relative to the fixture. `.target` names the target to build for, such as `browser/wasm32`, and is read by `fixture.read_target`; without it the program builds for `linux/amd64`.
- `testdata/lint` holds programs and library modules without a `main` whose `low lint` warnings must equal `.warn`, one per line with relative paths. A program without a `.warn` file has none.
- `testdata/test` holds directories whose tests run with `low test`:
  - `.module` names the path to test, joined to the directory's path from the repository root: a submodule of a project, `...` for every module below the directory, or `name_test.low:LINE`. Without it the directory itself is tested.
  - `.args` lists extra arguments for `low test`, one per line, after the path and `--notime`.
  - `.out` and `.err` are the expected stdout and stderr, empty when absent, with the absolute path of the directory removed. Module names are paths from the repository root, which is the working directory of `lowtest`.
  - `.status` is the expected exit status, `0` when absent.
- `bench/<name>/main.low` are the benchmark programs; the compile benchmark and the determinism test use them.

### Checks

- The `run` and `examples` suites compile every `testdata/run` program and std example for Linux, run it with a one-minute limit, compares stdout with `.out` and stderr with `.err`, and applies `.maxrss`, `.maxtime`, `.needed` and `.tags`. These suites skip on a Windows host.
- The `windows` suite builds every `testdata/run` program and std example that has a Windows build, and every `testdata/windows` program, for Windows and compares stdout, stderr and `.maxtime` the same way, running a program with a `.maxtime` or `.maxrss` on its own. It runs only on a Windows host.
- The `fail` and `lint` suites check every `testdata/fail` and `testdata/lint` program against its sidecar.
- The `test` suite runs `low test --notime --nocache` on every `testdata/test` directory, compares stdout, stderr and the exit status with its sidecars, and runs on both hosts. A test build cannot be expressed in a `testdata/fail` program, so the rejected test shapes are fixtures here. The fixtures also cover a crashing test, a test that times out, skipped tests, `--run` on a subtest, `--skip`, `--failfast`, `--count`, `--shuffle`, `--json`, `--list`, selection by `file:line`, a test file for each operating system that defines the same test, a `...` walk that leaves out `testdata`, `examples`, dot and underscore directories while another module fails to build, a module with no test files and a test that reads a file beside its module. `testdata/run/test_files_left_out` holds a test file that does not compile, which shows that `low build` and `low run` leave test files out.
- `low test std/...` runs the unit tests in the `_test.low` files of the standard library modules, built against the standard library embedded in `low`. The files test one module each through its private code, so they use its names without an import, and each covers one area of it:
  - `std/testing`: `selected` for each `/` level of a filter, `settings_from` for every flag and each flag error, the failure message of each assertion that returns, and `sub`, `table` and `table_of` against a `T` the test builds.
  - `std/path`: `clean`, `join`, `base`, `dir`, `ext`, `stem`, `is_absolute` and `matches` over empty paths, `.`, `..`, repeated and trailing separators and the root.
  - `std/filepath`: every function against `std/path` on paths without a volume, with `/` turned into the target's separator, and on Windows `volume`, `is_absolute`, `clean`, `join`, `base` and `dir` over drives, drive-relative paths, shares and device paths.
  - `std/text`: splitting, `cut`, trimming, searching, `replace`, integer parsing and formatting in bases 2 to 36, `quote` and `unquote`, `fixed`, padding, `position`, `wrap`, `append`, `append_char`, `char_len`, `title`, `edit_distance`, `has_token` and `lower`, including empty input, multi-byte text and bytes that are not UTF-8.
  - `std/crypto/mldsa`: the number-theoretic transform against schoolbook multiplication, the rounding and hint functions against their definitions, and hint decoding for a well-formed encoding and for each malformed one (count above the limit, decreasing or repeated indices, decreasing counts, nonzero padding).
  - `std/crypto/slhdsa`: `base_2b` over nibbles, twelve-bit groups and groups that cross bytes, the WOTS+ checksum digits, the compressed address layout, `chain` composition, the WOTS+, XMSS and FORS signatures recovering their public keys, the hypertree rejecting a changed message, leaf or tree index, and the signature size of every parameter set.
  - `std/crypto/falcon`: the negacyclic transform against schoolbook multiplication for degrees 2 to 1024, the compressed signature and 14-bit public key codecs against an encoder written in the test, each non-canonical encoding (`-0`, a coefficient beyond 2047, trailing bits, a truncated or short buffer, a public key coefficient of 12289 or more, a wrong length), the trimmed private key coefficients and their excluded lowest value, the point hash and the strict norm bound.
- The `browser` suite builds every `testdata/run` program and std example for `browser/wasm32`, runs it with `low run --target browser/wasm32` and compares stdout, stderr and the exit status with the same sidecars; compiler warnings on stderr are ignored, `.maxtime`, `.needed` and `.tags` are not applied, and `.maxrss` becomes `LOW_WASM_MAX_PAGES`, the limit divided by 64 KB, except for the programs `fixture.browser_page_environment` names, whose native peak stays below the size of their data because pages never touched are never committed (`heap_lazy_zero`, `region_reuse`, `rt_heap_release` and `ssa_fill_memory`), while the browser runtime zeroes every page it hands out. It skips entirely when `node` is not on `PATH`. With `LOW_GC=stress` in its environment the suite runs the programs under the stress option of `### WebAssembly`: it builds each module with `LOW_GC` removed, because the compiler's own collector would also stress, and runs it with `node` and the `host.mjs` embedded in the harness. Its summary line counts three groups apart from the passes and failures:
  - Skipped: programs the loader rejects with a `browser/wasm32 has no` message because they reach `std/sys` or a C library, and `region_freed`, whose use after a region faults only where a region unmaps its memory.
  - Unsupported by the back end: programs whose build fails with a `browser/wasm32:` message, such as `wait_readable` and `wait_writable`.
  - Any other build failure is a failure.
- The time zone programs read zone rules from the embedded `std/time/tzdata`, because host databases differ by release and by build (Debian's includes `backzone`). They read the host database only for the behaviour of `zone.named` and `zone.local`, through zones whose rules every build agrees on.
- The `std/cdp` programs print `launch failed` when no browser starts.
- `compiler/tests/low` compiles each program twice in one process and requires identical SSA form and assembly: every `testdata/run` program for Linux release, every benchmark for Linux release, Linux dev and Windows, and every `testdata/windows` program for Windows. It does the same for the `browser/wasm32` module: every `testdata/run` program and std example the browser target builds, and `compiler/playground`, are compiled twice for the release profile and must give identical module bytes. A program the loader or the back end rejects for the browser target is skipped. It also builds a program with `low build` and installs it with `low install`, each from inside the program's directory and by its path, runs the results and checks the path each install prints. In projects of its own it checks that a `gui` declaration gives a program the GUI subsystem under `low build`, `low run` and `low install` and a sibling `program` the console subsystem, that `--subsystem` overrides the declaration, that the `icon`, `manifest`, `version` and `file_info` declarations give every program of the project, console or windowed, the resources they name under `low build` and `low install` and give a program of another project none, that `low test` builds a console harness, that `low build`, `low run` and `low install` with no program take the declared ones, that a project with a modules directory builds, installs and tests by import path from the root and from inside the directory, and that a dependency's `program` and `gui` change nothing in the dependent. It builds `low` and runs `low test` on a small project in a temporary directory with its own `LOW_CACHE`, which shows that passing tests are replayed until the file, the environment variable or the directory listing they read changes, that a failing test and a test that starts a process are never replayed and that `--nocache` replays nothing.
- `compiler/tests/ir` requires `ir.is_reducible` of every function's control-flow graph after optimisation, with the release profile, over `compiler/low`, every benchmark and every `testdata/run` program, and names each irreducible function by program. It also checks that predicate on small graphs with one and with two entries into a cycle, and that a callee over the size limit is inlined when its call is its only reference and kept when it has two.
- `compiler/tests/compiler` compiles inline programs, benchmarks and fixtures and checks what the optimiser and code generator did with them: inlining, specialisation, bounds checks, register use, string comparison, switch lowering and allocation. It also checks the project root, the `build.low` reader, including the `program`, `gui` and `modules` declarations, where imports and tested modules are placed under a modules directory, and version ranges.
- `compiler/tests/fetch` publishes tagged versions to local `git` repositories, served in place of `https://` through `GIT_CONFIG_*`, and checks that resolution picks the newest accepted tag, keeps a locked version, moves with `update` and names every range when none is accepted. It skips without `git`.
- `compiler/tests/check` requires each public builtin in `compiler/check/builtins.low` to have an entry in `## Builtins` with the same name, parameters and `mut` markers, and each entry to name a builtin.
- `compiler/tests/lsp` starts the server over its protocol and checks diagnostics, hover, go-to-definition, references, rename, completion, signature help, inlay hints and symbols. One test opens each `testdata/run` fixture of at most 5,000 bytes and requests hover and definition at every word. Further tests open a module's test file and check that it answers every request through the test build of its module, that edits to the module's other files reach it, and that no answer names a generated file.
- `compiler/tests/editors` compares the keyword, contextual keyword, builtin function, type, constant and constraint lists of the editor grammars and `site/gen/highlight.low` with the lexer and the checker, requires the copied editor files to match their sources, and requires the Zed `rev` to name a commit whose `editors/tree-sitter-low/src` equals the current one. The rev check skips outside a git checkout.
- The UI tests are listed under UI tests.
- `compiler/tests/hot` tests reload against running programs on Linux and on Windows hosts:
  - state kept across a code swap;
  - functions added;
  - compile errors leaving the program running;
  - heap data surviving a reload while the collector runs;
  - stored function values, closures, callbacks and spawned threads running patched code;
  - a program with more functions than the smallest `fn_table`;
  - instances of an edited generic function patched;
  - restarts on layout, signature and capture changes.
- `compiler/lowfuzz` generates well-typed, terminating programs from a choice stream (`compiler/fuzz/gen`), predicts their output with a reference interpreter (`compiler/fuzz/model`), builds each for the host with the release profile and with the dev profile, the same optimisation and hot-reload code generation as `low build` and `low dev`, runs both, and reports any of these (`compiler/fuzz/oracle`):
  - compiler panic;
  - rejected program;
  - link failure;
  - timeout;
  - release/dev difference;
  - difference from the prediction;
  - browser difference: when `node` is on `PATH` and `-browser` is not false, the oracle also builds each case for `browser/wasm32` through the same hidden `__build` command, runs the module under `node` with the `host.mjs` that `wasm.host_script` returns, and compares its stdout and exit status with the native release run. The browser side is skipped, with one notice, when `node` is missing.

  The generator covers every integer width, `f32` and `f64`, shifts, conversions, `if` expressions, functions, and `for` loops with labelled `break` and `continue`. Float values are observed through clamped integer conversions and NaN comparisons.
- `compiler/fuzz/reduce` minimises each new signature. It removes functions, parameters and statements, unwraps `if` and loop bodies, replaces expressions with literals or their operands, and shrinks literals, keeping only candidates that still produce the signature. `-reduce-tests` bounds the compiler runs.
  - The minimised program is saved under `-out`, one per distinct signature.
  - With `-fixtures` it is also written there as a `testdata/run` fixture (`.low` with the predicted `.out` and `.err`, named by content hash) that fails until the bug is fixed.
- `compiler/tests/asm` assembles programs with `as` or `clang` and requires the same object bytes. Every program carries the same runtime assembly, so the corpus is every `testdata/windows` program, two benchmarks and the `testdata/run` programs that together contain the most distinct instruction forms (mnemonic, operand classes, addressing forms, directives), each for Linux and Windows. `LOW_ASM_FULL=1` covers every `testdata/run` and `testdata/windows` program and every benchmark. The same program also links every corpus program, `compiler/low` and the programs that link C libraries through the text path and through the chunk path for Linux and Windows, and requires identical images. `compiler/tests/link` links programs with `ld` or `lld-link` and requires the same segments, sections, symbols and loaded bytes for ELF and the same bytes for PE. `LOW_LINK_FULL=1` covers every `testdata/run` program and benchmark. It also reads the `.rsrc` section of PE images back: the resource tree and the icon, version and manifest resources in it, the version block against the bytes `llvm-rc` writes for the same strings, the numbers of the fixed version and the resources `pe` rejects.
  - The padding and relaxation choices these tests compare differ between tool releases, so `compiler/oracletool` pins GNU binutils 2.47 and LLVM 23.1.4. The tests skip when a tool is missing or reports another version.
